This chronicle unfolds in the wind-scoured, salt-kissed corners of Elizabethan Devon, where Atlantic gales carry the sharp tang of distant uncharted shores and every cottage hearth holds a sailor’s half-spun tale of gold, glory, and ghostly omens seen far out beyond the horizon. These are the true, vivid adventures that will unfurl across the pages ahead, stitched from quayside letters, tavern tales, and parish records of the bold men of Bideford and Clovelly who sailed west to claim their fortune under the flag of the Virgin Queen:
First, you shall learn how Plymouth mariner Mr. John Oxenham spied a strange white bird wheeling over empty ocean, the unshakable omen that would lead him to his tragic, unmarked fate on the Spanish Main. Next comes the homecoming of fiery, sea-bitten Amyas Leigh, stepping off the deck of a returning merchant man for the first time since boyhood, already hungry for the voyages he has dreamed of through every long, storm-tossed journey.
You will meet two quick-witted Welsh gentlemen who run with the hounds one hour and vanish like the hunted deer the next, and learn of the two very different ways a heart can be crossed in love: one soft and sorrowful enough to break a gentle man, one sharp enough to turn a pious young scholar astray for decades. We will step across the threshold of Clovelly Court in the days when it was a safe haven for privateers and sea-rovers, and wander the wild, untamed combes of Devon’s far western edge, where gorse blooms gold all summer and outlaws hide from the king’s men in hollowed-out cliff caves.
We shall recount the grim, tragical full history of John Oxenham, whose name would become legend across every Devon quayside, before joining the noble Brotherhood of the Rose: that secret, oath-bound band of mariners who swore to stand together against Spaniard, storm, and the cruel officers of the Inquisition. You will sit at the raucous Christmas feast Amyas throws for his ragtag crew, watch the Mayor of Bideford strike a devil’s bargain that he will come to bitterly regret, and follow zealous young Eustace Leigh as he crosses paths with the Pope’s own legate deep in a European seminary.
There is a feast for the ages to attend, when the entire company of Bideford Bridge dines at the great manor of Annery, before we all line Plymouth’s docks to cheer as Sir Francis Drake’s legendary *Golden Hind* sails back into harbour, heavy with New World plunder. We will hear the old wild tale of Salvation Yeo, the puritan mariner who slew the cursed king of the local outlaw band the Gubbings, before we learn how simple, honest Mr. John Brimblecombe learned exactly how sacred an oath sworn before God truly is.
We shall sail across the Atlantic ourselves aboard the good ship *Rose*, through her most chivalric, daring adventure of all, making landfall on Barbados only to find the island entirely empty of all human inhabitants. Next we raid the pearl fisheries of Margarita, outrun Spanish cannon fire at La Guayra, and witness the brutal clash between Spanish war bloodhounds and the great English mastiffs the mariners brought with them for the fight. We will kneel with the whole crew to take communion beneath a gnarled old higuerote tree on a foreign shore, and face the unspeakable horrors of the Spanish Inquisition running rampant across the Indies.
Our voyage will carry us up the green, snake-haunted banks of the River Meta, where Amyas will be tested by the most terrible temptation the devil can lay before him, before we fall upon the great Spanish gold train hauling plunder across the jungle, and capture the vast, treasure-laden great galleon that no Englishman had ever dared seize before. It is there that Salvation Yeo, who had searched for his stolen little daughter for decades, will find her at last, alive and well.
When Amyas makes his third and final homecoming to Devon, he finds the kingdom in turmoil: the great Virginia colonial fleet is held back by the Queen’s own urgent command, and old veteran Admiral John Hawkins speaks out fiercely against all the cowards and croakers who say England cannot stand against Spain’s coming invasion. The Great Armada, that fabled fleet of a hundred galleons, sails up the Channel, and in the heat of the final battle Amyas will cast his sword into the foaming waves, his long quest over, his penance done. At last, the apple he has waited twenty years to see fall will drop from the bough, and his storm-battered life will find its quiet rest.
*Westward Ho* — the cry that sent a generation of Englishmen sailing over the horizon, to write their names into the uncharted history of the New World.

Any wanderer who has drifted through the soft, sun-dappled countryside of North Devon will surely know the little white-washed port of Bideford, climbing gently upward from its broad tidal river where yellow sands glint low beneath the ebb, its many-arched medieval bridge strung across the current where salmon leap and wait for the autumn spate. To the west, sweet upland slopes roll back, and above the town the hills crowd close, cushioned deep in ancient oak woods where fern-fringed slate crags jut out here and there through the leaves. Further down the coast the hills soften, melting away into low rounded knolls, patchwork fields of red loam and lush green pasture, until they sink at last into wide hazy salt flats, rich marsh meadows, and rolling dunes where the River Torridge meets its sister the Taw, and the two flow quiet together out toward the thundering Atlantic surf that booms unceasing over the harbour bar.

Beneath its soft, almost Mediterranean sky, fanned day and night by sharp, briny ocean breeze that keeps both harsh winter frosts and the sweltering midland summer thunder-heats far at bay, the little town has stood pleasantly for nigh on eight hundred years. The first Grenville, cousin to William the Conqueror, settled here fresh from subjugating South Wales, gathering around him loyal Saxon serfs, golden-curled free Norse rovers, and dark, stocky Silurian Britons from the Swansea coast, a mingled bloodline that still gives the people of the Devon coast their unusual strength of mind, sharp wit, and even in these leveling modern days, their distinct, striking beauty of face and form.

But in the year this tale takes place, Bideford was no sleepy coastal backwater whose quay was only haunted by a handful of small trading smacks. It was one of the greatest ports in all of England: it furnished seven full ships to fight the Spanish Armada, and even a full century later, chroniclers recorded it sent more vessels out on the northern trade routes than any other English port save for London and Topsham. It was the beating heart of a thriving local civilization of bold seafarers and merchants – small, perhaps, measured against the colossal industrial efforts of our modern age, but no man worth his salt dare mock the day of small things, when that day proved to be the dawn of something mighty. England owes the very foundation of its naval and commercial glory to the sea-labor and daring of Bideford, Dartmouth, Topsham, Plymouth (then no more than a tiny fishing hamlet), and a dozen other little western coastal towns. It is to the men of Devon – the Drakes, the Hawkinses, the Gilberts, the Raleighs, the Grenvilles, the Oxenhams, and a whole host of forgotten worthies the world has yet to honor as they deserve – that England owes its trade, its colonies, and very existence. Had they not first crippled the ill-gotten hoards of Spain through their bold West Indian raids, and then crushed the last great invasion force of the Spanish king in Britain’s Salamis, the glorious fight of 1588, what would we be now but a papist vassal state of a global tyranny as cruel as old pagan Rome, and far more devilish in its cruelty?

I write this book in memory of these men: their voyages and their battles, their faith and their valor, their heroic lives and no less heroic deaths. If at times my tone rings a little stilted, a little grand, you must excuse me for the sake of my subject, which was always fit to be sung rather than spoken – fit to be proclaimed to every true English heart not as a common novel, but as an epic, the kind the songs of Troy and the tales of the Persian Wars once were to the old Greeks.

One bright summer’s afternoon in 1575, a tall, fair-haired boy lingered along the Bideford quay, clad in his scholar’s gown, satchel and slate slung over one shoulder, staring wide-eyed at the bobbing masts and the yelling, gesturing sailors. He had just passed the foot of High Street when he came opposite one of the many waterfront taverns whose windows looked straight out over the river. Merchants and local gentlemen sat in the open bay window, sipping their afternoon draught of sack, while a tight crowd of sailors had gathered on the doorstep, leaning in to listen earnestly to the man standing at their centre. The boy, starved for every scrap of sea news, squeezed his way through the throng until he stood among the other young sailor lads, peeping and whispering under the elbows of the grown men, and heard the following speech, roared out in a loud, bold Devonshire accent, peppered thick with unapologetic oaths:

“If you don’t believe me, go sail there and see for yourselves, or stay here rotting till you grow all over blue mould. I swear on my honor as a gentleman, I saw it with these two eyes, and so did Salvation Yeo there, through a lower window in the custom house. We measured the heap ourselves, as I am a baptized man: seventy foot long, ten foot broad, twelve foot high, all solid silver bars, each bar weighing between thirty and forty pounds. And Captain Drake turns to us and says: ‘There, my Devon lads, I’ve brought you all to the mouth of the world’s treasure house, and it’s your own fault now if you don’t sweep it out as empty as a dried stockfish.’”

“Why didn’t you bring any of that silver home with you then, Mr. Oxenham?” a voice called from the crowd.

“Why weren’t you there to help carry it? We would have brought the whole hoard away safe enough, young Drake and I had already pried open the custom house door, but Captain Drake drops dead in a sudden faint. When we knelt down to look at him, he had a gash in his leg you could have laid three fingers across, his boots were full of blood, and had been for an hour or more. But his heart was so fierce he never felt the wound till he collapsed. His brother and I dragged him back to the boats, him kicking and struggling the whole way, yelling at us to let him go back and fight, every step he took in the sand leaving a pool of blood. And tell me, ye sons of soggy salted herrings, wasn’t saving him worth more than all that dirty silver? Silver we can get again, brave lads: there’s more fish in the sea than ever swam into a net, and more silver in Nombre de Dios than would pave every street in the whole west country. But for captains like Franky Drake? Heaven never makes more than one at a time. If we lose him, good bye to all England’s luck, that’s what I say, and any man that disagrees can name his weapons, and I’m his man.”

The man who delivered this harangue was tall and broad and sturdy, with a florid black-bearded face, and bold, restless dark eyes. He leaned against the tavern wall, legs crossed and hands planted akimbo, and to the wide-eyed schoolboy he seemed a grand, magnificent figure, fit to be a prince or a duke at the very least. Against all the strict sumptuary laws of the time that reserved crimson velvet for nobility, he wore a full suit of that rich red cloth, a little worn around the cuffs perhaps. A long Spanish rapier hung at his hip, alongside a brace of daggers with gaudy, gem-encrusted hilts. His fingers sparkled with rings, two heavy gold chains looped around his neck, and large gold hoops hung in his ears, behind one of which he had tucked a bright red rose jauntily into his glossy black curls. On his head he wore a broad velvet Spanish hat, fastened with a huge gold clasp that held a whole perfect quetzal bird, its iridescent plumage of fretted golden-green glowing like one solid, uncut precious stone.

As he finished his speech, he took off the hat and stared at the bird perched on it. “Look ye, my lads! Did you ever see such a fowl as that before? That’s the bird the old Aztec kings of Mexico forbade anyone but themselves to wear. And so I wear it – I, John Oxenham of South Tawton – as a sign to all brave Devon lads that if the Spaniards think they are masters of the Indians, we are the masters of the Spaniards.” He settled the hat back on his head.

A murmur of applause rippled through the crowd, though one sailor muttered that he doubted the Spaniards were far too many for them to fight.

“Too many?” Oxenham roared. “How many men did we sail out of Plymouth Sound with to take Nombre de Dios? Seventy-three, no more. Before we ever sighted the Spanish Main, half of them were gastados – used up, as the Dons say – rotting from scurvy. We picked up Captain Rawse of Cowes and his thirty men at Port Pheasant, and that left us barely fifty-three hands total, that handful of men picked the lock on the entire New World! And the only man we lost was our trumpeter, who stood out in the middle of the main square braying like an ass instead of taking care of his neck like a sensible Christian. I tell you those Spaniards are rank cowards, just like every bully that ever lived. They pray to a woman, the idolatrous rascals! No wonder they fight like frightened girls.”

“You’m right, captain!” sang out a tall, gaunt weatherbeaten man standing at Oxenham’s elbow. “One westcountry man can fight two eastcountrymen, and an eastcountryman can beat three Dons any day. Eh, my lads of Devon?
For O! it’s the herrings and the good brown beef,
And the cider and the cream so white;
O! they are the making of the jolly Devon lads,
For to play, and eke to fight.”

“Come on then!” Oxenham called, waving a hand. “Who lists? Who lists? Who’ll make his fortune?
Oh, who will join, jolly mariners all?
And who will join, says he, O!
To fill his pockets with the good red goold,
By sailing on the sea, O!”

“Who’ll list?” the gaunt old man – Salvation Yeo, the gunner’s mate – shouted back. “Now’s your time! We’ve got forty men waiting for us at Plymouth right now, ready to sail the minute we step back into the harbor, and we just need a dozen Bideford lads, and a boy or two, and then we’m off and away, to make our fortunes or go straight to heaven.
Our bodies in the sea so deep,
Our souls in heaven to rest!
Where valiant seamen, one and all,
Hereafter shall be blest!”

“Now then,” Oxenham challenged the crowd, “you won’t let the Plymouth men say Bideford lads dare not follow them? North Devon against South, it is. Who’ll join? It’s no long hard voyage, after all, the sea will be as smooth as a duck pond the second you round Cape Finisterre. I’ll bet twenty pounds I can sail a little Clovelly herring boat all the way there and back without shipping a single bucket of seawater. Don’t think you’re buying a pig in a poke! I know the route, and Salvation Yeo here, who was our gunner’s mate, knows the charts of the Spanish Main just as well as he knows the English Channel, maybe better. Ask him to show you his map, and he’ll rattle off every reef and crag as well as Drake himself.”

At that, Yeo pulled a great white buffalo horn out from under his arm, its surface covered in rough etchings of coastlines and islands, and held it up for the admiring crowd to see. “See here, boys all, behold the picture of the place, drawn as natural as life. I got it off a Portuguese sailor down in the Azores, he pricked every mark onto it himself, every coast he ever sailed, every thing he ever saw. Take it in your hands, Simon Evans, take it in your hands, look it over, and I’ll warrant you’ll know the way out to the Indies in five minutes as well as any shark that swims the southern seas.”

The horn was passed from hand to hand, and while the crowd gawked at it, Oxenham called through the open tavern window for a great tankard of spiced sack, which he passed around the ring after the horn.

The schoolboy, who had devoured every word and sight of the gathering, had by this time edged himself right into the inner circle, and stood face to face with the hero wearing the glowing green quetzal, craning to see the wonder as close as he could. When he watched one sailor after another hand the horn back and step forward to sign on for Oxenham’s voyage, his soul burned hot inside him to get a proper look at that wondrous horn, which seemed as magical as the enchanted horns in the old tales of Tristram or Ariosto. When the crowd began to break up and Oxenham turned to lead his new recruits into the tavern, the boy stepped forward boldly and asked if he might look at the marvel one more time. Yeo handed it over at once.

To his wide, astonished gaze, the horn revealed whole cities and sheltered harbors, dragons and elephants, whales locked in battle with sharks, great Spanish plate ships loaded down with silver, islands swarming with apes and fringed with palm trees, every place marked out with its name, and scrawled here and there in English, no doubt added by Oxenham’s own hand: “Here is gold,” “Much gold and silver.” The boy turned it round and round, lingering over every mark, and thought the man who owned it more fortunate than any emperor or khan on earth. Oh, if he could only possess that horn, what else could he ever need to make him perfectly happy?

“I say,” he blurted out. “Will you sell this?”

“Aye, marry, I’d sell my own soul too, if I could get the worth of it.”

“I want the horn – I don’t want your soul. For all I know it’s a stale old sole rotting down at the bottom of the bay, and there are plenty of fresh ones to be had.” He fumbled in his pocket for a long moment, and pulled out a single worn silver tester, the only coin he owned. “Will this buy it?”

“That? No, not twenty of them would buy it.”

The boy thought for a moment about what a proper knight errant would do in such a spot, and squared his shoulders. “Tell you what, I’ll fight you for it.”

“Thank ‘ee, sir!” Oxenham laughed. “Break this little jackanapes’s head for him, Yeo.”

“Call me jackanapes one more time, and I’ll break yours, sir.” The boy lifted his fist fiercely.

Oxenham stared at him for a minute, grinning. “Tut, tut, my little man! Pick a fight with someone your own size, spare little old fellows like me.”

“I may be a boy in age, sir, but I’ve got a man’s fist. I turn fifteen this month, and I know how to answer anyone who dares insult me.”

“Fifteen, my young cockerel? You look more like twenty.” Oxenham glanced at him admiringly, taking in his broad limbs, sharp bright blue eyes, curling golden hair, and round, honest open face. “Fifteen? If I had half a dozen lads like you, I’d make knights of every last one of them before I died. Eh, Yeo?”

“He’ll do,” Yeo rumbled. “He’ll be a brave fighting gamecock in a year or two, if he dares ruffle up that soon at a tough old sea dog like the captain.”

The whole crowd laughed, Oxenham loudest of all, then he bade the boy tell him why he was so desperate for the horn.

“Because,” the boy said, looking him straight in the eye, “I want to go to sea. I want to see the Indies. I want to fight the Spaniards. Even though I’m a gentleman’s son, I’d far rather be a cabin boy on your ship than stay here forever stuck at school.” He blurted the whole thing out fast and fierce, then dropped his head shyly.

“And you shall go!” Oxenham roared, swearing a great hearty oath. “You shall take a whole Spanish galleon for your prize, and dine off roasted Dons for supper. Whose son are you, my gallant fellow?”

“Mr. Leigh of Burrough Court.”

“Bless his soul! I know him as well as I know the Eddystone Reef, and I’ve eaten many a good meal at his table. Who’s supping with him tonight?”

“Sir Richard Grenville.”

“Dick Grenville? I didn’t know he was in town. Go home right now and tell your father John Oxenham will come and keep him company at supper. Off with you! I’ll smooth all this over with the good gentleman, and you shall have your turn sailing with us. And as for the horn, Yeo, give it to the boy, and I’ll give you a gold noble to pay you for it.”

“Not a penny from you, captain. If the young master will take a poor old sailor’s gift, it’s his, for the sake of his love for the calling, and Heaven send him all the luck on the seas.” The good old gunner, generous as any true sailor always is, thrust the horn straight into the boy’s hands and walked away fast to avoid his thanks.

“And now,” Oxenham called to his new recruits, “my merry men all, make up your minds what sort of men you intend to be before you take your shilling bounty. I want none of your lurching longshore vermin, who take five pounds from one captain, ten from the next, and then hide away under some tavern girl’s shawl or in a cellar till the ship sails without them. If any man here has that sort of trick in him, he’d better cut himself up and salt himself down in a pork barrel before he ever meets me again. I swear by this light, if I catch him, even seven years from now, and I don’t cut his throat right there out in the street, it’s a pity. But if any man will be a true brother to me, I will be a true brother to him, no

Old Epigram on Drake

Five years have slipped past. It is nine o’clock on a still, glinting November morning in Bideford, and the parish church bells are ringing two full hours past their usual scheduled service time. They do not toll with the sober, measured pace the town knows so well: every five minutes they burst out into giddy, leaping peals, tumbling headlong in unashamed ecstasy that no bell-ringer can rein in.

Bideford’s streets blaze like a cut flower bed, thronged with seamen, town burghers, and their wives and daughters all decked out in their finest holiday finery. Garlands of autumn leaves and late wild roses are strung across the road, every upper window draped with rich woven tapestries that catch the sun. The ships bobbing in the river pool fly every flag and pennant they own, firing volleys of cannon shot of every size to roar their own joy out across the water. Every stable within miles is crammed full of horses, and Sir Richard Grenville’s manor house is busier than a popular roadside tavern: servants run back and forth with tankards of ale and platters of spiced beef, grooms unsaddle steaming mounts, and the whole place hums with unruly celebration.

The little churchyard is packed tight with women, and through them streams all the gentle blood of North Devon: tall, stately lords and fair, sharp-witted ladies, the kind of ruling class that earned their place leading the people not just by birth, but by personal courage, physical grace, and sharp, learned minds. First comes the Countess of Bath, escorted cap-in-hand by Sir Richard Grenville (her husband the Earl Bourchier is in London attending the queen). Close behind are the Bassets of Umberleigh, the Carys of wild, beautiful Clovelly, a whole host of Fortescues from Wear, Buckland, and half a dozen other family estates, the Coles of Slade, the Stukelys of Affton, the St Legers of Annery, the Coffins of Portledge, and even the Coplestones who rode thirty miles all the way from Eggesford. Last, but so far from least that the crowd automatically parts to make space for them, comes Sir John Chichester of Ralegh, followed in the old patriarchal fashion by his eight daughters and three of his five famous sons. One of his boys is off fighting in Ireland to avenge his murdered brother, and will one day rule there wisely as Lord Deputy and Baron of Belfast. At the church lychgate he meets his cousin from Arlington, who trails behind him four daughters and nineteen sons: the very last of that brood is still passing the town hall, while the eldest has already reached the gate. They laugh and joke as they step aside to let the elder, though shorter, Chichester line go first, and the whole crowd files into the church.

The churchwardens and sidesmen, who have never had to seat so many grand, status-conscious guests in their lives, bustle about red-faced and flustered, nearly frantic as they try to sort out who takes precedence over whom. They end up begging Sir Richard Grenville to step in and tell them which families belong to the elder branch, which carry eight quarterings on their coat of arms, and which only four, before they accidentally spark a generations-long feud between half the fine ladies of the shire. All the elderly men are already settled safely in the corporation pews, and the young lords are not bothering themselves with the seating disputes: they only care about grabbing a spot where they can get a good look at the ladies.

At last a hush falls. Every head turns toward the church doors, and distant music drifts up: flutes, hautboys, drums and trumpets, braying and thundering merrily until they stop right on the doorstep. The churchwardens hurry down with their wands of office, and a soft murmur ripples through the congregation: many a woman wipes happy, unbidden tears from her cheeks, even a few men mutter quiet blessings, as the wonder of the day steps over the threshold. The rector does not open the regular morning service: he launches straight into the ancient, beloved thanksgiving liturgy for a victory at sea.

The whole town has gone wild with that old “pious mirth and goodly joy” only remembered in fragments now in modern Psalm translations, and for good reason. All eyes fix on five weather-beaten seamen, their coats pinned with knots and silk ribbons stitched for them by loving hands. Most of all, they stare at the gigantic figure walking at their head: he is barely more than a boy, no beard yet on his jaw, but built like Hercules, standing a full head and shoulders above every other man in the congregation, his loose golden hair spilling down over his shoulders. As the five men walk instinctively up to the altar rail and drop to their knees, every eye turns to a quiet pew at the side, where Mrs. Leigh of Burrough has hidden her face in her hands, her hood shaking with soft, overcome sobs of joy. This is Amyas Leigh of Burrough, alongside John Staveley, Michael Heard, Jonas Marshall of Bideford, and Thomas Braund of Clovelly: the very first English mariners to circumnavigate the globe with Francis Drake, home at last to give God thanks for bringing them safe across the world.

The story of how Amyas came to sail with Drake stretches back years, nearly to the point our first tale left off. For more than a year after Mr. Oxenham sailed away, young Amyas kept his promise to stay home, save for the occasional bursts of fierce, unruly energy common to all strong-willed teenage boys. His Latin studies still made barely any progress, but his real-world education thrived: still barely out of childhood, he was already becoming an excellent archer, a fearless rider, and a sharp hand at the old traditional school of buckler swordplay. Then his father traveled down to Exeter for the assizes, and caught the gaol fever that ran rampant in overcrowded, unhygienic prisons of the era. He sickened right there in the courtroom, and died within a week.

Mrs. Leigh was left to raise her fierce, untamed lion-cub of a son alone, guided only by her own faith and quiet strength. She had loved her husband deeply and reverently. He had been a often peevish, melancholy, disappointed man: his father’s foolish mismanagement had left the family estate deep in debt, and his own youthful ambition had drawn him to Elizabeth’s court, where he wasted years and his whole fortune chasing after empty honors. He was a man with every possible gift save the ability to use them: a fine scholar, a polished courtier, and a brave soldier, but past forty he found himself with no steady career, no clear purpose in life. A stubborn shyness, fierce pride, and delicate sense of honor always kept him from playing the cruel, political game required to get ahead at court, even as he still ached for the prizes it offered. Eventually he met Miss Foljambe, a quiet young lady from Derbyshire then serving in the queen’s household, who was just as tired as he was of the corruption and vanity of court life. The two fell in love, won the queen’s grumbling permission to marry (she was never pleased to see her ladies leave her service), and retreated to the quiet manor of Burrough to live in peace. Her husband knew full well what a treasure he had found in her.

Mrs. Leigh had been, since her girlhood, one of those noble, unassuming English Protestant women of that heroic age: devout, free of superstition, and never cruel or judgmental. A faint, gentle sadness hung over her all her life, forged in the fires of Mary Tudor’s persecution. She could clearly remember, twenty-five years prior, the burning of blind martyr Joan Waste at Derby, and of Joyce Lewis, a gentlewoman of good birth just like herself. She still sometimes woke at night hearing her own mother’s cries to God in her head, begging him either to spare her the agony of being burned alive, or to give her the strength to bear it, as other Protestant martyrs had before her. Her mother had been a lady in Catherine of Aragon’s bedchamber, the closest friend and devoted follower of Anne Askew. She had sat in the crowds at Smithfield, blood running cold in her veins, to see Anne carried to the stake in a chair, her limbs broken and crippled by the rack, just a month after she had been the paragon of beauty and wit in Henry VIII’s court. Thunder had rolled overhead that day, heavy rain drops hissing on the crackling firewood, as if the heavens themselves wept at the injustice.

She had hidden for months among the caves and moors of the Peak District during Mary’s reign, a marked heretic, and only survived because her husband had boldly declared to every constable, justice, and even bishop who came near his home that he would run through the body any man who dared serve an arrest warrant on his wife, no matter their rank. The shadow of that suffering never fully left her, and it touched Amyas too as he grew up in her quiet household. She lived fully for her husband, her children, and the poor of the nearby town of Northam, and was beloved by all the great Devon families around her: the Grenvilles, Fortescues, Chichesters, all of whom admired her husband’s sharp wit and respected his learning. She followed quiet, gentle spiritual austerities that her husband often teased her about gently, but no thought of trying to earn God’s favor or appease any supposed wrath ever crossed her mind. She was raised on the freest, most hopeful doctrines of Luther’s school, and the little ancient text *Alt-Deutsch Theologie* – which Luther claimed he owed more to than any book save the Bible and St Augustine – was her constant daily comfort and guide.

Now just past forty, a widow still beautiful of face and figure, she carried a divine calm about her that rippled through every word and gesture: a sweetness forged by storm as much as sunshine, a gift the world had never given her and could never take away. No wonder Sir Richard and Lady Grenville loved her, no wonder her children adored her. When Amyas had grieved his fill of his father’s death, he realized that it was no longer his mother’s turn to care for him: it was his turn to protect her. The very day after his father’s funeral, he walked straight from the grammar school up to Sir Richard Grenville’s house, and asked to see his godfather.

“You must be my father now, sir,” he said, steady and unflinching.

Sir Richard looked at the boy’s broad, strong, earnest face, and swore a great, holy old oath “by oak, ash, and thorn” that he would be a father to him, and a brother to his mother, for Christ’s sake. Lady Grenville took Amyas by the hand and walked home with him to Burrough, where the two women fell into each other’s arms and wept together: one grieveing for the husband she had lost, the other, with a quiet prophetic instinct, grieving for the loss she knew awaited her, for she knew her husband’s fiery, fearless spirit would never die peacefully in his bed, but fall sword in hand fighting for duty and honor, as he prayed he would every night. They swore an everlasting sisterhood that they kept for the rest of their lives. Amyas still rode, shot, boxed, and wandered the quayside at Sir Richard’s side, for Mrs. Leigh was far too wise to change a single part of the rough, robust training her husband had planned for his younger son.

Her elder son Frank had long since chosen the very different path of quiet learning that she had once hoped both her boys would follow. He had won renown at Bideford grammar school, then Exeter College, Oxford, where he became a close friend of Sir Philip Sidney and a whole circle of other brilliant, promising young men of rank. In the summer of 1572, on his way to Heidelberg University, he stopped over in Paris with letters of recommendation to Sir Francis Walsingham at the English embassy – a twist of luck that saved his life, just as it saved Sidney’s, during the St Bartholomew’s Day Massacre. He stayed at Heidelberg for two years, earning more respect from everyone who knew him, turning down Sidney’s repeated invitations to travel to Italy because he would not be a financial burden on his parents. Instead, he took a position as tutor to two young German princes, traveling with them to Italy to complete their princely education. He talked about the art of writing sonnets with Tasso, debated history with Sarpi, listened to the radical, unproven theories of Galileo with equal parts awe and disbelief, took his pupils to Venice to have their portraits painted by Paolo Veronese, wandered the palaces built by Palladio, watched Tintoretto work on his huge, dramatic canvases, and even secured a private audience with the ancient, white-bearded Titian in his studio, who told the young Englishman stories of Raphael, Michelangelo, the sack of Rome, and the time King Francis I of France had knelt down to pick up his paintbrush for him. He even traveled to Rome, much to the shock of his more strictly Protestant acquaintances back home, met the sitting Pope, heard Palestrina conduct his own music beneath the dome of St Peter’s, until the memory of the blood in the Paris streets he had witnessed during the massacre woke him from his fascination with the beauty of Catholic art and ritual.

A few months before his father died, he dropped his pupils back home in Germany, and was dismissed with generous rich gifts. Mrs. Leigh’s heart swelled at the thought her wanderer was coming home at last – but instead, a letter arrived from Frank, written unaware of his father’s death, describing his travels through the Alps and the valleys of the Waldenses, and saying that he had made friends with the great Hungarian scholar Stephanus Parmenius, commonly called Budaeus, who had convinced him to sail down the Danube to Buda to complete his studies of Hungarian scholarship. For nearly two years he wrote letters home addressed to his father, never getting a reply, until he grew afraid something terrible had happened and hurried back to England, to find his mother a widow, and his brother Amyas already gone to the South Seas with Captain Francis Drake of Plymouth.

Even then, he was not allowed to settle down at home. Sir Richard, who could not tolerate idleness as anything but a sin, got him a position in Queen Elizabeth’s household within six months, secured through the influence of Carew, Sidney, and his uncle the Earl of Leicester. Where Amyas was huge and rough-hewn, Frank was delicately, strikingly beautiful, and he soon became a favorite of the queen’s court, basking in the favor of Gloriana, the attention of fair court ladies, and the fast friendship of Philip Sidney, who was then one of the most celebrated men in Europe, patron of all the poets and scholars of the age.

Mrs. Leigh, who had long since learned to live for her children rather than for herself, submitted without a single murmur, and only smiled at her stern friend Sir Richard. “You took my rough mastiff puppy away to sea,” she said, “and now you must needs take my gentle greyhound too.”

“Would you have your greyhound grow into a true, sturdy Cotswold hound that can pull down a ten-point stag?” Grenville replied, “or one of those silly smooth poppets Florence ladies lead around on leashes, with little bells around their necks and flannel petticoats wrapped around their loins, good for nothing but looking pretty?”

Mrs. Leigh submitted, and a few months later was rewarded with a personal letter from Queen Elizabeth herself, thanking her for “the loan of that most delicate and flawless crystal, the soul of your excellent son.” She praised her far above the famed ancient Roman matron Cornelia, who had only shown off her sons as her private jewels: “you, madam, who have two sons as precious as any that Roman lady ever bore, have lent them both, far beyond her courage, to your country and your queen. I hold myself deeply indebted to you for that gift, and with God’s grace I will repay it as befits us both.” Mrs. Leigh bedewed the letter with tears of joy, and tucked it away in her cedarwood box of precious household treasures, right next to all Frank’s diplomas and letters of recommendation from princes, scholars, and nobles across Europe. She would pore over the Latin texts for hours even though she barely understood a word, just to find the line that called him *Noster excellentissimus Franciscus Leighius Anglus* – “our most excellent Englishman Francis Leigh” – that filled her mother’s heart to bursting.

But why did Amyas sail off to the South Seas in the first place? He set out for two very ordinary reasons that had sent many a bolder man to far worse places long before him: first, because of a petty feud with an old schoolmaster, and second, because of a beautiful young girl. To tell that story, we turn back to that fateful afternoon in Bideford grammar school.

Vindex Brimblecombe, formerly a poor servitor student at Exeter College Oxford – known colloquially around town as Sir Vindex, per the custom of the era – was headmaster of the grammar school. At heart he was a godly, good-natured pedant, but like most schoolmasters of the flogging era, his soul had been hardened by years of unrestricted power to inflict pain on boys weaker than himself. That kind of power may well have been short, sharp, and wholesome for the boys, but for the man holding the rod it erodes all but the noblest of spirits of every trace of chivalry, tenderness for the weak, and even self-control. After Amyas’s father died, Vindex decided it was his Christian duty to take extra special care of the orphaned boy, and the only way he knew how to do that was to flog him once a day, rather than the usual two times a week. The consequence of that new routine would soon land back on the pedagogue himself.

Amyas had never once, for a single moment, let go of his dream of going to sea. When he could not wander the quay staring up at the tall masts of the merchant ships, or sit on the pebble ridge at Northam staring out across the wide blue ocean that seemed to call him out into endless, uncharted space, he passed the time in grammar class drawing imaginary ships and islands on his slate instead of paying attention to his Latin grammar.

One afternoon, he was deep in work on a masterpiece: a bird’s-eye view of an enchanted island, with a great stone castle on it, and a terrible fire-breathing dragon guarding the castle gates. In the foreground was a huge gallant ship flying

*“I know that Deformed; he has been a vile thief this seven years; he goes up and down like a gentleman: I remember his name.”* — *Much Ado About Nothing*

Amyas slept that night heavy-limbed from the day’s triumph, but his rest was far from easy. His mother and brother Frank, hovering over his pillow, could see his brow furrowed and his lips moving, as if his mind was tangled in a dozen half-spoken dreams.

No wonder. Even above the roar of the harbor crowds and the thrill of being home after years at sea, the memory of lost adventurer John Oxenham had sunk its hooks deep into his brain. All that evening, as he sat in the bay window where he had first met the man, he replayed every look, every gesture of Oxenham’s, turning the memory over and over till he crawled to bed, only for the vision to follow him straight into his sleep. One moment he found himself sailing due west, chasing the wake of the setting sun, straining to catch up to a tiny distant sail that he knew belonged to Oxenham’s lost sloop. A cold dread gnawed at him—if he did not overtake it in time, some unnameable horror would unfold. But his ship would not move. The great tangles of sargasso weed stretched for miles all around, wrapping their snaky fronds around his bows and dragging him to a standstill. He strained at the rigging, he willed the ship forward, till the sun dipped below the horizon and the world dropped to utter blackness. Then the moon rose.

Oxenham’s wreck was suddenly right alongside him. Her sails were tattered rags flapping in the gale, pitch streamed in black rivulets down her rotting sides, and her bulwarks crumbled at a touch. A line of dark shapes dangled along her mainyard: hanged men, swaying gently in the moonlight. Worst of all, slung from the yardarm directly over Amyas’s head, hung John Oxenham himself. His corpse’s eyes glowed with the pale cold light of the moon, and he beckoned, pointed, strove to speak but could not, not ahead toward home, but back the way they had come. When Amyas turned to look, the snow-capped peaks of the Andes glittered at his back, and he knew he was adrift in the South Seas, with the whole breadth of the Americas standing between him and Devon. The corpse kept pointing back, its face twisted with a yearning agony, as if its lips ached to spill some terrible secret, till Amyas jolted upright with a shout of raw terror, wide awake in his little curtained chamber at Burrough, the soft gray light of autumn morning seeping around the edges of his window.

Feverish and restless, he tossed for an hour before he gave up on sleep, pulled on his clothes, and tramped down to the shingle ridge he had swum in since he was a small boy. As he passed his mother’s door, he glanced in. The bed was untouched, its pillow never pressed that night. His mother stood kneeling at her prie-dieu in the thin dawn light, clad in her long white nightgown, lost in her prayers. He slipped in soft as a shadow and knelt beside her. She turned, smiled, slipped an arm around his shoulders, and went on praying silently. He knew those prayers were for him, and he prayed too: for her, for lost John Oxenham, for every last man of his vanished crew.

When she finally rose, she bent over him, brushed the stray yellow curls back from his forehead, and looked at him for a long, soft moment. No words were needed—there was no secret between these two, their souls as open and clear as glass. She kissed his brow, and as she turned away, a single tear dropped onto his forehead. He spied her small bare feet peeking out from under the hem of her gown, knelt, and kissed them again and again. When he looked up, grinning a little to excuse himself, he said, “You have such pretty feet, mother!”

She hid her feet behind her skirts with a soft, laughing huff, the instinct of a woman who had once been one of the greatest beauties of Devon, and still was, to anyone who could see deeper than rose-pink cheeks and glossy hair. “Your dear father said exactly that, thirty years back.”

“And I say it still. You were always beautiful. You are beautiful now.”

“What a silly boy you are, playing the lover to your old mother?” she chided gently. “Go on, take your swim, and think of younger ladies, if you can find any worthy of you.”

So he tramped off to the sea, and she went back to her prayers.

The great pebble ridge rose before him, a rampart of smooth gray boulders two miles long, heaped up over centuries by the bay’s own surges, curved and fitted and worn as neatly as if a mason had laid it by hand, holding back the high spring tides to protect the stretch of rich flat alluvial turf that ran behind it. Sniffing the sharp salt air like a young hound, he stripped off and plunged headfirst into the breakers, diving and rolling and tossing through the foam, till a hail rang out from the shore. He looked up, and saw his cousin Eustace Leigh standing tall on the crest of the ridge.

Amyas was half disappointed to see him. He had been daydreaming of Rose Salterne all the walk down, and had no use for a companion who would break that reverie on the way back. Still, it had been three years since they had seen each other, so he clambered out of the water, dressed quickly, while Eustace paced up and down on the turf inside the ridge.

Eustace was the son of the younger brother of the Leigh of Burrough, who had cut himself off from most of his family and his neighbors by remaining a stubborn Roman Catholic. He had converted to Protestantism under Edward VI, then back to Catholicism under Mary, and refused to flip a second time when Elizabeth took the throne—an uncharacteristic bit of honesty for the era, when hundreds of gentry shifted their faith as easily as they shifted their hats to keep their lands. He lived far out of the way, in a great rambling dark old house called Chapel, perched on the Atlantic cliffs in the parish of Moorwinstow, not far from Sir Richard Grenville’s home at Stow. The penal laws against recusants never troubled him; the crown had no interest in harassing quiet Catholic landowners who stayed out of conspiracy, and even the ones who courted martyrdom were mostly left alone till they went out of their way to provoke Elizabeth’s council. Hidden away at the far western edge of Devon, Chapel House was a perfect refuge; Jesuits and seminary priests slipped in and out all year round, unsuspected by most, hiding in tiny secret turret rooms and celebrating mass behind a trap door in the roof, playing at persecuted saints to their hearts’ content. Once, the local Puritan parson of Moorwinstow complained loudly to Sir Richard Grenville that Chapel House harbored “mass priests and idolatry,” demanding he enforce the new laws—but Grenville only berated him for being a meddlesome fool, threatened to run him out of the parish if he didn’t mind his own business, and the parson wisely settled for accepting Mr. Leigh’s dinner invitations and politely pretending not to notice the soft-spoken “tutor” who sat across the table from him at meals was actually a wandering Jesuit priest.

But those priests held a heavy weight over Eustace’s father Thomas Leigh’s conscience: he owned broad lands that had once belonged to Hartland Abbey. He was half-convinced he would be damned for holding stolen church property, but not quite convinced enough to give the lands back—just like the vast majority of Mary’s Catholic subjects, who were perfectly happy to return to the Church as long as they got to keep all the abbey plunder they had looted. Most compromised their guilty consciences by petted foreign priests, sending one of their sons off to Rheims or Douay as a sort of sin-offering, to be trained as a seminary priest. That was exactly what had happened to Eustace Leigh. His father had given his son over to the Jesuits, paying for the sin of his greed by sacrificing the fruit of his body to be turned into a loyal agent of their order.

And Eustace had made a very fine, polished dissembler. He was not cruel or bad at heart, not exactly—but the priests had spotted in him a talent for hazy, hysterical fear of the unseen, that vague devoutness that so easily curdles into double-dealing when it is twisted to their ends. Now he was tall and handsome, fair-skinned, with a high, broad forehead and a tiny tight mouth, his face set in a permanent, forced attempt at a smile: he knew a good Christian man should show cheer and charity, so he forced his lips up into dimples whenever he could, but his pre-planned pious smiles looked no more warm or natural than a skeleton’s rictus. He had returned to England only a month before, obeying a royal proclamation ordering all families to recall their children and wards studying overseas within four months—though he still carried out secret, unspoken missions for the Jesuit order that had raised him, the very business that had brought him into Bideford the night before.

He sat down beside Amyas on the shingle, glancing at him gently out of the corners of his eyes, as if he would not hurt even a fly on his cousin’s back. Amyas turned full to face him, grinning the broadest, heartiest grin, and held out a great paw of a hand. Eustace grabbed it rapturously, and they shook hard: Amyas’s grip was firm, steady, warm, as if to say plainly *I am so glad to see you*. Eustace’s thin white fingers pinched hard, sawing their joined hands violently up and down in a great show of warmth, as if to cry *Can’t you see how delighted I am?* It was the very opposite of a natural, unforced greeting.

“Easy there, old man,” Amyas laughed. “You’ll snap my elbow. Where’ve you wandered from?”

“From going to and fro in the earth, and walking up and down in it,” Eustace replied, with a little smug, mysterious nod.

“Like the devil, eh?” Amyas said. “Well, every man’s got his own pattern. How’s my uncle?”

Eustace was terrified of Amyas, more than any other man on earth. For one thing, he knew Amyas could kill him with a single blow. For another, he was perfectly certain Amyas despised him. Before Amyas had left for sea, Eustace could never win an argument with him, simply because Amyas wrote off all his pious Jesuit talk as “wind and moonshine” and treated him like a harmless, half-baked lunatic, too silly to waste a proper debate on. Eustace resented it bitterly, and swore to himself he would prove he was more than a match for his big, brute-force cousin—only to immediately twist himself into a spasm of self-reproach for prioritizing his own pride over “the glory of God,” as the priests had taught him.

Eustace began to murmur piously that he had prayed nightly for Amyas’s safe return, that the saints had interceded for him, especially the Blessed Virgin, the star of the sea that guided all mariners. Amyas cut him off. “Halt there, coz. If those saints are half the good fellows you say they are, they’d help me out even if I never asked.”

Just then Frank wandered over, greeted them both, and sat down on the shingle beside them. Amyas laughed and told his brother that Eustace was already trying to convert him, claiming the Virgin was responsible for all his safe voyages.

Frank smiled, and replied that Amyas owed his safety far more to the prayers of another peerless, spotless virgin: Queen Elizabeth herself, whose loyal subjects prayed for her seamen daily, and for whose glory they sailed to spread English power across the seas.

Eustace’s smile turned thin and venomous. “At least I know the patroness I name is an undefiled virgin.”

Both brothers’ faces darkened instantly. Amyas, lying back on the warm pebbles, called out to the gulls wheeling overhead, “I wonder what that Frenchman I beheaded in the Azores is thinking about that, right now.”

Frank stared. “You cut off a Frenchman’s head?”

“Aye, I did,” Amyas laughed, and told the tale: he and George Drake had stepped into a tavern, and found a loudmouthed French bully ranting against Queen Elizabeth, spouting the foulest, lewdest slanders about her honor, the sort of lies spread by the corrupt hangers-on of the French court. Amyas grabbed the man by the ears to haul him out into the street, the man drew his sword, and Amyas drew his. The Frenchman fought with that fancy new unchristian point-fencing trick, pricking and stabbing and dancing about, and got his blade straight through Amyas’s left arm before Amyas could close with him. Then Amyas lost his temper, leapt in, wrenched the man’s wrist out of the way, and swung one clean side-stroke that sent the Frenchman’s head tumbling onto the tavern table, slandering no more.

“So perish all her enemies!” Frank said warmly.

Eustace, who had been pretending not to listen, rose stiffly to his feet. “I trust you do not number me among them?”

“As far as that speech goes, I half do,” Frank said. “For your own sake, I beg you to trust the word of the people who see our queen’s virtue every day, the same way we see the sun rise and the earth grow crops, instead of the tattle of cowardly back-stair rogues who curry favor with the Guises. Come, walk back with us to Appledore, and we’ll go home for breakfast.”

But Eustace declined, saying he had urgent business in Northam, then back to Bideford. He left the two brothers to laze on the beach for half an hour more, before they set off walking across the turf toward the little white fishing village of Appledore, two miles up the coast, at the meeting of the Torridge and Taw rivers.

But Eustace had lied. He had no business in Northam. His true destination was Appledore, same as theirs. He walked just far enough to the first house on the edge of Northam to salve his conscience, then struck sharp left across the fields, muttering prayers to the Virgin all the way, going miles out of his way—just as all crooked, Jesuit-trained souls tend to do, he only outwitted himself. For Amyas and Frank, walking straight down the open turf road as honest men do, arrived at the door of the *Mariner’s Rest* inn in Appledore just in time to see four of Thomas Leigh’s horses standing outside, saddled and packed, three men mounting them: Eustace, and two complete strangers.

“Now there’s one lie already this morning,” Amyas growled. “He told us he was going to Northam.”

“We don’t know he didn’t stop there first,” Frank said mildly.

“Bless your innocent heart,” Amyas laughed, clapping his big hand on Frank’s head and mimicking their mother’s tone. “Come on, coz, let’s pop into that little shop there and buy a pennyworth of whipcord.”

“What in the world do you want whipcord for?”

“To spin my top, of course. And so I have an excuse for lingering, so I can see exactly what our friend Eustace is hiding from us.”

They slipped into the tiny shop unspotted by the group at the inn door, and Amyas snickered at the sight of the third stranger, a tall meek-looking man draped in gaudy fancy clothes, a great plumed hat on his head, and a sword so long and heavy it swayed uselessly between his thin stick-like legs. He was hopping awkwardly, trying to clamber up the wrong side of his horse, his thin legs clawing at the horse’s ribs like a cat scratching an elder tree, so clumsy he looked like he had three legs.

“David wearing Saul’s armor,” Frank chuckled. “He never proved he can wield that sword.”

“Look,” Amyas said, snorting, “his third leg is about to turn into a tail. Someone ought to yank that belt of his in half a yard, for charity’s sake.”

> “I could not love thee, dear, so much,
> Loved I not honor more.”

All this while, what has become of that fair, notorious heartbreaker I have not yet formally introduced to you? None other than Rose Salterne, the wealthiest merchant’s daughter in Bideford, who found herself cooped up in a tiny whitewashed farmhouse tucked beside a watermill, sunk deep in the green, mist-draped folds of Combe Valley, halfway between Stow and Chapel. She sulked as deeply as her naturally sweet, sunny disposition would allow, furious to be barred from all the grand feasts, dances, and pageantry unfolding down in Bideford town, stuck observing something like an unasked-for Martinmas Lent in that far-flung western glen.

The valley was so lonesome, in fact, that though she felt no small aversion to Eustace Leigh, and as a devout Protestant, a great deal of quiet suspicion of his hidden Papist sympathies, she could not bear to turn away his visits. He found some half-baked errand to call at the mill nearly every day, lingering to chat. At first, her aunt and uncle—poor distant cousins tasked with chaperoning the rich heiress—frowned stiffly on these calls, and the aunt made a point of acting as third party to every conversation. But Eustace was a local squire’s son, a respected neighbor, and a steady customer for their milled flour and dairy; they dared not be rude to him. Nor did they dare cross Rose, who had a will of iron hidden behind her soft smiles, and who had wisely begged her aunt never to leave her alone with him, saying “I cannot abide the sight of Mr. Eustace, but I must have someone to talk to out here, else I shall go mad.” Her simple country aunt, not wanting to second-guess the ways of “town-bred folk,” soon let the visits go unchallenged.

Eustace knew full well that the rift between his Catholic faith and Rose’s Protestant upbringing was the greatest obstacle to winning her, so he hid his true opinions deep beneath a mask of mild conformity. Instead of trying to convert the mill household, he bought milk and flour from them daily, and gave the goods away to the poor widows of Moorwinstow, who all agreed that for a Papist, he was a godly enough young man. At last, after conferring with the Jesuit priests Campian and Parsons over the political plots brewing across the realm, he and the two men resolved to attend the local parish church the following Sunday. The pair, travelling under the aliases Evan Morgans and Morgan Evans, had memorized every line of the Anglican rubrics beforehand, and behaved with such unremarkable orthodoxy that no one could find fault with them. Even Eustace played the part so well that all the good Protestant folk of the parish were thoroughly astonished—though every housewife whispered to her neighbor after the service, “He turn Protestant? The devil turn monk! That young hypocrite is only chasing Mistress Salterne.”

But if the two Jesuits found it prudent to pretend conformity to Elizabeth’s government for the sake of their holy cause, they worked none the less furiously in private to plot its overthrow. They had been darting across England in hiding since the previous April, lying low now only until they received word from Ireland that would signal the launch of a great “Rising of the West,” meant to sweep that “stiff-necked, persecuting, excommunicate, illegitimate usurper” calling herself Queen Elizabeth off her throne forever.

They had persuaded themselves, as many of their order would go on persuading themselves for centuries after, that the entire heart of England secretly sided with them, that the nation was mere days from flocking back to the bosom of the Catholic Church and handing Elizabeth over to be led in chains to the Pope’s feet in Rome. That fair hope danced always a step ahead of them, as unreachable as the spot where a rainbow touches the ground, and they spoke of it in terms that left old Mr. Leigh, Eustace’s father, trembling with fear. One day, Eustace arrived home after his usual visit to the mill to hear raised voices in his father’s private study: Parsons blustering as usual, old Mr. Leigh peevishly protesting, and Campian, the gentlest of the three, trying to pour oil on the roiling waters. Eustace stopped outside the door to listen, for what he told himself was the good of the cause.

“My good sir,” old Mr. Leigh was saying, “does not my very protection of you here prove my devotion to the Catholic faith? But I cannot forget, for all that, that I am an Englishman.”

“England?” Parsons thundered back. “It is nothing but a heretic, schismatic Babylon, of which it is written, ‘Come out of her, my people, lest you be partaker of her plagues.’ A country is no more than an arbitrary line drawn on the map by mortal princes, who rise and fall to nothing. All their authority comes only from our Holy Father the Pope, who holds all power in heaven and earth. Strip away his sanction, and what is a king? A creation of the people, who can unmake him just as easily as they made him.”

“Sir, I have sworn allegiance to Queen Elizabeth!”

“And you were absolved of that oath the moment Pius V’s bull declared her a heretic, excommunicate, and stripped of all right to rule. Since 1569, England has no queen, no magistrates, no lawful laws at all. To swear loyalty to her officials, or to plead in her courts, is to defy the apostolic command: ‘How dare you go to law before the unbelievers?’ Rebellion is not merely permitted now. It is a sacred duty.”

“Take care, man, for God’s sake take care!” Mr. Leigh begged. “Right or wrong, I will not have such treason spoken in my own house, not for the sake of my wife and children!”

“Peace, brother Parsons, be gentler with the flock,” Campian interposed. “Your view, while widely held among our order, is too bold to put to good men here. The opposite view, that Catholics may still keep their oaths of peace to the crown, is far kinder to tender consciences, and we are sent here to ease their burdens, not add to them.”

The argument raged on, until old Mr. Leigh broke down in tears, furious that the Jesuits had used his devotion to the Church to take his money, his son, and now threatened his very life to push their plots forward. In a burst of loyalty he had not expected from himself, Eustace stepped to his father’s side, declaring he would not stand by and see his family ruined, and that he was still an English citizen, not a vowed servant of the order. The two Jesuits were stunned, realizing their most promising young acolyte had slipped their grasp—until Parsons spotted the flush on Eustace’s face, and guessed the truth: a woman was the distraction.

They resolved to change their tactics, and soon Campian manipulated the household chaplain into letting him hear Eustace’s confession. Through a series of careful, probing questions, he wormed the truth out of the lovesick young man: Eustace was utterly besotted with Rose Salterne, the 18-year-old heiress of Bideford’s richest merchant. Blind with passion, Eustace swore that if the Church would only let him court Rose, for even a single day, he would devote every penny of her fortune, every breath of his life, to the cause.

That was all Campian needed. He absolved Eustace of his sin of passion, and handed him a secret coded mission: the next day, the 25th of November, he was to go to the shore at Clovelly at dead low tide, and wait for a boat rowed by a red-bearded Portuguese man. If he asked the sailor “How many?” and got the answer “Eight hundred and one,” he was to take the secret dispatches the man carried, which held the final plans for the Catholic rising.

The two set out together for the mill farm, and had just reached the top of the hill between Chapel and Stow mill when Rose tripped up the lane, wearing a brand new scarlet hood, her long dark curls spilling out from beneath it to her waist, her dark eyes glowing soft and bright. Even Campian paused to stare at her innocent beauty as she curtsied and passed by. Eustace, shaking from head to toe, begged leave to run after her, to speak just a few words. Campian nodded, and Eustace darted across the field to catch her at the next turn in the road.

She shrieked softly in surprise. “Mr. Leigh! I thought you had gone on ahead.”

“I came back to speak to you, Rose—Mistress Salterne, I mean. I must tell you all, or die!” He pressed close to her, and she shrank back, frightened. “Do not run, I beg you! Only hear me!” He seized her hand fiercely, and poured out every wild, passionate declaration of love he could summon, heaping her with every extravagant compliment he had ever read or heard.

Rose had heard such words a hundred times from the gallants of Bideford, but the raw, hungry fire in his eyes made her recoil. “Let me go at once, sir! You are far too rough!” She struggled to pull her hands away.

“Rougher than the soft lads who serenade you and write you sonnets?” he said, grabbing both her wrists now. “But far more true, Rose! Do not turn away! I shall die if you take your eyes from me! Tell me—here, now, this very moment—before we part—may I love you?”

“Go away!” she sobbed, fighting to free herself. “This is unforgivably rude! I may be only a merchant’s daughter, but I am God’s own child. I am alone here. Leave me, or I will call for help!”

Eustace had read somewhere that such protests from a woman were no more than pretty modesty, signs she did not truly want to be left alone, and he tightened his grip on her wrists, leaning in closer. But Rose was in deadly earnest, and drew in a breath to scream as loud as she could, to call for help from the farmhands not far off. To save his own reputation, he knew he must flee—but one final, desperate question burned in his throat, and he would risk everything to hear her answer.

*It was among the ways of good Queen Bess, Who ruled as well as ever mortal can, sir, When she was stogg'd, and the country in a mess, She was wont to send for a Devon man, sir.*
—West Country Ballad

The sun spilled thin gold over the silver curve of the Torridge at dawn, but Amyas Leigh was nowhere to be found. He had not fled to drown his sorrows in the river’s eddies, nor hidden in the woods to bewail some lost hope: when Frank Leigh tracked his horse’s hoof prints down the lane, it was plain the giant young man had ridden hard for Sir Richard Grenville’s estate at Stow, determined to beg a fighting commission in the queen’s Irish army. His mother had seen his purpose at a glance, and sent Frank spurring after him to bring the runaway home, or at least make him pause and weigh his choice.

Frank rode ten miles over the heather-cloaked hills, and found the sun dipping low enough that he faced another ten miles of rutted, steep track before reaching Stow, with no roadside inns to rest himself or his mount. As was the universal, unwritten hospitality of the Elizabethan West Country, he turned his horse down the winding lane that led to Clovelly Court, the honey-stone manor perched high above the Atlantic cliffs, to beg a meal and a stall for his horse from the squire, Mr. Cary.

He walked unannounced, as welcome as the tide, through the great oak door into the long, dark wainscoted hall, redolent of peat smoke and spiced cider, and the first sight that met his eyes was Amyas himself, settled at the far end of the heavy oak table, alternating between shoving great chunks of venison pasty into his mouth and tipping ale down his throat, his sorrows apparently only sharpening his ravenous appetite. Young Will Cary knelt on the bench opposite him, elbows propped on the table, leaning in to argue fiercely in a low, heated tone.

“Hullo, lad!” Amyas roared when he spotted him, pasty crumbs flying from his beard. “Come over and deliver me out of the hands of this fire-eater. I swear he’ll run me through before the sun sets if I don’t help him pick a fight with some poor soul first.”

“Ah, Mr. Frank!” Will said, scrambling to his feet, for every young gentleman across north Devon held Frank Leigh as a paragon of wit, learning, and chivalry. “You could not have turned up at a better time. I’ve been half mad wanting your advice on a dozen matters. Sit, eat, the ale’s still frothing.”

“None so early, thank you,” Frank laughed, hanging his riding cloak by the hearth.

“Ah, of course!” Amyas bellowed, tilting a full tankard to his lips and mimicking Frank’s precise, scholarly tone. “Avoid strong ale before noon, you know. It heats the blood, thickens the animal spirits, and obfuscates the cerebrum with frenetical and lymphatic idols that cloud the quintessential light of pure reason. Eh? Young Plato, young Daniel, come to sit in judgment on us sinners! Well, even if my wits are half fuddled already, I can see plain enough that you, Will Cary, are not going to fight Tom Coffin.”

“Am I not?” Will snapped, stabbing a finger at the table. “Who says that? Mr. Frank, you be the judge, just hear me out.”

“Court is in session,” Frank said, settling comfortably into a bench and helping himself to a slice of pasty. “Proceed, appellant.”

“I was telling Amyas,” Will huffed, “that Tom Coffin of Portledge has gone too far. I won’t abide it a day longer.”

“Let him be, then,” Amyas mumbled around a mouthful of meat. “Last I saw him he could stand on his own two feet well enough.”

“Plague take you, hold your tongue! What right has he to glower at me every time we pass each other on the road?”

“Depends how he glowers,” Amyas shrugged. “A cat may look at a king, long as he doesn’t mistake him for a mouse and pounce.”

“Oh I know exactly how he looks, and what he means, too! He’ll stop staring, or I’ll stop him. The other day, when I made a casual remark about Rose Salterne—”
“Ah!” Frank groaned. “The apple of discord again.”
“Never mind what I said! He burst out laughing straight in my face. Is that not provocation enough for a fair quarrel? And what’s more, I’ve learned he wrote a sonnet to her, and sent it all the way to Stow by a market woman. What right has he to scribble love verses when I have not dared to send one? That’s not fair play, Mr. Frank. I’d be a Jew, and a Spaniard, and a Papist myself if that was fair!” He slammed his fist on the table so hard the pewter plates danced.

“My good knight of the burning ire,” Frank said, grinning, “I have a proper chivalric solution, approved by every rule of knightly order. We shall send summons by tuck of drum to every unmarried gentleman under thirty, living within fifteen miles of our peerless Oriana Rose Salterne, to attend a grand tournament. We’ll let all the prentice lads fight first, with good quarterstaves, in Bideford market square, till every last head is well thwacked. Any man whose head is unbroken after that will be deemed a coward, and soundly thrashed on the back for his dishonor. After that grand melee, which will make the old Battle of Tottenham look like a trivial brawl between frogs and mice, all the young gentlemen shall adjourn to a soft, boggy field—best a bog, because no man can run away when he’s sunk up to his knees in peat—strip to their shirts, and fight with rapiers of equal length. Each man shall slay his opponent, the survivors shall fight on like gamecocks till all are dead of their love wounds, and the very last man left alive shall fall gracefully on his sword, wailing to heaven of the slaughter Rose’s bright eyes have caused. That shall end all our lovelorn squabbles. Placetne Domini? As they used to ask in the old Oxford senate.”

“Confound you and your long words, sir!” Will groaned, red-faced. “You’re mocking me.”

“Patience, Signor Cavaliere. This is no mockery, it’s deadly earnest. For why fight a single petty duel when we can settle the whole rivalry once and for all? Though,” he added, his tone softening to a quiet, serious warmth, “to tell you the truth, I would not have you fight Tom Coffin with anything longer than a bodkin. Do not mistake me, I know well what the sting of love feels like, and the far sharper sting of jealousy. But does it not seem a waste to squander that divine, fiery wrath— which Plato says is the very root of all virtue, that God gave you not to spend on your own petty quarrels, but to devote to the service of our queen, whom all wicked men fear and all good souls adore? What Devon gentleman worth the name dares call his sword his own, while Elizabeth sits on the throne? Are there no Spaniards to fight, no oppressed Irishfolk to free, that two brave lads of Devon must needs flesh their blades in each other’s hearts?”

“By heaven, you speak truth, Frank!” Amyas boomed. “I’ve long thought Christian gentlemen ought to leave love quarrels to bulls and rams.”

“And the heir of Clovelly,” Frank added, smiling, “surely can find a nobler example to follow than the stags in his own deer park.”

“You’re a great scholar, Mr. Frank,” Will said, looking properly penitent. “But gentlemen must fight sometimes, or where is their honor?”

“I speak not merely as a scholar,” Frank said, a little proud, “but as a gentleman who has drawn his blade before, and has killed his man—may God have mercy on his soul. And I count it my greatest pride that I have never fought for my own private cause, and I pray I never shall. There is no work more noble than fighting for those you love, but a Christian man ought never to raise his sword for his own petty grievance, unless refusing to do so would cost him his life or his utter honor. Even then, the good book bids us overcome evil with good, not return blow for blow.”

“And I can back that, Will,” Amyas put in. “I’m no man afraid of ghosts, but once, when I cut off the head of a French privateer who slandered the queen, I thought to myself: if that man had been slandering me instead, I’d see his grinning head on my pillow every night before I slept.”

“God forbid!” Will shuddered. “But what am I to do? I’m going to Bideford market tomorrow if it’s full of Coffins, each with a ghost in his own coffin to back him.”

“Leave it to me,” Amyas said. “I’ve got my own plan, just as clever as Frank’s. If there’s a quarrel brewing in the market tomorrow, you’ll see I step in before any blades are drawn.”

“You’re both good fellows,” Will laughed. “Let’s call for another tankard.”

“And we’ll drink to the health of Tom Coffin, and every gallant lad in north Devon,” Frank agreed. “But I’ve got business, remember: I’m here to drag this runaway giant here back home to his mother. If he won’t come quietly, she bade me tie him across my saddle.”

“Your nag had better have a very strong back, then,” Amyas grinned. “But I mean to ride on to Stow and see Sir Richard today. Jests are all well and good, but my mind is made up to go to Ireland.”

“Stop here tonight, the both of you,” Will begged. “First for the sake of good company, and then because I need your advice, our oracle, our paragon. Speak low, though—here comes my father. Hand me that letter I gave you a minute ago.”

The old squire, Cary of Clovelly, trundled in a moment later, his peregrine falcon perched on his gloved wrist, a pair of fluffy spaniels trotting at his heels. “Eh, lads? Company! Welcome, the lot of you. I wish there were more of your sort in these degenerate days. How is your good mother, Frank? Will, I’ve been round the home farm all morning. That sheeted heifer of Prowse’s is ailing, her coat standing up like a hedgehog’s. Send Jewell to bring her in before nightfall. Then I went up to the forty-acre field, flushed two coveys of partridge, took a brace out of them. That new Irish hawk is still wild as any stray haggard, never going to make a proper bird. I handed her off to Tom, took the little Clovelly peregrine—nothing beats a Clovelly hawk, anywhere! Heigh ho, I’m starving. Half past twelve, dinner not served? Master Amyas, spoiling your appetite with ale? You ought to try sack, lad, have a cup with me.”

He settled himself on the broad stone bench inside the chimney breast, set his falcon on a perch above his head, and his spaniels curled up in the warm peat ash at his feet while Will knelt to pull his boots off, fending off his father’s repeated warnings not to jostle his sore corns.

“Come, Master Amyas, a pint of white wine and sugar, with a pickled prawn or a rasher off the coals to whet your appetite before dinner.”

“Thank you, sir,” Amyas said, grinning. “I’ve drunk a hundred different odd liquors, good and bad, over the last three years at sea, and I’ve never found aught that beats plain good Devon ale. It needs no chaser, no fancy spice, it keeps honest men happy all on its own.”

“You speak sense, lad,” old Cary nodded. “All these newfangled hot wines and aqua vitaes that’ve come in since the wars do nothing but muddle men’s wits and fire their blood up to fever pitch.”

“I fear that trouble is not over,” Frank said. “My friends write from the Netherlands that our men there have taken to swilling ale as heavily as the Dutch. Heaven grant they do not bring that drunken fashion home with them.”

“They say you have to drink heavily or die of the ague in those vile swamp lands,” Amyas pointed out. “Once they’re back home in Devon, they’ll have no need for it.”

“Heaven grant it,” Frank said. “I’d hate to see Devon turn into a drunken shire, and hundreds of our lads are out there with Mr. Champernoun right now.”

“Ah, well,” old Cary said, “that proves the queen’s saying true: Devonshire is her right hand, and her young men are like arrows in the hand of a giant.”

“Arrows that are full-grown giants themselves, if you ask me,” Will laughed, nodding at Amyas. “He’ll be off doing great deeds soon, I’ll warrant.”

“That I shall!” Amyas said. “I’ve got brave deeds planned—enchanters to bind, dragons to slay, whole empires to win, though not in Holland.”

“You do?” Will asked, sharp, having half suspected Amyas was hiding a grander purpose.

“Aye,” Amyas said, dropping his jest. “I’m off to what Walter Raleigh calls the Land of the Nymphs. I hope to sail for Ireland within the month.”

“Call that home, not abroad,” old Cary said. “Ireland is full of Devon men from one end to the other, you’ll not meet a stranger anywhere. George Bourchier of Tawstock commands the army in Munster, Warham St. Leger is marshal, George Carew rides with Lord Grey of Wilton. Poor Peter Carew was killed at Glendalough last year, after that villain Desmond ambushed and butchered Herbert and Price’s men. They reinforced the companies with six hundred Devon lads under Arthur Fortescue, so our county holds her head high in Ireland just as she does in the Low Countries and the Spanish Main.”

“Is Davils of Marsland there, too?” Amyas asked. “The old man who taught me to catch trout when I stayed at Stow? He was a rare good soldier.”

“Ah, lad,” old Cary said, his face falling. “That is a sad tale. I thought all England had heard it.”

“Surely he is not dead?” Amyas said, shocked.

“Murdered foully, like a dog! By the man he treated as his own son, the false knave who used to call him father. Last July, that Jesuit fox Saunders landed there, posing as the Pope’s legate, with bags of gold, papal bulls, and a blessed banner, stirring up rebellion. With him was James Fitzmaurice, the rascal who knelt in the church at Kilmallock and swore on every saint in the calendar to be loyal to Queen Elizabeth, so loud that Perrott said he had to stop his own ears. He went to the King of France first, got nothing but mockery, then went to the King of Spain, promising to hand all of Ireland over to him and restore Popery. The King of Spain fitted him out with ships and men, just like that other rogue Stukely—though I will say, for the honor of Devon, if Stukely lived like a fool, he died like an honest man.”

“Sir Thomas Stukely is dead too?” Amyas gasped.

“Wait, lad, I’ll tell you that tale after. Fitzmaurice and the Jesuits landed at Smerwick with three ships, built a little fort, blessed it with their holy water to purge it of heretic taint. But a Courtenay of Haccombe, anchored nearby with his king’s warship, took their three ships in a midnight raid, cut them off from the sea. John and James Desmond brought their ragged rabble over to join the Spaniards. Earl Desmond would not fight for them, but he would not fight against them, waiting to side with whichever side won. So poor Davils was sent by the Lord Deputy to order the Desmond brothers, in the queen’s name, to attack the small Spanish force. Folks said it was rash, but Davils was the boldest, shrewdest soldier alive. The young Desmond boys used to call him father, he was so kind to them. But he learned the danger of trusting Englishmen who’d gone native Irish. The Desmonds suddenly decided the Spaniards were far too strong to attack, though they outnumbered them five to one.
