Davils went back to Tralee that night to gather reinforcements, sleeping in the same bed as Arthur Carter, St. Leger’s lieutenant, as brave an old soldier as himself. Their young lacquey boy, now in Sir Richard’s service at Stow, slept on the floor at their feet. In the dead of night, in came James Desmond, sword in hand, with a dozen of his ruffians, each with their Irish fringe over their faces and a skene dagger hidden in their palms. Davils woke up, naked, and asked only ‘What is the matter, my son?’ The villain struck him down before he could even say a prayer, screaming ‘Thou shalt be my father no longer, nor I thy son!’ All the rest fell on him and Captain Carter. The poor lacquey boy threw himself over his master to shield him, got three stab wounds, and was left for dead. The ruffians murdered every last Englishman in the house, except the boy, who crawled out a window, bleeding, to escape. Desmond went back to the Spaniards, his elbows dripping with blood, and showed them his bloody blades, saying ‘Will you take this as proof I am loyal to you?’ That was the end of Henry Davils. That’s what becomes of any man who trusts the word of those wild savages.”

“I would ride a hundred miles to see that Desmond hang!” Amyas roared, great tears rolling down his sunburnt cheeks. “Poor Mr. Davils! Now tell me the tale of Sir Thomas Stukely.”

“Your brother can tell you that, lad. I’m winded.”

“And I have a right to tell it,” Frank smiled. “Do you know I was very nearly made Earl of the Bog of Allen, and a peer of

> *Far, far from hence
The Adriatic breaks in a warm bay
Among the green Illyrian hills, and there
The sunshine in the happy glens is fair,
And by the sea and in the brakes
The grass is cool, the sea-side air
Buoyant and fresh, the mountain flowers
More virginal and sweet than ours.*
— Matthew Arnold

The glens cut into the high rolling tableland straddling Devon and Cornwall are exactly like the verses above describe, save that each of their gorges opens not on the Adriatic, but out toward the endless, storm-kissed Western Ocean. No other English scenery matches them: each combe has steep inland walls draped in rich ancient oak, nearer the sea banks thick with dark golden furze, then smooth sheep-cropped turf, then jagged black ironstone cliffs that jutt far out into the deep Atlantic, shaped like spires, broken castles, and sharp ragged fins. Tucked in each hollow is a narrow strip of lush watermeadow, a crystal trout stream winding back and forth between the feet of the hills, a weathered grey stone mill with spray sparkling and humming around its dripping waterwheel, dark rock pools above the tide line where salmon trout rest after swimming up from their Atlantic wanderings each autumn flood, a ridge of blown sand bright with golden trefoil and crimson lady’s finger bloom, and a bank of smooth polished pebbles down which the stream rattles on its last rush to the sea.

At the mouth of every cove lies a stretch of jagged, shark-tooth sharp rock that paves the whole bay, streaked here and there with thin lines of pink shell sand, crisscrossed with white foam from the never-resting surf. Primeval earthquakes tilted the rock strata on edge, stacked at wild, crooked angles that stretch straight out west into the water: one single rasp of that sharp stone jaw could grind the stoutest ship’s timbers to splinters in seconds. To landward, all is softness, richness, quiet peace. To seaward, it is a wild, howling wilderness of rock and rolling swell, barren to the fisherman and a death sentence for any shipwrecked mariner.

Only one of these coves, called Marsland Mouth, has a patch of protected landing ground, guarded by a long natural rock sea-wall that breaks the force of the Atlantic rollers. This is the home of Lucy Passmore, known across the shire as the White Witch — and as Sir Richard Grenville rightly guessed, this was exactly where the fleeing Jesuit priests had made for.

But the Jesuits had not arrived first. Two figures stood on the lonely shingle beach under the bright harvest October moon: Rose Salterne, and the White Witch herself. Fevered by curiosity, steeped in the old country superstitions, and drawn on by the very wildness and danger of the magic rite she’d come to perform, Rose had kept their midnight appointment, and stood shivering on the grey pebbles minutes before the clock would strike twelve.

“You’re quite safe here tonight, miss,” Lucy assured her. “My old man’s snoring sound abed, and no other soul sets foot here after dark, save the odd mermaid passing through. Good Lord, where’s our boat? It should be pulled up high on the pebbles, not down there!”

Rose pointed to a strip of sand forty yards closer to the waves, where the small craft lay half beached.
“That lazy good-for-nothing!” Lucy huffed. “He’s been out after pollock round the rocks this evening, and never bothered to haul it up high. I’ll trounce him good when I get home. I only hope he tied it fast, that’s all! He’s more trouble than all the money in the world will ever be worth. Oh deary me!”

She bustled down the shingle toward the boat, Rose trailing after her.
“Aye, it’s tied fast, sure enough, oars and all! Well I never! Oh the lazy thief, leaving the oars out where someone could steal them! I’ll just sit here in the boat and keep watch, dear, while you go down to the tide line. You have to be all alone, you know, or the spell won’t work. Here’s the magic mirror. Go on now, dip your head three times in the water, mind you don’t look back to land or toward the open sea until you’ve said the words and looked into the glass. Hurry now, it’s almost midnight.”

Lucy coiled herself comfortably in the stern of the boat, while Rose faltered the twenty yards further down the sand, slipped off her outer clothes, and stood trembling for a moment before stepping into the cold dark water. She was framed between two walls of rock: the one on her left, twenty feet high, hid her in deepest shadow, while the lower rock on her right caught the full blaze of the midnight moon, draped in thick festoons of purple living seaweed that hung over dark crevices that looked like lairs for every sea goblin in the old tales. The peaks of the left-hand rock loomed ghastly black overhead, while the high down above her right glowed pale and cold under the moon.

The breeze had died entirely away. Not even a small swell broke the perfect stillness of the cove. The gulls were all asleep on their ledges. It was that deep autumn silence so thick you could almost hear it hum. Rose stood awed, listening for any small sound that might prove there was another living thing beside her in the world.

A faint bleat, like that of a newborn lamb, drifted down from high above her head. She started and looked up. Then a wail echoed from the cliffs, like a crying hurt child, answered by a second wail from the opposite rock face. They were only passing snipe and an otter calling her cubs, but to Rose’s overwrought mind they were mysterious supernatural beings, come to answer her spell. They only quickened her anticipation, though: Lucy had told her no harm would touch her if she performed the rite correctly. She could hear her own heart hammering as she stepped deeper into the cold water, mirror held tight in her hand, then stopped aghast.

A ring of lambent, pale blue flame circled her waist. Every inch of her submerged limbs glowed with soft light. All the tiny countless life of the autumn sea, stirred by her wading steps, had flashed into sudden glowing glory:
> And around her the lamps of the sea nymphs,
Myriad fiery globes, swam heaving and panting, and rainbows,
Crimson and azure and emerald, were broken in star-showers, lighting
Far through the wine-dark depths of the crystal, the gardens of Nereus,
Coral and sea-fan and tangle, the blooms and the palms of the ocean.

She could see every small shell crawling on the white sand under her feet, every little rock fish darting in and out of crevices to stare at her with wide bright eyes, while the great brown palm-like oarweeds waving in the half-lit water below her seemed to reach up with long soft hands to beckon her down to a cold grave among their fronds. She almost turned to flee, but it was too late to back out. She dipped her head three times in haste, waded quickly back to the tide line, pushed dripping hair out of her eyes, and spoke the incantation:
> A maiden pure, here I stand,
Neither on sea, nor yet on land;
Angels watch me on either hand.
If you be landsman, come down the strand;
If you be sailor, come up the sand;
If you be angel, come from the sky,
Look in my glass, and pass me by;
Look in my glass, and go from the shore;
Leave me, but love me for evermore.

The words had barely left her lips, and her eyes were straining into the mirror (which so far only showed the sparkle of water drops dripping from her own hair) when she heard the fast clatter of hooves and the scuff of men’s boots on the pebbles. She darted into a narrow crevice cave in the high rock and dressed herself in frantic haste. The footsteps came straight for the boat. Peeping out, half-dead with terror, she saw four men: two had just leaped off their horses and set them loose, while the other two helped drag the boat down toward the sea.

Then Lucy Passmore’s portly figure rose from the stern sheets like an angry ghost, and shrieked at the top of her shrill voice:
“Eh! You villains, you rogues! What do you think you’re doing stealing a poor widow’s boat in the dead of night?”

The whole party recoiled in terror, and one man turned to run up the beach yelling at the top of his lungs: “It’s a mermaid! A mermaid asleep in Willy Passmore’s boat!”
“I wish to God that were true,” a familiar grumbling voice groaned. “It’s my wife, oh dear!” Willy Passmore cowered down, already bracing for the hearty cuff he duly received when Lucy leaped out of the boat, daring any of them to lay a hand on it, and thundered at her husband to get back home to bed.

The wily old woman was dragging the fight out for two reasons: first, to give Rose all the time she needed to hide safely, and second, because she had already spotted the suspicious Welsh gentleman leading the party, the same Popish priest she’d foretold would turn up on her doorstep long ago. She was a fiercely loyal subject to Queen Elizabeth, and hated the idea of her husband sneaking out to help these “Popish skulkers” escape the law — unless, of course, they offered her a very handsome sum of money for the trouble.

Parsons the priest thundered at her to stand aside, Campian the other Jesuit entreated her politely, Mr. Leigh’s groom swore wildly, and Willy Passmore danced around in a perfect agony of mingled fear and greed.
“No!” Lucy roared. “As I am an honest, loyal woman! This is what you left the boat down on the tide line for, is it, you old traitor? To help noxious Popish spies escape her Majesty’s judges? Stand back, cowards! D’you dare strike a defenceless woman?”

That last line was only a warning that she intended to strike them. She grabbed one of the oars, swung it round her head in great circles, and cracked Father Parsons such a hard blow across the shins that he retreated yelping.
“Lucy! Lucy!” her husband shrieked in the shrillest Devon falsetto, “Are you mad? Are you daft, lass? They promised me two gold nobles just to lend them the boat!”
“Two nobles?” Lucy shrieked back, her tone thick with ineffable scorn. “And you call yourself a man?”
“Two nobles! Two nobles!” Willy hopped about, just out of oar’s reach. “Ten? Ten nobles?”
“Ten?” Lucy jeered. “You wouldn’t sell your soul for less than twenty, you daft old thing!”

“Oh, for heaven’s sake!” poor Campian cried. “Give her ten, brother Pars — Morgan, I mean! Watch your shins, you brute — Oh, virago! What fury a woman can wield! She must be a Lamia, a Gorgon, some sort of —”
“Take that for calling an honest woman a Gorgon!” Lucy swung the oar again, and knocked poor thin Campian’s legs right out from under him. She straddled his chest exactly like the famous virago in the old ballad of Hudibras, and roared “Ten nobles, or I’m keeping you all here till the Queen’s men turn up at dawn!” The ten gold nobles were immediately pressed into her hand.

Now their dragon guardian pacified, the men dragged the boat down toward the sea, right past the narrow cave where Rose was squeezed into the darkest, furthest corner, pressed against wet seaweed and rough barnacles, holding her breath as they passed. Lucy trailed right behind them for her own reasons, spotted the dark cave mouth right at the tide line, and planted her broad person square across its opening to block any chance of the strangers noticing Rose, while she grumbled at her husband, at the priests, and most of all at Mr. Leigh’s groom, whom she prophesied very plainly would end up in Launceston Gaol and hang before the month was out. The poor serving man, who would as soon have dared leap off the top of Welcombe Cliff as talk back to the White Witch, begged for her mercy and tried every trick to hide behind one of the other men, terrified her famous evil eye would “overlook” him and curse him to ruin.

But the night’s adventures were far from over. Just as the boat was being pushed into the water, a faint halloo echoed down the beach. A minute later, a horseman came thundering down the shingle, pulled his horse up hard on its haunches right beside the terrified group, and half-fell, half-jumped out of the saddle.

The groom, who would never dare fight a witch, knew full well how to face a swordsman. He drew his blade and lunged at the newcomer, then recoiled:
“God forgive me, it’s Mr. Eustace! Oh dear sir, I thought you were one of Sir Richard Grenville’s men! Oh sir, you’re hurt!”
“Just a scratch, nothing more,” Eustace almost moaned. “Help me into the boat, Jack. Gentlemen, I have to go with you.”
“Surely not, my dear son,” kind-hearted Campian said, shocked. “We are nothing but wandering outcasts with no home to our name.”
“With you forever. Everything is lost for me here. Wherever God and the cause lead, I go,” Eustace staggered toward the boat.

As he passed the cave, Rose saw his ghastly, blood-soaked face, half-bound with a handkerchief that could not hide the convulsions of rage, shame, and despair twisting his once-handsome features. His wild, glaring eyes swept round the cove once, and locked directly on hers through the crack in the rock. His gaze was so sharp, so intense, so dreadful that Rose forgot she was hidden completely, and almost shrieked out loud.
“He’s overlooked me!” she shuddered to herself, remembering his threat from the day before.
“Who wounded you?” Campian asked.
“My cousin Amyas. He attacked me, and stole the secret letter I was carrying!” Parsons stamped up and down the sand in a fury. “The devil take him, then!”
“Aye, curse him if you want — I dare not. He spared my life, he sent me here,” Eustace groaned, making a lurch for the boat.
“Oh my dear young gentleman,” Lucy’s soft woman’s heart burst out at the sight of his pain, “you can’t sail away with a great wound like that. Let me bind it up for you, just a minute now —” She stepped forward.
Eustace thrust her roughly back. “No! Better to bear it. I deserve it. Devils, I deserve it! Push off, or we shall all be lost! William Cary is right behind me!”

At that news the boat was shoved into the water faster than it had ever moved before, and they made their escape just in time. They were barely round the edge of the headland and out of sight when the rattle of Cary’s horse hooves echoed loud down the beach.
“That rascal groom of Mr. Leigh’s is for it now, the Popish villain!” Lucy called out loud. “You stay still there, dear heart, and settle your nerves. You’re safer than a rabbit in its burrow. I’ll climb up this rock ledge and see what happens, if I die for it.”

She squeezed through a narrow cleft up to a high viewing ledge, and peered down.
“There he is, in the meadow, trying to catch the loose horses! There comes Mr. Cary! Good Lord, how he rides! He’s over the stone wall already! Run Jack, run! You’ll make the river! No, he won’t! Goodness gracious! Mr. Cary’s caught him! He’s down, he’s down!”
“Is he dead?” Rose called out, shuddering.
“Aye, as dead as a nit! And Mr. Cary’s off his horse, standing over him. No, he’s not dead! He’s getting up now. Looks like Cary hit him with the flat of his sword. What’s he doing now? Talking to him for a minute. Oh dear, oh dear!”
“Has he killed him?” poor Rose cried.
“No, not at all! He’s kicking him as hard as you’d kick a football! Good Lord, who ever saw the like! He’s kicked him straight into the river, got on his horse, and ridden off!”

Lucy climbed back down to the cave.
“Now then, my dear, we’d best head home and get you something warm to drink. You must be mortal cold by now. I was so scared for you, but I kept those rascals off clever, didn’t I?”
“I wish I’d never seen Mr. Leigh’s face,” Rose whispered.
“Aye, dreadful, weren’t it, poor soul. A terrible night for his poor mother.”
“Lucy, I can’t get that face out of my head. I’m sure he overlooked me, cursed me.”
“Oh, nonsense,” Lucy laughed. “When young gentlemen overlook young ladies, it’s almost never a curse, I’ll warrant. Don’t think on it.”
“But I can’t help it,” Rose said. “Wait — should we head home yet? Where’s that servant?”
“Never mind him, he won’t see us under the lee of the hill. I’m far more worried about my old man. I’ve got a funny feeling in my gut, the kind I get before something bad happens, like I’ll never see him again, I swear. Well, he was a biddable old soul, all said and done. Oh — all this while we’ve forgotten the whole point of this night! Who did you see in the mirror?”
“Only that face,” Rose shuddered.
“Not in the glass, maid? Tell me you didn’t see it in the glass?”
“Would to Heaven I hadn’t! Lucy — what if he’s the man I was fated to marry?”
“Him? Why, he’s a Popish priest, he can’t marry even if he wanted to, poor wretch!”
“He is not, and I have cause enough to know it!” Rose poured out the whole story of her meeting with Eustace the day before, to the only confidant she had.
“He’s a fine wooer, isn’t he?”

“The fair breeze blew, the white foam flew; The furrow follow'd free; We were the first that ever burst Into that silent sea.”

It was far too late and far too dark the night before to explore the old Grenville family seat at Stow, so we wander its grounds now under a bright October sun, as Sir Richard Grenville and young Amyas Leigh pace the south-facing terraced garden just past eleven o’clock in the forenoon. Amyas slept through until a late luncheon, waking only an hour prior, but Sir Richard, unfazed by the bustle of last night’s arrivals, was up long before dawn at six, tramping the valley with his two sturdy terriers to course badgers and breathe in the sharp, sweet West Country air.

Old Stow House – the original, not the later grand folly – stood some four miles past the Cornish border, on the northern slope of the largest, loveliest of the sheltered combes described earlier. Eighty years after Sir Richard’s time, a vast, gaudy Palladian pile would be raised there, crammed full of the most vulgar excesses of bad taste, built, local legend says, by Charles II for Sir Richard’s great-grandson, heir to the famed Sir Bevil Grenville who routed Parliament’s troops at Stratton and fell fighting valiantly soon after on Lansdowne Hill outside Bath. But like most works born of Stuart patronage, that great new mansion rose only to fall: an old man who had watched its foundations laid as a boy lived long enough to see it pulled down to the last brick, its timbers sold off on the spot. Now the old stables are a plain farmhouse, the former tennis court a sheep cote, the great central quadrangle a rick yard, and all the rush of outward-spreading civilization has ebbed away from this lonely western corner of the land – let us hope, only for a little while.

But we do not tell of that later Stow, nor of the gallant Sir Bevil, whom the poet Prince immortalized alongside his grandfather Sir Richard in the famous epigram:
> “Where next shall famous Grenvil's ashes stand?
> Thy grandsire fills the sea, and thou the land.”

Our story belongs to a simpler, sterner age, and the old house that had stood there in part since grey, mythic medieval days, when the first Sir Richard, son of Hamon Dentatus, Lord of Carboyle, grandson of Duke Rollo of Normandy, settled at Bideford after slaying the Prince of South Wales and the Lord of Glamorgan, and gifted all his conquered South Welsh lands to the Cistercian monks of Neath. It was a sprawling, rambling half-castle, half-manor, much like the surviving Compton Castle near Torquay – home of Humphrey Gilbert, Walter Raleigh’s half-brother and Sir Richard’s dearest friend, of whom we will hear more soon. To the north, west, and south, the lofty old curtain walls of the original Norman bailey still stood, lined with machicolated turrets, narrow arrow slits, and deep hidden murder holes designed to drop stones and boiling pitch down on the heads of besiegers, relics of a far more violent, unsettled era. But the southern bailey had long been converted to a flower garden, stepped with quaint terraces, dotted with stone statues, laid out with knots of scented blooms, clipped yews and hollies, all the careful, pedant art of the topiarian’s craft. To the east, where the valley view opened wide, the old fortifications were gone, and the frowning Norman keep ruined in the Wars of the Roses had been replaced by the rich, carved, bright architecture of the Tudors. The whole house, like the era it stood in, was in a state of transition, a perfect mirror of the old Middle Ages giving way to the new, bright, blooming life of exploration and discovery that was bursting across all of Europe.

From the house, the hill sloped steeply down on three sides, and the garden path where Sir Richard and Amyas walked unfolded a view that could only be called truly English. One turn of the path gave them a glimpse over the western wall of the blue Atlantic, dotted with far-off passing sails; the next revealed range after range of fertile deer park, stately elm avenues, golden autumn woodland, and purple heather moorland, rolling one over the other up the valley sides to the old pre-Roman British earthwork, black and overgrown with gorse, perched on its conical peak. And rising sharp against the sky on the highest ridge closing the valley to the east was the tall tower of Kilkhampton Church, lined with the monuments and memorial gifts of five centuries of Grenvilles. A soft yellow autumn haze hung low over park, wood, and moor; red cattle lowed to each other as they stood swishing flies away in the shallow brook far below; foals in the horse paddock close at hand whinnied as they chased each other, and their sires from the Queen’s Park on the opposite hill answered back in deeper, fainter calls. A rutting stag’s deep roar shook the quiet woodland, and a rival far up the valley called back a ringing note of challenge, echoed again from the mist-shrouded heather brows high overhead. And close by, on the terrace directly before the house, surrounded by romping spaniels and golden-haired Grenville children, sat Lady Grenville herself – the beautiful St. Leger of Annery, the central jewel of that whole glorious place – looking first down at her children, then up at her noble husband, then out over that broad, sunlit western paradise, until life felt almost too full of happiness for mortal souls to bear.

All the while Amyas and Sir Richard paced slowly, talking long, earnestly, and low, for both knew this was the great turning point of the boy’s young life.

“Aye,” Sir Richard said, after Amyas, in his plain, blunt way, had told him the whole tale of his foolish infatuation with Rose Salterne, and his brother Frank’s choice to renounce that fruitless love too, “aye, sweet lad, you have chosen the better path, you and your brother both, and no man shall take that gift from you. Only be strong, lad, and trust that God will make a true man of you.”

“I do trust Him,” Amyas said.

“Thank God for that,” Sir Richard replied. “You have lifted the great weight off my heart that I carried for you from the day your good father, who sleeps in peace, gave you into my care. For all the best things in the world, Amyas, when misused, turn to the very worst: the love of a woman, which can lift a man’s soul all the way to heaven, can drag him just as easily down to hell. But you have learned better. You know, as our old German forefathers did, as Tacitus wrote, that *late is the love of young men, and so their strength of manhood never runs dry*. And more than that: that silly, frivolous fashion of the French and Italians, where no boy counts himself a man unless he hangs at some woman’s apron strings, fawning over maids and married wives alike, that habit is no less poisonous to the soul than open sin. It breeds vanity and waste, envy and bitter resentment, even hatred and murder, all too often. Even if you escape that, you squander the rich treasure of your manly, full-hearted worship on a dozen trivial flings, so that when your own bride at last comes to you, all that is left for her is the dregs, the empty leftover of your heart, and she becomes no more than a pretty ornament for your table, a mere means to give you heirs. God, who saved me from that living death, save you too!” As he spoke, he glanced down at his wife on the terrace below, and she, as if guessing instinctively that he spoke of her, looked up with a smile so soft and sweet that Sir Richard’s stern, weathered warrior face melted into a glow of pure, bright happiness.

Amyas looked at them both and sighed, then turned the conversation sharply.

“Then I may leave for Ireland tomorrow?”

“You shall sail on the *Mary* for Milford Haven, carrying these dispatches to Captain Winter. If the wind holds, you may tell the master to drop down the river tonight and put to sea at once – we cannot waste a single day.”

“Winter?” Amyas frowned. “He is no friend of mine, the coward who abandoned Drake and the rest of us in the Straits of Magellan.”

“Duty does not wait for private quarrels, even just ones, lad. But he will not be your commanding officer. When you reach the Lord Deputy, give him this letter, and he will set you to work – hard work, I promise you.”

“I want nothing better.”

“Well said. The best reward for work well done is more work to do. The man who is faithful over a few small things will always be given charge over many greater ones. That is the only true, heroic rest worthy of gentlemen and sons of God. As for the men who look for idleness in this life or the next, who hope God will feed them nice things like a fussy nurse feeds a spoiled child, I count them cowards and base, even if they call themselves saints and the elect.”

“I wish you could make my poor cousin see that.”

“He has yet to learn what it means to lose your life to save it, Amyas. Bad men have taught him – and I fear these Anabaptists and strict Puritans at home teach little else – that the one great business of every man is to save his own soul after he dies, every man for himself alone, that this selfish preoccupation, not divine self-sacrifice, is the one thing needful, the better part Mary chose in the Gospel.”

“Men are selfish enough already, without being taught to make a virtue of it,” Amyas said.

“Aye, lad. For my part, if I could hang up such a teacher high for an enemy of mankind and a corrupter of youth, I would do it gladly. Is there not enough cowardice and self-seeking already in the hearts of us fallen sons of Adam, without these false prophets using the bait of heaven and the terror of hell to turn our dirtiest vices into holy virtues and the surest way to bliss? Farewell to chivalry and desperate valor, farewell to patriotism and loyalty, farewell to England and the manhood of England, once our preachers start bidding every man, Jesuit-like, to care first and foremost for what they call the safety of his own soul. Every man will be afraid to die at his post, because he will fear he is not fit to meet his end. Amyas, do your duty like a man, to your country, your queen, and your God, and count your own life a worthless thing, as all the holy men of old did. Do your work, lad, and leave your soul in the care of the One who is just and merciful, who rewards every man according to his deeds. God shows no favor to persons. Now come in, take your letters, and mount your horse. If I hear you died at Smerwick Fort, all your wounds in front, I shall weep for your mother, lad – but I shall never weep for you.”

If any reader is startled to find a fine gentleman and hardened warrior like Sir Richard quoting and thinking deeply on Scripture, let them turn to the writings of that age, and see for themselves how thoroughly the men of that world were steeped in the Gospel, free from any taint of superstitious fear or false, performative piety. The faith of those days was one no soldier need ever have been ashamed to confess. Sir Richard died as he lived, without a shudder, without a whine: these were his last words, fifteen years later, when he lay shot through and through, a captive among Spanish Catholics, surrounded by priests, crucifixes, and all the trappings of the Inquisition: “Here die I, Richard Grenville, with a joyful and quiet mind; for that I have ended my life as a true soldier ought, fighting for his country, queen, religion, and honor: my soul willingly departing from this body, leaving behind the lasting fame of having behaved as every valiant soldier is in his duty bound to do.” Those were the last words of the man the pulpits of Elizabeth’s day had taught to serve his God and his country fearlessly.

But back to our tale. The day’s events were far from over. When Sir Richard and Amyas stepped into the great hall, the old steward came hurrying up, flustered, in search of his master.

“There’s a roguish sort of masterless man at the gate, Sir Richard, a forward fellow who swears he won’t leave till he speaks to you.”

“A masterless man? He’d better not speak to me, unless he’s got a fancy for gaol and the gallows.”

“Well, your worship,” the steward said, “I reckon that’s just what he wants, for he’s sworn he’ll not budge an inch from the gate until he sees you.”

“Sees me? By Halidame, he shall see me, here and at Launceston Gaol too, if he likes. Bring him in.”

“Faith, Sir Richard, we’re half afraid – with your good leave –”

“Hey, Tony,” Amyas laughed. “Who ever heard of being afraid when Sir Richard gives you leave?”

“What, does the fellow have horns and a tail?”

“Mass no! But we fear it’s treason for your honor, for the man’s pricked all over with heathen tattoos, brown as a filbert nut, tall and raw-boned as a plough ox, a foreigner, and carrying a great heavy staff. We think he’s some sort of Jesuit, or wild Irish rebel. The grooms won’t lay a hand on him, and the dogs won’t go near him, else he’d have had a ducking under the pump by now – the men that saw him coming up the hill swear he breathed fire out of his mouth.”

“Fire out of his mouth?” Sir Richard huffed. “The men are drunk.”

“Pricked all over? That’s a sailor’s mark,” Amyas said. “Let me go out and see the fellow. If he needs throwing out –”

“He’s not so big you couldn’t tuck him in your coat pocket. Go on, lad, while I finish writing my dispatches.”

Amyas went out to the back gate, where the tall, ragged, raw-boned man leaned on his heavy staff, as covered in tattoos as the steward had said.

“Hey, mate!” Amyas called. “Before we talk, you’ll be so good as to lay down that Plymouth cloak of yours.” He nodded at the cudgel, the common West Country sailor’s nickname for the stout club all mariners carried.

“I’ll warrant,” the old steward muttered, “where he found that ‘cloak’ he found a purse he stole not long before.”

“But no hose or doublet to go with it, so the magic power of his staff hasn’t done him much good. Lay it down, man, and speak like a Christian, if you are one.”

“I am a Christian, even if I look like a heathen, and no rogue, though masterless, alas. I want nothing, deserve nothing, I only ask to speak to Sir Richard before I go on my way.”

There was a quiet, humble dignity to the man’s tone that caught Amyas’s attention. He asked more gently where he was bound, and where he had come from.

“From Padstow port, sir, bound for Clovelly town, to see my old mother, if she is yet alive, which God alone knows.”

“A Clovelly man! Why didn’t you say so in the first place?” all the grooms cried out at once, for any West Countryman was a brother to them. The old steward spoke up.

“What’s your mother’s name, then?”

“Susan Yeo.”

“What, the one who lived under the old archway by the quay?” a groom asked.

“Lived?” the man said.

“Aye, sure enough, we heard she died three days back, poor soul.”

The man stood perfectly silent, unmoving, for a full minute, then said quietly to himself, in fluent Spanish: “That which is, is best.”

“You speak Spanish?” Amyas asked, more and more curious.

“I had to, young sir. I was five years in the Spanish Main, and only set foot on English soil two days ago. If you let me speak to Sir Richard, I will tell him a tale that will make the ears of everyone that hears it tingle. If I cannot, I will go on to Mr. Cary of Clovelly, if he still lives, and unburden my soul to him. But I would far rather speak to a man who is a mariner, like myself.”

“And you shall,” Amyas said. “Steward, we will bring this man inside. Rags and all, he is a man of wit and honesty.” He led the sailor in.

“I only hope he is not one of those Popish murderers,” the old steward said, keeping a safe distance from him as they crossed the hall.

“Popish, old master? There’s little fear of that. Look here!” He pulled back the ragged edge of his shirt, to show a ghastly, silvery scar that circled his wrist and wound all the way up his forearm.

“I got that on the rack, in the Lima Inquisition,” he said, quiet as anything.

“O Lord, why didn’t you tell us you were a poor persecuted Christian?” the steward stammered, suddenly penitent.

“Because I have never asked for pity, and I was not going to turn myself into a beggar or a sideshow for that scar. The Lord delivered me, that is enough.”

“By heaven, you are a brave fellow!” Amyas cried. “Come straight to Sir Richard’s study at once.”

They walked in to find Sir Richard at his desk, surrounded by books, dispatches, state papers, and warrants. Though he was not yet, as he would be in later years, admiral, general, Member of Parliament, Privy Councillor, and justice of the peace all at once, there were few great men in the realm he did not correspond with, few great state matters he did not have a hand in.

“Hey there, Amyas! Have you tied up this wild man already, and brought him in to swear allegiance to the queen?”

Before Amyas could answer, the sailor looked at him, sharp. “Amyas? Is that your name, sir?”

“Amyas Leigh, of Burrough by Bideford, at your service

“It is virtue, yea virtue, gentlemen, that maketh gentlemen; that maketh the poor rich, the base-born noble, the subject a sovereign, the deformed beautiful, the sick whole, the weak strong, the most miserable most happy. There are two principal and peculiar gifts in the nature of man, knowledge and reason; the one commandeth, and the other obeyeth: these things neither the whirling wheel of fortune can change, neither the deceitful cavillings of worldlings separate, neither sickness abate, neither age abolish.” -- LILLY's *Euphues*, 1586.

It falls to me now to tell the story of the founding of that chivalrous, storied Brotherhood of the Rose: a band that within a handful of years would win renown across their native Devon, and strike fear into enemies across Ireland, the Netherlands, the Spanish Main, and the uncharted heart of South America. Should any reader dismiss this chapter as quixotic, over-fantastical nonsense, let them pause to remember: the generation of Elizabethan men who spoke and acted this way over matters of love and honor were also seasoned, fearless soldiers, shrewd and far-sighted politicians. The man who wrote *Arcadia* was, for all his youth, one of the sharpest, most subtle diplomats in all of Europe. The poet who penned *The Faerie Queene* also drafted the sober, hard-headed *A View of the Present State of Ireland*. If any sneer and quote Lily’s *Euphues* at me like it is nothing but foppish nonsense, I will only ask: have they ever read it? If they have, they would find it, for all its occasional tedious pedantry, as brave, righteous, and pious a book as any man could hope to read. I ask no better proof of the nobility of the Elizabethan age than the simple fact that *Euphues* and *Arcadia* were the most popular romances of the day.

Sir Walter Scott may have used the foppish Sir Piercie Shafton to mock the Euphuists and the performative courtliness that travelled Englishmen of the era adopted, but that character is riddled with anachronisms, not least that the Euphuist knight in Scott’s story references Sidney’s duel with Lord Oxford a full ten years before it ever happened. Even more to the point: that absurd fop bears no resemblance to the earnest readers of Lily’s work. His true spiritual ancestors are the quarrelsome Gabriel Harvey and Lord Oxford, not the earnest young Devon lads of our story.

Let us stop sneering at these old Elizabethan gallants, and see them for what they are: our own ancestors, wild with the fire of youth, already bearing all the core virtues that make a true Englishman. Their daring on the sea and on the battlefield, their sharp political wit, their deep reverence for the law, their solemn belief in the great purpose of the English nation: all these are the ancestors of those same traits that live in us still. Their famous chivalry is nothing but a different, older wrapping for that same tenderness and mercy that still stands as the twin of English valor today. Even their eagerness to learn from Continental manners and literature speaks to that old Anglo-Norman curiosity and open-heartedness that has let us absorb the wisdom and civilization of every age and every land, never losing our own distinct national character in the process.

So on with the story. If any reader does not care for it, they are welcome to turn the page to a tale that suits their fancy better.

Amyas could not sail the next day, nor the day after that: a stiff southwester gale was blowing dead against the bay, and the crew had only just finished moving his ship *Mary Grenville* down the river to Appledore Pool, anchored and ready to cast off the second the wind shifted. He went home quietly, and when his mother set off riding pillion behind their old serving-man to visit Clovelly where Frank lay wounded, he escorted them as far as Bideford. There on the High Street he met a procession of horsemen led by Will Cary, clad head to toe in shining polished armor, his sword at his thigh and his helmet slung from his saddlebow: as gallant a young gentleman as any Bideford housewife had ever gawked at through a door or window. Behind him on shaggy country ponies rode four stout serving-men, carrying lances, baggage, longbows, swords, and bucklers, and at the very back, much to Mrs. Leigh’s joy, Frank himself, lying comfortable in a horse-litter.

Frank insisted his wounds were only shallow flesh cuts, the attacker’s dagger having glanced off his ribs. He refused to go home to Burrough, and said he would lodge instead with Will Cary at the Ship Tavern, right at the foot of Bideford Bridge, so he could see Amyas off on his voyage before he left. No sooner was he settled on a couch there than he held open court, gossips of the whole town filing in to call on him, spinning all kinds of little white lies about who could have possibly put him in such a sorry state.

While the crowd milled around them, Frank and Amyas hatched a plan that they put into action the very next day, market day. First Amyas gave the innkeeper orders to start a bustle of roasting, boiling, and frying the likes of which the Ship Tavern had never seen in all its history. Then Amyas wandered out into the market, and invited every single one of Rose Salterne’s known suitors to a private, merry supper, one by one, each man thinking he was the only honored guest.

Each man arrived expecting an intimate, exclusive feast, and found himself seated at the same table with half a dozen rivals for the Rose’s hand, men he had not spoken to in half a year out of petty jealousy. All were far too well-bred to let the Leighs see how furious they were, though. Frank propped himself up on his couch at the head of the table, Amyas took the seat at the foot, and the two of them plied their guests with such heaps of good food that for a long no one had to make awkward small talk. The two hosts joked, swapped tales, and carried the whole conversation so easily that Will Cary, who never could resist a good laugh, cracked the first jest, then another, and soon he was teasing the tavern waiters so mercilessly that the men stumbled out of the room holding their sides, bursting with laughter.

By the time the table was cleared and the sack and sugar were passed around, the whole company had drunk the toast “Queen and Bible” with all the proper ceremony. Frank made his first move.

“Gentlemen, I have a toast for you. To the gentlemen of the Irish wars! May Ireland never lack a St. Leger to stand by a Fortescue, a Fortescue to stand by a St. Leger, and a Chichester to stand by both!”

The toast broke the ice further: the three young men from those families each drank to each other’s houses, returned thanks, and the conversation flowed freely. Young Fortescue then raised a toast to “Amyas Leigh, and all bold Devon mariners,” and Amyas replied in his plain, blunt way that he could ask for no better fortune than to sail all the way around the world again with every man at that table as his fellow adventurer, and give the Spaniards another taste of what Devon men were made of.

The wine flowed sweet, every man’s tongue loosened, and the last of the awkward frost melted away. This was the exact moment Frank had been waiting for.

“Gentlemen,” he said, “let me give you a health I know none of you will refuse to drink with all your hearts. A lady whose beauty and virtue are so great that no one notices the lowliness of her birth beside them. A lady I would call peerless in loveliness, if not for the fact that every gentleman here has sisters who could easily challenge her for the prize of Venus’s girdle — and yet those very same fine ladies, who know far more of beauty than I, have openly given her the golden apple in my hearing. If the goddesses themselves vote on the fairest, Paris himself has no business sitting on the judge’s seat. Gentlemen, your hearts already know who I speak of. Drink to the Rose of Torridge!”

Had Rose herself walked through the tavern door, she could not have caused more shock. Every guest turned red, then pale, then red again, and stared at the man next to him as if to say, “What right have you to even speak her name? Lift that glass and I will dash it out of your hand.” But Frank drank on, unflappable.

“To the health of the Rose of Torridge! And a double health to that worthy gentleman, whomever she shall choose to grace with her love!”

“Well played, cunning Frank Leigh!” boomed the blunt, hearty Will Cary. “None of us can quarrel with you now, even if we are all still sulking at each other. Every last one of us here is fool enough to think she likes us best. You just toasted all of us, you rascal!”

“And so I have,” Frank smiled. “What better thing can you do, gentlemen, than drink each other’s healths in turn, and prove yourselves true gentlemen, true Christians, and true lovers? For what is love, but the very breath of that God whose name is Love? The ancients called Eros the eldest of all the gods, the power that turned the clashing chaos into harmony and order. Why then would lovers make strife out of love? The soul cannot be filled with that divine love and then burn with envy and jealousy, division and distrust. The rose has thorns, but it leaves poison and stings to the nettle. Cupid has his arrow, but he hurls no scorpions. Surely anyone who loves rightly will not only find love lovely, they will grow more lovely themselves.”

His rapid, passionate speech had kept every man so stunned none had time to grow angry — until he paused, out of breath from his wounds. Mr. Coffin, the eldest of the country squires, who had sat bolt upright staring at the wall this whole time, got quietly to his feet and made for the door, fed up with what he saw as Frank’s impertinent interference. One more minute, and the old tale of the Lapiths’ bloody wedding feast might well have repeated itself.

But Frank never faltered.

“Mr. Coffin!” he called, in a tone that made the man stop in his tracks and turn around. His face was so tender, so earnest and pleading that no man could have looked at it for five minutes and held a grudge. “My dear Mr. Coffin! If my eagerness made me forget the bounds of courtesy, I beg you to forgive me. Do not add the grief of losing a friend to the heavy griefs I already carry. Only hear me out patiently, and if you are still angry after that, I will beg your forgiveness a second time.”

Coffin, who had never been to court, had always been a little jealous of Frank’s polished court manners, and a little nettled at being lumped in with boys barely eighteen years old. But when Frank humbled himself so openly and threw himself on Coffin’s mercy, the honest, good-natured squire — who had known Frank since they were both toddlers — had no choice but to sit back down again and listen.

“Believe me, gentlemen,” Frank went on, “I do not think myself better than any of you, any more than a sailor washed ashore by the surf thinks he is better than the comrade still fighting the waves. I, too, love her. The sun that dazzled you dazzled me. The lodestone that drew you drew me. But I have learnt to love you all for loving what I love, to admire you for admiring that which I admire. Why should worship of the same beautiful woman not be a bond of friendship between us, just as loyalty to the same queen binds subjects, or devotion to the same God binds worshippers? Why treat each other like enemies, when we are all fellow worshippers? Shall we make ourselves unworthy of her by the very jealousy we think will help us win her? A lady will never favor the man whose hands are stained with the blood of the people she likes. Our only chance of winning her, if she is worth winning, is to honor who she honors, to love who she loves. If we must compete, let us compete in virtue, in loyalty to our queen, in courage against her enemies, in kindness to the afflicted and the oppressed. And why not make this shared love for her the sacrament of our shared friendship? We will vow to abide by her choice, to rejoice in her joy, and whoever she picks for her husband, we will swear to be his loyal, faithful friend. As I do now, openly, before all of you.”

There was a long pause. At last young Fortescue spoke.
“That sounds like a very generous bargain for you, sir — you seem far more likely to win her than any of us country oafs.”

“Set your mind at rest,” Frank laughed. “I have not a scrap more idea what she thinks of any man than you. For my own peace of mind, I have sworn a vow not to see her, or even hear news of her, for three full years. Dixi — I have spoken.”

Coffin stood up again.
“Gentlemen, I may not be able to match Mr. Leigh’s fine words, but I will not be outdone in generosity. If he is leaving this part of the country for three years, so am I.”

“And I am off to Ireland, as Amyas knows, to cool my hot temper in a bog,” Will Cary cried, holding out his hand to Coffin. “Give me your hand, old man! You are heart of oak, not some soft wishy-washy elm plank. I was planning to fight you today, you know —”
“I would have been delighted to oblige you, sir,” Coffin said, bowing.
“But now I am all love and charity to the whole world! Does anyone want to pull my nose? Send me on an errand? Borrow five pounds off me? Buy a horse from me that’s worth twice the asking price? Come on! All of you, join hands, swear eternal friendship. We need a name for this sacred order, Frank Leigh — you’re the clever one, christen us!”

“The Rose,” Frank said quietly, seeing his plan had worked perfectly, determined to seal the moment before anyone could change their mind.

“The Rose!” Cary yelled, grabbing Coffin’s right hand and Fortescue’s left. “Come on, lads, all of you!”

One by one, every man in the room gave in, whether it was Frank’s chivalrous speech, Cary’s infectious good humor, Amyas’s spiced sack, or just the goodness inherent in every young man’s heart if someone will take the trouble to call it out. They joined hands all round, and swore on the hilt of Amyas’s sword that they would no longer make fools of themselves over petty jealousy. They would stand by each other, honor the Rose, and never grumble no matter who she chose to dance with, flirt with, or marry. To spread the fame of the Rose and their brotherhood across the whole world, they swore to get their fathers’ permission (easy enough to win in those bold days) to go off to wars wherever there was fighting to be done abroad, to emulate the courage and courtesy of great knights like Bayard, Sidney, and Walter Manny.

Frank smiled his soft, quiet smile — the kind where his eyes crinkled even when his lips did not. “Gentlemen,” he said, “you will never, ever regret this day.”

“Regret?” Cary snorted. “I already feel as saintly as you look, Saint Silver-Tongue. Wait — was that a sneeze? The cat?”

“More like a lion, by the sound of it,” Amyas said, lunging for the heavy arras hanging on the wall behind him. “There’s a door behind here!”

He ducked through the hidden gap in the tapestry, and dragged out by the scruff of his neck one very sheepish Mr. John Brimblecombe.

You may well remember him: that fat schoolmaster’s son Sir Richard Grenville punished for tale-bearing three years before, sending him off to Oxford instead of Coventry. He was twenty-one now, a Bachelor of Arts, hanging about Bideford before he went back to university after Christmas to study divinity, planning to come home one day and serve as parson for his home parish.

Jack was built exactly like a prize show pig: plump mulberry complexion dotted with a few sparse black bristles, sleek skin that looked like it might burst any second, short waddling legs, a little curved back, a squeaky cracked voice, tiny twinkling eyes, and a little upturned nose that was always sniffing the air for the nearest savory smell, perfectly content to make do with even the worst meal if a better one wasn’t on offer. He’d snuck in behind the arras earlier to beg a bite of the feast, and gotten so caught up in the ceremony he’d accidentally sneezed loud enough to give his hiding place away.

“Take aim, you noble musqueteers, And shoot you round about; Stand to it, valiant pikemen, And we shall keep them out. There's not a man of all of us A foot will backward flee; I'll be the foremost man in fight, Says brave Lord Willoughby!”

It was the blessed, soft Christmas afternoon. The golden light was fading fast, evensong had rung out and ended, and Bideford’s good townsfolk trooped home in merry chattering groups: fathers herding laughing children, sweethearts arm in arm, all bound for spiced cakes, frothing nut-brown ale, fiery flapdragon games, and mummers’ plays, for the full joy of Christmas night. Only one lady, wrapped tight in a somber black muffler and followed by her quiet maid, walked swiftly yet sadly toward the long stone causeway and bridge that led over the water to Northam town. Sir Richard Grenville and his wife cantered up beside her and stopped her with gentle courtesy.

“You will come home with us, Mrs. Leigh,” Lady Grenville said warmly, “and share a pleasant Christmas night by our fire?”

Mrs. Leigh smiled soft and sad, laid one hand on Lady Grenville’s arm, and pointed westward with the other. “I cannot bring myself to feast merrily while that sound hums in my ears.”

The little group around her turned to follow her gaze. Above their heads the soft blue dusk streaked to gray, and the first misty stars pricked through here and there. But westward, where the rolling downs and woods of Raleigh’s estate met those of Abbotsham, the blue sky was laced with wispy white sea-fret, streaked with iridescent afterglow that blushed all the shifting colors of a dying dolphin, and low on the far horizon stretched a long band of pale grassy green over the Atlantic. But the sound that troubled Mrs. Leigh? None of the merry townsfolk, their ears dulled by the day’s bustle, had noticed it till that moment. Listen now, though: not a breath of wind stirred a single blade of grass inland, and yet the air thrumming with a low, deep roar that hovered over down and wood, salt marsh and river, like the roll of a thousand cartwheels, the tramp of endless marching armies – the thunder of a massive storm surge pummeling the boulders of the far-off pebble ridge, two miles out to sea.

“The ridge is loud tonight,” Sir Richard remarked. “There’s a gale blowing somewhere far out in the Atlantic.”

“The gale is blowing now, where my boy is. God help him,” Mrs. Leigh whispered, and all around knew she spoke the plain truth. The Atlantic storm had sent its herald ahead: that smooth, roaring ground-swell that carried the sound of the coming tempest miles inland. By tomorrow, the pebbles that now rattled down the ridge with every retreating wave would be leaping to its very crest, hurled like cannon shot far ashore across the marsh by the force of the oncoming surf, fleeing before the wrath of a full western hurricane.

“God help my boy,” Mrs. Leigh said again.

“God is as near a man at sea as He is on land,” good Sir Richard reminded her gently.

“That is true enough. But I am a lone mother, and my heart has no stomach for feasting this night. I will go home and pray.”

And so she walked on up the lane, and spent all Christmas night pacing between her prayers, listening to the thunder of the distant surge, till long before sunrise that low rumble was swallowed whole by the full, howling roar of the unleashed storm.

Where was Amyas on that same Christmas afternoon?

Amyas sat bareheaded in the stern of a pitching small boat in Smerwick Bay, sharp spray whistling through his curly hair, shouting cheer over the gale: “Pull hard, my merry men, never mind if you ship a sea! Cannon shot don’t spoil for a little salt water!”

His mother’s premonition had come true. The still, dark, steamy lulls of early winter had ended the night before, and a brutal western gale had raged nonstop over the Irish coast for twelve full hours.

The short light of the winter day was fading fast. Behind the boat, lines of frothing billows were lashed to mist by the tempest. Beside them, green foam-fringed walls of surf hurled themselves against black jagged rocks, shattering into a thousand white cascades. Ahead lay the deep, sheltered bay, but they could see little of it: four miles out to sea, a sloping roof of thick gray whirling cloud stretched over their heads, all the way inland, cutting off the cliff tops halfway up, swallowing the Kerry mountains, and plunging the hollows of the distant sea lochs into pitch-black night. Under that awful swirling roof of mist, the storm howled inland, sweeping great clumps of foam and stinging salt spray before it, till all the land was hazy, dim, and dun.

But let the wind howl. There was more smoke than salt spray rising into that gale, more thunder than sea-surge echoing off the Smerwick cliffs. Red sparks flashed in the evening gloom from the sandhills that lined the bay, sparks that never fell from heaven. The Spanish fort the invaders had named Del Oro, flying the hated golden flag of Spain, held San Josepho and eight hundred of his Spanish and Italian mercenary troops. Three nights prior, Amyas, Salvation Yeo, and the rest of Captain Winter’s ablest seamen had slung four heavy culverins off the admiral’s main deck, floated them ashore through the surf, and dragged them up to the battery built among the sand-dunes. Now the hour had come to see if foreign condottieri could hold British ground against the rough men of Devon.

No one could blame Amyas, in the thick of the action, for forgetting his lonely mother back at Burrough Court. His mind was fixed on those quick, bright flashes of muzzle fire across the sandhills, where Salvation Yeo was hurling eighteen-pound shot with deadly aim, watching with a cool, bitter smile of triumph as sand flew and gabions crashed apart. Amyas and his crew had risked their lives running back to the ship for fresh ammunition, for the battery had run out of proper roundshot four hours earlier, and had been firing rough paving stones for want of better. They ran their boat ashore through the surf in the only little cove that made landing possible, not caring if the hull stove to splinters, and every man scrambled over the dunes with two heavy cannon balls slung across his shoulders. Amyas leaped straight into the trenches, shouting merrily to Yeo: “More food for the bull-dog guns, Gunner! Christmas plums for the Spaniards’ pudding!”

“Don’t speak to a man while he’s at his work, Master Amyas! I’ve missed five times running, and I swear the foul fiend himself is turning my shot aside. But I’ll bring that cursed Popish rag down, as I’m a living sinner.”

“Down with it then! No one’s asking you to shoot crooked. Use good iron, not footy paving stones.”

“Thank the Lord, here’s proper roundshot at last. Stand clear, men!”

Yeo’s eighteen-pounder roared once more. And glory be! The great yellow Spanish flag, streaming in the gale, lifted clean into the air, flagstaff and all, pitched wildly headfirst far away to leeward.

A roar of cheers burst from the English sailors, answered by the soldiers in the trenches, shaking the very low cloud above their heads. But before the echoes died, a tall officer leaped onto the parapet of the fort, grabbed the fallen flag, spiked it to the end of his lance, and held it firm against the gale while his men hauled a new staff up inside the walls.

A dozen English longbows drew on the daring foeman in an instant – but Amyas shouted from behind: “Shame on you, lads! Stand down! Give a brave gentleman his due courtesy!”

The archers lowered their bows. Amyas sprang onto the battery’s rampart, doffed his hat, and bowed deep to the man holding the flag. The Spanish officer, as soon as he had handed the flag off to his men, returned the bow courteously before descending back out of sight.

By now it was nearly dark, and firing slackened on all sides. The gunners covered their culverins with tarpaulins for the night, and Amyas volunteered to stand watch till midnight. The rest of the force ate their scanty supper of dry biscuit – provisions were running very thin now – then curled up under arms among the sandhills and grumbled themselves to sleep.

Amyas had paced up and down the gusty dark for an hour or more, exchanging a quiet word now and then with the sentries, when two men strolled into the battery deep in conversation. One was clad in full armor; the other wore the plain short cloak of a scholar and man of peace. Their talk had nothing to do with sieges, sallies, catapults, bombardments or culverins – it was all about English hexameters.

Do not think, gentle reader, that the pair were fiddling while Rome burned. In that critical year of 1580, the great commonwealth of English poetry and letters was in far greater danger from those very hexameters than the miserable Irish campaign, as Raleigh called it, ever was from the Spaniards.

Copying classical Greek and Roman metre, “versifying” as the learned called it in opposition to simple rhyming, was all the rage among Cambridge’s wits. Stonyhurst and others had churned out clumsy hexameter translations of epic poems that were little more than doggerel, and every young wit was trying his hand at iambics, sapphics, elegiacs, twisting the Queen’s English into unnatural shapes that offended the ear. Gabriel Harvey, the Cambridge pedant, had even persuaded Spenser, Dyer, Sidney, and the whole clique of courtly literary minds around them to follow his stilted model, and had sneered at the opening book of *The Faerie Queene* (still in manuscript) as a shameful decline from classical rigor into romantic “trash.” Spenser, still raw from being jilted by a fair maid of Kent, had been wasting his mighty genius on that awkward doggerel, when a publisher had just released a volume of letters between Harvey and Spenser pushing the “new reformed versifying” that had all the London wits buzzing.

The two men bantered back and forth. Raleigh, the unabashed romantic, teased Spenser mercilessly for stretching English words till their joints snapped like an inquisitor torturing a heretic, saying a rough old fishwife’s simple rhyming ballad was worth all the frilly classical metres in the world.

“Listen,” Raleigh laughed, “there’s our young giant over there comforting his soul with a proper old ballad. No tortured words, no splitting ‘blindfold’ into three syllables to fill a metre, just rhyme and reason together.”

And Amyas, who had been humming half to himself, burst out full-throated with an old, simple Christmas carol his mother had taught him as a boy:
> “As Joseph was a-walking He heard an angel sing –
> This night shall be the birth night Of Christ, our heavenly King.
> His birthbed shall be neither In housen nor in hall,
> Nor in the place of paradise, But in the oxen's stall.
> He neither shall be rocked In silver nor in gold,
> But in the wooden manger That lieth on the mould.
> He neither shall be washen With white wine nor with red,
> But with the fair spring water That on you shall be shed.
> He neither shall be clothed In purple nor in pall,
> But in the fair white linen That usen babies all.'
> As Joseph was a-walking Thus did the angel sing,
> And Mary's Son at midnight Was born to be our King.
> Then be you glad, good people, At this time of the year;
> And light you up your candles, For His star it shineth clear.”

Spenser fell silent for a long moment, then sighed. “That brave lad shames me. Singing the hymns his mother taught him, right under the muzzles of Spanish guns, while I have been moping unmanly over my own petty heartbreak, writing verses of cowardly despair. I will set my heart to higher work than whining over the hand that chastens me.”

Raleigh laughed and said they would carve out a place for Amyas in *The Faerie Queene*, as the Knight of Common Sense. They called over to him, and the three chatted for a time, Raleigh cynically explaining how slander and flattery got men ahead at court, while Amyas brushed it all off, saying he would rather be honest and wake to heaven than rich and damned. Then Raleigh said the Lord Deputy had sent them to fetch Amyas to supper, where a good hunk of boiled horse meat waited for him, but Amyas refused to leave his post. “I won’t budge till morning,” he said. “There’s a stir in the fort, and I don’t trust deserters’ reports that their spirits are broken. If a man is false to his own masters, he’ll be false to us.”

Raleigh teased him a little more, then he and Spenser stumbled back off into the dark, leaving Amyas to pace the battery alone. Amyas puzzled over Raleigh’s sharp, cynical words and Spenser’s soft melancholy, and decided that the cleverest, most learned and ambitious people were almost always the unhappiest. He thanked his lucky stars he was no scholar, no courtier, no poet – just a plain Devon man, with a clear duty to do. He ate his horse meat as if it were venison, said his prayers, thought of his lonely mother back home, of his brother Frank feasting at the Queen’s glittering court, then of Rose Salterne, whom he tried very hard not to think about, and inevitably thought of all the more.

The hours dragged on, past eleven o’clock. All lights were out in the battery and the ships anchored in the bay. No sound broke the dark but the steady tramp of the two sentries, and an occasional grunt from the men sleeping under arms twenty yards away. The rain started pouring down hard.

Suddenly Amyas heard a rustle in the harsh sand-grass. The wind whistled through it loud enough, but that sound was not the wind. Then a soft sliding noise: something had slipped down the face of a sand-dune, dislodging a small shower of sand behind it. Amyas stopped dead, crouched down beside his culverin, and pressed his ear to the rampart. He heard the distinct, crunching sound of approaching feet. Not rabbits, he thought, shrewd and alert. Men.

“And therewith he blent, and cried ha! As though he had been stricken to the harte.” — *Palamon and Arcite*

That panic-stricken, heart-stopping shock that struck Chaucer’s knight in his prison cell struck Don Guzman de Soto too, and it came about in this wise.

The Spanish captive settled easily enough at Bideford on his parole, lodged in far finer quarters than he had known for years, and went with the flow like any seasoned soldier of fortune. Barely a month into his stay with Sir Richard Grenville, old Mayor Salterne invited himself to supper. At first Don Guzman had blinked in confusion at the strange English custom of welcoming plain burghers, men who kept ledgers and took on apprentices, to sit above the salt on equal footing with noblemen — but he knew full well that Richard Grenville was too proud a gentleman to break local custom, so he set his Spanish pride aside. Before the evening was out, he found the mayor neither foolish nor vulgar, and found himself drawn into easy conversation. Salterne was courteous enough to show deference to his guest’s high rank, but held his own opinions with a quiet dignity the Spaniard had only ever seen among the merchant princes of Genoa and Venice.

As supper ended, Salterne begged Grenville to grace his humble home for a meal the next evening, then turned frankly to Don Guzman. He knew, he said, that a Spanish grandee would stoop far to sit at a simple tradesman’s table, but if the Don would deign to come, he would find cheer fit for any rank, no matter the company. Bored and looking for any amusement to pass the slow parole days, Don Guzman accepted the invitation, and was treated to an excellent feast and a cellar of fine wine, had he chosen to drink it.

Shrewd as any Elizabethan merchant with a nose for new trade — as he would later prove when he poured his wealth into the Virginia settlement project — Salterne was determined to pry every secret he could about the Indies out of his unusual guest. He first tried to loosen the Spaniard’s tongue with copious draughts of sack, a plan that failed entirely: Don Guzman was as abstemious as a Trappist monk, drinking little but water. His second gambit, a stream of carefully crafted questions about the Spanish Main, Peru, the Moluccas, China and all the far flung trade routes, fared little better, for the Don was as wily as a fox and answered only with empty, vague pleasantries.

Midway through this gentle war of words, the Rose of Torridge stepped into the room, as radiant as ever. She added her own soft, artless questions to her father’s, and Don Guzman could not find it in him to dodge them. He revealed no critical commercial secrets, but spun tales of distant lands that entertained the two of them through the rest of the evening.

The old scholar Burton wrote in his *Anatomy of Melancholy* that Eros, for all the grand tales of him being Jove and Venus’ eldest son, is often little more than the child of idleness and full bellies. Don Guzman’s circumstances certainly lent that old slander credence: idle as all captives are forced to be, and feasted well on Sir Richard Grenville’s generous table, he had already half-heartedly looked for a casual romance to while away the weeks. He had fleetingly, shamefully, thought of Grenville’s wife first — but he was a man of honor, and his host’s guest, so he prided himself on his self-denial to banish that thrilling, dangerous fancy from his mind.

With Lady Grenville out of reach, Rose Salterne slipped into his thoughts instead, with no distinct intent to wrong her, just for amusement at first, and then because he could not help himself. The Rose of Torridge loved to be admired by all, but there was an innocent, unworldly freshness to her he had never encountered before. “A peacock’s poise and a dove’s gentle heart,” he thought to himself, the combination so enchanting that if he could persuade her to love no one but him, he might well fool himself into loving her back. He resolved prudently to keep out of her way — but the days dragged, Lady Grenville left for Stow and left him alone rattling around Grenville’s great house, and there was nothing for it but to wander down to the archery butts gardens, show off his fine black cloak and feathered hat, play rackets with the town’s young men, bowls with the elders, and inevitably get himself invited back to supper at the mayor’s house.

There he was treated like royalty, not just for his rank, but for the rare stories he could tell as a man who had travelled the whole world. This was the dawn of Elizabethan English commerce: every merchant in Bideford, and across the whole country, had their imagination fired by dreams of new discoveries, trade companies, royal patents, and far settlements. The Ottoman sultan had just granted unprecedented trading privileges to the English, Roger Bodenham had sailed the Levant aboard the *Aucher* to great profit, Captain Hare had just set sail for Brazil aboard the *Minion* with Devonshire kersies and Manchester cottages to trade for sugar at Santos, Drake had proven you could plunder the Spanish American coasts and carve out an East Indian trade route, Frobisher and Davis were pushing through the northern ice in search of a Northwest Passage to China, and Anthony Jenkinson of the Muscovy Company had travelled as far as Bokhara and Siberia, where the brave Sir Hugh Willoughby and his entire crew had frozen to death three decades prior.

Don Guzman watched these sharp Devon merchants talk broad, nasal Devon dialect, and he saw exactly the threat they posed. Spain claimed the entirety of the New World by right of the Pope’s grant, she had crushed the Dutch, turned Venice into her bank and Genoa into her mercenary, absorbed all of Portugal’s vast East Indian trade — but none of that would ever make her mistress of the world while these stubborn islanders insisted on free seas and free trade for all, backed up first with Scripture and policy, then, if that failed, with sharp shot and cold steel. He knew then, as every other Spaniard of his day knew, that the great clash that would spawn the Armada was inevitable.

One evening, after he had casually hinted that any Englishman who strayed into Spanish claimed waters would be sent home in chains, the table erupted in snorts and shouts:
“We’ll see about that, sir!”
“Depends on who says there’s no right to go there!”
“You claimed the Indian seas by might, we’ll claim our right there with might when we choose!”
“You are insolent burghers!” Don Guzman snapped, and stormed out of the house, fuming at his own foolishness for wasting his dignity on common tradesmen.

The next day, Salterne — his head full of the profits waiting to be made from a few leaked trade secrets — sought him out to beg pardon, promising none of the rude townsmen who had spoken out would ever be invited back if the Don would only grace his home again. The Don, to his own disgust, agreed to return, sneering at himself the whole walk there. “Fool that I am, that girl has bewitched me,” he muttered. “I’ll go, and eat my pride for her sake.”

He began dropping hints to the mayor, saying he had taken such a liking to him that he would share secrets he would never reveal to any other Englishman, and that there were easy fortunes to be made for a single shrewd trader who did not meddle with Spain’s general claim to monopoly. Salterne’s greedy weak spot was struck, and the mayor, blind to all but the dream of riches, soon began deliberately sending Rose to draw stories and secrets out of the Spaniard. He was utterly convinced no harm could come of it: Rose was a staunch Protestant, so she would never dream of marrying a Papist Spaniard any more than she would marry a man on the moon, and Don Guzman’s grand Spanish pride would never stoop to wedding a mere merchant’s daughter. He trusted her virtue absolutely, and assumed the Don was far too fond of his own neck to ever dare make untoward advances to the mayor’s only child.

For months after that, Salterne’s parlor was Don Guzman’s favourite haunt, and he saw Rose every day. No man she had ever known was like him: he had all the easy grace of Frank Leigh, but a bolder, more travelled edge. His pride never curdled into the boasting every other visitor complained of from Spaniards; he had more tales of wonders than even Amyas Leigh, and he told them with a soft, passionate eloquence the plain, straightforward Amyas could never muster. Almost all the local young gallants were gone, off fighting in the Netherlands or the Irish wars, so Don Guzman was the only interesting man within thirty miles, and Rose fell slowly, irrevocably, in love.

Don Guzman, for his part, carefully cultivated casual friendships with every young lady of rank within miles, to make Salterne think he had no special preference for Rose, but he still drifted back to the mayor’s house every chance he got, spinning tales of cannibals, headless tribes, active volcano craters, the horrors of the siege of Famagusta where he had escaped flaying alive by the Turks by the narrowest of margins, the Battle of the Three Kings where he had held the dying King of Portugal’s head in his lap. One evening he laid his tragedy out to her, all smooth and deliberately designed to win pity: his sisters were dead to him in a convent, his brother was a Jesuit, his father had been killed by Indians in Mexico, his mother worked as a companion to a Sevillian princess, he had lost his sword, his fortune, his rank, and there was no one left in the world who loved him. Rose cried for him, and listened wide-eyed to his retellings of Italian romances long before they ever reached the English stage.

Before a year was out, Rose loved him far more deeply than he loved her, and neither spoke of the truth: she was too afraid, he too proud of his blood, knowing he would have to sacrifice his family’s noble name to marry a heretic tradesman’s daughter.

One day, taking advantage of the old English custom of greeting ladies with a soft kiss on the cheek, he leaned in to salute her when no one else was in the room, and she drew back.
“No, sir. You must know my cheek is not for you.”
“Every counter-jumper in Bideford seems to help themselves to it,” he snarled, smothering his anger.
“They are my equals,” she said apologetically.
“And I?”
“You are a nobleman, sir. You should remember that.”
“It is a strange taste to prefer the shopkeepers,” he sneered.
“Prefer? It is nothing but a silly formality. They are nothing to me.”
“And am I less than nothing?” he demanded.

Rose blushed bright red, but she stood her ground. “Why should you be anything to me? You have done us far too much honor with these pleasant evenings already. Do not mistake that honor for anything more. If you take one step closer, I will call my father.”
“Do so! I care no more for his anger than you care for my misery,” he cried, and threw himself at her feet. “I adore you! I have forgotten all the foolish differences of rank, forgotten everything but you. My light, my lodestar, my princess! I was Lucifer himself to everyone else, but to you I am a beggar grovelling for mercy — for I am so lonely, so homeless, so lost. Look, the tears are in my eyes! Are there tears in yours too?”

“Go!” Rose shrieked, suddenly breaking free of her trance. “Leave me at once, and never come back!” She darted out of the room and locked herself in the upper chamber.

Don Guzman bowed with perfect, serene grace, and walked out of the house, leaving Rose peeping down at him through the curtains, her heart sinking as she saw him stroll off as if nothing had happened. He never set foot in the mayor’s house again. Rose spent weeks reproaching herself as the cruelest woman alive, convinced she had broken the heart of the lonely nobleman, and hid from the window the moment she saw him walk past, half hoping he would look up, half terrified he would.

Outside the small, tight world of Bideford, greater tides were turning. The fate of all Europe hung on the question of Queen Elizabeth’s proposed marriage to the Duke of Anjou. Sir Humphrey Gilbert was moving heaven and earth, and all of Devonshire most of all, to push through his royal patent to plant new settlements across the Atlantic — a patent that would one day give England Newfoundland, Nova Scotia, Canada, and the whole of the northern American colonies, and Gilbert himself a far better, everlasting crown of glory in the world to come.

“Misguided, rash, intruding fool, farewell! Thou see'st to be too busy is some danger.”
— *Hamlet*

It is the bitter spring of 1583. High gray March skies hang clamped hard and cold over jagged black mountain peaks, and a sharp, dry gale scours miles of desolate bog still streaked with the rust-red and pale yellow stains of leftover winter frost. Only one low, rounded knoll breaks the endless brown waste, where a handful of leafless, wind-battered oaks stretch their moss-draped bare arms like giant furred hunchbacked spiders, hunkered over a desolate pool whose surface the biting breeze nips into a thousand shivering tiny ripples. Over the howl of the wind, a faint, wavering, mournful cry drifts up, thin and fitful.

A squad of English soldiers pushes fast along the bog’s edge, picking their path around crumbling rock outcrops and squelching green spring seeps. They are clad cap-a-pie in dented helmets and thick quilted jerkins, arquebuses slung over their shoulders, pikes trailing in the peat muck. These are stern, seasoned men who two years prior manned the guns at Smerwick Fort, and have cut their teeth on a dozen bloody skirmishes since, with many more still ahead of them. Two captains ride at their head on shaggy, rough-coated Irish ponies: the taller wears armor crusted with old rust, storm salt, and dried mud, while his companion cuts a far brighter figure, inlaid cuirass glinting, a gaudy silk sash slung across his chest, a curling plume nodding in his polished helmet, and a gold sword hilt that glints even through the thin bog mist. He is Walter Raleigh, and his finery makes a droll, vivid contrast against the scruffy little garron pony carrying him. Tied at their heels to a pikeman’s wrist with a length of rough hemp cord trots a bare-legged Irish kerne guide, clad only in a ragged saffron-yellow mantle and the long matted “glib” of uncut hair that all but hides his face, his eyes darting left and right from under the tangles in equal parts fear and sullen resentment. He is leading the patrol on their hunt for the rebel Lord Baltinglas, and he knows full well he will be run through on the spot if he leads them astray.
