“A pleasant country, truly, Captain Raleigh,” the mud-stained officer — Amyas Leigh — calls out to his gaudy companion. “I wonder how, having once escaped it for the comforts of Whitehall, you have the courage to come back and ruin that fine new suit of yours with bog water and mud.”
“A very pleasant country, my friend Amyas,” Raleigh laughs back. “What you say in jest, I say in earnest.”
“Now that’s a turnabout. I’m sick of the place already, just as you once warned me I would be. Would to Heaven I could hear word of some new adventure *Westward Ho!* and get these big bones of mine swinging in a ship’s hammock once more. What on earth has made you so fond of bog and rock that you’d volunteer to tramp through them with us? I thought you’d long ago spied out how bare and unyielding this land is.”
“Bare? Look at that soil, rich and fat as any in Devon, if only there were men to till it. Those rocks yonder? Who knows what minerals they hold? I’ve heard tell of gold and gemstones found in half a dozen parishes, and my brother Humphrey’s German assayer swears these formations are exactly the same as the silver-bearing ones that make Peru so wealthy. Tut, man! If her gracious Majesty would but grant me a few square miles of this wilderness, I’d make it blossom like the rose in seven years, with God’s good help.”
“Humph. If that’s true, I’d be half inclined to stay on myself.”
“Then you shall! Be my agent, once the land is mine. You can collect my mine rents, my corn tithes, my fishing dues, eh? Think you could keep accounts, old bear’s-paw knight?”
“Easy enough for the profit side of your ledgers, at least. No, no — I’d sooner cart lime all my days from Cauldy back to Bideford than spend another twelvemonth in this land of ire, among these children of wrath. There’s a curse hanging over every inch of the ground here, that’s certain.”
“There is no curse here save the old one of man’s sin: ‘Thorns and thistles it shall bring forth to thee,’” Raleigh counters. “But root up those thorns and thistles, Amyas, and no fiend alive can stop you sowing wheat in their place. Till the ground like a man, and you plough right through all the schoolmen’s silly fables about a native curse, too.” It was that same bold, unflinching line of talk that would later earn him the false accusation of atheism, a charge no good Christian ever less deserved.
“Sword and bullet are what’s needed first, before plough and harrow,” Amyas grumbles. “Not till more of these rebel Irish lords are gone the way the Desmonds went will there be any lasting peace here.”
“Not so far wrong, I fear,” Raleigh says, his tone softening. “And yet — these very traitors carry better English Norman blood than the men hunting them down. When our comrade Yeo here slew the Earl of Desmond the other day, he shed no more native Irish blood than if he’d run through the Lord Deputy himself.”
“His blood be on his own head,” rumbles Yeo, stepping forward. “He looked as wild a savage as the worst of them, more shame to his noble name. Our ancient sergeant here almost hacked his arm clean off before he told us who he was, and what with the price on his head and him bleeding to death anyway —”
“Enough, good man,” Raleigh cuts him off gently. “You did what was given you to do. Strange, Amyas, is it not? Noble Normans sunk into utter savagery — *Hibernis ipsis hiberniores*, more Irish than the Irish themselves! Is there some uncivilizing venom in the air over these bogs?”
“Venom enough to turn even loyal Englishmen to traitors. But the common Irish folk are good enough, if their tyrant lords would only leave them be. Look at Inchiquin, everyone says he’s the rightful Prince of Themond, and would be king of all Ireland if every man got what he was owed. No liegeman alive is truer to the queen than he is.”
“Don’t talk of rights in a land built entirely on wrongs,” Raleigh says. “But even these rebel lords — are they any worse than you or I might be, if we were raised as masters over the bodies and souls of a remote land where no law or order ever reached? Look at Desmond: raised a savage among savages, a Papist among Papists, a despot among slaves, with a hundred wild maidens counting it honor to serve his pleasure, a hundred ruffians counting it piety to carry out his revenge. Let him who is without sin cast the first stone.”

The conversation lulls for a while as they ride, and Raleigh sinks into quiet thought. What would he have become, if he had been born Desmond, the lord of all those vast estates he now hoped to claim? Would his own children sink into that same savage, tyrannical state? Could he be different? Could he make the desert bloom, build peaceful plantations, serve his queen well, far away from the petty backstabbing of the court? Or was his grand dream just another mask for the same old, hungry ambition that had ruined Desmond, Essex, John of Austria, and a hundred other great men before him?
“Leigh — what was that noise?” he says suddenly, breaking the silence.
“An Irish keening cry, I thought,” Amyas replies. “It came from out over the bog. Might just be a plover’s call, though.”
“Something’s not right there, sir,” the old ancient sergeant puts in, his voice tight with unease. “There’s ugly stories around here about pucks and banshees, all sorts of ghosts. There it is again, wailing just like a woman. Folks say the banshee cried all night long before Desmond was slain.”
“Then maybe this one’s crying for Baltinglas, since his turn will be next soon enough — not that I believe a word of those old wives’ tales,” Raleigh says. He turns to their guide. “Shamus, do you hear that cry out in the bog?”
The guide puts on the blankest, most stupid face he can muster, and answers in broken English: “Shamus hear naught. Perhaps — otter, fishing in ta pool.”
“An otter, he means, and I think he’s lying,” Amyas says. “Shamus, you’ll end up sharing Desmond’s fate if you lie to us. Ancient, take a few men and go see what that noise is. There might be a poor soul set upon by robbers, or starving to death — I’ve seen dozens of them out here before.”
“Aye, poor wretches, and no fault of their own or ours,” the sergeant says. “But when their lords take to quarrelling, and steal each other’s cattle, and burn each other’s fields —”
“I know,” Amyas cuts him off impatiently. “Why don’t you take the men and go, then?”
“Begging your pardon, noble captain, but — I fear nothing born of woman,” the sergeant stammers.
“Well, what of that?” Amyas grins.
“But these pucks, sir. The wild Irish say they haunt the pools. They don’t harm you when you walk up to them, sir — but the second your back is turned, they leap on your back like apes, sir! Who can fight that kind of fiend?”
“Then by your own account, ancient,” Raleigh teases, “you can go see it all safely enough, and if the puck jumps on your back when you return, just drag him straight to me. I’ll pay you a noble for him, and you can lock him in a cage and make a fortune showing him off to the crowds in London as a new monster.”
“Good Heavens forefend, Captain Raleigh! You talk far too rashly!” the sergeant squawks. “But if I must —
*Where duty calls,
To brazen walls,
How base the slave who flinches*
Lads! Who’ll follow me?”
“You’re begging for volunteers like you’re leading a forlorn hope,” Amyas laughs, dismounting. “Don’t waste your usquebaugh swallowing Dutch courage when your English nerve has run dry. I’ll go myself.”
“And I’m coming with you,” Raleigh says, already off his pony. “As the queen’s true knight-errant, I’ll not be left behind on any adventure. Who knows but we might stumble on a wicked magician about to behead some saffron-mantled Irish princess?”
“Oh sirs, sirs, to risk your precious lives —” the sergeant blubbers.
“Pooh,” Raleigh waves him off. “I wear an amulet, and I’ve a spell of magic at my tongue’s end, so no ghost can see me, nor I them. Come with us, Yeo, the Desmond-slayer. We’ll shame the devil, or be shamed by him.”
“He may shame me, sir, but he’ll never frighten me,” Yeo replies, drawing his sword. “But what of the bog, captains?”
“Tut! We’re Devonshire heath-trotters born. We know how to pick our way across a peat moor!”

The three splash and scramble for a quarter of a mile across the bog, the cry growing louder and clearer the closer they get to the knoll.
“That’s no ghost and no otter, sirs,” Yeo says. “It’s a real Irish keening, just like Captain Leigh said. And I’d bet a month’s pay that sly Shamus knew exactly what it was the whole time.”
By now they can hear the wild, wavering “Ochone, Ochonorie” of a grieving woman. They scramble over the knoll’s boulders and stumble straight onto the scene. A young, unkempt Irish girl sits on a stone, clad in nothing but her ample saffron mantle, tearing at her dishevelled black hair, throwing her head back every few seconds to let loose that long, mournful wail — as Yeo puts it, “like a dumb four-footed hound, not a Christian soul.” On her knees rests the head of a middle-aged man clad in the long black soutane of a Roman Catholic priest, his limbs already gone rigid in the posture of death.

All three men pause, awed. Raleigh, always sensitive to wild, poetic scenes, feels the full weight of the desolation: the bleak bitter sky, the shapeless bog, the stunted oaks, the savage girl alone with the corpse in utter, endless waste. She bends her head over the dead man’s face, calling wildly to someone who cannot hear her, unmindful of the intruders at her back, and the men feel a quiet sacred horror, almost enough to make them turn away and leave her undisturbed. But Yeo, sturdier of nerve, speaks up.
“Shall I search the fellow, captain?” he asks.
“Better do it, I think,” Amyas replies.
Raleigh steps gently over to the girl and speaks to her in English. She stares up at his glinting armor and his plume with wide, wondering eyes, shakes her head, and sinks back into her lamentation. Raleigh lays a soft hand on her arm and lifts her gently to her feet, while Amyas and Yeo bend over the corpse.
The body is that of a large, coarse-featured man, wasted and shrunken down to a skeleton by starvation, his hands and feet cramped, his spine bowed as if he died from cold and hunger long before his time. Yeo pulls back the soutane from his thin, gaunt chest, and the girl screams and weeps but makes no move to stop him.
“Ask her who he is, Yeo. You speak a little Irish,” Amyas says.
Yeo calls out to her in the rough local tongue, but the girl stays silent. “The stubborn jade won’t talk, of course, sir. If she was a man I’d make her speak fast enough.”
“Ask her who killed him.”
“She says no one killed him — and I think she’s telling the truth. There’s not a single wound on his body. The man starved to death, sirs, as I am a sinful man. God help him, even though he is a priest. Wait — what’s this? A big leather pouch at his waist, stuffed full of something.”
“Hand it over,” Raleigh says. They tip the pouch open onto the mud: piles of papers, no scrap of food, and a large sealed parchment.
“Latin,” Amyas says. “You’ll have to read it, scholar.”
“Is it possible?” Raleigh breathes, after scanning the lines for a moment. “This is a prize indeed. This is Saunders himself!”
Yeo leaps back from the body as if he has touched a viper. “Nick Saunders, the Pope’s legate, sir?”
“Nicholas Saunders, the man who brought all the Pope’s bulls and the Spanish promises to stir up this entire rebellion.”
“The villain! Why didn’t he wait for me to kill him myself?” Yeo snarls, kicking the corpse lightly in disgust.
“Quiet! Mind the poor girl,” Amyas says, as she shrieks at the indignity. Raleigh half talks to himself as he reads on:
“Yes, this is Saunders. Misguided fool, and this is the end! All your plotting, your conspiring, your lying and boasting, your consecrated banners and papal bulls, your Agnus Deis and holy water, the blessings of all saints and angels, your devotion to the Immaculate Conception! You called on Heaven to judge between you and us, and here is Heaven’s answer.” He flicks through the rest of the documents: wild, overblown pastoral letters denouncing Queen Elizabeth as an “illegitimate adulteress”, frantic promises of gold and soldiers from the Pope and the King of Spain, filthy slanders against every great loyal English statesman and soldier of the age, bombastic threats of endless hellfire for anyone who opposed the “true faith”, fulsome praise of every traitor hanged over the past twenty years, tedious rants about the bull *In Caena Domini*, Elizabeth’s excommunication, the sacred “duty” to rebel and kill heretic princes. Raleigh shoves all the foul, fanatical papers back into the pouch with a gesture of disgust. They leave the girl alone with her dead, rejoin their patrol, and remount to ride on towards the Desmonds’ former lands.

An hour later, a different Englishman stands beside the wailing girl, a dozen shag-haired Irish kernes with skenes at their thighs and javelins in their hands gathered around him, adding their own loud lamentations to hers. This is Eustace Leigh: still a layman, but still deep in the old conspiracies he has worked at for two full years, tramping from one end of Ireland to the other to try and satisfy his conscience for refusing the celibate “higher calling” of the priesthood. His brow is lined now, his features sharp and harshened, the old scar on his face hidden by a thick bushy beard from everyone but himself — though he never forgets the day Amyas Leigh gave it to him. He had been wandering the moors with Desmond for months, dodging English patrols, and was on his way to find Lord Baltinglas to bring him word of Desmond’s death, leading a small band of rebel clansmen too fierce or too deeply stained with crime to beg the queen’s for mercy like their countrymen.
Eustace stares down at the dead legate. This was the man he had dreamed would regenerate Ireland, sweep the English heretics out of the “Isle of Saints”, place the whole island back under the gentle yoke of the universal Catholic Church. All of it — all the grand promises of Spanish fleets, of a million Irish Catholics rising as one even to the babies at the breast, all the loyal noble lords pledged to die for the faith — ended here, starved to death alone in a bog hole. Eustace twists the bitter failure into fanatical glory in his own mind: this is no defeat

> Three lords sat drinking late yestreen,
> And ere they paid the lawing,
> They set a combat them between,
> To fight it in the dawing
> — Old Scotch Ballad

No one who has ever wandered the lanes of Bideford can miss its famous stone bridge: it is the beating heart, the very anchor of the town, just as Edinburgh is defined by its castle, Rome by its Capitol, and Egypt by its pyramids. Bideford is not truly Bideford without that long, weathered span arching across the Torridge. But few outsiders know the strange, old magic that has moved and guarded the bridge for five hundred years. According to local legends recorded by Prince and Fuller, it is the greatest wonder of all Devon: first a bridge that won souls, fed the poor, paid for schoolmasters, seemed almost alive, and last — far from least — threw legendary annual feasts.

Few know that when builders first tried to lay the bridge foundations half a mile upstream, invisible hands carried every stone down the river each night, until the parish parson, Sir Richard Gurney, went to bed distraught certain a devil was haunting his sheepfold, and saw an angel in a vision. The angel told him to build the bridge exactly where the stones had landed: that was the only spot in miles with solid ground beneath the wide, shifting sands of the ford. Few know that Bishop Grandison of Exeter issued proclamations across his whole diocese promising indulgences, blessings, and a share in all eternal spiritual rewards to anyone who gave money to build the crossing, so travellers might save their souls and their bodies at once, and make the best of both worlds.

Even the local folk can’t all tell you that though the bridge’s foundations are said to rest on bales of wool, it shakes at the lightest step of a trotting horse; or that a man named William Alford, a modern Milo, once wagered he could carry four bushels of salt all the way across its twenty-three arches, and won. It is a legal esquire, no less, with its own official coat of arms: a ship and a bridge on a plain silver field, and it owns lands and cottages across half a dozen local parishes. Over centuries that miraculous bridge founded charities, built schoolrooms, fought lawsuits in the courts, and saved for its famous yearly dinners the best stocked wine cellar in all of Devon.

It was to one of those 1583 feasts that every notable citizen of Bideford was invited, plus Mr. St. Leger of nearby Annery House — brother to the Marshal of Munster and to Lady Grenville, a famously generous, hospitable gentleman who gave away so much of his wealth to neighbours during his life that he spared his children all risk of the corrupting temptations that come with excess riches.

Halfway through the dinner, a heated argument broke out between St. Leger and one of the bridge trustees: was a salmon caught below the bridge in the river better or worse than one caught upstream? The only way to settle such a weighty question was with a practical test, St. Leger declared. He vowed that since the bridge had treated him to an excellent dinner, he would return the favour. He bet five pounds that he could pull a salmon out of the pool below Annery that was just as firm, just as flaky, as the Appledore salmon they had just finished eating — and on the strength of that joyful impulse, he invited every single guest at the table to come dine at Annery House three days later, each bringing a wife or daughter along. Don Guzman, the Spanish nobleman staying in town, got an invitation too.

The feast in Annery’s great hall was one the house had not seen since old Judge Hankford hosted King Edward IV there generations before. Plates were piled high, wine flowed free, and all the while, Rose Salterne and Don Guzman pretended not to see each other across the tables, even as they watched each other more closely than they had watched anyone else all night. Rose had good reason to be careful with her glances: her father sat right beside her, and directly across from her were William Cary and Arthur St. Leger, two lieutenants in the Queen’s Irish army who had only just come home on furlough a few days prior.

Rose and the Spaniard had not exchanged a single word in six months, though they had crossed paths dozens of times. The Don never avoided her in public, save for stepping clear of her father’s house: he only followed the unspoken rule she had set when she spurned him, greeting her with the most formal, distant courtesy when he arrived or departed, and acting as if he did not notice her at all the rest of the time. But he took every chance to shine in her presence: wittier, more eloquent, full of wilder, more romantic tales of far-off lands than he had ever been before. His first tactic of open wooing had failed, so he was trying a far more dangerous one. He would punish her first for daring to refuse a Spanish nobleman, and what better punishment than to withdraw the honour of his attentions entirely? It was both conceited and clever: the Don knew better than most that people, women especially, will value a man exactly as highly as he values himself, and that the more dignity he claimed for his own worth, the more eager others would be to grant it.

He would not make the crude, obvious mistake of flirting with another woman to make her jealous — that would only spark rage, or disgust, or hopelessness, and never make her turn to him. Instead, he would quietly, unobtrusively prove he could live perfectly well without her. Rose, spoiled as she was by months of admirers fawning at her feet, found herself soon wondering exactly what made him so self-sufficient, when she could no longer pretend she was unaffected by him. Did he harbour some great secret ambition? What a thrill it would be to share it. Did he hold some hidden, rare knowledge? What a joy to learn it. Did he love deeper than any ordinary English gentleman she knew? It would be heaven to be the only one to claim that love. Step by step, she found herself drifting toward the man she had once turned away, ready to beg his favour as he had once begged hers. That temptation, to hold a private, secret treasure no other woman in the world could access, to be the only initiate of his hidden life, was fast wearing down all her resolve. The Spaniard knew well that women by nature far more often crave to worship than to be worshipped, to surrender than to rule — that all the coy, sharp scorn they hide behind is just a mask they soon tire of wearing themselves.

That day at the Annery feast, Rose was utterly tired of her mask of cold indifference. Don Guzman saw it in her downcast, restless face, and thinking he had punished her long enough, began stealing soft glances her way across the table. He did not look away when their eyes met and she dropped her gaze, cheeks flushing bright, and he noticed her growing quieter, more distracted by the minute. At last, he caught her peeking up at him to see if he was looking at her, and he knew his prize was his. He asked her, only with his eyes, *Do you forgive me?* She froze mid-conversation with the lady next to her, stammered, dropped her gaze, then lifted it again a minute later to meet his, answering as clear as day with every line of her soft face and every tilt of her neck, *And do you forgive me too?*

At once Don Guzman burst into giddy, sparkling good humour, churning out tales, jests, and sharp repartee until he was the undisputed soul of the whole party, the most charming gentleman in the room. Rose knew she was the cause of his sudden change of mood, and she berated herself for her weakness, even as her chest flooded with a giddy, shameful delight. She longed for the feast to end so she could hurry home, lock herself away, and indulge in all the soft, impossible daydreams she dared not face as reality.

That midsummer afternoon, the great stone terrace of Annery was a perfect sight: fine ladies in their bright, embroidered gowns strolled up and down in pairs and trios before the grand old house, or leaned over the balustrade to look out at the park below — the ancient gnarled oaks, the herds of grazing deer, and the wide, still, landlocked stretch of the Torridge spreading like a lake far beneath them, glinting gold in the hot sun. They chattered and laughed, compared dress trims, gossiped about neighbours’ husbands and maidservants, while the two ladies of the house — Mrs. St. Leger the hostess, and her sister-in-law, the fair Lady Grenville — presided graciously over the group. Only Rose kept apart, hovering at the edge of the group, half wanting to laugh and half wanting to cry, she could not tell which.

“Our pretty Rose looks sad,” Lady Grenville said, stepping up beside her. “Cheer up, child! We’re all waiting for you to come play for us.”

Rose mumbled some reply she barely heard, and followed her mechanically. She picked up a lute sitting on a stone bench beneath the house, and the rest of the ladies gathered around her.

“What should I sing?”
“Give us your favourite old ballad, *Earl Haldan’s Daughter*.”

Rose shrank back. It was a loud, rousing, swashbuckling song that did not fit her mood at all — and Frank had praised it, once, in the happy, careless days long past. The song felt suddenly like a bad omen, but she dared not refuse for fear of drawing unwanted attention, so she began to sing, forced:
It was Earl Haldan's daughter,
She look'd across the sea;
She look'd across the water,
And long and loud laugh'd she;
'The locks of six princesses
Must be my marriage-fee,
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Who comes a wooing me?'

It was Earl Haldan's daughter,
She walk'd along the sand;
When she was aware of a knight so fair,
Come sailing to the land.
His sails were all of velvet,
His mast of beaten gold,
And 'hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat,
Who saileth here so bold?'

'The locks of five princesses
I won beyond the sea;
I shore their golden tresses,
To fringe a cloak for thee.
One handful yet is wanting,
But one of all the tale;
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Furl up thy velvet sail!'

'He leapt into the water,
That rover young and bold;
He gript Earl Haldan's daughter,
He shore her locks of gold;
'Go weep, go weep, proud maiden,
The tale is full to-day.
Now hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Sail Westward-ho, and away!'

As the last note faded, a measured voice with a soft foreign accent thrilled through her.
“In the East, they say the nightingale sings to the rose; Devon is luckier still, for it has the nightingale and the rose wrapped up in one lady.”

“We have no nightingales in Devon, Don Guzman,” Lady Grenville laughed. “But our little forest thrushes sing as sweetly as you hear, enough to delight any ear. What brings you away from the gentlemen inside so early?”
“These letters, that were just handed to me,” he said. “They call me home to Spain, and I could not bear to waste a single minute of the lovely company I must soon leave behind.”

“To Spain?” half a dozen voices exclaimed at once — the Don was a favourite with every soul in Bideford.
“Yes, and from there on to the Indies. My ransom has finally arrived, and with it a new post. I have been named Governor of La Guayra, in Caracas. Congratulate me on my promotion.”

A mist clouded over Rose’s eyes. The Spaniard’s voice sounded sharp, flippant. Did he truly care for her at all? Even if he did, was it all utterly hopeless? Her cheeks burned bright enough, she was sure, that everyone could see her distress. Desperate to draw attention away from herself, she blurted out the first foolish question that popped into her head.
“Where is La Guayra?”

“Halfway round the world, on the coast of the Spanish Main. It is the loveliest spot on earth, and the governor’s house stands in a palm forest at the foot of a mountain eight thousand feet high. I would only need a wife there to live in paradise.”

“I have no doubt you will persuade some fair Seville lady to come with you,” Lady Grenville said.
“Thank you, gracious madam. But the truth is, since I have had the joy of knowing English ladies, I have come to think they are the only women on earth worth wooing.”
“What a kind compliment! But I fear none of our free English maidens would ever submit to being chaperoned everywhere by a duenna. Eh, Rose? How would you like to be locked up all day by an ugly old woman with a horn on her forehead?”

Rose blushed so scarlet that Lady Grenville saw her secret in an instant, and would have turned the conversation away — but before she could speak, a merchant’s wife blundered in making a commonplace joke about how jealous Spanish husbands were, and another tittered out a coarse, ill-judged remark about the West Indies planters and their fair slaves.

“Ladies,” Don Guzman said, flushing with indignation, “believe me, all of that is nothing but ignorant slander. If we Spaniards are more jealous than other peoples, it is only because we love far more passionately. If some of our men abroad act recklessly, it is only because they have no wives at their sides — women who keep them pure, as the amethyst stone keeps the man who wears it sober and true. I could tell you tales of Spanish husbands in the Indies as loyal and constant as any story a romance writer ever dreamed of.”

“Tell us one then!” the ladies cried at once.
“I fear it would be far too long, madam.”
“The longer the better, senor. What better way to spend an hour, while the gentlemen inside finish their wine?”

Storytelling was a beloved pastime in those days, when books were far rarer than they are now, and everyone knew the Don was a master spinner of tales. All the ladies crowded around him, servants dragged out chairs and benches, and Don Guzman sat himself down at Lady Grenville’s feet, and began to speak, with quiet, proud humility.

“Fair, noble ladies, you have no doubt heard of Sebastian Cabot, who sailed forth forty-five years ago with a commission from my old master, the Emperor Charles the Fifth, to find the legendary golden lands of Tarshish, Ophir, and Cipango. When his ships ran short of provisions, he turned back at the mouth of the great South American river he named Rio de la Plata, sailed upstream a ways, and discovered the fair land of Paraguay. But few of you know that at the mouth of the Rio Terceiro, he built a fort that men still call Cabot’s Tower — and no one tells the strange, tragic tale that has made that tower a sacred spot for every true lover in Spain.

“When Cabot sailed home to Spain the next year, he left a garrison of one hundred and twenty men under the command of Nuno de Lara, Ruiz Moschera, and Sebastian da Hurtado — old friends and fellow soldiers of my invincible grandfather Don Ferdinando da Soto. With them was a treasure more precious than any gold Spain had ever owned: Lucia Miranda, Hurtado’s wife, famous across the Emperor’s court for her wisdom, modesty, and unrivalled beauty. She gave up all the pomp and luxury of palace life to marry a poor adventurer, and sailed across the world with him to face every hardship of the new lands. Mangora, the cacique of the local Timbuez Indians, who had struck up a false friendship with de Lara, saw her once, and lusted after her the moment he laid eyes on her. He plotted with all the savage cunning he had to seize her by force, for he knew he would never win her consent.

“Miranda saw his passion quickly enough, told her husband, and kept far out of his sight. Mangora kept pressing Hurtado to come visit his village, and bring his wife along with him. Hurtado, suspecting a trap but afraid to offend the cacique, politely excused himself on the grounds of his soldier’s duties. Mangora, mad with frustrated desire, began plotting to kill Hurtado.

“Weeks went by, until food ran scarce at the tower, and Hurtado and Ruiz Moschera took fifty soldiers with them up the river on a foraging mission. Mangora saw his chance, and struck at once.

“Ladies, I have spoken to men who saw Cabot’s Tower with their own eyes: it stands on a knoll where the two rivers meet, and a wide, dreary marsh full of tall grass and thick bushes stretches inland from its walls. The perfect hiding spot for four thousand Indian warriors. Mangora left his whole army hidden in that marsh, and walked boldly up to the tower with thirty of his own men, loaded down with sacks of grain, fruit, game, and every delicacy his forests could provide.

“Smiling all the while, he told de Lara how sorry he was that the Spaniards were going hungry, and begged him to accept the food he had brought. As he expected, de Lara invited him inside to share a cask of Spanish wine.

“Mangora and his thirty bandits walked right into the tower, and the feast of songs and drinking lasted far into the night. Mangora looked around for Miranda again and again, and finally had the gall to demand she be brought out — but she had locked herself in her chamber, claiming she was ill.

“It was a useless plea. No sooner had the drunken, unwary Spaniards fallen asleep, leaving Mangora and his men still inside the walls, than the garrison woke to the screams of burning thatch, the explosion of their gunpowder magazine, and the roar of four thousand warriors pouring in from the marsh.

“I will not sicken your gentle ears with the gory details of the slaughter. In a few terrible minutes, every unarmed, bewildered Spaniard was dead. The only survivors were Miranda, and four other women with their small infants, all bound and led away in triumph through the forest toward the Indian village.

“Stunned by the sudden massacre, and even more terrified of the fate she knew awaited her, Miranda walked all night through

“The spirits of your fathers Shall start from every wave; For the deck it was their field of fame, And ocean was their grave.”

The low oak-panelled parlour in old Plymouth glowed soft by firelight, its shelves crowded with Spanish silver plunder, crudely drawn coast charts, strange foreign bird skins, and a portrait of old Captain Will Hawkins, favoured of Henry VIII, hung above the hearth. Tacked above that portrait was the tattered Spanish ensign John Hawkins had torn from the defences of Rio de la Hacha fifteen years prior, when he and two hundred men had stormed the town against a garrison of a thousand and watered his ships right from the enemy’s wells. The two figures sat by that crackling fire: Adrian Gilbert, tall, broad of studious brow, his eyes weakened from hours bent over crucibles and furnace mouths, and Mrs. Hawkins, once a famously comely Plymouth lass, now worn thin by decades of fretting for her sea captain husband at sea.

“So you see, my dear Mrs. Hawkins,” Adrian was saying, tapping a sample of rough Cornish ore laid out on the table between them, “you can see the silver glint plain, not to mention the lead, manganese, copper, and above all this gossan the locals prize—what I suspect is no mere ore matrix, but the very crude first matter of all metals. Follow me? If the alchemical recipes Doctor Dee gave me succeed even half as well as I expect, I will refine out the silver first, then turn every scrap of the remaining ore to pure gold. At that rate, Peru and Mexico’s hoards become irrelevant, and England sits mistress of all the globe. Strange? No doubt. Distant? Surely. But possible, my dear madam, entirely possible.”

“And what good would that do you, Mr. Gilbert?” Mrs. Hawkins sighed, her fingers twisting in her linen apron. “If you could find a philosopher’s stone to turn sinners into saints, now—only God’s grace can do that, and it often drags its feet coming, doesn’t it?”

“Ah, but think, madam!” Adrian leaned forward, earnest. “That Comb Martin mine could turn out to be an inexhaustible lode of gold, enough to fund my Northwest Passage patent in full. My brother Humphrey already holds Newfoundland, and the endless pine forests there will build us fresh China-bound ships year after year, no end to them.”

“Sir Humphrey has far kinder things in his heart than gold, Mr. Adrian,” she said, shaking her head. “He has walked close and gracious with the Lord these seven years past. I wish my own Captain John was half so steady.”

“And what makes you assume I care for nothing but gold?” Adrian protested. “Is gold not the very strength of the Spaniard, the mainspring of all that Antichrist stands for? It is only by outstripping their hoards that we can outfight them. You shake your head, but tell me: England must have gold, will it not? Is it better to refine it bloodlessly at home in our own mines, or to spill the blood of honest English seamen and innocent foreign folk to steal it across the ocean?”

“God’s blessing never rests on greed, Mr. Gilbert,” she said softly, pointing upward to a carved boss on the ribbed oak ceiling above them, where the family herald had emblazoned John Hawkins’s new crest: a bound Demi-Moor, proper in every detail. “Ever since he first sailed to Guinea to hunt those poor negroes, my heart has never known a single day of real light. The very day we hung that crest in our new house, the parson read that verse in the first lesson: ‘Woe to him that buildeth his house by iniquity, and his chambers by wrong. Shalt thou live because thou closest thyself in cedar?’ It went into my ears like fire, and settled in my heart like lead. All those stolen souls weigh on me from dawn till dusk. We are grand now, money flows in fast, but the Lord will require their blood at our hands yet, I know He will.”

Adrian tried to soothe her, reminding her that John had already paid for those misdeeds when he lost half his fleet and nearly his own life at San Juan de Ulúa, but Mrs. Hawkins would not be comforted. The queen had been sharp with him over the fate of the enslaved people then, she said, but it had not mended him. He grew greedier by the day, niggardly with his coin, endlessly plotting new voyages for profit, jealous of Drake and every other man who had struck richer fortune on the high seas. Plymouth, once a quiet, God-fearing little port, had turned rotten with hunger for gold ever since the men had found their way to the Indies: every sailor’s wife nagged her husband to sail off to bring back coin for farthingales and crisping pins, and the whole place was fast forgetting how to walk in peace.

The two sat quiet for a long moment, Adrian half ashamed of his own grand alchemical ambitions, when the parlour door swung open hard. In stepped Amyas Leigh, wrapped in a salt-crusted rough sea gown, his boots caked in harbour mud.

Adrian shot to his feet, pale as ash. “Amyas! What brings you here? Where is the Golden Hind? Is Humphrey well?”

“Your brother is in perfect health, sir,” Amyas said, his voice heavy. “The Golden Hind sailed on to Dartmouth with Master Hayes. I landed here to make my way north to Bideford before heading to London. I stopped at Drake’s house first, but he was away.”

“Golden Hind home already? The expedition went wrong, didn’t it?” Adrian cried, rushing to hold a candle up to Amyas’s haggard, wind-scoured face. “Speak! Don’t drive me mad waiting.”

Amyas laid a heavy hand on Adrian’s shoulder, his lip trembling. “Your great, glorious brother, sir, is far better bestowed than settling new lands in Newfoundland. He is with the God he spent his whole life serving.”

Adrian shrieked, staggering back against the table. “Dead? But he was always as close to the Lord as Enoch! Don’t parable me, man, tell me the whole of it.”

And slowly, Amyas laid out the whole tragic tale that Master Hayes would later record for all Devon to read: the mutinous, ragtag crew of conscripted ruffians, daily conspiracies to steal the ships and desert, men deserting in droves to plunder local fishing vessels, until Sir Humphrey, too kind to punish the treachery, let all the faint-hearted sail for home. They had set out from St John’s to map the southern coast, and Gilbert, stung by taunts that he was afraid of the sea, had insisted on sailing the tiny 10-ton *Squirrel*, crammed full of nettings, small arms, and ordnance, rather than the larger, safer Golden Hind. Seven days out from Cape Raz, their largest ship the *Delight* struck uncharted shoals at dusk: the crew had played trumpets and drums and hautboys all through their last night, and sang their own death knells before the waves swallowed all but fourteen of their number. The two remaining ships limped on, worn thin with cold and famine, before the crews begged Gilbert to turn for home for England. Just then, a great lion, as large as any on land, slid across the surface of the waves right toward them, roaring, before vanishing back into the deep. Gilbert had laughed it off as a good omen, a sign they were fighting the devil himself.

Amyas saved the last, worst part for last, his voice cracking: off the Azores, the storms rose to fury, seas breaking short and pyramid-high. On the 9th of September, Sir Humphrey sat out on the stern of the tiny *Squirrel* with a book in his hand, and called out to the men on the Golden Hind every time they drifted close enough to hear: “We are as near heaven by sea as by land.” An hour later, the look-out cried out that the *Squirrel*’s lights had vanished. The little frigate, the great knight on her deck, had been swallowed whole by the dark Atlantic in an instant.

Adrian moaned, crumpling into a chair. “My brother! The glory of our house, the glory of Devon!”

Mrs. Hawkins wept, crossing herself. Amyas told him Sir Humphrey had worn the queen’s jewel round his neck to the last, his personal motto *Mutare vel timere sperno*—I scorn to change or to fear—lived out to his very last breath. He had grown far meeker in his final months, Amyas said, saying that a refiner far better than any alchemist had followed him across all the seas, and purified him seven times over in the fire, before calling him home.

The talk lulled. Adrian had lost his entire fortune to the failed expedition, Amyas had nothing left of his own voyage save the rags on his back. Amyas apologised, asking if Mrs. Hawkins could lend him five pounds and a spare shirt of her son Richard’s to get himself and his few surviving men north to Bideford.

“Five? Fifty pounds, Mr. Leigh!” she cried, bustling to the iron strongbox by the wall. “John Hawkins’s wife will never turn a penny away from a distressed mariner, especially one of gentle Devon birth. Sit down, eat a hot supper first—you look half starved.”

As he ate, she chattered of local news, of Adrian seeing his mother in London a few months prior, before she dropped the piece of news that hit Amyas like a musket ball to the chest: the Spanish prisoner he had sent home from Smerwick had escaped Bideford, and taken no less a girl than Rose Salterne, the Rose of Torridge, the mayor’s only daughter, off with him.

Amyas fell back in his chair as if he had been shot. He waved off Mrs. Hawkins’s cries of alarm, claiming the heavy Plymouth ale had turned his weak, sea-sick head dizzy. She pressed a mug of spiced aqua vitae on him, fussed over him, and saw him to bed. But Amyas did not sleep a wink all that long night, tossing and turning as every thought of Rose ran through his head. By dawn, he had already secured a horse and coin from Mrs. Hawkins, and set off north along the Bideford road, his face set hard for the west.

“Ignorance and evil, even in full flight, deal terrible backhanded strokes at their pursuers.”--HELPS.

It is no credit to the England of Elizabeth’s day, I must admit, that the overland road from Plymouth to the north of Devon was no safe journey for any traveller. Unless you were willing to waste days looping far out of your way, you had no choice but to cross the territory of a foreign, hostile potentate: a man who had repeatedly raided royal lands, and defeated detachments of the Queen’s own soldiers, known behind his back by the mocking title the King of the Gubbings. As the old historian Fuller once wrote, “I dare name them now, secure in my distance, when no braver man would dare breathe the word to their face for fear of drawing their fury down on his head. No man I have met yet can give a good account of where their name came from. We call the worthless little shavings left from cleaning fish gubbings, and the folk who bear the name well know it carries a taint of shame.
My learned friend Joseph Maynard suggests it comes from *inhabitare montes gibberosos*, “the dwellers on hunchbacked hills”, but most men will smile at that clever etymology and doubt its truth. I have read of an England that lies beyond Wales, but the land of the Gubbings is a little Scythia right inside Devon, and they are pure heathens in its bounds. It sits on Dartmoor’s edge, near Brent. Tradition says some two hundred years ago, two pregnant fugitive women fled there to hide, and a crowd of idle, lewd men followed them: that was the start of their line. They have made their own little world, answerable to no bishop, no archdeacon, no civil law of any kind. They live in hovels more like holes in the ground than houses, as swine do, sharing all goods in common, multiplying without the formality of marriage until they number in their hundreds. Their speech is the dregs of the dregs of Devonian dialect, and the more learned a man is, the less he will understand a word of it. During the civil wars, no commander dared quarter soldiers on them, for fear his men would end up quartered among them as captives. Their wealth is made of other men’s goods: they live by stealing moorland sheep, no constable has the power to search their dens, and no sheriff thinks the task worth the trouble. They are faster than most horses, longer-lived than most men, untouched by the soft luxury that shortens civilized lives. They hold together like a hive of bees: offend one, and all will turn out to avenge the wrong.” Fuller closes his account with a dry quip about the dissenting Anabaptists of his day, glad to note the Gubbings have lately begun to send their children for baptism and rejoin the rest of Christian humanity.

It was this wild, lawless land that Amyas Leigh and his small armed party approached at dusk, Amyas himself clad in full steel plate astride a strong warhorse, with lance, broadsword, and two heavy horse pistols at his saddle. Behind him rode Salvation Yeo, grim old Puritan arquebusier, and five or six north Devon veterans who had served under Amyas in Ireland, all wearing steel headpieces and thick quilted jerkins, each armed with pike and sword. Two shaggy moorland ponies carried their baggage, as they pushed hard past Tavistock to reach Lydford and a safe inn before nightfall. But delays buying fresh horses had kept them from starting out till noon, and full dark fell the very moment they rode over the border of Gubbing territory.

It was a desolate stretch under the lurid glow of the last sunset: a high open heath plateau, Dartmoor’s jagged crags rising to their right, sloping away west to the great cone of Brent Tor, that ancient extinct volcano, its peak crowned by the tiny votive church a long-dead Plymouth merchant had vowed to build when he first sighted English land after a storm at sea. Far down the waste slopes below them, thin threads of blue smoke curled up from the hidden hollows where the Gubbings made their dens. More than once the party halted, staring hard at clumps of gorse and stray grazing ponies, half convinced they were enemy sentinels. It is easy to laugh at their caution now, but in that day it was no jest, as they would soon learn.

A mile further on, they found the only wayside inn for miles around: Rogues’ Harbor, a desolate lump of lichen-crusted granite, its windows patched with scraped paper, its rotting thatch weighted down with heavy stones to keep the wind from tearing it off. Pigs and barefoot grubby children loitered in the rambling muddy back yard, grunting in unholy communion of dirt. In the doorway stood the innkeeper, six feet of brawny, blear-eyed brutishness, holding his hose up with one hand for want of proper points, scratching his matted elf-locked hair where stray goose feathers stuck thick: proof he had only just stumbled out of bed after a night spent out stealing sheep, and thought the feathers made him look as if he slept on soft down. He spotted Amyas’s party, counted them, shook his head at the sight of Amyas’s full armor, and let out a sleepy howl for his daughter Mary.

The strapping red-cheeked lass, clad only in a thin green bodice and scanty red petticoat, brought out his fishing rod and basket. He fumbled with the line, swore when he found the tiny fly was missing, and roared that the old gander must have eaten it. When Mary dodged the blow he aimed at her head, she clouted him back hard across his own, and he shambled off yelping, yelling to go fetch the Patrico. Mary tidied her hair, slipped on her best gown and a pair of stockings over her dirt-smudged legs, and greeted the arriving guests. The men pulled faces at how mucky the place was, but agreed it was far better to sleep under its leaky roof than bivouac out on the heath within raiding distance of the Gubbings camps.

The old hen that had swallowed the missing fishing fly was soon plucked, roasted, and set on the table, alongside cuts of “black Dartmoor mutton” that wafted the distinct, rich scent of red deer venison. Amyas stabled his horse and the pack ponies in a shed that had neither lock nor key, and came back grumbling, already worried his fine war steed would be stolen in the night. Yeo, ever suspicious, stationed himself by the front door, while the rest of the men lingered by the fire to “superintend” Mary’s cooking.

Presently Yeo stepped back inside, all a-twitter.
“Sir, there’s a gentleman just riding up, alone.”
“Ask him to join us, tell him he’s welcome to supper,” Amyas said.

Yeo trotted out, and came back five minutes later. “He slipped round the back, through the yard, sir, strange as can be.”
“Odd tastes he has, to make himself at home in this den,” Amyas shrugged.

Yeo vanished again, and burst back in moments later, quivering with excitement. “Come out, sir! For the Lord’s sake come quick! I’ve got him, safe as a rat in a trap!”
“Got who?” Amyas asked.
“A Jesuit, sir! I saw the way he trod, soft on his toes like he feared he’d offend God with every step, I knew he was one of them Popish villains the moment he came over the hill. I spied through the crack in the stable gate, saw him slip up to the back door, call Mary’s name all quiet, give her one of them Popish Agnus Dei charms. She kissed it, crossed herself, he blessed her like a priest, then he tiptoed right into the stable. I shut the door behind him, wedged a full cart against it, posted one of the men on guard. That wench is out there crying fit to burst.”

Amyas huffed that he might have locked up an honest traveller by mistake, but Yeo was unyielding. “Honest men don’t hand out Popish charms to maids. If the Lord’s enemies are delivered into my hand, I’m bound to hold them, sir.”
Amyas followed him out, grumbling, dragged the heavy cart away, and pulled open the stable door. Trapped inside, staring at him half foolish, half venomous, like a fox caught digging its way out of a trap, was his own cousin Eustace Leigh.

For a long minute neither spoke. At last Amyas laughed. “Well, cousin, have you added horse theft to your other tricks now?”
“I am here only for the Lord’s work,” Eustace said meekly. “I came to minister to these poor neglected folk, whom your Protestant Church leaves in heathen ignorance.”
Eustace’s temper snapped suddenly when Yeo sneered that he was wasting his blessings on thieves. “You would leave these poor souls to rot untaught? You can hand me over to hang at Exeter jail if you wish to disgrace your own family, but no man, not all the Queen’s soldiers, shall drive me away while there is one soul here left unsaved.”

Amyas sighed, and waved Yeo off. “Come out of the stable, man. I won’t inform on you. I promise you guest’s protection. Come in, eat supper with us, let bygones be bygones.”
Eustace agreed, for he saw a chance to turn Amyas’s soft heart to the good of his own Church, and the two cousins sat by the fire, talking of old childhood days in Bideford, their aunts and uncles, old play haunts, until the tension between them thawed. When Eustace asked why Amyas was heading north, Amyas told him the whole story of Rose Salterne’s disappearance, of her flight with the mysterious Spanish sailor.

Eustace shrieked aloud, leaping to his feet, stamping up and down the room in a wild frenzy, clutching at empty air. “Fool that I was! That villain broke his promise to me on Lundy Island! I loved her! I had as much right to love her as any of your puffed-up Brotherhood of the Rose!” He had betrayed far more than he meant to, and he suddenly checked himself, glowering at Amyas from under his brows, sharp and defensive.

Amyas offered to team up with him to find Rose, but Eustace snapped back that their paths were too far apart, and hinted darkly that Amyas was now deep in the territory of men who answered to him, a fellow Catholic, not the Queen’s Protestant laws. Amyas’s temper flared, but he remembered his promise of safe conduct, and Frank’s old kindness to Eustace back in Clovelly, and he only opened the front door and gestured him out, telling him to go his own way. Eustace vanished into the dark.

Amyas noticed Yeo was missing, and assumed the old Puritan had wandered off to say his prayers. He told his men to bed down around the fire, and stretched out on the wooden settle, using his saddle as a pillow.

At midnight, a deafening racket jolted him awake: thousands of panicking geese squawking and flapping around the house, then Yeo’s voice, yelling at the top of his lungs for help. The men grabbed their swords, burst out the back door into the courtyard, and found Yeo backed up against the stable door, fending off a dozen Gubbing raiders single-handed, sword in one hand, buckler in the other. The raiders turned and fled the second they saw Amyas and his armed party come pouring out of the inn.

“Not a scratch on me, praise the Lord!” Yeo panted. “I got the two ringleaders. Your warhorse kicked the other one dead before he could run. This big brute here, I ran him through.”
They lifted the big man up: he was the King of the Gubbings, their feared Patrico, the raider chief the whole tribe obeyed. He was bleeding out fast, gasping for breath. The second man was already cold in the stable straw, trampled to death by the panicked horses. They stumbled back through the low kitchen door to carry the wounded chief inside, and found the rest of the raider plot unspooling: the innkeeper, the Jesuit Father Parsons in half-dressed disarray, and Eustace Leigh, all caught trying to slip out the front door with Mary’s help, bound for the moor to rouse the rest of the Gubbings tribe.

The wounded chief lying on the floor heard Parsons’ voice, and moaned out loud for his Patrico. Parsons preened, stepping forward, his eyes full of smug triumph. “You see, sir. The sheep know the voice of their shepherd, and I shall prepare this my wounded lamb for the account your man’s cruelty has untimely sent him to render.”

“The Kynge of Spayn is a foul paynim,
And lieveth on Mahound;
And pity it were that lady fayre
Should marry a heathen hound.”

Some six weeks after the duel on the sands outside Bideford, the miller of Stow trundled up to the Grenvilles’ great house, white-faced and shaking, to beg the loan of the estate’s prized bloodhounds. Rose Salterne had vanished from her bed in the dead of night, and no soul knew where she had gone.

Sir Richard was away on business in Bideford, but old steward Anthony took matters into his own hands, dispatching for the hound-keepers at once. A throng of idle parish lads trailed the serving-men down to the mill, chattering and delighted at the prospect of a maiden hunt for sport, all of them certain the poor girl had been cruelly wronged. They roundly reviled the miller and his wife as hard-hearted heathens, fully convinced their harshness had driven Rose to throw herself off the jagged north Devon cliffs or drown in the frigid Atlantic surf. The women of Stow, for their part, were of one single, unanimous mind: the hussy had run off with some rascally man, and pride was bound to have its fall, as it always did.

The facts of Rose’s disappearance spoke far more gently of her: all her trinkets and finery were left untouched in her chamber, so at the very least she had gone away honestly. The only items missing were a small bundle of her linen, which old Anthony broadly hinted would likely turn up in other people’s households soon enough. The sole clue the search party found was a tiny, delicate footprint under her bedroom window. They leashed the great bloodhound to the mark, and after a single soft whimper of recognition, the beast lifted his deep, thunderous bay and dragged the panting keeper behind him, out the garden gate, up the sunken lane, and across the high windswept downs, heading straight for Marslandmouth. The whole posse comitatus stumbled breathless to a halt at the door of Lucy Passmore’s cottage.

As noted earlier, Lucy was now a widow, and her state of widowhood had proven surprisingly profitable. The dark augury she had spoken over her drunken old husband had come true: he had never returned from that night he sailed out with Eustace Leigh and the rogue Jesuits. She shed a few showy, dutiful tears, dried them quickly enough to save whatever she might need for business, then made a bold play to avoid any suspicion of collusion: she traveled to Stow and told Lady Grenville a wildly dramatic tale, swearing her husband had gone out pollock fishing and never come back, that she had heard horsemen gallop past her cottage window after midnight, and that those Popish skulkers the Jesuits had dragged him off by force, no doubt murdering him and salting his body down for provisions on their voyage back to Rome and the Pope. She begged for Lady Grenville’s protection from any further Jesuit mischief, and hinted the Queen herself owed her a pension for losing the love of her heart to idolatrous traitors.

Lady Grenville, who swore by Lucy’s herbal remedies for the children’s “housty” – the local name for sore throats – pleaded her case to Sir Richard so effectively that Lucy was comfortably secure for the next two years. Now, though, the door to her cottage was locked fast. Her flock of goats huddled on the step bleating for milk, while her donkeys wandered the downs above, braying in raucous chorus at the bloodhound’s bay.
“They’m laughing at us, keeper, them neddies,” one local man grumbled. “Sure as fate we’ve wasted our time here.”

The hound nosed around the cottage perimeter for a few more minutes, then loped straight down the glen, never pausing until it reached the edge of the sea.
“They took to the water! Let’s go back and turn the old witch’s house inside out.”
The crowd swarmed back to the cottage, ransacking every corner half in indignation, half – if the truth be told – in the hope of finding plunder. But there was nothing to take. Lucy had fled with every last bit of her moveable wealth, leaving only a huge black cat curled in the embers of her hearth. At the sight of the bloodhound, the cat shot straight up the chimney (the villagers swore they caught a strong whiff of brimstone as it went) and vanished into the woods, no doubt living many long happy years plaguing every rabbit in the glen. The mob drove the goats and donkeys back to Stow, then slunk home, sheepish once their initial fury faded, and more than a little afraid of what Sir Richard would say when he returned. He sold the animals at market, gave all the proceeds to the village poor, and promised to refund the full sum if Lucy ever returned to turn herself in to justice. But Lucy never came back. Her cottage was avoided by neighbors as if it were haunted, and slowly crumbled away to four fern-choked walls, the little stream trickling past it the only sound to break the glen’s silence for years.

A few days later, Sir Richard stopped by Clovelly Court on his ride home from Bideford, and offhandedly mentioned a piece of news that made Will Cary leap so far out of his chair he nearly hit the ceiling. Someone patrolling the cliffs the night Rose vanished, he said, had spotted a pinnace sailing hard for Lundy Island.
Will was half out of his mind. Half an hour later, he and four armed servants were aboard a trawling skiff, sailing for Lundy. He did not return for three days, and when he did he brought grim news: an elderly foreign man, who looked for all the world like a mass-priest, had been living in the ruins of Moresco Castle on Lundy for months, renting the space from a local fisherman named John Braund. A few weeks prior, a younger foreign man had joined him off a Baltic trading ship, which came and went several times with the young man aboard. A few days before Will arrived, a lady and her stout maidservant had come ashore with the younger man, spoken at length in secret with the elder man, stayed the night, and then all three had sailed away at dawn. The local fishermen had heard the younger man call the older one “father” – and they knew better than to ask any more questions.

Old Cary and Sir Richard sent Will back to Lundy with the Hartland parish constable, and he returned with the hapless John Braund in chains. During endless questioning, Braund feigned extreme deafness and total loss of memory of any events, and was sent to Exeter Gaol on charges of harbouring Jesuits, gypsies, and other suspect traitorous persons. Poor Braund, who had only hidden the men to pay his overdue rent and feed his seven children, never survived the transition from Lundy’s crisp sea air to the gaol’s pestilent stench. He took the gaol fever within a week, and died raving in his noisome cell, his secret buried with him. Only vague, cruel rumour remained of Rose Salterne’s fate.

Nearly every dame in Bideford, who had long resented Rose for her unrivalled beauty, gossiped that she had run off with the Spanish heathen, and got exactly what she deserved. Local gentlewomen who had long claimed the girl was “getting above her station” shook their heads and said they had always expected she would end in ruin. Every young maiden in town was glad the fair rival was gone, and before long the bustle of daily life swept Rose’s name out of most people’s heads, leaving only a tender faded memory in a few hearts.

Will Cary, meanwhile, was half distracted with guilt. He cursed himself endlessly for disgracing Rose during the duel, and driving her straight into Don Guzman’s arms. St. Leger tried in vain to calm him, telling him the fault was not his – the two had clearly been drawn to each other long before the duel, and old Salterne’s harsh treatment of his daughter was far more to blame than Will’s anger. But Will refused to be comforted. He wrote to Frank Leigh at Whitehall, confessing every last detail, calling himself a fool and a villain, and begging for forgiveness.

Frank wrote back at once, telling Will he had no reason to blame himself at all. He said a “synastria” – a sympathy of the stars – had bound Rose and her chosen man together, and to fight that bond was to fight against heaven itself. As one of the Brotherhood of the Rose, he swore that Rose could never make anything but a worthy, virtuous choice, and that even if the man she loved was a Spaniard and a Papist, he was owed their full loyalty and respect. Will had no idea how much agony that letter had cost Frank, who was quietly wasting away of unspoken love for Rose, tormented by fears he dared not name. He showed the letter triumphantly to St. Leger, who agreed Frank was an angel even as he privately suspected Frank’s true feelings were far more turbulent.

The matter rested for a few days, until Jack Brimblecombe arrived. He was now curate of Hartland Town, “passing rich on forty pounds a year”, and he looked at Will Cary with steady, sober eyes.
“I hope no offence, Mr. William,” he said, the name Rose sticking tight in his throat. “But when are you and the rest going after – after her?”

Will tried to laugh it off, teasing him for being the “blood-drinking Catiline” from the night they had all gotten drunk and sworn their oaths as Brothers of the Rose. But Jack would not be swayed.
“I drank nothing worse than red wine that night, sir, as far as I can tell,” he said firmly. “And oaths are oaths. There’s no getting out of them.” He asked how far it was to Caracas – he had heard Don Guzman had been made governor there, and that was surely where they had taken Rose.

Will could not believe his ears. “You cannot mean to sail all the way to the Indies to look for her?”
“That depends on if I can scrape together the money, or get a berth on a westward-bound ship,” Jack said. “God’s will must be done, either way.”

Will stared at him, wondering if he was drunk or mad – but the little pig-like eyes behind his round spectacles were completely sane and serious. Jack explained that his mother was comfortably off if his father passed, his sisters were all strong, capable girls who could make good servants, and he begged Will to look after them if he never came home. He offered all his books as pawn if Will would lend him five pounds for travel fare.
“You’ll be seized by the Spanish Inquisition the moment you step ashore, and burnt alive!” Will protested.
“I know that,” Jack said, tears welling in his eyes. “I’m a great coward, Mr. Will, a dirty coward, as you well know. But I cannot bear this not knowing! Not knowing if that pure angel I have always revered is living in sin, or if those Papist devils are torturing her to make her renounce her faith. I am a deacon! I swore to tend Christ’s lost sheep, and no other soul will go for her – no one else loves her enough.” He said, if Amyas Leigh were home, he would not hesitate to set sail at once.

Will managed to persuade Jack to wait until Amyas returned, rather than rushing blindly to his death, and Jack agreed to sleep on it. He vanished for a week, then came back to Clovelly Court grinning, and said he had arranged for the parson of Welcombe to cover his services for two Sundays, and he planned to walk all the way to London to talk to Frank Leigh. Will could not dissuade him, and pressed a little extra money into his hand before he set off.

Three weeks later, Jack stumbled back into Clovelly Court thin and footsore, and told Will he had found Frank half dead with grief for Rose, wasting away for love. Frank agreed their oath was binding, and that they *must* go to find her – but he told Jack they must wait for the right time, and so Jack had come home to bide his time.

The thought of the voyage would not leave Will Cary’s head, and a few days later he ran into Mr. Salterne on Bideford Quay. The old ship merchant told him bluntly that all the young men of spirit ought to sail west to the Indies, to find new trade and new wealth for Devon, instead of moping around the shoreline. That was all the push Will needed. He went straight to Amyas, and told him he was ready.
“Westward-ho, with you,” he said. “Come foul weather or fair.”

Amyas agreed at once. Even if they did not find Rose in La Guayra, he said, the Spanish Plate fleets would be there, heavy with gold and silver from the mines. They would not come home empty handed. Will laughed and told him his old father had recently married a buxom young tenant’s daughter, who had already borne him a fat baby, so there was no shortage of heirs to Clovelly Court, and no one would miss Will if he left. He joked about his new stepmother Abishag Jewell, who endlessly bragged about her distant relation to the great Bishop Jewell, and he could no longer stand to see her occupy his own dead mother’s place at the head of the table.

They agreed at once that they could not leave Jack Brimblecombe behind – if he did not get to sail with them, he would no doubt hurl himself off Harty Point in despair, and his ghost would haunt them for the rest of their days. After a long consultation, they decided Amyas would travel up to London first, to talk to Frank and his mother, to get their blessing for the voyage. The rest of the Brothers of the Rose were scattered far and wide across the country at their posts, and it would take far too long to gather them all, as Amyas sagely noted: “Too many cooks spoil the broth, and half a dozen gentlemen aboard one small ship are as bad as two Kings of Brentford.”

The next morning, Amyas set off for London, leaving Yeo behind to help Will make all the final preparations for their westward voyage.

> “He is brass within, and steel without,
> With beams on his topcastle strong;
> And eighteen pieces of ordinance
> He carries on either side along.”
> — Sir Andrew Barton

Let us climb aboard a small wherry at Whitehall Stairs, just as Amyas Leigh did, and slip downstream ahead of him, gliding beneath the weathered arches of old London Bridge, all the way to Deptford Creek. There, preserved almost as if embalmed in the Thames mist, rests the famous *Pelican* — the stout little ship in which Francis Drake had sailed clean around the whole circumference of the world. She sits drawn high and dry on the reed-fringed riverbank, like a grizzled old veteran finally resting after decades of hard campaigning. Nailed to her mainmast are verses and epigrams honouring both the vessel and her captain, three of them penned by a scholar of Winchester College that the historian Camden later recorded in his chronicles. Queen Elizabeth herself had consecrated the ship solemnly, feasted aboard her decks, and there and then bestowed the honour of knighthood on Drake. On that very day, the plank bridge the royal party had used to board her collapsed under the press of cheering onlookers, pitching a hundred men into the mud below — yet not a single soul was harmed. It was said ever after that the *Pelican* had been born under a lucky, protective star.

For years now she has stood there as a public curiosity, and a favourite dining hall for jovial groups of London citizens. This afternoon, it is clear a grand feast is underway: bright banners flutter from her masts, the roar of revelry and the rich scent of spiced roasted meat drift from the hatches that once served as gun ports, and white-aproned waiters from the neighbouring Pelican Inn rush back and forth across that famous lucky plank bridge, alongside liveried serving men bearing the City of London’s badge. The Lord Mayor himself is hosting a feast for a party of gentlemen from the Earl of Leicester’s household, all of them invested in new overseas discovery — and what possible venue could be more fitting than Drake’s own circumnavigating ship?

Look around at the guests gathered at that long table: you will seldom see a nobler, bolder company in one room. And if you are American, pause to look long at their faces and hold them in reverence, for their wisdom, their daring, and their sacrifices laid the very foundations of your mighty nation.

At the head of the table sits the Lord Mayor, whom every reader will recognise at once: Sir Edward Osborne, the famous cloth worker, ancestor to the Dukes of Leeds, whose romantic story of daring is still told by every fireside. He has aged little in the decades since he leapt from a London Bridge window into the roaring, frothing tide below to rescue the infant who would grow up to be his wife. The quick, chivalrous courage of that young apprentice has hardened into the thoughtful, steady daring of a wealthy merchant adventurer. Beside him sits the Earl of Cumberland on his right, and Walter Raleigh on his left, the three of them talking low of the vast, wealthy undiscovered lands that are rumoured to stretch between Florida and the mouth of the great Canadian river. Raleigh’s half-scientific speculations, full of quotations from the learned alchemist Doctor Dee, carry far more weight with Cumberland (who has backed dozens of foreign adventures, and failed at all of them, simply because he never sailed on any himself, sending other men in his stead) than they do with the practical Osborne. Raleigh soon turns to draw the quiet Oxford scholar at his left hand into the conversation: Richard Hakluyt, the great collector of voyage narratives. But Hakluyt is deep in talk with a white-bearded old elder whose beard falls nearly to his waist, whose face is crisscrossed with the lines of a thousand gales: Anthony Jenkinson, the legendary Asiatic traveller, who is regaling him with tales of reindeer sleds streaking across Siberian steppes, and the fossil ivory the Tungus people dig out of Arctic sea ice cliffs, plain proof of Noah’s flood. Next to them sits Christopher Carlile, Walsingham’s son-in-law (as Philip Sidney also is now), a valiant captain who would later lead the land forces in Drake’s triumphant 1585 West Indian raid, where he would share a very fine bottle of wine with the Bishop of Carthagena himself. He is deep in conversation with Alderman Hart the grocer, Sheriff Spencer the cloth worker, Amyas’s own merchant cousin Charles Leigh, the Mayor of Bristol Aldworth, and William Salterne — alderman of Bristol, and cousin to the Rose Salterne the Leighs know in Bideford. For these men, alongside Secretary Walsingham, have spent two years pooling funds to back Humphrey and Adrian Gilbert’s grand North-West Passage expeditions, ventures Carlile had once planned to sail on himself, before circumstances (the exact reason no one ever uncovered) kept him ashore.

Across the table sits a group no less fascinating. Martin Frobisher and John Davis, the two trailblazers of the North-West Passage, are chatting with Alderman Sanderson, the great geographer and maker of globes, alongside Mr. Towerson, Sir Gilbert Peckham, their old acquaintance Captain John Winter — and last, but far from least, Philip Sidney himself. With his usual generous courtesy, Sidney has given up his rightful place near the head of the table so he can cluster here with this knot of fellow exploration enthusiasts, bringing his two closest intimates Edward Dyer and Francis Leigh along with him. They too are deep in talk of the North-West Passage: Sidney is lamenting that he is tied to court diplomacy, and gushing with affectionate, flattering envy of old Martin Frobisher’s adventures at sea. Frobisher just snorts and replies, “It’s all very well talking of it here, sitting on dry land with a good glass of wine before you, Sir Philip. You’d find it a very different sort of business bashing through icebergs with your beard frozen fast to your ruff, especially if you had a weak stomach.”
“That would be a trivial hardship to endure, my good man, if I could win half the honour Her Majesty bestowed on you, when she waved that ivory white handkerchief from Greenwich Palace windows to farewell your ship,” Sidney laughs back.
“Well sir, folks say you’ve no cause to complain of lack of royal favour, and no cause to deserve less of it,” Frobisher grins. “If you can win honours staying ashore, don’t go chasing them out on the ocean, that’s what I say, eh Master Towerson?”
Towerson’s gray beard, weathered by decades of fair and foul foreign voyages, nods in grim agreement. But at that very moment, a waiter hurries into the cabin.
“By your leave, my Lord Mayor’s worship, there’s a tall gentleman outside begging a word with the Right Honourable Sir Walter Raleigh.”
“Show him in at once. Sir Walter’s friends are our friends,” Osborne calls.

Amyas steps across the threshold, pausing hesitantly in the doorway.
“Captain Leigh!” half a dozen voices cry out at once.
“Whyever did you hang back out there?” Osborne asks warmly. “You know your way about these decks well enough.”
“Well enough, my lords and gentlemen. But — Sir Walter, you must excuse me,” Amyas says, giving Raleigh a sharp, meaningful look that the quick-witted explorer understands instantly. Raleigh blanches, stands, and follows Amyas out into a side cabin. The two are gone for five minutes, before Amyas steps back out alone.
In a few plain, heavy words he tells the whole company the terrible news they had not yet heard: Sir Humphrey Gilbert had gone down with his ship *Squirrel* in the mid-Atlantic, no survivors. Before he finishes speaking, noble tears glisten on the faces of these hardened, battle-scarred men.
“The ancient Egyptians,” Sir Edward Osborne says heavily, “would set a corpse out among their guests at feasts, to remind them of the fragility of human life. Have we forgotten God and our own weakness in our celebration today, that He Himself has sent us this message from the dead?”
“Nay, my Lord Mayor,” Sidney says softly, “it is no message from the dead, but a greeting from the realm of everlasting life.”
“Amen,” Osborne answers. “But gentlemen, our feast is done. There are men here brave enough to drink on merrily in spite of private losses, but not a single one of us can raise a toast now with the news of such a great, good man’s death still ringing in our ears.”
It was true. Many of these men had lost great sums of money backing Gilbert’s expedition, but all of that vanished from their minds in the shadow of the tragedy. The party breaks up sadly and hurriedly, every man turning to his neighbour to mutter, “What will the Queen say?”
Raleigh returns a few minutes later, silent, pressing the hand of every friend he passes before he slips out to hail a wherry, beckoning Amyas to follow. Sidney, Cumberland, and Frank Leigh take another boat behind them, leaving Raleigh and Amyas to sort through the sad details of the wreck.

They land back at Whitehall Stairs. Raleigh, Sidney, and Cumberland head into the palace to break the news to Elizabeth, while the two Leigh brothers make their way to their mother’s lodgings.
Amyas had rehearsed what he would say to Frank about Rose Salterne, but now that the moment is here, he cannot bring himself to start, wishing Frank would broach the subject first. Frank too shrinks from the difficult conversation, all the more so because he has no idea Amyas passed through Bideford on his way to London, and already knows all about Rose’s disappearance.
When they climb the stairs, both of them are relieved to find their mother is out visiting Westminster Abbey. They drift over to the deep bay window overlooking the Thames, making small talk of trivial things, gazing at each other’s faces long in the fading dusk: it has been three whole years since they last met.
The years and their wildly different lives have only deepened the contrast between the two brothers. Frank smiles with affectionate, proud admiration as he looks up at Amyas, and sees he is no longer the rollicking, quick-handed young sailor lad he remembered, but a stately, self-assured warrior, every line of his face and gesture bearing the marks of “the reason firm, the temperate will, endurance, foresight, strength, and skill” one would expect of a man tutored by Drake and Grenville, then mentored by Raleigh and Gilbert. His long wild locks are cropped close to his head, offset by a rich golden beard covering his lips and chin. His skin is tanned dark by a thousand tropical suns and Atlantic gales, a long scar from a fight in Ireland cuts across his right temple, his great broad frame has filled out with hard muscle, and the hand he rests on the windowsill is as heavy and calloused as a blacksmith’s. Frank lays his own slender hand on top of it and sighs, and Amyas looks down, shocked at the contrast: Frank’s delicate fingers are thin, bloodless, almost translucent. Amyas stares anxiously into his brother’s face. He has changed drastically in their years apart. The bright red flush still burns on his two cheeks, but the skin around it is dull and pale, his lips are colourless, his features sharpened, and his eyes glint with an unnatural, feverish brightness. When Frank teases him for looking far older than his years, Amyas cannot help but think the remark applies far more truly to Frank himself.
Amyas tries to steer the conversation to lighter things, pointing at every new building along the riverbank to ask its name.
“So this is old Father Thames, lined all along with palaces?”
“Aye,” Frank says softly. “His banks are stately enough, but you see, he does not stop to look at them. He hurries down to the sea, the sea flows on to the ocean, and the ocean — westward ho, forever. Everything moves westward ho. Perhaps we will move that way ourselves one day, Amyas.”
“What strange talk is that?”
“Only that the great tides of the world follow the primum mobile of the heavens, and flow forever from east to west. Is it so strange to think of that, after we spent all afternoon toasting success to all our westward ventures?”
“And much good that toast has done!” Amyas grunts. “I lost the best friend and noblest captain on God’s green earth, not to mention all my life’s savings, swallowed up in that same cursed westward gulf.”
“Aye, Sir Humphrey Gilbert’s star set in the west — why not?” Frank says. “The sun, moon, and all the planets sink below the western horizon. Why not the meteors of this lower world? Why not a will-o’-the-wisp like me, Amyas?”
“God forbid that should happen to you, Frank!”
“Why not? Is not the west the land of peace, the land of dreams? Does not our own heart tell us that, every time we watch the sun sink, and long to float away on those golden clouds with him?
