They bury men facing east. I would rather my face was turned to the west when I die, for I think some divine instinct made the old ancient poets guess that Elysium lay beneath the setting sun. That longing for the west is woven deep into every human heart. I do not blame any man who sails out beyond the farthest sea to find it, as David once wished to flee, to find peace.”
“You do not blame anyone who flees westward?” Amyas asks, sharply. “I do.”
Frank stares at him inquiringly, then smiles. “If I was to blame anyone just now, it would be you, for seeming tired of chasing that westward dream.”
“Then you would have me go?”
“God knows,” Frank says, after a long pause. “But I suppose I must tell you all, once and for all. Things have happened back in Bideford that —”
“Spare us both, Frank. I know everything. I passed through Bideford on my way here. I came to London not just to see you and our mother, but to ask for your advice, and her permission.”
“True, noble heart!” Frank cries, throwing his arms around Amyas. “I knew you would be true to your vow.”
“Westward ho it is, then?”
“We have no choice, do we?”
“We?” Amyas repeats, shocked.
“Amyas, does not the same oath that binds you bind me too?”
Amyas staggers back, holding Frank by the shoulders at arm’s length, and feels beneath his clothes that his brother’s arms are little more than skin and bone.
“You? Dearest heart, a single month of that hard voyage would kill you!”
Frank laughs softly, tossing his head in that familiar, graceful way of his. “I follow the school of Thales, who said the ocean is the mother of all life. I have no more fear of returning to her bosom than Humphrey Gilbert did.”
“But Frank — what of our mother?”
“Our mother knows all. She would never have us prove unworthy of her.”
“Impossible! She will never let you go.”
“All things are possible for those who believe in God, brother, and she believes. This past summer, Doctor Dee the wise astrologer gave her all sorts of readings about me, you see. It seems I was born under a cold, watery planet, and if I want to live a long life, I must travel closer to the warm, life-giving sun, to bask out my short little life there like a fly on a sunlit wall. To tell you the truth, he ordered me to stop spending cold winters here in the east, and go back to the sea breezes of our home shores to heal my frozen lungs. He filled my mother’s head with stories of sick men everyone thought were dying in Germany and France, who got their youth back, like serpents or eagles shedding their old skin, by travelling to Italy, Spain, and the Canary Islands. She will be more eager to let me go than I will be to leave her all alone. And yet I must go, Amyas. It is not just that my heart burns, as Philip Sidney’s does, as every gallant man’s should, to join that noble band of Argonauts who are spreading God’s word and claiming new lands for our queen. It is not just that love calls me — tyrannous, uncontrollable love, that grows stronger with absence, deeper with despair. Honour binds me too, Amyas — my oath —”
He breaks off, gasping for breath, and bursts into a violent fit of coughing, leaning heavily on his brother’s shoulder.
“Fools that we were — fools that I was — to make that ridiculous, fantastical vow!” Amyas groans.
“Not so,” a soft voice says from behind them. “You made that vow for the sake of peace on earth, and goodwill to all men, and ‘Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called the children of God.’ No, my sons, be sure that such self-sacrifice as you have shown will meet its full reward at the hand of Him who sacrificed Himself for you.”
“Mother! Mother!” Amyas cries. “Do you not hate the very sight of me, coming here to take your first-born son away from you?”
“My boy, it is God who is taking him, not you. If I dare put my faith in such predictions, Doctor Dee assured me that a great, extraordinary honour awaits both of you in the west, each according to your deserts.”
“Ah,” Amyas says, grinning a little sadly. “I suppose my blessing will be like Esau’s, to live by my sword out there. And you, Jacob the gentle spiritual man, will inherit the kingdom of heaven, and an angel’s crown.”
“Be it what it may, it will be a blessing so long as you stay the good, honourable boys you have always been. At the very least, my Frank will be safe from court intrigues and the temptations of the city. Oh, how I wish I could come with you, and share in your glory!” she says, laying her head on Amyas’s chest and looking up with that familiar winning smile. “I have heard of heroic mothers who marched to battle beside their sons, and cheered them on to victory. Why should I not come with you on a far more peaceful errand? I could nurse the sick, if any fall ill. I could speak to that poor lost girl, and win her back far more easily than any of you men could. She would listen to words from a woman — a woman who has known love — that she would never hear from your rough voices. At the very least, I can mend your clothes and wash for you. Surely it is just as

“The sun's rim dips; the stars rush out;
At one stride comes the dark.”

Land! Land! Land!
There it blazed far off to the south and west, a long unbroken bar of soft cobalt blue strung between crimson-tipped waves and the honey-gold glow of the sinking sun. Land at last: land holding cool, sweet mountain streams, tart thirst-quenching fruit, and wide open space for limbs cramped for weeks in swinging hammocks, wasted and swollen by the creeping curse of scurvy. And who knew what else? Gold, uncut gems, all the fabled wealth of the Indies? The dull, prosaic old world of English cottages and strict merchant laws lay thousands of miles behind them, and all around stretched uncharted realms of wonder, old sailors’ fables, and hope so bright it felt tangible.
Sick men dragged their swollen, bleeding gums up over the hammock rails, their wobbly legs barely holding their weight. Strong men dropped straight to their knees on the scuffed oak deck to pour out thanks to God. Every eye strained toward that faint blue cloud, which faded a little as the sun dipped lower, only to swell broader and sharper as the ship sprinted forward on the back of the warm, steady trade wind that curled gently around their brows and tugged at the sails, as if whispering to them, I am the faithful friend of those who dare.
“Blow harder, good trade wind,” Frank Leigh murmured to himself, his eyes fixed on that first scrap of New World soil that held everything he cared for. “It is written that He makes the winds His angels, breaths sent to sustain the heirs of His salvation. Blow freshlier still, if not to save me from death, then to save her from a fate worse than death. Blow on, carry me to her feet, that I may lead my lost lamb home, and die.”
His cheeks were gaunt and wasted, the characteristic hectic flush of his sickness burning bright crimson on both cheekbones, glowing even brighter in the sunset’s red light. Minutes later, all the vivid rainbow hues of the western sky bled away: emerald, topaz, amethyst, and ruby of the clouds dimmed to soft silver grey, and overhead the dark sapphire of the night sky opened, with the moon and Venus hanging low and bright over the glassy sea.
“That’s Barbados, your worship,” said Drew, the ship’s master. “Unless my reckoning’s gone wildly off, which Heaven knows it has no right to be, not after a passage as smooth as this. God be praised for it.”
“Barbados? I’ve never heard the name before.”
“Like enough, sir. Yeo and I sailed here with Captain Drake once, and I came again later with poor Captain Barlow. There’s good sheltered anchorage to the south and west of the island, as I remember plain as day.”
“And no Spaniards, no cannibals, no other wicked beast there,” added old Yeo, the gruff gunner, his eyes lighting up. “A very garden of the Lord, sir, hidden away in the middle of the seas, waiting to be an inheritance for those that love Him. I heard Captain Drake say he meant to plant a settlement here one day, if he ever got the chance.”
“I remember now,” Amyas put in, suddenly recalling old stories. “He and poor Sir Humphrey Gilbert talked at length about an island in these very waters. I wish to God he’d sailed for this place instead of Newfoundland!”
“Nay, sir,” Yeo said gently. “The good man’s in bliss with the Lord now. You wouldn’t take that from him, would you?”
“He’d have waited as patiently to sail for this island as he went off to Newfoundland, if it meant he could serve his queen,” Amyas laughed. “Well, my masters, what say you? Could we do better than lay over here a few days, get all our sick men back on their feet, before we push on to the Main and set about our work?”
Every man on board cheered the idea, save Frank, who stayed quiet, his gaze still fixed on the distant shore.
“Come, fellow adventurer,” Will Cary clapped him on the shoulder, “we need your vote too.”
“To my great impatience, Will, there is only one place on this whole earth I long to reach, and all day I’ve ached for wings to fly straight there,” Frank said low, aside to him. “But the counsel is right. I give it my full approval.”
The decision was announced, and met with a roar of genuine, hearty cheer from the entire crew. Long before dawn, they had sailed the length of the island’s southern shore, and were picking their way slowly into the sheltered bay where Bridgetown would one day stand.
Every eye was locked on the low, wooded hills sleeping soft in the moonlight, dotted with fireflies that flickered like a million tiny dancing stars. Every nostril drank deep of the sweet land breeze blowing out over the waves, heavy with the perfume of a thousand unknown tropical flowers. Every ear welcomed the new, rich hum of life that broke the monotonous lap and whisper of the sea: the droning of insects, the deep snore of tree toads, the soft plaintive cries of shore birds that fill a tropical night with noisy, joyful life.
At last the anchor dropped, the chain rattling loud through the hawsehole, and a spontaneous cheer burst from every throat, even with the risk of hidden Spaniards or hostile Caribs lingering on the shore. Amyas had all he could do to hold the crew back from leaping over the side to wade ashore right then, dark as it was, by pointing out that dawn was less than two hours away.
“Never did two hours drag so slow,” fidgeted one young cabin boy, pacing back and forth across the deck.
“You never sat in the Inquisition’s cells then,” Yeo grunted at him. “You’d learn what slow time really is. Stand still, and thank the Lord you’re alive and free on the edge of Paradise.”
“Hey, Gunner,” the boy pressed, “is there gold on this island?”
“Never heard tell of any, and that’s all to the good,” Yeo said dryly.
But a poor scurvy-crippled seaman, his lips parched and swollen, leaned forward and licked his lips. “What about oranges and lemons, Gunner? Are there any of those?”
“None I saw on my last visits, but there’s plenty of good fruit growing right down on the beach, thank the Lord. And look—there’s dawn breaking at last.”
Pink flushed the horizon, and the sun leapt up over the treeline, its level golden rays glinting off the smooth pale trunks of the palm trees, painting rainbows across the surf breaking over the coral reefs, and gilding the lonely uplands far inland, where stately plantation homes and busy steam engines would rise centuries later. Long lines of pelicans flew clanging out to sea to fish, the droning of night insects faded away, and a thousand tropical birds burst into jubilant, ringing song. A thin blue mist drifted up from the shore toward the inland downs, then vanished, leaving the rolling grasslands shimmering in the bright tropical glare. The cool land breeze that had blown out to sea all night died away, leaving the surface of the water glass-smooth, and the first full day in the West Indies began.
The sick men were lowered over the side in the longboats, carried ashore load after load to stretch out in the soft shade of the palm trees. Within half an hour, almost the entire crew was scattered across the beach, save for a dozen volunteers who’d agreed to stand watch on the ship until noon.
The first, instinctive cry of every man was for fruit. The lame, scurvy-ridden men dragged themselves across the sand, plucking greedily at the tiny purple grapes of the creeping shore vine, staining their mouths bright red and blistering their lips on the sharp spines of prickly pears, ignoring all Yeo’s warnings and pleas to be careful. A group of able-bodied young men set to work hewing down coconut palms to get at the fresh nuts, blunting their hatchets on the hard bark for almost no reward, until Yeo and Drew gathered half a dozen sensible men, struck off inland, and returned an hour later heaped high with the riches of that untouched wild orchard: tangy junipa apples, soft sweet guavas, and hundreds of plump golden pineapples, the queen of all fruits, growing thick across the sunbaked ledges of the low tufa cliffs.
They all sat down on the warm sandy turf to feast, swatting away galliwasps and jack Spaniard wasps and every other stinging insect that buzzed around them, while old blue land crabs huddled in the mouths of their holes shaking their claws in defiance at the invaders, and solemn herons stood stock still in the shallow surf, heads tilted to one side, wondering when was the last time they’d seen featherless bipeds break the quiet solitude of their island.
Frank wandered up and down the shore silent, his face bright with wonder rather than heavy with his usual quiet grief, while big Amyas trundled after him, his mouth full of junipa apple, playing the part of tour guide with a patronizing, easy air, as if he’d seen all these wonders before and was far too seasoned a sailor to be surprised by any of them.
“Everything is new, completely new,” Frank murmured, half to himself. “Oh, what an awe-striking feeling. Every single thing around us is changed, down to the tiniest fly and the smallest flower—and yet we are still the same, unchanged, forever.”
Amyas, who found such philosophical utterances completely unfathomable, just pointed and blurted out: “Look there, Frank! That’s a colibri. You’ve heard tell of colibris, right?”
Frank looked at the tiny living gem of a bird, which hung humming loud in the air above a bright exotic bloom, before darting away to call its mate, whirring and dancing in circles around the flower-dusted bushes, flashing new rainbows with every shift of the sunlight.
He watched it for a long solemn moment, then breathed: “If even this small created thing is so fair, how magnificent must be the shaping power of Nature? Oh my God, how glorious must Your true heavenly world be, if even the phantoms of this earth are so beautiful.”
“Phantoms?” Amyas said, frowning a little uneasily. “That’s no ghost, Frank. That’s a jolly little honey-sucker, with a tiny wife and babies no bigger than peas, greedy little rascals, I’ll warrant you.”
“Not phantoms in the sense you mean, good friend,” Frank smiled. “But phantoms to one who has learned how little value all earthly things hold.”
“I’ll tell you what, brother Frank,” Amyas said, shaking his head gently. “I know you’re far wiser than I am, but I can’t stand to see you turn your nose up at God’s good earth like that. He made every last one of these things, and maybe no man at all set eyes on them until fifty years back. But they were just as pretty then as they are now, right from the day the world was made. Why do you think God put them all here, if not to enjoy the sight of them?” He pulled his hat off his head for a second in a gesture of reverence. “If something is good enough to please the Lord, it’s certainly good enough to please you and me.”
“Your rebuke is just, dear simple-hearted old friend,” Frank said, chastened. “God forgive me, if with all my useless learning, and all my longings that have brought me no peace, I dare at moments, miserable sinner that I am, to be more fastidious than the Lord Himself. He walked in Paradise among the trees of the garden, Amyas. And so will we, and be content with whatever He sends. Why should we long for the next world, before we are even fit to live in this one?”
“And in the meanwhile,” Amyas grinned, taking another bite of fruit, “this earth is more than good enough, at least right here on Barbados.”
“Do you believe,” Frank asked, turning his thoughts to new stories, “the old Spanish tales that say you can hear sirens and tritons singing in these waters?”
“I couldn’t say for certain,” Amyas shrugged. “There’s more fish in the sea than any man ever pulled up in a net, and more wonders in the world than we ever dream of. But I never ran into any mermaids down in the South Sea, though Yeo swears he heard fair, soft music one night up in the Gulf, far from any land.”
“The Spaniards say that at certain times of the year, whole choirs of these sea nymphs gather on the waves, and sing of their watery loves with ravishing, sweet voices. It might well be true,” Frank mused. “Nature, which filled the land with rational souls, would never leave the sea completely empty of them. Especially when we remember that the ocean is the very fountain of all fertility, and its slime, as the most learned philosophers agree with old Thales of Miletus, is that primal raw material from which all things were created one by one. That is why the ancients told the wise fable that Venus, the mother of all living things—by which they meant the creative, shaping force of Nature—was born of sea foam, and rose from the deep to drift ashore on the isles of Greece.”
“I don’t know anything about this ‘plastic force’ nonsense,” Amyas laughed. “But I wish I’d been there to see that pretty poppet rise up out of the waves. The closest I ever saw to that was a group of native maidens swimming alongside our ship in the South Seas, trying to climb aboard. But Drake chased them all off, said they were a pack of naughty hussies, and I reckon he was right. Look at those butterflies over there, now! Don’t you wish you were a small boy again, not too proud to run and chase them with your cap?”
The two men wandered on together through the glorious sun-dappled tropical woods, then wandered back to the beach to find the sick men already bright and cheerful, many of whom had not been able to stir from their hammocks that very morning now pacing up and down the sand, growing stronger with every step.
“Well done, lads!” Amyas called out to them. “Keep your spirits high. We’ll bring the musicians ashore after dinner, since we’ve no mermaids to sing for us, and anyone who’s able can dance.”
Those four days slipped by in a happy haze. The men were like schoolboys on a long holiday, giving themselves over to simple, uncomplicated merriment. They did not forget their duties, though: they washed all their soiled clothing, hauled in barrels of fresh spring water, and stored up a supply of fruit that would keep for the rest of their voyage. They tramped through the woods hunting for gold that every bush seemed to promise them, until even the most stubborn gave up, no trace of the metal to be found despite their searches, just as Yeo had warned them there never was any on the island. After that, they lounged about on the sand like overgrown toddlers, collecting bright seashells and delicate sea-fans to bring home to their sweethearts back in England, smoking plump agoutis out of hollow tree trunks with shouts and laughter, and chasing every wild creature they came across until not a single land crab dared peek out of its hole, and no armadillo would unroll itself from its armour until the English ships had sailed safely out of the bay. They were the unconscious first pioneers of all the wealth, bustling commerce, beauty and scientific discovery that would, in centuries to come, turn that lovely little island into the richest gem in all the tropic seas.

*P. Henry. Why, what a rascal art thou, then, to praise him so for running! Falstaff. O' horseback, ye cuckoo! but a-foot, he will not budge a foot. P. Henry. Yes, Jack, upon instinct. Falstaff. I grant ye, upon instinct.*
— *Henry IV, Part I*

They slipped past Grenada’s southern headland under cover of dark, and at last crossed the threshold of that fabled ring of islands where Nature had lavished every shade of her beauty, and man had heaped up every sin of greed and cruelty. If Barbados had glowed like something half-magical to these English newcomers, how much more did the stretch of sea they now sailed feel like a dream: calm almost year-round, untouched by the hurricanes that raged hundreds of miles north, sky and water and far green islands bleeding into one soft, endless rainbow. The hard-bitten veteran sailors scarcely noticed the grandeur, their heads full only of Spanish gold and pearl harvests; even Amyas, long used to tropical skies, was busy calculating how he might root out every last Spaniard and claim the whole of the West Indies for Queen Elizabeth. But for all their unpoetic pragmatism, none of the men could look on these famous, barely-touched lands without a jolt of awe and fire: they were sailing waters scarcely ever ploughed by an English keel, no more than four or five other English voyages having ventured here before. The penalty for failure here was not just death, but the slow, searing torments of the Inquisition. Aboard the *Pelican* and *Minion*, old mariners who had sailed with John Hawkins himself were badgered all day for tales of every hidden cove, every strange fish and bright-plumed bird, while Frank stood apart, quiet and serious, a look of wonder softening his face.

That wonder clung to him like a veil, not sad, but luminous: he seemed to drink in the glow of the landscape around him, murmuring to himself now and then that this was the very gate of heaven, too caught up in watching to think of food or rest. Every new league the ship sailed unveiled another fantastical sight: islands and headlands hung suspended in mid-air, their inverted reflections glowing in the glassy water beneath, long sand dunes rolling and warping in the heat haze. Bright yellow flower beds and gnarled giant cacti like candelabra twisted and flicker on the shore slopes, the whole scene shifting into a blazing phantom world, the sun itself warping into strange oval and pear shapes as it sank through banks of crimson mist.

While Frank marveled, Yeo the old sailor rejoiced. Far to the south of the setting sun, a line of tall blue peaks jutted from the water: by his reckoning, they were the mountains of Macanao, at the western tip of Margarita — the Isle of Pearls, whose name was famous across every Mediterranean city and every great trade fair in Germany, second only in richness to the Panamanian pearl isle where John Oxenham had met his death fifteen years prior.

By the next dawn, they were cruising along Margarita’s north shore, having slipped unseen past the Spanish fort built on the island’s eastern tip to guard the pearl fisheries. At last they rounded a headland to find a deep, quiet cove, thick with trees right down to the waterline, and a single Spanish caravel riding at anchor, three small boats bobbing beside her. In an instant every man of the crew was on deck, shouting out his own plan of attack. Some wanted to sail straight into the cove, the fresh afternoon trade wind blowing fair toward the shore, but after spotting jagged reefs breaking the water at the bay mouth, they agreed it was safer to slip past out of sight of the caravel, then send in the pinnace and the longboat to board her. Yeo suggested they run up Spanish colors to avoid alarming the crew, but Amyas refused flat. “I count it a mean, cowardly trick to tell such a lie unless we’re in mortal peril, or fighting for the crown itself,” he said.

They held their course till the next headland hid them from the caravel’s view, then launched their boats: Will Cary took the larger longboat with twenty men, Amyas the smaller pinnace with fifteen, including John Brimblecombe — who insisted on coming along in his clerical cassock and bands, armed with his great-uncle’s old broadsword that he treasured above all his possessions.

When they reached the cove mouth, they found exactly the reefs they had feared, stretching for acres under the clear turquoise water, forcing them to skirt the edge for half an hour hunting for a narrow channel. The new men to the Indies leaned over the gunwales, gawking at the living coral flower beds and sunlit underwater gardens of the sea gods, when a dark school of sharks came gliding up below them, some as long as the boat itself, their wicked slitted eyes staring up at the men hungrily.

“Jack,” Amyas teased, sitting right next to him, “look how that great brute is eyeing you. He’s got a taste for that plump hide of yours, thinks you’ll eat far sweeter than any suckling pig.”

Jack’s face blanched white, but he said nothing. Just then, the very shark Amyas had pointed to lunged up at a parrotfish darting out of a coral crevice, two more right behind him. The poor fish had nowhere to run, so it leaped straight out of the water, nearly landing in the boat, while three great rough brown shark snouts clashed together less than two yards from where Jack sat, baring their rows of saw-sharp teeth before sinking back down to glower for new bait. Jack whispered so quiet no one but Amyas could hear, *“Into thy hands, O Lord,”* and turned his eyes firmly back to the boat’s interior, no desire left to look at sharks ever again.

Through the channel they rowed, the caravel now less than musket-shot away. Cary laid her alongside before the Spanish crew could even reach their guns, standing in the stern sheets to shout for them to yield. The captain called boldly back, demanding to know in whose name they made the attack. “In the name of common sense, you dogs!” Will laughed. “Don’t you see you’re fifty men to our twenty?” He scrambled over the gunwale before they could answer, the captain firing a pistol straight at him. Cary knocked him unconscious rather than kill him, and the rest of the crew surrendered at once, some falling to their knees, others leaping overboard to swim for shore.

While that was happening, Amyas had pulled round to the caravel’s stern to board the second of the small boats, whose crew surrendered without a blow, screaming *“Mercy!”* while their enslaved oarsmen dived overboard and swam for shore like sea-dogs. The third boat, no more than an oar’s length away, turned to flee at once. That was when the great farce befell Jack Brimblecombe: desperate to prove his courage, he grabbed a boat-hook, clawed it hard into the stern of the fleeing Spanish boat, and yelled, “Stop, you Papists! Stop, you Spanish dogs!” — and of course, with ten Spaniards yanking the other way, he was hauled right over the gunwale, landing flat on his face in the sea, the hook still stuck fast in the boat’s stern.

Panic seized him at once, his imagination filling the whole water with the sharks he had just seen. He roared like a raging town bull, and so flustered he never thought to turn back for his own boat, but struck out swimming furiously after the Spanish craft, his cassock billowing out behind him like the fins of some great black monk-fish, howling and spitting salt water: “Stop, you Spanish dogs! Help, all good fellows! Don’t you see I’m a dead man? They’re nibbling at my toes already! One of them has my leg! My right thigh is bitten clean off! Oh, that I was back preaching in Hartland pulpit! Stop, you Papist cowards, yield before I mince you to bits, and pull me aboard! Yield, I say, or my blood is on your heads! I’m no Jonah — if he swallows me, he’ll never spit me back up! It’s far better to fall into the hands of men than devils with three rows of teeth apiece. Into thy hands, O Lord — pray for my soul —”

He ranted on, more frantic than Panurge at his most seasick, till the English crew, convinced he would be snapped up by a shark or brained by a Spanish oar any second, fired a volley over the heads of the fleeing boat. Every Spaniard in the boat leaped overboard at once, and Jack scrambled over the empty craft’s gunwale, drew his sword with one hand while wiping salt water from his eyes with the other, and laid about him like a raging lion, slashing empty air as he yelled for the idolaters to surrender. When he finally caught his breath and realized he was the only man on board, he plopped down panting in the stern sheets and started yanking off his hose. Amyas, half-convinced the poor fellow had gone mad from sunstroke, pulled alongside to ask what on earth he was doing. Jack swore through puffed, salt-stung lips that his right thigh had been bitten clean through to the bone, that his hose was full of blood, and would have fainted dead away from the imaginary injury had not his friends argued with him for five minutes till he agreed he was completely unharmed.

They set to ransacking their first prize then, finding the caravel loaded with hides and salt pork — but that was not all. Tucked in the captain’s cabin, and stowed in the stern of the little boat Jack had captured, they found neatly wrapped baskets of palm leaves, stuffed full of lustrous, slightly browned pearls. The Spaniards had damaged their shine, rushing to pry the oysters open over fire rather than letting them rot slowly in the old Arab way to preserve their luster, but their value was still immense. A mischievous hand set fire to the caravel before they left, and its load of smoldering hides filled the whole cove with a thick, reeking stench.

Amyas was furious at the senseless waste, pointing out that Drake himself would never have done such a thing — but Cary had a jest ready. “Ah, but we’re ‘Lutheran devils’ to these Spaniards, aren’t we? Of course we have to vanish like any other fiend, leaving an evil stink behind us.”

As soon as they were back aboard their own ship, Amyas took Jack aside and told him he’d do better to fight bravely and save his breath, rather than roar before he was hurt, if he ever wanted the crew to take his sermons seriously. Frank, overhearing, said he would deal with Jack himself, and led him off to tease him gently.

“Come here, recreant Jack, lily-livered Jack, hysterical Jack. You’ve read Plato’s dialogues and Aristotle’s logic, haven’t you?”

Jack meekly said yes.

“Then you must agree the greater must contain the less?”
Jack nodded.
“And that which is more than a part must contain that part?”
Jack agreed again.
“Then tell me — is a priest not greater than a layman?”

Jack, who was always very loud about the dignity of the priesthood, puffed up and said “Of course, it is.”
“Then a priest contains a man, and is a man, plus something extra: his holy office. Which means if a priest proves he is no man, he proves he is no priest either, doesn’t he?”

Jack crossed his arms. “You may be right by logic, Frank, but sharks don’t care a fig for logic, and they sure don’t understand it.”

Frank laughed, and prodded him further till Jack admitted he had no defense for howling like a stuck pig at the sight of a non-existent bite, then teased him that he was a Manichee heretic for calling sharks evil fiends’ spawn, when God had made them to eat useless dead carcasses like his own. As a brother of their secret Rose fellowship, Frank declared, he was bound to face danger joyfully — so for his cowardice, they stripped him of his chaplain post for the brotherhood, and warned him that unless he performed a deed worthy of the Seven Champions of Christendom within seven days, he would be forbidden to carry a sword or dagger ever again, allowed no more iron than the pen nib he used for writing his sermons.

Amyas piled on, telling Jack it was no wonder young men laughed off the gospel when their preachers were soft, cowardly lads who were parsons before they were men, that a priest of the Church of England was meant to make good men first, before he made good Christians, and that his one minute of screaming fear had undone every good word he had ever preached. They badgered him from both sides till Jack clapped his hands over his ears and ran away, and Frank laughed so hard his usual quiet, serious face lit up like a boy’s.

The next day was Sunday. They barely convinced Jack to lead divine service, he was so shamefaced, and he flatly refused to give a sermon. After the service, Amyas read out the ship’s shared articles of agreement, then spotted a sloping beach nearby with a clear stream running down to the sea, and announced they would land to wash their clothes and refill their water casks, having found fresh water scarce at Barbados. Jack insisted on coming along, strapped with his old sword and a heavy arquebuse, saying he’d dreamed the previous night Spaniards had ambushed him, and he was sure the dream would come true. He added he’d almost rather die than be the crew’s laughing stock forever, badgered with Plato’s “devilish dialectic” and called a heretic at every turn.

They landed without trouble, washed their clothes, and cast their seine net in the shallows, catching all manner of strange bright fish, plus a manati fully seven feet long, its back crusted with barnacles like a piece of driftwood. The old chronicler noted the manati was a gentle, almost pious creature, that would watch over a corpse in the water for days till it decayed, carrying its young tucked under its flipper to suckle like a human mother, crying out with a human wail when wounded. Frank swore he heard the far-off, melodious song of the sirens while they were anchored there, which the rest of the crew joked was just the manati’s cry. One of the men shot two great black curassow birds with a crossbow, bigger than eagles, and that evening they feasted on roasted and boiled manati meat, tender and sweet as four-month-old veal.

They were hauling their full water casks back to the boat when they heard a shout: the Spaniards were on them. Fifty armed arquebusiers burst out of the treeline less than twenty yards from where Jack had sat down alone, half hidden under a great tree, reading his Bible, sword girt at his waist and arquebuse at his side. Jack calmly slipped a leaf into his Bible to mark his page, set it down on the roots, grabbed his arquebuse, and ran straight at the mounted Spanish captain at the head of the troop. He shot the man dead through the chest, hurled his arquebuse at the nearest soldier’s head, then drew his sword and laid about him like the mythic giant Colbrand, cutting through the ranks of arquebusiers with such wild, ferocious strokes that the Spaniards were convinced he was the devil himself, or Martin Luther risen from the dead to plague them. They fired half a dozen shots at him at once, but were so panicked they only grazed his thigh, a single shallow gash that left him limping for weeks. He kept advancing, sword flashing, while the rest of the Englishmen rallied behind Amyas and charged. The Spaniards turned and fled into the woods as fast as they had come, Cary laughing that “the dogs got such a taste of the parson, they didn’t dare wait for the rest of the congregation.”

Jack never stopped running till his foot caught on a root, and he pitched headlong to the ground, knocking all the breath out of his body. When he came to, he blinked up at Amyas and yelled “Glory be to God! How many did I kill? How many?”

“Nineteen at least,” Cary joked, “seven of them felled by a single backhand stroke.” He pointed to the dead Spanish captain at Jack’s feet, two dead arquebusiers, and three more wounded Spaniards limping away into the trees.

“There!” Jack panted, wiping sweat from his brow. “Will you laugh at me now, Will Cary, or say I can’t fight just because I’m a parson?” Cary grabbed his hand and apologized for all his earlier teasing, saying Jack had been the bravest man on the shore that day. Jack laughed, and reminded him of the old prank at Bideford School when Cary had stuffed great noisy drone beetles into his school desk. They pulled back to the ship thanking God for their narrow escape, Jack growing faint from the slow bleeding of his real thigh wound, the very wound he had screamed about the day before as imaginary. That evening, when Frank sat by his bunk to ask what had made him so suddenly brave, Jack just grinned, and said he had remembered the teasing about no man being no priest — and he’d be damned if he’d let a crew of Spaniards prove his friends right.

“Great was the crying, the running and riding,
Which at that season was made in the place;
The beacons were fired, as need then required,
To save their great treasure they had little space.”

The crew would gladly have lingered a little longer around the Margarita and Cubagua islets, hunting for another prize of pearl beds. But Amyas, as he phrased it, had already “fleshed his dogs” on their successful raids, and was loath to linger in the islands now that their presence had been sounded as an alarm. So they set their course southwest, crossing the wide mouth of the great bay that stretches from the Paria Peninsula all the way to Cape Codera, leaving the isle of Tortuga on their starboard hand, and the low, grassy Piritoos islands to port: two long green ridges that rose barely inches above the still, tideless Caribbean swell.

Yeo and Drew knew every fathom of these waters, and had good reason to: they had been the very first English mariners to sail this coast, decades earlier, trading alongside John Hawkins. Now, dead ahead, the mighty bulk of the Caracas Mountains rose sheer out of the sea, peak after peak climbing higher and higher, dwarfing every hill the rest of the crew had ever laid eyes on. Frank, of course, had traversed the Alps, and Amyas had seen the Andes; but Will Cary’s frame of reference for mountain height stopped at McGillicuddy’s Reeks back in Ireland, and Jack Brimblecombe’s at the rolling tops of Exmoor. To Cary’s endless amusement, the gentle parson spent a full day clinging to the rigging, chin tilted higher than his nose, staring up at the heights until he worked himself a stiff neck.

Soon the sea turned choppy and rough, even with the soft, fair breeze at their backs, and before they pulled abreast of Cape Codera, they ran headlong into that fierce eastward current that foils westbound mariners all through the winter months. All night long they fought through the billows, the thousand-foot sheer wall of Cape Codera hanging over their port side, and beyond it, range after range of mountain glowing pale in the yellow moonlight.

When dawn broke, they spotted a large Spanish ship that had passed them on an opposite course during the dark, now a full ten miles to the east. Yeo was all for turning back at once to take her: he said the task was simple enough, for the offshore gale blew as a “soldier’s wind,” fair for going out and fair for coming back. But Amyas and Frank both refused.

“Why Yeo, you said one more day’s sailing would bring us straight to La Guayra,” Amyas protested.

“All the more reason to do the Lord’s work thoroughly, when He has brought us safely so far on our journey,” Yeo insisted.

“That ship will sail on harmless enough, no loss to us,” Frank said.

“Ah sirs, sirs, she was delivered straight into your hands, and you will have to answer for letting her slip,” Yeo sighed.

“Good Yeo,” Frank said gently, “I trust we will give a good account of many a tall Spanish vessel before we return home. But you know full well that La Guayra, and the safety of the lady we believe dwells there, was the very first purpose of this whole voyage.”

Yeo shook his head sadly. “Ah sirs, it was a lady that brought Captain Oxenham to ruin.”

“You dare not compare her to our Rose?” Frank and Cary snapped in unison.

“God forbid, gentlemen! But no adventure prospers unless the eye be single to the Lord’s work — and that work, as I see it, is to cripple the power of Spain, and exalt our Queen Elizabeth. I had thought nothing was dearer to Captain Leigh’s heart.”

Amyas stood wavering, torn. His loyalty to the Queen bade him turn back and seize the Spanish prize. His vow to the woman he had promised to rescue bade him sail on to La Guayra. The dilemma seemed far from far-fetched to him, it felt sharp and practical.

In the end Frank’s counsel won, and they held course for La Guayra. Amyas half-hoped the Spanish ship would spot them and give chase, but the course they set eastward that day changed the whole shape of the story, for better and worse.

Around midday, the first canoe they had seen since leaving the islands staggered toward them, rigged with a huge three-cornered sail. As it drew close, they spotted two tall Indians on board.

“See, metal floats in these seas!” Cary laughed. “There’s a new marvel for you, Frank.”

“Explain yourself,” Frank said, half-ready to believe any wonder after all he had seen on the voyage.

“Why else would those two bronze statues dare sail the open ocean in such a cockleshell, eh? Did I catch you out there, master courtier?”

“I’m long past being caught out by tricks, Will. But look how noble those men are! How fearlessly they’re coming alongside! Can they know we are English, the avengers of the wronged Indian peoples?”

“I suspect they just think we’re Spaniards, and are coming to sell us their coconuts. Look, the canoe is piled high with fruit and vegetables.”

“Call out to them, Yeo!” Amyas ordered. “You speak the best Spanish, I need to talk to one of them.”

Yeo hailed them, and the canoe pulled right alongside without hesitation, the men lowering their felucca sail as a tall, splendid Indian scrambled up the ship’s side as nimbly as a cat.

He stood a full six feet high, bearing himself as boldly and gracefully as Frank or Amyas themselves. For a moment he smiled, showing bright white teeth, but the next instant his face darkened. He sprang for the gunwale and shouted to his comrade in Spanish, “Treachery! These are no Spaniards!” and would have leaped straight overboard if a dozen strong sailors hadn’t grabbed him before he could make the jump.

It took some effort to subdue him, for he was powerfully built, his naked slippery limbs hard to get a grip on. Amyas called out alternately to his men not to harm the Indian, and to the Indian to be still, promising no hurt would come to him. After five minutes of chaotic struggle, the stranger relented, sullen but compliant.

“Don’t bind him,” Amyas said. “Let him loose, make a clear ring around him. Here, my man, take this dollar.”

The Indian’s eyes glinted, and he took the coin.

“All I ask of you is to tell me what ships are anchored in La Guayra, then sail with us there and point out the governor’s house and the customs house.”

The Indian laid the dollar down on the deck, crossed himself, and looked Amyas straight in the face. “No, señor. I am a free man and a cavalier, a Christian Guayqueria. My forefathers were the first of all Indians to swear fealty to the King of Spain, and to this day he calls us in all his proclamations his most faithful, loyal, and noble Guayquerias. God forbid that I should reveal anything to his enemies, who are my enemies too.”

A low growl rose from the sailors who understood his words, more than one hinting that a cord twisted tight around his head, or a slow match held between his fingers, would drag the information out of him fast enough.

“God forbid you should harm him!” Amyas said. “This is a brave, loyal man, and I will treat him as such. Tell me, my brave fellow, how do you know we are enemies of His Catholic Majesty?”

The Indian smiled shrewdly, pointing to half a dozen small details of their rigging, saying to each, “Not Spanish.”

“Well then, what of that?”

“Only Spaniards and free Guayquerias have the right to sail these seas.”

Amyas laughed. “You are a right valiant bit of copper. Pick up your dollar, and go your way in peace. Make room for him, men. We can find what we need without his help.”

The Indian paused, incredulous and astonished. “Over the side with you!” Amyas laughed. “Don’t you know when you are well off?”

“Most illustrious señor,” the Indian began, in the slow, sententious drawl of his people, “I was lied to. I heard that you heretics roasted and ate all true Catholics — as we Guayquerias are — and that all your padres had tails.”

“Plague take you, sirrah!” Jack Brimblecombe squeaked, turning around. “Do I have a tail? Look here!”

“Quien sabe? Who knows?” the Indian huffed through his nose.

“How do you know we are heretics?” Amyas asked.

“Hmph. But to repay your kindness, illustrious señor, I will warn you: do not sail on to La Guayra. There are ships of war there waiting for you. What is more, the governor Don Guzman sailed east only yesterday to hunt for you. I marvel that you did not cross paths with him.”

“To look for us? He was on the watch for us?” Cary said. “Impossible! Lies! Amyas, this rascal is just trying to frighten us away.”

“Don Guzman sailed out yesterday to hunt for us? Are you sure you speak the truth?” Amyas demanded.

“As I live, señor. He and one other ship, which I mistook at first for yours.”

Amyas stamped his foot on the deck. That was the ship they had spotted at dawn, ten miles to the east! “Fool that I was! I was close upon my enemy, and let the chance slip! If I had but done my duty, all would have gone right!”

But it was too late to grieve over the mistake, and for all they knew the Indian’s story might well be a lie. “Off with you!” Amyas said. The Indian bounded over the gunwale back into his canoe, leaving the whole crew marveling at the dignity and courtesy of this bold sea-cavalier.

So they held course westward, sailing beneath that mighty northern cliff, the highest sheer face on earth, seven thousand feet of bare rock separated from the sea only by a narrow strip of bright green lowland. Patches of sugar cane and clusters of coconut palms dotted the shoreline, reminding them they were in the tropics, but above, all was savage, rough, and bare as an Alpine precipice. Deep clefts in the rock let the southern sun pour golden light down to the water’s edge, offering glimpses far overhead of strange, stately trees lining the glens, and a veil of perpetual mist shrouding the innermost mountain summits. Soft white clouds hung motionless between the ship and the mountain side in the burning tropical air, amplifying the overwhelming sense of vast, solemn stillness.

“Three thousand feet above our heads, up in those mountains,” Drew the master told them, “lies Saint Yago de Leon, the great city the Spaniards founded fifteen years back.”

“Is it a rich place?” Cary asked.

“Very, so they say.”

“Is it a strong place?” Amyas asked.

“No forts at all, by all accounts. The Spaniards boast that Heaven has already built such perfect walls around it that no man need add any.”

“I wonder,” Amyas said. “Lads, do you think you could climb those hills?”

“A sight higher than Harty Point, sir, but it depends what’s waiting for us on the other side.”

Then they rounded the final headland, and the spot they had sailed four thousand miles across the ocean to find came full into view. A low black cliff topped with a stone wall, a battery at either end. Inside, a few narrow streets of white houses running parallel to the sea, squeezed onto a strip of flat land barely two hundred yards wide, and behind it all, the towering mountain wall casting the whole settlement in deep shadow. It seemed impossible that anyone could climb that cliff to reach the inland country, but Drew, who had sailed off this coast before, pointed out a narrow path winding up through a glen that looked nearly sheer perpendicular. That was the only road to the capital, if any man dared attempt it. Even under the mountain’s shadow, the whole town looked dusty and blindingly bright, the breaths of air blowing off the land stiflingly hot — no wonder, for La Guayra, baked by the heat radiating off that vast wall of sun-scorched rock, is one of the hottest spots on the face of the earth.

Where was the harbor? There was none. Nothing but an open roadstead, where five vessels tossed at anchor. The two outermost were small merchant caravels. Behind them lay two long, low, ugly craft, at the sight of which Yeo let out a long, low whistle.

“Galleys, as I am a sinful saint! And what’s that big one behind them, Robert Drew? I see more than just hawseholes in that idolatrous black hull of hers.”

“We’ll get a clear view of her stern past the galleys in another minute,” Amyas said. “Look sharp, Cary, your eyes are sharper than mine.”

“Six round gun ports on her main deck,” Cary reported.

“And I can see the brass cannons glinting on her poop deck,” Amyas said grimly. “Will, we’re in for it.”

“In for it we are, captain. Farewell, farewell, my parents dear, I’ll never see you more, I fear,” Cary laughed. “But let’s go in all the same, and pound the Don’s ribs, my old comrade from Smerwick. Eh? Three to one is very fair odds.”

“Not under those fort’s guns, if you please sir,” Yeo protested. “If the Philistines will only come out to meet us on the open sea, we will make an end of them like Zeba and Zalmunna.”

“True enough,” Amyas agreed. “Game cocks are game cocks, but reason is still reason.”

“The Philistines aren’t coming out, but they’re sending a messenger instead,” Cary yelled. “Watch out, all you thick skulls!”

As he spoke, a puff of white smoke rolled from the eastern fort, and a heavy cannon ball plunged into the water between the battery and their ship.

“I don’t like that at all,” Amyas frowned. “What do they mean by firing on us without warning? And what are these warships doing here? Drew, you told me the Spanish armadas never anchored in this roadstead.”

“Nor do they, as far as I know, sir, the anchorage is far too rough, as you can see. I’m mortal afraid that rascal Indian’s story was true, and the Dons have got wind of our coming.”

“Run up a white flag, at any rate. If they were expecting us, they must have known long enough ago, otherwise how would they have gotten all these ships here?”

“True, sir. They must have sailed all the way from Santa Marta at the very least, maybe even from Cartagena. That would take a full month, going and coming.”

Amyas suddenly remembered Eustace’s threat back in that country inn on their way to the Devon coast. Could he have betrayed their whole purpose? Impossible!

“Let us call a council of war, Frank,” he said.

But Frank was paying no attention, completely absorbed in a different sight. Half a mile east of the town, two or three hundred feet up the steep mountain side, stood a large, low white house half-buried in trees and gardens. There was no other building of similar size anywhere nearby, no space for one. And wasn’t that the royal flag of Spain flapping in the breeze before it? That must be the governor’s house! That must be the home of the Rose of Torridge! Frank stared at it wild-eyed, until he had convinced himself he could make out the figure of a woman walking on the front terrace, a figure that could be none other than the one he had traveled so far to find. Amyas had to practically drag him away to join the war council, a gloomy, barely civil gathering on the ship’s poop deck.

The three leaders, with Brimblecombe, Yeo, and Drew, drew apart, each staring the other in the face. Their months of careful planning and bright hopes had been dashed to nothing in a single hour.

“It’s impossible, you see,” Amyas said at last, “to surprise the town from the landward side while these warships are here. If we land our men, we leave our ship completely undefended.”

“And just as impossible to challenge Don Guzman to a fight when he’s not even here,” Cary added.

“I wonder why the warships haven’t already opened fire on us,” Drew said.

“Maybe they respect our flag of truce,” Cary said. “Why not send a small boat in to parley with them, and ask after —”

“After her?” Frank cut him off. “If we let them see we know she is here, her name will be forever tarnished in the eyes of those jealous, suspicious Spaniards.”

“And as for them respecting our flag of truce, gentlemen,” Yeo said, “take the advice of an old man: do not trust them. They will keep the same faith with us as they kept with Captain Hawkins at San Juan d’Ulloa, that accursed business that was the start of all these wars. We could have seized the entire plate fleet, two hundred thousand pounds worth of gold on board, and we did no such thing. We only asked leave to trade like honest men. And after they granted us that license, tricked us with fair speeches into landing ourselves and all our ordnance, the governor and the whole fleet turned on us, five to one, and gave no quarter to a single soul they captured. No sirs, the only reason they aren’t attacking us now is because their crews haven’t gotten back on board their ships.”

“They will soon enough, then,” Amyas said. “I can see soldiers pouring down the town’s landing stairs right now.”

Sure enough, boats piled high with armed Spanish men-at-arms were pushing off the shore, heading straight for the anchored warships.

“Thank the Lord we didn’t sail here two hours earlier,” Drew said. “The sun will set before they can get their ships ready for sea, and those fellows won’t have the stomach to chase us out on the dark ocean.”

“So much the better for us. If they stay holed up in the roadstead, we can slip away under cover of night, then circle back to the town, and try our luck. I can’t find it in my heart to sail away

The old ballad of brave Lord Willoughby hums true enough to the day:
> Full seven long hours in all men's sight This fight endured sore,
> Until our men so feeble grew, That they could fight no more.
> And then upon dead horses Full savorly they fed,
> And drank the puddle water, They could no better get.
> When they had fed so freely They kneeled on the ground,
> And gave God thanks devoutly for The favor they had found;
> Then beating up their colors, The fight they did renew;
> And turning to the Spaniards, A thousand more they slew.

When the tropical sun burst over the horizon at dawn, flooding the Caribbean with sharp, golden light, Amyas Leigh paced his deck like a caged lion. His hair hung wild and uncombed, his tunic was torn and caked with salt spray, his eyes bloodshot from a night spent alternating between roaring rage and bitter, heaving sobs for his lost brother Frank. No words can rightly sum that agony—you who have lost a kinsman you loved more than your own life will know it already, and you who have not, thank heaven for the mercy of that ignorance.
He strode back and forth, his mind teeming with wild, impossible schemes as the *Rose* heeled hard into the choppy, wind-lashed waves. He would sail straight back to that accursed villa and burn it to ash. He would storm La Guayra, put every man in the garrison to the sword to pay for Frank's life. "We can do it, lads!" he roared, turning to his crew, fist clenched. "If Drake could take Nombre de Dios, we can take La Guayra."
A roar of "Aye!" burst from every throat on deck.
"We'll take the town, and get Frank back yet," Will Cary called out, clapping Amyas on the shoulder—but Amyas shook his head. Some deep, unnameable instinct told him no port in all of New Spain could ever give back the sight of that beloved face he had watched die the night before.
"Aye, he'll have full vengeance, and no mistake. Look yonder!" He pointed out to the shoreline, where three sails bobbed not five miles to windward, between them and the far-off, snow-capped peaks of the Silla. "There are the Spanish bloodhounds we saw off Guayra yesterday, hot on our heels. Back to our stations, lads—welcome them, even if there were a dozen of the curs."
A murmur of approval rippled through the ranks. If any young hand had a moment's qualm at the thought of fighting three enemy vessels at once, it was snuffed out by the roar of older mariners and the stentorian boom of Salvation Yeo's voice.
"If there were a dozen of them, the Lord is with us still, as it is written: One of you shall chase a thousand. Clear the decks, lads, and see the glory of the Lord worked this day."
"Amen!" Cary cried, and the *Rose* was hauled even closer to the wind to meet their pursuers.

The rush of coming battle jolted Amyas out of his fog of grief. He forgot his wounds, forgot the heavy sorrow weighing on his chest; even the memory of Frank's soft, angelic last smile dimmed a little as he bustled about barking orders, and within a quarter hour his voice rang out clear, firm, and cheerful as it had been in the old days, before the loss.
"Now my masters, first we serve God, then we breakfast, and after that we clear for action."
Parson Jack Brimblecombe led the daily prayers, the special litany before a sea fight, his honest voice trembling as he slipped a line into the Prayer for All Conditions of Men, even over Amyas's despair: "And we pray especially for our dear brother Mr. Francis Leigh, held captive among the idolaters, if it be thy will to spare him." The crew rose from their knees together.
"Now to breakfast," Amyas said. "A Frenchman fights best fasting, a Dutchman drunk, an Englishman full, and a Spaniard when the devil is in him—and that's always."
"Good beef and a just cause are a match for any devil alive," Cary laughed. "Come down below, captain—you have to eat too."
Amyas shook his head, pried the tiller out of the steersman's hands, and bade the man go below to fill his belly. Cary vanished below decks, and came back five minutes later with a heaped plate of bread and salt beef and a great jack of ale, coaxing and cajoling till he had got every last bite and drop down Amyas's throat like a nurse feeding a sullen, grieving child, before he darted back out of sight, tears rolling fast down his own cheeks.
Amyas stayed at the tiller, steering. In one single night, his face had aged seven years. A terrible, iron calm had settled over him. Woe to any man that crossed his path that day.
"There are three of them, you see, my masters," he called as the rest of the crew trundled back on deck. "The big galleon up front, two galleys trailing astern of her. The big ship is a fast race-built craft, but if we can just get the wind of her, we'll see if our low, handy build isn't more than a match for her long, unwieldy length. We'll give her the slip, and take the two galleys first."
"I thank the Lord," Yeo boomed, "for giving such wise heart to so young a general—a very David and Daniel, saving your presence, lads. If any man dare not follow him, let him be counted among the cursed men of Meroz and Succoth. Amen! Silas Staveley, go knock that young monkey of a powder boy over the head! What's he doing lingering up here instead of manning the powder room door?"
Yeo set about tending his guns with the sure, steady hand of a man who knew exactly what he was doing, who meant to do it thoroughly, and who had unshakable faith he was doing God's work.
Every man set to his task. Though the *Rose* had been kept in near-perfect fighting order all through the previous night, there was still deck-clearing to do, nettings to lace to catch falling rigging, makeshift bulwarks to rig, waist cloths to string, tops to fortify, pikes to grease, yards to sling, sheets and tacks to double. It was enough preparation to satisfy even the famously pedantic Sir Richard Hawkins himself. Amyas took charge of the poop deck, Cary of the forecastle, Yeo the master gunner of the main deck, and Drew the ship's master took his post in the waist. All was ready, and more than ready, long before the leading Spanish galleon was within two miles of them.

Now, while the mastiffs of England and the bloodhounds of Spain draw ever closer across the rolling swells, both thirsting for each other's blood, let us pause a minute to consider the cause that let the English of that day face and conquer Spanish fleets twice, three times their size, boasting that through the whole of the Spanish war, only one royal queen's ship, the *Revenge*, and (if memory serves) one private man-of-war, Sir Richard Hawkins's *Dainty*, ever struck their colors to the enemy.
What was it that let Sir Richard Grenville's *Revenge*, in that last terrible fight off the Azores, hold off eight Spanish galleons for twelve hours before she struck, two of them three times her size sinking beside her, all her masts gone, repelling three boarding attempts, defying a whole fleet of fifty four ships that circled her like hounds around a dying lion? What let young Richard Hawkins's *Dainty*, half her guns useless thanks to a careless or traitorous gunner, keep up a running fight for three days against two Spanish ships her equal in size, double her firepower, ten times her number of men? What let Sir George Cary's *Content* fight single-handed from seven in the morning till eleven at night against four great galleons and two galleys, her heaviest gun a mere nine-pounder, with only thirteen men fit for duty for half the battle? What let two London Turkey merchantmen, the *Merchant Royal* and the *Tobie*, and their three small consorts cripple an entire Spanish galley fleet sent to intercept them off Pantellaria in the Mediterranean, and sail back triumphant through the Straits of Gibraltar? And what, soon after, let the English fleet shatter the Great Armada of 1588, losing nothing more than one small pinnace and one gentleman of note?
There were many reasons. First, the English ships were far shorter than the Spanish, which with the rig of the day gave them maneuverability so sharp it left the Spaniards completely bewildered. Camden himself wrote of the 1588 fight that the English ships charged the enemy with marvellous agility, fired their broadsides, and darted away into deep water again, their shots striking true every time, while the great heavy Spanish galleons lumbered too slow to escape. Second, the Spanish warships built for the West Indies were flush-decked, or "race-built" to carry more cargo, leaving every man on deck exposed, while the English raised their poop and forecastle as high as possible, added stout bulkheads and "close fight" stockades to turn their decks into a line of separate small forts, loopholed for musket fire and arrows.
But the greatest advantage of all was the men themselves. The Elizabethan sailor was every inch an amphibious jack-of-all-trades, capable of sewing a sail, carpentering a broken spar, manning a cannon, or fighting hand to hand with equal skill. The whole nation was required by law to practice archery from boyhood, to treat swordplay and quarterstaff as a normal part of a man's education, the fiercest free people on earth, every man fighting for his own skin, trusted to use his own wits to finish the job he was given. Officers did not hold themselves above the men: Drake once snapped at a group of prissy gentleman adventurers on his round the world voyage that he would not stand for any man, no matter his rank, refusing to lay a hand to a rope. Elizabeth's court promoted brave, sharp men no matter their birth, and the merchant navy was a nursery for great warriors, where every captain, high born or low, learned to fight for their lives since Spanish crews would fire on English merchant ships on sight to wipe out competition.
No wonder English mutinies were almost unheard of, while Spanish ships saw near constant unrest. The Spaniards had crippled their navy with absurd rules of rank and etiquette that would eventually freeze their whole empire to death. They split their crews into three separate, unequal classes: soldiers who never touched a line, gunners who were almost all foreign hirelings from Flanders or Germany, and mariners treated like slaves, forced to work day and night on the open decks in sun, storm, rain, with no shelter at all, forbidden to rest below. No wonder the free English seamen always beat them.

Back to the fight. The big Spanish galleon is now within two musket shots of the *Rose*, her golden royal standard flapping bright above her poop, her trumpets blaring challenge across the breeze from a dozen brazen throats. The *Rose* answers with a cheer of her own, St George's cross flying high on her poop, the arms of Leigh and Cary side by side on her foremast, above the town arms of their home port of Bideford.
"Silence the trumpets! Waits, play up 'Fortune My Foe'! God and the Queen be with us!" Amyas roared.
Up rose the beloved old Elizabethan ballad, blaring from cornet and sackbut, fife and drum. Parson Jack Brimblecombe had planted himself among the musicians on the poop, sawing away at his violin as fiercely as old Volker the minstrel of the Nibelungenlied.
"Well played, Jack! Your elbow flies like a lamb's tail!" Amyas laughed, forcing a light jest.
"It shall fly to better work than a fiddle bow soon enough, sir, if I get the chance—"
"Steady the helm!" Amyas cut in. "What's the Dago up to now?"
The Spaniard, who had been charging down full sail before the wind, was furling all her light canvas.
"He doesn't know what to make of us waiting for him so bold," the steersman said.
"Aye he does, and he means to fight, too!" another hand called. "See, he's hauling up his mainsail foot. He's trying to keep the wind of us."
"Let him try," Amyas grinned. "Keep her closer still. No one fires till I give the word. Man the starboard guns, all small arms men wait. Pass the word down to Yeo to fire high, take out their rigging."
The Spaniard's bow gun boomed once, the shot flying far wide, then two more in quick succession, while the English crew fidgeted, checking the priming on their muskets, loosening their arrows in their quivers.
"Lie down, men, and sing a psalm. I'll call you when I need you. Closer still, helmsman. We'll see if a short, handy ship can't out-sail a long lumbering one two points closer to the wind."
Amyas was right. The Spaniard wanted to cross the *Rose's* bows to rake her, but dared not, knowing the English would fire into her full length before she could complete the turn. Her only other move was to head up into the wind and wait for the *Rose* to come to her.
Amyas laughed to himself. "Hold on a little longer. There's more ways to kill a cat than choking it with cream. Drew, are your men ready?"
"Aye aye, sir!"
They closed fast, till they were within pistol shot.
"Ready about!"
The *Rose* turned like an eel, slipping onto the opposite tack directly under the Spaniard's stern. The Spaniard gaped in astonishment at the speed of the maneuver, hesitated a heartbeat, then tried to turn about too—but it was too late. Her great lumbering hull hung dead in the eye of the wind, and Amyas's bowsprit almost scraped her quarter as the *Rose* drifted slowly ten yards from her stern.
"Fire! With a will!" Amyas roared. "At them, archers! At them, musketeers!"
A storm of bar shot, chain shot, round shot and canister swept the whole length of the Spanish deck, while through the smoke musket balls and the deadly English cloth-yard arrows whistled through the air. Down went the helmsman, every man on the poop. Down went the mizzen topmast. The stern windows and quarter galleries shattered. When the smoke cleared, the gilded, ornate painting of Our Lady of Sorrows, her heart pierced by seven swords, that had hung framed on the Spaniard's stern, was smashed to splinters. The great golden Spanish standard that had flaunted over the heads of the crew a moment before now trailed in the water. The galleon, her tiller shot away and her helmsman dead, staggered helplessly for a minute, then luffed up dead into the wind.
"Well done, men of Devon!" Amyas shouted, and a roar of cheers split the sky.
"She's struck!" some man cried as the hurrahs died down.
"Not a bit of it," Amyas said. "Hold on, helmsman. Let them patch their tackle while we go settle the two galleys."
The *Rose* shot merrily away, long before the galleon could get her rigging sorted, two miles to windward, where the two galleys were sweeping down on them fast, forty oars to a side.
They were venomous craft, snout pointed like swordfish, crammed full of soldiers in their square forward forecastle, cannon muzzles grinning from portholes facing dead ahead, so they could fire constantly at any ship before them. Their long low waists were packed full of chained slaves, five or six to every oar, and the English could see the overseers pacing the central gangway with whips in their hands. The raised stern quarterdeck was crammed with more soldiers, their armor glinting in the sun. The English could hear the crack of whips, the wild, animal yells of the beaten slaves in response, the rhythmic rattle of oars, the great heave "Ha!" from the slaves with every stroke, the oaths and curses of the overseers, and a sickly, musky stench drifting over the water, like the smell of a kennel of hounds. More than one young sailor shuddered at the sight of these floating hells, whose cruelties they had heard about for years from escaped shipmates who had endured years of captivity chained to the oars.
"Must we fire on the slaves?" more than one man asked.
Amyas sighed.
"Spare them as much as you can, in God's name. But if they try to run us down, we will have to rake their decks, and God forgive us for it."
The two galleys were coming abreast, forty yards apart. Amyas knew he could not outmaneuver their oars the way he had outmaneuvered the galleon's sails. To turn and run would just trap them between the two galleys and the damaged galleon behind.
He made up his mind, as always, to play the desperate hand.
"Lay her head up into the wind, helmsman. We wait for them."
The galleys opened fire from their bow guns at musket shot range, but the choppy, rough sea threw their aim wildly off. Amyas held his fire, as usual.
The crew stood at their stations, lips compressed, not knowing what was coming next. Amyas towered motionless on the quarter deck, giving his orders calm and decisive, and the men saw he did not fear for himself, so they trusted him completely.
The Spaniards cheered for joy, seeing him wait for them—was the Englishman mad? The two galleys converged fast, planning to ram him full on, one on each bow.
They were forty yards away, one minute from impact. The *Rose's* helm went hard over, her yards creaked round, and she plunged straight for the larboard

“Follow thee? Follow thee? Wha wad na follow thee? Lang hast thou looed and trusted us fairly.”

Amyas would no doubt have fallen victim to the rampant yellow fever raging through his crew, were it not for one simple truth he laid plain to Cary: he had no spare time to fall sick, not while his men were wasting away around him. It was a motive as noble and valid as any that kept a generation of brave souls fighting through fever and frost in the Crimea: the fire of urgent duty holds back illness for a time, but it too often burns out the moment the work is done, leaving the hero to sink into the very danger he had so often outrun.

The next morning he called a council of war — though it was far more a desperate sanitary commission, for he was quite at his wits’ end. The men were panicking, on the brink of open mutiny, and Amyas first appealed to their sense: he told them there was no profit to be had in killing their captain, nor in being cut down by him, for that matter, before he retired below to consult his closest comrades.

The ship’s doctor prattled empty theories of humors, complexions, and animal spirits. Parson Jack Brimblecombe spoke of nothing but divine visitation, framing the fever as a blow from God. Cary jested his way through a very palpable despair. Old Yeo recited quiet stoic fatalism, backing his resignation up with carefully quoted scripture. Drew, the ship’s master, had nothing to offer but blunt common sense: “My business is to sail the ship, not cure calentures.”

At that, Amyas clutched at his hair as was his habit, and burst out fiery as a torch: “Doctor! A fig for your humors and complexions! Can you rearrange a man’s humors, or change the color of his skin? Can an Ethiopian shed his blackness, or a leopard his spots? Do not foist your own ignorance off on the Lord, sir. I ask you what causes this sickness, and you do not know. Jack Brimblecombe, do not speak to me of God’s visitation — this stinks far more of the devil’s work, to my mind. We are doing God’s work out here, and He will not hinder us. So down with the devil, say I. Cary, laughter killed the cat, but it will not cure a dying Christian. Yeo, when an angel comes to tell me it is the Lord’s will we all die like dogs stuck in a mangrove ditch, I will call it His will — but not one moment before. Drew, you say your place is to sail the ship? Then sail her out of this poison trap this very instant, if you can — which you cannot. The mischief is in the air, nowhere else. I felt it seep into my bones last night when I came down the river, and it stank like an open sewer. If the poison was not in the mist, why did my boat’s crew fall sick first? Answer me that.”

No one spoke.

“Then I will tell you why: that mist we rowed through rose no more than five or six feet above the river surface, and my boat’s men were right down in it, while you all on the ship stood above the fog line. And every last man who fell sick on board this morning slept out on the main deck, and every one of them was already shaking in fear of the fever. So two lessons we may draw from that: stay as high above the ground as you possibly can, and fear nothing but God, and we may yet all live.”

“But the fog rose as high as the round-tops at sunrise today,” Cary pointed out.

“I know it. But we who stood on the half-deck were far less exposed than the men sleeping below, what’s more, the evening heat draws that poison out of the ground, and when the air cools towards dawn, the venom dissipates somehow.”

Amyas could not name how it dissipated, for no man alive in that age understood the laws of tropical epidemics — that knowledge would only come after two centuries of thousands of fatal deaths across the Americas, rules even a modern schoolchild learns today. But plain common sense was on his side, and old Yeo rose to speak, nodding his white head.

“As I have said many a time, the Lord has sent us a young Daniel to judge our troubles here. I heard the Spaniards tell long ago that these fevers never rise more than a few hundred feet above the sea, that they cling only to the low mud flats.”

“Then we will go up those few hundred feet,” said Amyas.

Every man glanced at his neighbour.

“Gentlemen, you must look the devil straight in the face if you mean to strike him a mortal blow. We cannot sail the ship out of here in her current state, and if we could, we will not sail home empty-handed. Nor can we stay here to rot of fever. We will leave the ship, and march inland.”

“Inland?” The cry burst from every throat but Yeo’s.

“Up that high ground the Spaniards spoke of. Up to the mountains, build a fortified stockaded camp, and carry our sick and all our provisions there. Once we are all rested and recovered, we will march over the mountains, and take St. Jago de Leon completely by surprise.”

Cary swore a great hearty oath. “Amyas, you are the boldest devil I ever met!”

“Not a bit of it. It is the very path of prudence.”

“So it is, sir,” said Yeo, rising. “And I follow you, all the way.”

“And so do I,” squeaked Parson Jack, all eagerness.

“Then I will not be outdone by a parson,” laughed Cary. “I say aye too.”

“Mr. Drew?”

“At your service, sir, to live or die. I know nothing of building stockades, but I verily believe Sir Francis Drake would have given this exact same counsel, were he standing in your boots right now.”

“Then tell the men we march in one hour. Yeo and Drew, you win over the old Pelican hands, and the rest will follow, like sheep jumping over a hedge.”

The veteran Pelican sailors, and the freed galley slaves who had joined their crew, signed on to the plan at once, but the rest of the men gave Amyas a full hour of stormy pushback. Their biggest grievance was that they could not see more than fifty yards through the thick mangrove thicket, and could not believe the hills were anywhere near.

“The hills are no more than three miles southwest of you right now,” Amyas told them. “I marked every ridge of them when we sailed into the bay.”

“I suppose you planned all along to drag us there?” The question lit a fuse, and angry suspicions blazed up one after another — but Amyas doused them with a threat the men knew he was fully capable of carrying out.

“Fools! If I did not have the wit to look further ahead than the lot of you, where would you be right now? Are you as mad as you are reckless, to mutiny against your own captain just because he has a second plan to save your lives? Go my way, I say, or as I live, I will blow this whole ship sky high, every soul on board with it, and save you the slow torture of rotting here one inch at a time.”

The men knew Amyas never spoke an idle threat, and they agreed to march at once, their faith restored when they saw the old Drake veterans turn to the work with whole-hearted cheer.

There is no need to drag the reader through the full six hours of misery under that sweltering stewing sun: the stumbles over mangrove roots, the hacking through thorn thickets, the dragging of sick men and heavy provisions up steep slopes, the curses, the mosquito bites, the false alarms of hidden Spaniards, every misery the human flesh can suffer save cold. By sunset that evening, they had reached their goal: a flat level spot a full thousand feet above the sea, backed by an inaccessible cliff that formed the shoulder of a great mountain, protected below by steep wooded slopes, needing only a few hours of tree felling to turn it into an impregnable fortress.

Amyas settled his sick men under the arched, twisted roots of an enormous cottonwood (ceiba) tree, then made a second trip all the way back down to the ship to haul up hammocks and blankets for them. Yeo proved his worth a hundred times over: he was the pioneer who traced the small mountain brook they followed upwards, he cheered on the men who collapsed begging to stop, he trundled back down the path a dozen times to coax stragglers up the slope. Cary, bringing up the rear, bullied and cajoled the men who sat down refusing to move, drove back a handful of deserters at sword point, carried their burdens for them, and sang raucous old sea shanties to keep their spirits up at every halt. That night, overjoyed that the two men he trusted most were more than worthy of that faith, Amyas whispered a secret to them both: “Cortez burned his ships the moment he landed in Mexico. Why should not we?”

Yeo leapt to his feet, then sat back down fast, whispering back, “Do you say that, captain? That must be a thought sent straight from heaven — for it has been hanging on my own mind all day long.”

“No rush for it,” Amyas laughed. “We have to clear all the supplies off the ship first, of course.” Cary said nothing, only sat musing, for it was clear Amyas had far more grand schemes turning over in his head than he was ready to reveal.

The men were far too exhausted to work that evening, but before the sun was up the next morning, Amyas had them all hard at work fortifying their camp. The position was already almost unassailable by nature: though high cliffs rose on three sides of them, no enemy could possibly cross the vast, unbroken mountain range behind them, and even if they did, the dense forest covering the cliffs above would hide the camp completely. Any attack would have to come up the slope from below, and Amyas defended that approach quickly: he felled all the smaller trees along the crest of the slope, laying them with sharp boughs facing outwards to form an abatis that would stop any man, horse, or dog dead in their tracks after two steps.
