The larger trunks were sawn into logs, laid lengthwise and braced with stakes and packed earth, and three or four hours of hard work built a stockade no force short of artillery could breach.

When the work was done, Amyas scrambled up the thick hanging lianes into the boughs of the giant ceiba tree, sitting high in its branches to admire his handiwork. He looked out across thousands of miles of forested plains, out to the bright blue Caribbean sea glinting on the horizon, thinking in his plain, straightforward way of what he should do next.

He could not stay up there forever. He could not march down to take his revenge on La Guayra. And at first it seemed equally impossible to move on without finding out if his brother Frank was alive or dead. But should he sacrifice Brimblecombe, Cary, and all eighty of his loyal men for the sake of his own private grief? Amyas wept with rage, then wept again in earnest honest prayer, before he made his choice. There were a hundred chances to one Frank was already dead. Even if he was not, Amyas knew full well he had long since fallen into the hands of the Inquisition — no man could pull a soul out of that pit, least of all him. Crying aloud in his agony, “God help him! For I cannot!” Amyas made up his mind to move on.

Alone in his airy treetop nest, he thought for hours before he climbed back down, calm and cheerful, and pulled Cary and Yeo aside. He told them they could never refit their ship down on the swamp without half of them dying of fever before the work was done. Even if they did manage to repair her, they would almost certainly face a waiting force of Spaniards, for the captured Indian’s story had been true: they had been warned the *Rose* was coming, and Eustace had been the traitor who gave them word.

“Let us march on St. Jago then,” he said. “Sack the town, come around on La Guayra from the rear, seize a fresh seaworthy ship there, and sail for home.”

“Nay, Will,” Yeo shook his head. “If they have already fortified La Guayra, where they had very little to lose, they will have doubly fortified St. Jago, where all their treasure is stored. The town is large, new but wealthy — and besides, how do we cross these mountains without a guide?”

“Even with a guide,” Cary sighed, staring up at the vast tiers of rock and forest rising range after range for miles inland. “It is strange to see you, of all people, pouring cold water on a daring plan.”

“What if I have an even bolder plan?” Amyas grinned. “Did you ever hear of the golden city of Manoa?”

Yeo let out a grim, joyful laugh. “I have, sir. And so have every old hand from the Pelican and the *Jesus of Lubec*, you may be sure.”

Cary sat half stunned as the full wild legend of El Dorado unfolded before him, Amyas recounting everything the captured Spaniard had told him, Yeo piling on every old rumour and sailor’s tale he had collected over decades at sea. At last Cary laughed. “So that is why you wanted to burn the ship! Well, no one is waiting for me back home, one less at my family’s dinner table will not be missed. You want to play Cortez, eh?”

“We shall never need to be as harsh as Cortez,” Amyas said. “He was freeing the land from a nation of cannibal fiends, and we shall have no such tyranny to overthrow. And I trust we fear God enough never to act as cruelly as Pizarro did.”

The conversation dropped for a time, but none of the three men forgot a word of it.

They spent more than ten days in that mountain nook. Several of the sick men died, some of the fever complicated by unhealed wounds, some almost certainly bled to death by the ship’s doctor, whose eagerness to cut their veins Amyas put a stop to by force, much to the surgeon’s fury. The rest of the men recovered steadily, thanks to the herbal remedies Yeo administered. Every day they made trips down to the ship to haul up more provisions, and Yeo and the old Hawkins veterans caught raccoons, monkeys, and other small game, while the foraging parties brought back fresh tropical fruit, tender mountain cabbage from the areca palms, and the sweet refreshing milk of the cow-tree, all foraged from the land as welcome payment for their quiet stay.

A constant watch was kept from the branches of the giant ceiba tree. It was a wonder of the tropics: the hugest English oak would have looked like a stunted little bush beside it. Borne up on twisted, wall-like buttress roots a full twelve feet high, wide enough to house the entire crew, all their ammunition and all their supplies comfortably between them, the trunk itself was forty feet around, smooth as a lighthouse for a hundred feet, crowned with boughs each as large as a full-grown oak, whose topmost twigs rose two hundred and fifty feet above the ground. Smooth, knotless lianes hung straight from the very top of the tree all the way down to the ground, perfect natural ropes for the sailors to climb, and once up in the branches, they were in an entire new world, suspended between heaven and earth. Every limb was draped with hanging cacti, blazing orchids, and wild air pines, half the tree heavy with rich green foliage, the other half completely leafless but covered in clusters of brilliant golden yellow flowers around which hummingbirds whirred all day long. Parrots darted in and out of every cranny, iridescent lizards basked on the bark like living gems, butterflies of every imaginable size and color drifted over the highest twigs, and when the sun went down, tree toads came out to croak and snore all the way till dawn. More life thrived on that one single tree than in a whole square mile of English farmland.

One noon, as Amyas stood his watch in the branches, he spotted a large Spanish ship slipping slowly along the coast westward, under easy sail — he was certain it was the very vessel they had passed on their original voyage. She hove to close to the shore, and soon two boats pulled in from her and vanished behind the mangrove line.

Amyas slid down a liane to alert his men, who cheered wildly at the news, tired of weeks of inactivity. They dragged up four brass swivel guns, fixed them solidly in log mounts to command the only path up the slope, musketeers and archers clustered around them with their weapons loaded, and half a dozen volunteer marksmen climbed up into the ceiba tree with their arquebuses, certain they would have perfect shooting from that high perch. Prayers were said, dinner was eaten, and two long hours dragged by before any sign of the Spaniards appeared.

At last a curl of white smoke puffed up from the swamp, followed by the crack of a caliver. Then, to the Englishmen’s growls of rage, the Spanish royal standard floated up above the mangrove treetops, flying from the masthead of their own captured ship, the Rose. They were signalling their ship on the bay to send more men inland, and soon a third boat pushed off from the vessel and vanished into the forest.

Another hour of waiting passed, the men growing loud and restless, until Amyas had to warn them to keep their voices down, lest they give their hidden position away. He exacted a promise from every last man to stay perfectly silent until he gave the order to fire.

Hardly had he finished speaking when the glint of a Spanish soldier’s helmet peeked out from the path below, then another, and another.

“Fools,” Amyas whispered to Cary. “They are coming up single file, rushing straight to their own deaths. Lie close, men.”

The path was so narrow two men could barely walk abreast, and so steep the Spaniards stumbled and struggled upwards with great effort. The men hung back, clearly unwilling to advance, until Amyas heard a sharp, authoritative voice at the rear, and a man in full armour stepped out to the front, sword in hand, at the sight of whom both Amyas and Cary started.

“Is it he?” Cary breathed.

“I would know those legs in a thousand suits of armour,” Amyas whispered back. “Cary, this one is mine

This chapter may bear a weight too heavy for some hearts to bear, so I will spin it as short as my pen may move, yet it cannot be omitted. No reader may judge fairly the grim, fire-forged foes that English seafarers faced in those rough days without seeing them unmasked, at their cruelest work.

Three weeks have slipped by since we last stood with our company on the open main, and the scene shifts: we are deep in the stony, fetid corridors of Cartagena’s fortress prison, lined end to end with low, narrow cells. One cell door stands ajar, and within stand two cloaked figures, one of whom we have met before: Eustace Leigh. The other is a lay familiar of the Holy Office, the hood of his robe pulled low to shadow his face, an iron lamp held tight in one hand. Its guttering yellow light spills across a pile of scratchy straw on the cell floor, and onto the man sleeping atop it. That high, pale brow, those fine, sharp, delicate features—we know that face too. It is Frank.

The savage rage of the enslaved Afro-Caribbean raiders left him half-dead, only to spare him for a far more refined cruelty at the hands of men who called themselves civilized, who called themselves Christian. He had been dragged to the torture rack only that afternoon, and now his own blood cousin, his betrayer, has come to his cell. Is Eustace here to persuade him? To trap him? Even he can scarce tell which urge tugs the harder at his fraying conscience.

He would lay down his own life this instant to save Frank, if it would serve. His life? He long ago ceased to value it. He did all he has done out of what he tells himself is sacred duty, and now he has been bidden to do one last act of that duty: wake the sleeping man, argue, coax, threaten him into recanting his Protestant faith while “his heart is still soft and raw from the torment,” as his Inquisitor masters phrased their order.

But Frank sleeps so peacefully. Is it only a trick of the wavering lamplight, or is there a faint, soft smile curved on his lips? Eustace takes the lamp from the familiar’s hand, leans in closer to see, and as he does, he catches Frank’s half-muttered dream speech: first his mother’s name, then a name far higher, far holier, breathed in a quiet, trusting whisper. Eustace cannot find it in his heart to wake him.
“Let him rest,” he whispers hoarsely to his companion. “I fear my words will do no good here in any case.”
“Nor do I hold out much hope, senor,” the familiar replies. “Never did I see a more stubborn heretic. He did not even flinch from scoffing openly at the Holy Father and our most reverend inquisitors.”
“Ah,” Eustace breathes, crossing himself automatically. “Great is the depravity of the human heart, and great is the power of Satan over souls that stray! Let us withdraw for now.”

Eustace turns to leave, but a cold curiosity pricks him, and he asks, “Where is she?”
“Which of the two sorceresses, the old one or the young?”
“The young one—the—”
“The Senora de Soto.” The familiar smirks a little, then adds, feigning regret, “Poor thing. One might almost feel pity for her, if she were not a heretic. She is with the notary just now, undergoing questioning that they say is for the good of her soul.”
Eustace staggers half a step back, a violent shudder wracking his frame. He has to fumble for a moment to catch his breath enough to gasp out a hollow, forced “Amen.”
“Through there,” the familiar says, nodding carelessly at a stout oak door further down the corridor as they walk. “We may pause to listen a moment, if you wish. But do not give me away to the superiors, senor.”

Eustace knows full well the man is watching him like a hawk, ready to report the flicker of even a spark of sympathy for a heretic to his masters, to ruin him on the spot if he shows the slightest sign of wavering. But a horrible, roiling curiosity drowns out his fear, and he presses himself close to that fateful door. His face burns like it is on fire, his knees knock so hard he can scarce stand, his ears ring with a high, thin hum, and his heart pounds so hard against his ribs he swears it will burst free. He presses one shoulder to the cold stone wall, hiding his twitching, convulsed face as best he can from the familiar at his side.
Inside, a man’s low, flat, distinct voice carries clear through the thick wood: the notary, reading that old, lazy charge of witchcraft the Inquisitors love to level against their victims, to justify their cruelty to their own consciences, to whitewash their villainy for the watching public of the city.
Then a woman’s voice cuts through the hum, sharp with indignation and the edge of fresh agony: “Witchcraft against Don Guzman? What need of that, oh God, what need?”
“You deny the charge then, senora?” the notary replies, in a tone of mock sorrow. “We grieve for you deeply, but—”
A ragged, choked murmur rises from the woman, words tangled and half-stifled that could mean anything, nothing at all.
“She confessed,” Eustace whispers, half wild, pressing a hand to his mouth. “Saints be praised—she—”

A wail bursts out, high, piercing, that cuts through Eustace’s ears, his brain, his very heart. He lurches forward, ready to tear the door off its hinges to get to her, but the familiar seizes his arm and drags him away. Another wail, then another, as the man pulls Eustace down the corridor, Eustace stuffing his fingers in his ears in a useless, vain effort to block the sound—cries that follow him like avenging angels, echoing off the stone vaults of the prison long after he is out of earshot.
He bursts out into the open air, fragrant with jasmine and tropical bloom, under the glow of a golden tropic moon, in a garden so lush and perfect it might have been Eden itself. But the hell of man’s cruelty has followed him into God’s own heaven: those wails still ring in his ears, no matter how hard he tries to shake them off.
“Misery, misery, endless misery,” he mutters to himself, grinding his teeth until his jaw aches. “I brought her to this. I had to bring her to this! What else could I do? Who dares blame me? What devil’s sin did I commit to deserve this punishment? Was there no other man in all the Indies they could have chosen for this? But it may save her soul. It may bring her to repentance!”
“Indeed it may,” the familiar says, calm as ever, falling into step beside him. “She is delicate, after all. She will not bear much more torment. You know as well as I do, senor, how merciful the Holy Office is to those under its care.”
“I know it, I know it,” Eustace stammers, now trembling for his own skin. “All done in love—all done out of Christian love. A fatherly chastisement.”
“And the proof of her heresy is plain to all, on top of the strong suspicion of enchantment, and the well-known wickedness of her old attendant, that English witch. You yourself told the Holy Office, senor, that the old woman was a notorious witch back in England, long before the senora brought her over here to serve her.”
“Of course she was. Of course,” Eustace babbles. “There was no other possible course to take. And though my flesh may be weak, sir, no man can say I have not proved to the Holy Office how utterly willing my spirit is to serve its cause.”

And so Eustace took his leave, and before the next sun had set, he had sought out the head of the Jesuit order in Cartagena, poured out all his fraying heart to him—or as much of it, poor wretch, as he dared admit even to himself—and begged leave to finish his novitiate and take his final vows as a Jesuit, on the condition that he be sent at once back to Europe, or anywhere, anywhere away from Cartagena. “Otherwise,” he told the superior frankly, “I shall go mad, even if I am not already half-mad now.”
The Jesuit, a man of no unkindness, went at once to the offices of the Holy Office, settled the whole matter with the inquisitors, and recited Eustace’s long record of faithful, valiant service to the Church to set his name above all possible suspicion. No one needed Eustace’s testimony any longer. He left Cartagena for the port of Nombre de Dios that very night, and sailed a week later for no destination I can name.

I cannot tell you where he went. Eustace Leigh vanishes entirely from the pages of this story. He may have risen in time to become General of the entire Jesuit Order. He may have worn out his days tramping through steaming tropical forests, “winning souls” for the Church—while seizing control of their bodies, too—among the native Indian tribes. He may have slipped back into England under a false name, and been the very Ballard who was hanged, drawn and quartered three years later for his part in the traitorous Babington Conspiracy. I do not know.
This book is the history of living men: of their virtues and their sins, their great victories and their crushing defeats. But Eustace is no longer a man. He has become a thing, a tool of the Jesuit order, sent wherever he is bidden, doing good or doing evil with equal indifference, as his superiors command. By an act of utter moral suicide, he has thrown away his own will, his own soul, in the desperate hope of saving it. He no longer has a will, no conscience, no sense of responsibility (or so he deludes himself) to God or to any man on earth, but only to the Society of Jesus. In a word, as he himself so plainly says, he is dead. Twice dead, I fear. Let the dead bury their dead. We have no more business with Eustace Leigh, for now or ever.

> “My mariners, Souls that have toil'd, and wrought, and thought with me--Death closes all: but something ere the end, Some work of noble note, may yet be done, Not unbecoming men that strove with gods!”
> —TENNYSON'S *Ulysses*

Nearly three long years have slipped by since that small band knelt for evensong beneath the colossal ceiba of Guayra—years of wandering so unmarked by any European map that only scattered half-memories and misspelt native place names trace their path. They have trekked across eight hundred miles of untrodden hill and forest, four hundred miles deep into the interior, hunting the fabled Golden City of Manoa, and they have found nothing but disappointment.

They traced the wooded banks of the Orinoco, dared the roaring foam-choked falls of Maypures, and pushed up the vast upper waters of the Amazon. They sailed deep into Peru itself, and trod the cinchona groves of Loxa, unaware, as the whole world then was, of the fever-healing power hidden in the tree’s bark. They watched the virgin snows of Chimborazo glow white above roiling thunderheads, and saw the smouldering black cone of Cotopaxi glower down at them from the heights, only days before lava streams poured down its slopes. Foiled in their search behind the Andes’ western flanks, they turned east again, plunging from the alpine cliffs into what old travellers called “the green misty ocean of the Montana.” They fought their way slowly, painfully, north once more along the eastern foot of the inland Cordillera, and now they are camped on the banks of one of the many tributaries of the Meta, that tumbles down from the Suma Paz range into the wide, forest-choked plains.

Their watchfires glint on the dark river surface under the shadow of enormous hardwoods. Amyas Leigh, Will Cary, Jack Brimblecombe, old Salvation Yeo, and the young Indian lad who has followed them through every twist of their wanderings sit alive and well by the flames—still as far as ever from Manoa, its fairy lake, its gilded palaces, and every wondrous tale the native peoples once told them. Again and again across their miles they caught faint whispers of the city, struck off in a new direction to follow the rumour, and met only fresh letdown, that deferred hope which sours the heart.

Of the eighty-four men who knelt with them under the Guayra tree, only forty-four remain. Where are the rest?
> Their bones are scatter'd far and wide,
> By mount, by stream, and sea.

Drew, their sailing master, lies buried on the Rio Negro’s banks, five brave fellows at his side, killed by poisoned Indian arrows in a doomed attempt to force the mountain gorges of Parima. Two more froze to death in the Andean valleys, struck down by the biting slate hail that sweeps down from the condor’s nesting crags. Four drowned in the Orinoco’s churning rapids. Five or six badly wounded men were left behind at another set of falls in the care of friendly local tribes, to be fetched later if they healed—if the men ever made their way back at all. Month by month, fever, venomous snakes, jaguars, alligators, man-eating piranha, and numbing electric eels have thinned their ranks. No trace of their march through the untouched wilderness remains but those lonely, unmarked graves.

The survivors sit now beside the silent stream under the glow of the tropic moon: sun-dried, lean, but strong and bold as the day they left Devon. The quiet, unshakable fire of English courage burns undimmed in every eye, the easy, genial smile of English mirth still rests on every lip. They jest at danger, turn their toil into sport, as cheerily as they did when they sailed over Bideford bar in those days that now feel like they belong to a past life before they were even born. Their beards hang long down to their chests, their hair knotted thick on their heads like women’s to shield their scalps from the burning sun. Their leggings are stitched from the soft hide of the delicate Guazu-puti deer, their shirts patched with woven Indian cotton. Trophies of jaguar, puma, and monkey fur hang slung over their shoulders.

Their gunpowder ran out long ago, their muskets rotted through by the perpetual steam bath of the rainforest, left abandoned and useless in a cave above an Orinoco cataract—but their broadswords still gleam bright and terrible. They carry longbows of a strength no Indian arm can draw, arrows tipped with shards of their old dented armour, and many of them wield the native pocuna blowgun: deadlier still, because it strikes in total silence, no loud report to warn prey or enemy. They wander as unchallenged lords of the forest and its beasts, feared by all hostile native bands but kind, just, and generous to every people that deals fairly with them. A dozen smooth-faced Carib, Ature, Solimo, and Guahiba storytellers recount with awe the righteousness of these bearded heroes, who proclaimed themselves the sworn foes of the treacherous, murderous Spaniards, and spoke of the great, good queen across the sea who would send her warriors to deliver and avenge all oppressed native peoples.

The rest of the men sleep among the trees, some on the packed earth, others strung in grass hammocks slung between tree trunks. The whole forest is quiet save for the heavy splash of a tapir wading in the river to tear up water weeds for its night feed. Now and then a jaguar leaps from treetop to treetop chasing prey, waking the whole horde of monkeys clustered on the boughs, who rouse the roosting birds, and for ten minutes unearthly roars, howls, shrieks, and cackles ring through the woods as if all pandemonium has broken loose. But the racket dies away fast, and even while it lasts it is too familiar to wake the sleeping men, let alone interrupt the small war council gathered around the watchfire: Amyas, Cary, Brimblecombe, and the faithful Yeo.

They have held a hundred such councils before, every one fruitless, and for all they know this one will be no different. But this one is far more solemn than the rest: the two years they agreed to spend searching for Manoa are long expired, and they must pick a new path now, unless they intend to spend the rest of their lives lost in this green wilderness.

“Well,” says Will Cary, puffing smoke from a stolen cigar he traded for with the last band of friendly Indians they met, “at least we came away with something from those last natives. It’s a pure comfort to have a proper smoke after three weeks of going without.”

“For me,” Jack Brimblecombe chimes in, laughing, “Heaven forgive me, but when I get that magical leaf between my lips again I feel tempted to sit as still as a chimney and smoke till my dying day, never stirring hand or foot.”

“Then I shall take your tobacco away, Parson,” Amyas grins, “for we have to be up and away at first light. We’ve lazed here three whole days already, and nothing’s to show for it.”

“Shall we ever find anything? I swear the gold of Manoa’s like the gold at the foot of the rainbow—always in the next field you never quite reach.”

Amyas falls silent for a long minute, and the rest go quiet with him. No one can deny their hopes are all but spent. In the thousands of miles they have travelled, they have met nothing but disappointment.

“There’s only one last chance,” he says at last, “and that’s the mountains east of the Orinoco, where we failed the first time. The escaped Inca royals might well have moved their people there to hide.”

“Why not?” Cary nods. “They’d put all these forests, the wide grassy Llanos, and half a dozen great rivers between themselves and those Spanish dogs, wouldn’t they?”

“Shall we make one more try?” Amyas says. “This river must flow straight into the Orinoco. Once we hit that main waterway, we’ll be right at the foot of those mountains. What do you say, Yeo?”

“I can’t help but mind, your worship,” the old gunner says slowly, “that when we first sailed up the Orinoco the Indians told us terrible tales of those peaks. They stretch for hundreds of miles, they said, almost impossible to cross, with sheer cliffs up high and forests so thick in the valleys you can barely squeeze through. And haven’t we already lost five good men there?”

“What do we care? No forest can be thicker than the ones we’ve already hacked our way through. Half the time I wished I had a monkey’s tail to swing along the treetops, that would have been far nicer than tripping over hanging withes and getting eaten alive by crawling bugs from dawn till dark.”

“But don’t forget,” Jack adds, his voice a little nervous, “they warned us about the Amazons there, too.”

“What, Jack? Scared of a parcel of women?”

“Why wouldn’t I be?” Jack huffs. “I’d never run from a man, you know that. But a whole nation of women warriors? That’s not natural. They must be witches or devils. The Caribs were terrified of them. They said there were men there too with no necks, their eyes set right in their chests. How’s a Christian supposed to fight a creature like that?”

“He couldn’t very well cut their heads off, that’s certain,” Cary snorts, “but I reckon a good thump in the ribs will drop them just the same as anyone else.”

“Well,” Jack says, “if I’m going to fight, let me fight honest flesh and blood, no outlandish monsters. Who’s to say they aren’t invulnerable to blades, thanks to witchcraft?”

“Who’s to say they are?” Amyas laughs. “And as for those Amazons,” Cary adds, “a woman’s a woman the whole world over. I’ll bet you could wheedle them all round with a few pretty compliments, the same as you’d charm a Devonshire burgher’s wife. Pity I haven’t got my court suit and a fine Spanish feathered hat. I’d take an orange in one hand and a lace handkerchief in the other, go to them all alone as ambassador, and in a week I’d be as great a favourite with Queen Blackfacealinda as Walter Raleigh is with the queen at Whitehall.”

“Gentlemen!” Yeo speaks up, his deep voice steady, “where you go, I go. And not just me—every last man of us will follow, I’ve no doubt. But we’ve lost half our company now, used up all our shot, and without our good swords we’re no better off than the naked heathens around us. It was the noise and wonder of their ordnance, not to mention those breakneck horses I’d never trust a yard, that let Cortez and Pizarro, those imps of Satan, work their golden conquests. If we’d had the guns to astound the people of Manoa—”

“Assuming we ever found the said people,” Amyas laughs dryly. “It’s the old fable. Every tradesman thinks his own craft is the only thing holding the world together.”

“Well, your worship,” Yeo grins, “it may be that being an old gunner I prize guns too high. But you don’t need slate and pencil to work this sum out: are forty men with no shot as good as eighty who have plenty?”

“You’re right, old fellow, dead right,” Amyas says, his smile fading. “I was only jesting out of sheer sadness, laughing so I don’t start weeping. I think our chance of Manoa is gone, though I daren’t admit that to the men.”

“Sir,” Yeo says, his voice soft and grave, “I’ve had a feeling on me that the Lord’s hand is against us in this quest. Whether He means to keep that wealth for worthier men than us, or to hide that great city in the secret of His presence far from the greed of fighting men, to save those people from sinful man’s covetousness, and save England from that same sin and luxury I’ve seen gold breed among the Spaniards, I don’t know, sir. Who can ever know the counsels of the Lord? But for a long time a voice inside me has said, ‘Salvation Yeo, you will never see the earthly Golden City where heathens worship the sun and moon and the hosts of heaven. Be content then to look for that Golden City above, where there is no sun nor moon, for the Lord God and the Lamb are the light thereof.’”

There was a simple, quiet majesty about old Yeo when he spoke like this, that made his comrades—even Amyas and Cary—look on him the way pious Muslims look on holy madmen, as someone touched with flashes of secret inspiration. Brimblecombe, whose pious heart had long revered the old Anabaptist hero far above any petty churchman’s prejudice against his faith, answered gently, “Amen! Amen, all of us. It has been on my mind for months too, that Providence has turned us back every time we tried to push east. Look how these past two years, every time we struck east we ran into trouble and lost good men. Every time we turned westward-ho, we thrived, and still thrive to this day.”

“And what’s more, gentlemen,” Yeo adds, “if Scripture tells us dreams can come from the Lord, I swear mine last night came straight from Him. I lay by the fire, sirs, and I heard my little maid’s voice calling me, as plain as I ever heard it in my life. She was crying the very same words me and my old comrade William Penberthy taught her: ‘Westward-ho! jolly mariners all!’ It’s a silly, ungodly old sea shanty from our wild younger days, but there she stood calling it, plain as day, till I called back, ‘Coming, my maid, coming!’ And then the dear little lass fell silent—God grant I find her yet!—and I woke.”

Cary had long since stopped laughing at Yeo for talking about his lost little maid, and Amyas nodded. “So be it then, Yeo, if the rest agree. But what do we do sailing west?”

“Do?” Cary grins. “There’s plenty of work for our swords. They say there’s no end of gold, and no end of Spaniards, on the other side of these mountains. Our blades won’t be rusting for lack of adventure, my merry knights errant.”

They chatted on long into the night, and before the midnight hours had passed a desperate new plan was fully formed—what did those bold men care for risk? They would cross the Cordillera range to Santa Fe de Bogota, a city Yeo and Amyas had heard tales of for years when they sailed the Pacific, fabulously wealthy. They would try to seize the town, or at the very least intercept a gold convoy bound out of it, then make for the large north-flowing river they were told ran from that region, build new canoes, and fight their way back out to the Caribbean Sea. If fortune favoured them, they could seize a Spanish merchant galleon and sail home to England—not with the endless gold of Manoa, but with a fine, honest load of plundered Spanish treasure. It was a wild scheme, but no wilder than the one Francis Drake had already pulled off, and far less foolish than the one that cost Oxenham his life through his own reckless folly.

Amyas sat watching by the fire late that night, heavy of heart. To give up the dream he had cherished for years was hard. To face his mother back in Devon without the golden fortune he had promised her felt harder still. But he knew it was the only right path, for the sake of his surviving men. He laid the new plan before the camp at dawn, and the men cheered it wholeheartedly. They would sail upriver to the foothills of the mountains, rest a while, and if possible fetch back the wounded men they had left behind with their native allies, before setting off on their new venture, braced for whatever new dangers might come their way—they were long inured to hardship now.

They set off at sunrise the next morning, as cheerful as ever. For three hours they paddled easily up the glassy, windless stretches of the Meta, between two green walls of forest draped in bright flowering vines, alive with thousands of birds and iridescent butterflies. Long trailing vines hung from the overhanging boughs down to the water’s edge, their gorgeous blooms reflected clearly in the river’s still surface as if they were admiring their own beauty. River, trees, flowers, birds, insects—it was all a fairyland, but a colossal one, and the voyagers barely paid it any mind. It was an everyday sight to them now, to see two hundred foot trees smothered in yellow or purple bloom from root to highest twig, every branch draped in hanging gardens of crimson and orange orchids and vanilla vines. They had long grown used to the fantastic, enormous shapes Nature wears beneath the tropic sun and rain, to birds, fish, and butterflies brighter and stranger than any opium addict ever dreamed. The long processions of monkeys that kept pace with their canoes along the treetops, chattering, hooting, and howling, no longer made them laugh, the distant roar of a jaguar and rustle of a boa constrictor no longer made them start. When a brilliant green and rose-coloured piranha, flat-bodied like a bream, finned like a salmon, and saw-toothed like a shark, leapt straight into their canoe to escape the rush of a massive alligator—whose disgusting snout slammed against the canoe’s gunwale a foot away from Jack Brimblecombe’s hand—Jack did not turn pale, the way he once had at the sight of sharks off the South American coast. He coolly picked the fish up, laughed, and said, “Four pound weight! If you want to keep catching piranha for us like that, old fellow, you can hang around in our wake, and we’ll give you all the fish guts for wages.”

The human appetite for wonder is not nearly so infinite as folks back home fancy. All that craving for novelty, so ravenous in the grey everyday world of Europe, sates as easily as any other, leaving a man’s senses as dull to wild natural marvels as a city glutton’s is dull to fine feasts after a Lord Mayor’s banquet. Only the most cultivated minds

“Let us alone. What pleasure can we have To war with evil? Is there any peace In always climbing up the climbing wave? All things have rest, and ripen toward the grave In silence; ripen, fall, and cease: Give us long rest or death, dark death, or dreamful ease.”

The great traveller Humboldt once penned a curious reflection, staring at a ragged band of Indians huddled stupidly around their fires, caked in grease and ochre paint, gnawing at ants and clay. He noted that were it not for the fashionable science of his day that framed all humanity as slowly climbing upward from brute origins, he would have been forced to conclude these wretched beings were not the raw seed of humankind, but the last tattered remnants of a once-great race, fallen far from their old grace. That instinct of his was truer than he knew, aligned straight with the old Biblical truth our English voyagers held as unshakable fact: man left to his own devices in the wilds was not ascending, but fallen, a soul trapped in darkness, “captivated by the devil at his will”, as gruff old seaman Yeo would put it. They wasted no breath debating whether their new hosts had sprung from apes, but looked on them as brothers in Christ, lost deep in the Amazon forests, as worthy of rescue as any soul back home in Devon.

The village they landed at that bright, steamy morning was far cleaner and neater than the squalid camps of the savage Guahibas they had met further downriver. Palm-leaf sheds dotted the sun-dappled clearing, hammocks strung between tall palm trunks, and small plots of cassava and indigo spread through gaps in the jungle, tended with obvious care. But the first sound that hit their ears as their keel scraped the shore was no peaceful welcome: four or five strapping warriors lined the landing, beating tall wooden drums and blaring on long earthen trumpets swollen with hollow chambers, the din a cacophony of howls and rumbles loud enough to make the teeth rattle in their heads.

“They’re calling on their devil, sir,” grunted Yeo, squinting at the painted figures.

Cary laughed beside him, clapping pious chaplain John Brimblecombe on the back. “Small wonder none but the foul fiend Sir Urian would deign to turn up to listen to such a racket. You can have that blessing job, Jack.”

Amyas, his mouth full of smoke from a fresh Cuban cigar, waved off their unease. “Nonsense. We could cut every last one of them down in half an hour, and they know it well enough.”

A great pageant was clearly afoot. The villagers had arrayed themselves in two long lines down the clearing, men at the front, women behind, all slathered in bright red arnotto and deep blue indigo, crowned with plumes of wild parrot feathers. Then with a shriek that split the air, a figure leaped into the open space: a man dressed head to toe in a jaguar pelt, complete with long bushy tail, grinning carved teeth, curved horns, a plume of black and yellow feathers, and a huge carved rattle in his fist, made up to look like the very devil himself.

“Here’s the Piache, their conjuring rascal,” Amyas muttered.

“Ay, tricked out in Satan’s own livery,” Yeo growled. “You may be sure his works match the costume.”

The Piache had clearly meant to strike the strangers dead with awe, but at the sight of the Englishmen snickering behind their hands, he stumbled in his performance for a moment. A practiced charlatan, he recovered fast, whacking one of the drummers over the head with his rattle to silence the racket, and launched into a long, gesticulating harangue, the words rapid and guttural.

The young interpreter boy translated for Amyas: “He asks if you have seen Amalivaca, the great civilizer of their people, on the far shore of the great ocean? The one who carved all the strange statues on the inland cliffs before sailing home again.”

Amyas had heard this myth a dozen times from different Amazon tribes. He answered, as he always did, singing the praises of their own great monarch, Queen Elizabeth of England.

The Piache nodded solemnly, and replied that she must surely be one of Amalivaca’s seven daughters — the ones he had taken back across the sea with him, after breaking the legs of the rest to stop them running away, leaving them to people the forests.

Amyas laughed and assured him his queen had no broken legs, was the most graceful dancer in all her realms, and far more importantly, asked if the tribe would give them cassava bread, and let them rest peacefully on the small offshore island they had fortified, before marching inland to fight the “clothed men”, the hated Spaniards, on the other side of the mountains.

The Piache responded with a wild capering dance, rolling head over heels across the dirt before waving them to follow. The Indians were all unarmed, grinning broadly, so the English party went with him, their hands resting light on the hilts of their swords. The conjuror crawled on all fours to the door of a tight-shut, thatched hut, and began whining at the dark interior like a begging dog.

Amyas turned to his interpreter: “Ask the old cacique what fool game this is.”

The old chief, who was trailing after them, answered that the Piache was consulting the Daughter of the Sun, the tribe’s sacred oracle.

“This is the mare’s nest we’ve all been chasing, lads,” Cary whispered, as the Piache’s whines rose to screams, then violent convulsions that left him foaming at the mouth, his eyes rolling back in his head, before he collapsed as if dead.

“Better than any play on the London stage,” Cary murmured.

“The devil’s played his part,” Brimblecombe said, frowning. “Now by all the old morality play rules, Vice ought to come out next.”

“A very fair Vice it will be, a right sweet Iniquity,” Cary said. “Listen.”

From the dark of the hut rose a low, rich, clear song — not the shrill guttural whine of an Indian voice, but the rounded, full tone of a European woman, soaring high and soft, weaving through notes so sweet even the rough old English seamen, who knew every glee and catch and psalm tune ever sung in Devon, were struck silent with wonder. The Indians bowed their heads, reverent, as the song rose and fell like a nightingale’s call, then burst into one final triumphant, ringing note that made every ear hum, before the clearing fell dead still.

The Piache jumped upright as if he had never been unconscious, and began jabbering again at Amyas. The interpreter translated that the Daughter of the Sun had accepted them as friends: her people were their people, her foes their foes. The crowd of Indians cheered loud enough to rouse the parrots in the treetops.

“Then tell her to bring us cassava,” Amyas said, rolling another tobacco leaf in plantain, and lighting his new cigar.

The hut door swung open. Every Indian prostrated themselves face down in the dirt. Stepping out into the sunlight was the same fair, pale-skinned woman they had glimpsed once on a smaller river island, now draped in a robe of iridescent macaw feathers, plumes of every bright hue woven into her dark hair. She walked slow and stately, as one used to command, glancing over her prostrate followers, then gestured gracefully at the gardens, the huts, the forest around them — all of it, her sign said, was theirs for the taking. She lifted Amyas’s calloused hand gently to her forehead, in a gesture of total submission.

The crowd roared with rapture, and as the mysterious girl retreated back to her hut, the Indians swarmed around the Englishmen, caressing their armor, gaping at their swords, pressing fruit and flowers and calabashes of fermented palm drink into their hands. Brimblecombe and the pious old Yeo were so scandalized by the wild, gyrating dances the young villagers started, that they convinced Amyas to retreat back to their fortified island before the men got too drunk. They paddled back at dusk, all of them turning over the same puzzle in their heads: who on earth was this strange white “Daughter of the Sun”?

Amyas was certain she was a last survivor of the lost Inca royal line, perhaps the descendant of that fair wife of the Inca Manco, whom the tyrant Pizarro had stripped, scourged, and shot to death with arrows forty years before, just to torment the fugitive king who had escaped his clutches.

That night, as they did nearly every night after leaving England, they gathered for evening prayer, singing psalms then old drinking catches until the moon hung high over the roar of the nearby cataract. Twice they thought they heard an echo of their song drifting across the river from the dark jungle, but they paid it no mind, until Cary slipped away, came back, and tugged Amyas aside by the arm.

“Our sweet Daughter of the Sun is mimicking us, lad,” he whispered.

They crept to the river bank, and over the roar of the falling water, heard that same clear, rich voice floating out of the black treeline, repeating every snatch of the old English airs they had just sung, perfectly in tune. They stood for an hour staring out over the moonlit eddies, but saw nothing but the impenetrable wall of the jungle, until the song faded away.

They returned to the village every day for the next week, but the girl rarely appeared, and when she did, kept her distance, haughty as a crowned queen. When Amyas’s Spanish was good enough to question the old cacique about her, the old man shook his head mysteriously, refused to speak her name aloud, and only said she was a Child of the Sun, and nothing more needed saying.

Cary joked that they ought to tell the Indians they were the Sun’s children by his first wife, come to check how they had treated their step-sister, and threaten to blight all their cassava if they didn’t confess the truth. Amyas chided him for lying, but the threat was enough. The old cacique bundled them into his canoe and paddled a full mile downriver, far from the village, afraid the oracle might overhear, and told them the whole story in a low, chanting voice.

Many years before, he said, his Omagua people had lived far in the mountains, until the Spaniards had hunted them out, and they had fled north. On the lower slopes of Cotopaxi’s smoking cone, they had found this little white girl wandering alone in the forest, no older than seven, her skin pale as the moon, too tender to have survived the jaguars and the snakes on her own. The tribe worshipped her as a divine gift, certain she was a surviving Inca royal, one of the race the Sun himself had fathered, who the old legends said would one day return to drive the Spaniards out of their lands. They raised her with every royal honor, she grew wiser than all their elders, braver in war, swifter in the chase, and her strange songs held all the secrets of the forest, the birds, the rivers, and the flowers, given to her by the Sun himself. She was named Ayacanora, and no Indian warrior dared to ask for her hand, for the Piache had declared it sacrilege for mortal men to touch a Child of the Sun.

Amyas pondered the tale long and late. The lost Inca remnants he had dreamed of finding, the road to the golden city of Manoa, could be just a little further south. He made plans to send Cary back downriver with half a dozen men to fetch the sick crewmen they had left stranded on the Orinoco, a dangerous journey through the lands of the brutal Guahibas. Ayacanora, hearing of his need for porters, stepped up before the whole tribe, sang a song that (per the Piache’s terrified translation) threatened the whole village with the wrath of a nameless evil spirit if they did not volunteer to go, and the men signed on instantly.

Conflict soon brewed, though. The pious Jack Brimblecombe began preaching to the villagers, drawing huge crowds with the English evening hymns, and the Piache saw his power slipping away. He plotted to kill Brimblecombe with a poisoned arrow, and thought to win Ayacanora to his side — only to receive a ringing box on the ear, and a lashing torrent of abuse. Ayacanora ran straight to Amyas, told him the whole plot, and begged him to throw the traitor conjuror to the alligators in the river, and install Brimblecombe in his place.

Jack forgave the Piache magnanimously, and preached all the harder — but the conjuror still had one last trick. He owned a sacred magic trumpet, hidden in a cave in the hills, that the tribe believed had to be blown every night at the proper season, or the palm trees would bear no fruit, the manioc would rot in the ground, and disaster would fall on every household. He announced one day that the village’s new fondness for the white strangers’ foreign God had angered the spirits, and he would take the magic trumpet and leave, abandoning the Omaguas to blight.

The whole village erupted in wails and fury. The villagers had loved Brimblecombe’s songs, but none of them would risk the crops failing over the new white man’s Gospel. The mob turned on the English, the men grabbing bows and blowguns dipped in deadly curare poison. There were less than a dozen of Amyas’s men at the camp that day, facing nearly a hundred enraged Indians. The air hung thick with the threat of blood.

“It is God’s own quarrel, sirs,” Brimblecombe said, steady as a rock. “Let Him defend the right.”

At that very moment, Ayacanora’s magic song rose up from her hut, soaring high above the shouts and the fury, weaving through the green treetops like a messenger straight from the Sun.

“God will relent, and quit thee all thy debt, Who ever more approves, and more accepts Him who imploring mercy sues for life, Than who self-rigorous chooses death as due, Which argues over-just, and self-displeased For self-offence, more than for God offended.”

A fortnight of brutal, unrelenting mountain toil had passed, no harsher than the hardships this band of Devon adventurers had endured a dozen times before. Bidding a final, lingering farewell to the green sea of the eastern plains, they had scaled the jagged spine of the Cordillera, and paused long enough to glimpse the white-walled spires of Santa Fe, nestling amid lush irrigated gardens high on its sunlit plateau. It was far too large, far too heavily garrisoned a settlement for their tiny band to attack, but their time had not been wasted: their young Indigenous scout had learned word of a gold-train bound from Santa Fe down toward the Magdalena River, and the men now waited hidden beside the rutted, mud-choked track that passed for a royal road.

They camped in a stand of gnarled oaks that smelled so sharply of English woodland they might have fancied themselves back on Devon’s hills—save for the feathery tree ferns that choked the undergrowth, and the sheer thousand-foot gorges that yawned at their very feet. While the cool, temperate mountain breeze whipped their cheeks, they could peer down through banks of thick, steamy mist to spot the vivid plumage and broad fronds of the unbroken tropical rainforest far below.

Their camp was pitched among the ferns, just above a stretch of path that hugged a near-vertical cliff. Once, the road had been a marvel of Cundinamarca’s Inca civilization: paved, shored up with stone, linked by aqueducts and bridges to every corner of the mountain kingdom. But three centuries of Spanish misrule had left it little more than a narrow ditch of slipping mud and half-collapsed stone steps. The great llama flocks were gone, the gardens overgrown, the post-houses crumbled to dust. All the arts of Inca civilization had been abandoned in the mad scramble for gold, save for architecture: and that skill now only survived in the soaring Catholic cathedrals raised on the ruins of Sun-worshipper temples, where a faith that still countenanced brutal human sacrifice had replaced the gentle, nature-centered rites of the old Incas. Now, fast-spreading tropical foliage had reclaimed every inch of untended land, and Amyas Leigh and his crew were as utterly alone a few miles outside of a major Spanish settlement as they would have been in the deepest, uncharted reaches of the Orinoco.

Worse still: all their efforts to mine sulphur and nitre for gunpowder had come to nothing. To the red-faced fury of old Salvation Yeo, they were forced to rely entirely on their bows, arrows, and steel swords. It mattered not, the others laughed: Drake himself had stormed Nombre de Dios and seized its gold trains with no better weapons, and they could do the same.

They felled a massive oak across the path to block the road above, then settled back among the wild mountain flowers, chewing coca leaves to stave off hunger and thirst, while they puzzled over the low, unidentifiable roaring that had trailed them every night since they left the banks of the Meta. It was no jaguar’s snarl, no monkey’s howl: none of them could place the sound, or guess why it seemed to follow them for miles. But that mystery was trivial beside the gold train they waited for.

At last, a sharp, whipping crack echoed up from below, followed by a high, piteous wail. The crack was no snapping branch, no woodpecker’s tap. The cry was no parrot’s squawk, no monkey’s shriek.

“That was a lash of manatee hide,” Yeo breathed, his fingers tightening around his bowstring. “And a woman’s cry. They’re right on top of us, lads!”

“A woman’s? Do they drive women in their work gangs?” Amyas whispered, his jaw tight.

“Why wouldn’t those brutes?” Yeo snarled. “There they are, sir—see their helmets glint in the sun?”

“Men,” Amyas hissed to the party hidden in the underbrush, “not a shot, not an arrow, till I give the word. One volley of shafts, then draw your swords and charge. Pass the order down the line.”

The procession crept slowly up the path, and every man’s heart thundered in his chest as the first of the party rounded the bend. Twenty soldiers led the way, half of them on foot, the other half borne lazily in small wooden chairs slung across the backs of single Indigenous porters. The men who walked had slung all their heavy armor and their unwieldy arquebuses over the shoulders of enslaved attendants, who were prodded in the back with pike points every time they staggered under the weight.

“The fools have let their own weapons out of their hands,” one of the sailors muttered.

“An Indian would kneel and pray to an arquebus not to shoot him,” Yeo said with a dry snort. “They never fear their guns getting stolen, sir.”

“Look at those pompous villains,” another whispered. “They treat human beings like dumb pack animals.”

“Ten foot, ten litters,” Amyas counted quickly under his breath. “Will’s party up the top of the path can lock them in easy.”

Bringing up the rear of the soldier’s detachment was a swaggering junior officer, also borne in a litter. Every other second he pulled his cigar from his lips to hurl a string of oaths, invoking the Virgin, Saint James of Compostella, and every foul curse under the sun, the sort of profanity that had earned 16th century Spaniards the reputation of being both the most superstitious idolaters and the most shameless swearers in all of Europe.

“The blaspheming dog,” Yeo snarled, fumbling to nock an arrow, and would have loosed it straight through the man’s throat if Amyas had not caught his wrist. But the sight that rounded the bend a moment later drove even the officer’s cruelty from every man’s mind.

A line of Indigenous men, Black enslaved people, and mixed-race zambos toiled up the path, chained together at their left wrists, naked, emaciated, their skin crisscrossed with old scars from whips and iron fetters. They panted and sweated under the weight of heavy baskets strapped to their foreheads with leather thongs. Yeo had spoken no lie: there were old men, teenage boys, slender young girls, mothers with small children trotting at their knees. A low, universal murmur of righteous indignation rose from the hidden Englishmen, men who had been raised on Raleigh’s speeches that all men, no matter their skin, had a right to freedom, long before the 17th century’s terrible legacy of chattel slavery had brutalized English consciences too.

Amyas counted forty of the porters bearing baskets bound tight with oiled hide, packed so heavy they bulged. He knew that wrapping all too well.

“Captain—what’s in those packs?” a sailor whispered.

“Gold.” At that single magic word, every eye strained forward, and the whole party shifted with excitement, making so much rustle Amyas had to hiss at them under his breath, “Be men, fools, or you’ll spoil all!”

The last twenty porters carried lighter baskets, full of manioc, maize bread, and supplies for the Spanish party. Behind them came another twenty soldiers, and the commanding officer, lounging in his litter, twirling his elaborate mustaches, never dreaming a band of Englishmen waited in the trees above him. The ambush was perfect: the only question left was when to strike.

Amyas hated the thought of shooting down unsuspecting men in cold blood, even monsters as cruel as these—until one of the whip-wielding drivers lashed a staggering porter twice across the bare back, the crack of his manatee hide lash ringing out like a pistol shot. Their casus belli arrived of its own accord a moment later.

The second last man in the chained line was a gray-haired old Indian, followed close behind by his slender eighteen-year-old daughter. Just as they passed the ambush, the head of the procession rounded the upper bend, and a shout rang out: “Halt, sirs! There’s a tree felled across the path!”

“A tree across the path?” the commanding officer roared, unleashing a flood of curses against every saint and demon he could name. The line of terrified porters, told to halt from the front and beaten forward from the back, surged back and forth on the crumbling stone steps until the old gray Indian stumbled, and fell face-first in the mud.

The officer leaped down from his litter and hurried up to see what the delay was. He spotted the prostrate old man at once. “Spawn of Beelzebub! Is this a place to lie down and worship your fiendish gods?” he snarled, pricking the old man’s shoulder with the point of his sword.

The old man tried to rise, but the weight of the gold basket on his forehead was too much for him. He collapsed again, and went still. The driver lashed him twice across the loins with his whip, so hard the sound echoed off the cliff faces, but the old man did not stir.

“He’s spent, Captain,” the driver said with a careless shrug. “Been dying for three months now.”

“What’s the intendant thinking sending me out with worn-out useless cattle like this?” the officer shouted. “Clear that tree away, sirs—Pedrillo, hold the chain up!”

The driver lifted the chain fastened to the old man’s lifeless wrist. The officer stepped back, flourishing his exquisite Toledo sword, the blade so perfectly tempered Amyas was nearly tempted to steal it on the spot. He expected the man to make a show of severing the chain with a single stroke of his famous steel.

He was not prepared for the cold cruelty of the conquistador blood in that man’s veins. The blade flashed once, twice, in the mountain sunlight—and it fell not on the chain, but on the wrist it was fastened to. A girl’s shriek split the air, and a spray of crimson blood arced across the stone steps.

Before Amyas could nock his own arrow to put a shaft through the officer’s throat, the girl launched herself at the murderer with the ferocity of a wild cat, wrapping both arms around his waist, and hurled herself backward off the narrow path, dragging the shrieking officer with her over the thousand-foot drop.

The whole procession froze, every Spaniard leaning over the cliff edge. The girl dangled by her chained wrist over the void. The officer’s body had already vanished, crashing through the treetops far below.

“Pull her up! Tear her to pieces! Burn the witch!” the driver screamed, yanking hard on the chain to haul her over the edge back onto the path so they could kill her.

It was Amyas’s moment. Heaven had delivered them right into his hands. His arrow flew ten yards straight through the driver’s chest, and he gave a roar like a leaping lion as he burst from the underbrush, charging straight into the middle of the stunned Spanish party.

His first thought was the girl. With one heave of his broad shoulders, he hauled her to safety off the cliff edge, while the Spaniards stumbled back in terror, half convinced he was some mountain giant come to eat them. Their shouts of “English! Lutheran dogs!” rose too late. The rest of the Devon men charged behind him, a storm of arrows felling five Spaniards dead and wounding a dozen more, then Salvation Yeo came bounding down the path, his white hair flying behind him, leading twenty more good Englishmen with drawn swords.

The Spaniards fought like madmen, but they had no room on the narrow path to set up their arquebuses, no space to wield their long pikes. The English held the high ground, and five minutes of brutal, desperate fighting left no living Spaniard standing on the stone steps. Only two men in full armor at the very back of the procession escaped mortal wounds, fleeing down the hill at a dead run.

“After them! Michael Evans, Simon Heard—run them down, even if they flee a league!” Amyas shouted. The two long, lean Clovelly men, who had spent their whole youth chasing deer through the forests, covered two feet for every one the Spaniards took, and came back ten minutes later to say the job was done. The rest of Amyas’s party raced up the path to join Cary’s small detachment that had been guarding the fallen oak, where shouts and musket fire told of a brief skirmish. Their arrival ended the fight at once: all the remaining Spaniards were cut down save three who scrambled down the cliff face, only to be picked off by arrows before they could reach the shelter of the trees below.

“Cut their chains,” Amyas ordered, passing around the armorers’ tools they had looted off a dead Spanish soldier. “We are your friends. All we ask is that you help us carry this gold down to the Magdalena River, and then every last one of you is free.”

A few of the younger porters fell to their knees, kissing Amyas’s boots and hailing him as a child of the Sun. Most of the rest were too broken by decades of cruelty, overwork, and abuse to show any reaction at all: they simply sat down where they stood, staring into empty space, the horrors they had endured having burned every last spark of hope or joy out of them.

Only the young girl who had dragged the officer over the cliff moved. As soon as her own chains were cut, she ran to her father’s body, lifted it in her thin, emaciated arms, laid it across her knees, and kissed his cold lips, stroking his furrowed cheeks, making soft cooing sounds no one but her dead father could understand. A single long, heaving sob shook her frame. She stood up, still holding the old man’s corpse tight against her chest, and leaped off the cliff without a sound.

The men watched her dark, slender limbs wrapped tight around her father’s body as they turned over and over through the air, until a distant crash in the treetops told them she had reached the forest below, and the green foliage hid her from sight.

“Brave lass,” a sailor muttered, crossing himself.

“The Lord have mercy on her soul,” Yeo said. “But your worship—we’ve got all those Spaniards’ guns and powder to collect.”

“And their uniforms too, Yeo, if we want to sail down the Magdalena unchallenged, even past the gates of Santa Martha itself,” Amyas said, turning to address his whole crew. “By God’s grace, we’ve won more gold than any of us will ever need for the rest of our lives, and we haven’t lost a single man. But remember Oxenham and his crew, lads—don’t let greed or mutiny throw this gift from God away.”

“You can trust us, Captain!” the men shouted back. “We know you’ll lead us right.”

“Thank God for that,” Amyas said. “We won’t burden the women porters with heavy packs, no man of us will do that. But we need those Spanish uniforms to disguise ourselves to get past the settlements. Who’s going to volunteer to climb down the cliff and bring up all the armor, weapons, and supplies off the Spaniards who fell over the edge?”

A dozen men stepped forward at once, as they always did when Amyas asked for volunteers—for they knew he would be the first one climbing down the cliff to do the work alongside them. He left John Brimblecombe behind with the Indigenous scout to talk to the stunned freed porters and reassure them they would never be harmed again.

“Wait—who’s that coming up the road?”

Every head turned. There, trudging up the path, her feather travel clothes tattered and smudged from two weeks in the woods, blowgun in her hand and her bow slung over her back, was Ayacanora. The whole party stood utterly stunned. When she spotted Amyas, she gave a cry of joy, ran as fast as her legs could carry her, and collapsed panting and exhausted at his feet.

“I found you!” she gasped. “You ran away from me, but you could never escape me!” She fawned around Amyas like a loyal hound that had found its long-lost master, then sank down on a rock at the side of the path and burst into wild, heaving sobs.

“God help us,” Amyas groaned, clutching at his hair. “What am I going to do with her, on top of all these poor people?” There was no time to argue, though. He scrambled down the cliff with the rest of the volunteers, and Ayacanora sat on her rock, watching him work, never moving an inch.

Half an hour later, all the weapons, powder, armor, and Spanish uniforms were hauled up the cliff, bundled up to carry down the mountain. The remaining Spanish corpses were tossed over the edge, and the whole party set off for the Magdalena, Yeo snorting like a war horse at the smell of gunpowder he had thought they would never recover.

“We could go back and take Santa Fe now, sir, easy!” he cheered.

Amyas laughed and shook his head, saying enough was as good as a feast. The freed porters trundled behind them, still so stunned they half-expected a lash across their backs at every step, their surprise growing when they were given small handfuls of coca leaves to chew, no demands made on them beyond carrying the light gold packs. When the party paused for one last look at the site of their victory, they saw lines of vultures already circling down through the sky, and knew that in a few hours, the only trace of the fight would be a handful of white bones half-buried in mountain wildflowers.

At last Amyas had time to ask Ayacanora why she had followed them. She told him, through her broken, halting Spanish, how she had tracked them day and night since they left her village, making loud, deep noises every evening, hoping they would hear her and wait. Amyas suddenly realized that the mysterious roaring that had followed them for two weeks was her.

“What were you making those noises with?” he asked.

Ayacanora grinned, drew herself up with a mysterious, proud air, and pulled a two-foot long glazed earthen tube from under her feather cloak, painted with ancient animal figures and patterns, a relic of a long-forgotten civilization. “The holy trumpet!” she whispered, as if afraid it would hear her talking.

Brim

> “When captains courageous, whom death could not daunt,
> Did march to the siege of the city of Gaunt,
> They muster'd their soldiers by two and by three,
> But the foremost in battle was Mary Ambree.
> When brave Sir John Major was slain in her sight,
> Who was her true lover, her joy and delight,
> Because he was murther'd most treacherouslie,
> Then vow'd to avenge him fair Mary Ambree.”
> — Old Ballad, A. D. 1584.

Cast one last lingering glance over the burnished golden tropical sea, the honeyed sunsets that bleed tangerine and rose over the shore of New Granada, along the fabled, treasure-laden Spanish Main.

On the bay of Santa Marta, the land breeze ripples the surface into sheets of living flame. The vast, unbroken rainforests glitter with tens of thousands of drifting fireflies, and the wispy lazy mist coiled around the inland hills glows molten gold in the last of the sunset rays. Nineteen thousand feet above the shore, the jagged, snow-capped peak of Horqueta pierces the dark blue abyss of the sky, dyed soft rose-red against the fading twilight. The rosy cone fades to dull lead for only a breath, before the stars prick through the dark one by one, and Venus hangs bright as a second moon, gilding the eastern snowfields and stretching a long shimmering ribbon of rippling yellow light across the bay. Everywhere there is richness, unearthly glory. Is it any wonder the very soil of this enchanted land is as wealthy in its depths as it is on the surface? The sky, hills, and sea weave together like a garland of scattered jewels — why would not the earth beneath match? Why not every stream and earthen bank spangled with emeralds, rubies, grains of raw gold, and lacy wreaths of native silver?

That was the whimsical, poetic thought drifting through the Bishop of Cartagena’s mind as he sat in the grand state cabin of the mighty galleon *The City of the True Cross*, staring pensively out the window toward the shore. The good man was wrapped in a state of soft, holy calm: his ample frame propped on one easy chair, his stout ankles resting on a second, beside a table heaped high with oranges, limes, guavas, pineapples, and every other exotic fruit the Indies had to offer.

A young Indigenous girl, draped in bright scarves and strung with cheap gold chains, fended off the swarms of flies with a feather fan. Beside her master, in a pail of fresh ice hauled down from the peak of Horqueta — the gift of a pious Spanish noblewoman who had worked two Indian laborers to death to bring the precious offering down the mountain — sat more than one flask of smooth, vintage Alicant wine. The bishop was far too generous to hoard the ice and wine for himself: at his special invitation, Don Pedro, colonel of the soldiers posted aboard, Don Alverez, the King’s customs intendant for Santa Marta, and Don Paul, master mariner of the galleon, had joined him that evening, alongside two quiet friars sat at the far end of the table, all of them doing their best to distract the prelate from the lingering sting of the recent sack and burning of his cathedral town at the hands of an old familiar rogue: Sir Francis Drake.

“We have suffered greatly, señors — ah, most greatly,” the bishop snuffled, quoting Scripture in the glib, irreverent fashion of the day, twisting verses to suit his grumbles about the town’s ruin, his servant Tita, and the pestering mosquitoes. “Suffered greatly, yes — but there will be a remnant, ah, a remnant as when the olive tree is shaken, and the last grapes are gleaned after the vintage. Gold? Yes, I trust Our Lady’s mercies are not closed off, nor her arm shortened. Look, señors!” He waved a chubby hand grandly at the window. “The very light looks like gold! The air reeks of gold, if I may take a poet’s liberty. The ripples slipping past our hull sing of gold, gold, gold!”

“It is a rare privilege,” the intendant flattered him, “to receive such gentle comfort from a man who is both a churchman and a scholar.”

“And a poet, too,” added Don Pedro. “You have no idea the sweet sonnets he writes —”

“Hush, Don Pedro, hush! If I, a widowed, mateless bird, have spent an idle hour teaching young lovers how to sing, what of it? I am a churchman, yes — but I am still a man, señors. I can feel, sympathize, pardon, excuse. Who knows better than I how much human weakness lingers in all of us fallen sons of Adam? Tita!”

“Um?” the girl mumbled, shifting on her heels with a low, Indigenous grunt.

“Fill his excellency the intendant’s glass. Is there much more treasure due down from the mountains, illustrious señor? Might the poor of Our Lady hope for a few crumbs from their Mistress’s table?”

“Not a single peso, I fear,” the intendant laughed, pointing up at the looming snow peak of Horqueta. “The great white cow up there has been milked dry for this year.”

“Ah.” The bishop sighed, staring up at the magnificent cone. “Good only to cool our wine, eh? And as safe for now as King Solomon’s holy birds.”

“Solomon’s birds? Explain that obscure reference, my lord.”

“Enlighten us, your grace, enlighten us.”

“Ah, that is a tale worth telling. You know the hoopoes that trot about the Prado in Seville, all hooked beaks and cinnamon crests, under the ladies’ feet? Of course you do. The story goes that once, all hoopoes wore solid golden crowns on their heads — and they soon learned the price of that, eh? One day, all the beasts and birds came to pay homage to King Solomon, and the hoopoes came too, with a humble little request. What do you think it was? They begged Solomon to pray that they might trade their golden crowns for any other kind — for mankind hunted them without rest, shooting, trapping, snaring them for that gold, until life was no longer worth living. So Solomon prayed, and their golden crowns turned to feather crests, and ever since then they live as merry as crickets in an oven, and bear the honor of being called his Most Catholic Majesty’s own birds. Tita! Fill the commandant’s glass. Fray Gerundio, what are you whispering about down there, sir?”

Fray Gerundio had just murmured to his fellow Dominican, “O si sic omnia! Would that all gold in the world might turn to feathers the same way!”

“Then,” the other friar replied, sterner of face, “corrupt human nature would only invent some new bauble in a week, to kill and be killed over for no reason.”

“What was that, Fray Gerundio?” the bishop pressed.

“I only said it would be a blessing for the whole world if every man prayed for the same mercy as the hoopoes.”

“The world, sir? What do you know of the world? Go convert your Indians, and leave matters of state to your superiors. You must excuse him, señors,” he added, turning to the Dons and lowering his voice. “A pious, worthy man, but the son of a poor peasant, and — well, you understand. A little soft in the head, I fear, from too much fasting and late night prayers.” He tapped his own forehead knowingly, to signal that Fray Gerundio’s wits were not to be trusted.

The friar heard the insult, and smiled quietly. He was one of those rare, noble souls stirred to courage by the cruelty of his countrymen, like Las Casas and Gasca, who dared protest the evils of their age even before kings. True to his faith, he called the dark-skinned Indigenous and enslaved people his brothers, and defended their God-given freedom at a time when that belief was unheard of. The bishop’s petty insults were old news to him; he took them in silence and sipped his wine, as the conversation turned back to the state of the colony.

“They say,” said the colonel, “that this year’s treasure fleet sailing for Spain will be shamefully small.”

“What else?” the intendant huffed. “What do we have left to send, in the name of all the saints, since those accursed English Lutherans swept the whole place clean?”

“And even if we had treasure to ship,” the master mariner added, “what ships do we have left to carry it? That fiend in human flesh, Drake —”

“Ah!” the bishop winced. “Spare my ears, Don Pedro. Do not speak that man’s name here — it is a Tartarean, devilish name, unfit for polite company. Draco, a dragon, a serpent, the very symbol of Satan himself! He would devour our new Hercules, his Most Catholic Majesty, to get at the golden apples of the West. He deceived Eve with an apple, too — a wicked, wicked name, señors, a name that belongs in hell. Tita!”

“Um!”

“Fill my glass.”

“Nay,” the colonel swore a great oath, “this English serpent is of a far fiercer breed than Eden’s, I’ll warrant.”

“Your reason, señor, your reason?”

“Because this one would have sunk Eve to the bottom of the ocean before he let anyone but himself taste anything that glinted like gold.”

“Ah, very good, very good! But we laugh, brave señors, while the Church weeps. Alas for my poor flock!”

“And alas for their sheepfold! It will take four years to rebuild Cartagena. And Heaven only knows when we will get the blockhouse back up, while the king pours every last coin of the Indies into fending off that supposed English Armada. The town is as bare as an Indian’s back right now.”

“Baptista Antonio the surveyor sent a formal report home to the king with me, laying out how defenseless we are. But writing a report and acting on it are two very different things, as every statesman knows. Heaven grant we get orders to fortify the harbor on the next fleet, or we will be at the mercy of every English pirate who sails by!”

“Ah, that blockhouse!” the bishop sighed. “It was a villainous trick of theirs, demanding a hundred and ten thousand ducats ransom for the town! After they burned and looted half of it already — and made me dine with them, ah! Sit between that serpent Drake and his lieutenant general, drink my own private Xeres wine I had laid down nine years ago, and those shameless heretics even offered to take me back to England if I turned Lutheran, find me a wife, make an honest man of me — ah! And then they demanded even more ransom for the priory and the fort! Perfidious!”

“Well,” the colonel shrugged, “the cunning rascals had us beat on the fine print. We forgot to specify that the ransom only covered the town proper in our agreement. Who would have dreamed they would pull such a trick?”

“So I told the prior when he came to me to borrow a thousand crowns to pay his share. It was Heaven’s will, as sudden as a thunderbolt, to be borne patiently. Every man must bear his own burden. How could I lend him anything?”

“Your grace’s savings were all carried off by them long before,” the intendant said, though he knew perfectly well the bishop had hidden most of his hoard away.

“Just so! All my scanty life savings, stolen from me in my lonely old age. Ah señors, if my dear friend the Marquess of Santa Cruz — whom I pray for daily — had not sent us warning that those wretches were coming, we would have gone down quick into the pit like the sinners of old. I might have saved a little something for myself, but I thought only of others, and forgot my own safety, alas!”

“Warning or no, we had no right to be beaten by a handful of men,” the master mariner grumbled. “It is a shame that will hang over our names for generations.”

“Do you dare cast slurs on the courage and conduct of his Most Catholic Majesty’s soldiers?” the colonel snapped.

“I? No — but we were soundly beaten, even behind our own barricades, that is the plain truth.”

“Beaten, sir? You dare call the fortunes of war a defeat? What more could our governor have done? We lined every road with poisoned caltrops, posted Indian archers to guard the passes, blocked the lanes with earthen ramparts, raked the approaches with culverins and arquebuses! What familiar spirit did we have to warn us those villains would march along the empty sea beach, instead of coming down the high road like proper Christian men?”

“Ah!” the bishop nodded solemnly. “Diabolical intuition, no doubt. Satan himself aids those who serve him, and for my part I can only attribute the disaster to black magic. Those men are possessed by every devil in hell.”

“Well your grace,” the colonel agreed, “there must have been devilry in it. How else would men dare run straight into the mouths of our cannons, fire point blank at our noses, and leap right over those huge earthen ramparts like they were no more than small hurdles?”

“Doubtless driven mad by the fiends inside them,” the bishop confirmed.

“And then they came screaming blasphemies, cutting through us with sword and pike! I saw Lieutenant General Carlisle hew down that noble young ensign, your grace’s sister’s son’s nephew, who was armed cap-a-pie, with a single stroke of his blade. Is that not black magic? And that furious, blaspheming Lutheran Captain Young — I saw him catch our general by the head, even after the illustrious Don Alonzo had given him a grievous wound, throw him to the ground and capture him. Is that not sorcery?”

“Well,” the master mariner cut in, “if you are looking for magic, what do you say to them marching right through the flank fire of our galleys, with eleven of our cannons and two hundred arquebuses firing on them, like it was no more than a swarm of mosquitoes? Some of my men said they fired too high — but that was the English rascals’ trick, lying low on the tidal beach. But señor commandant, even if Satan taught them that trick, did he also teach them to carry pikes a full foot longer than ours?”

“Ah well,” the bishop sighed. “We are sacked. San Domingo is worse off than we are, and St. Augustine in Florida too. All that is left for a poor priest like me is to sail back to Spain, and beg his majesty’s pious clemency to grant a little relief for the poor of Our Lady. Perhaps — who knows? — I might be transferred to a quieter posting, now that I am old and tired after so many labors. Tita! Fill our glasses. I have saved a little something, as you may all have done, from the general wreck. The flock will be well cared for when I am gone, señors. Heaven’s mercies are infinite.
