New cities will rise from the ashes of the old, new mines pour their treasure into the sanctified laps of the faithful, new Indians flock to the life-giving standard of the Cross to take on the easy yoke of the Church, and —”

“And where will I be then? Ah, where? I long to rest, to depart this world. Tita! Hang my hammock. Señors, you must excuse the weaknesses of old age. Fray Gerundio, go to bed!”

The Dons rose to take their leave, as the bishop drowsily maundered to himself: “Farewell. Life is short. We shall meet in heaven at last. Are there really no more pearls left?”

“Not a single oyster, nor a grain of gold either,” the intendant told him.

“Ah well. A dinner of herbs where love is better than — Tita!”

“My breviary — ah, man’s gratitude is so short-lived, I had hoped — Have you seen anything of Doña Bovadilla?”

“No.”

“Ah, she promised — but no matter. A little keepsake, a gold cross, an emerald ring, nothing fancy — I forget. And why would I care for worldly wealth! Tita! Bring me the casket.”

Once his guests were gone, the old man mumbled prayers from his breviary while he fumbled over a hoard of jewels and gold, his eyes dull and greedy with the covetousness of old age.

“This might buy me a cardinal’s red hat yet! *Omnia Romae venalia* — everything is for sale in Rome. Put it away Tita, and do not stare too hard at it, child. Do not fall into temptation. The love of money is the root of all evil; and Heaven, in his love for the Indian, has made him poor in this world so he may be rich in faith. Ah — ugh — so.”

The old miser clambered into his hammock. Tita drew the mosquito net over him, wrapped a second net around her own head, and curled up on the floor to sleep. Master and slave were soon snoring in perfect unison, a droning bass harmony to the high thin whine of the mosquitoes.

It was long past midnight, and the moon had set. The sentries who had tramped their rounds and challenged passers-by until they thought their officers were fast asleep had slipped away from their posts, out of the dark, and fallen asleep as soundly as the bishop.

Two long, narrow white wherries glided out from behind the isolated rocks of Morro Grande, five hundred yards astern of the galleon. They were nearly invisible on the glimmering surface of the bay, their hulls painted bright white, and even a vigilant sentry would only have spotted them by the faint trail of phosphorescent sparks rippling along their sides.

The bishop stirred, half awake. The wine was wearing off, his shoulders had slipped down in the hammock, his heels were in the air, and his head ached. He sat upright, called for Tita, and pointed out the window.

“Put another pillow under my head, child. What is that out there? A fish?”

Tita glanced out. She did not think it was a fish — but she did not say so. She had her own reasons for not waking the bishop.

The

> “All precious things, discover'd late, To them who seek them issue forth; For love in sequel works with fate, And draws the veil from hidden worth.”
> — *The Sleeping Beauty*

So Ayacanora took up formal residence in Lucy’s tiny cabin, recognized by every soul on the ship as a full, accredited member of the crew.

But she proved a most troublesome addition, for that perilous, all-consuming crisis had fallen upon her: the same peril that erodes the bodies and souls of native peoples the moment they first mix with white European society, the same force that would within a few short years hasten the near-extermination of the North American tribes. Were it not for Amyas Leigh’s steady good sense and unshakable constancy, Ayacanora might have met an even more wretched end than the famous Pocahontas, Virginian king’s daughter: who was once received at King James I’s court with honors equal to a sovereign sister, became the fabled ancestress of a dozen old Virginia families, and died in squalor in a cramped Wapping garret.

A savage’s mind, crushed by the overwhelming sight of white men’s superior skill, wealth, and knowledge, loses its self-respect at first. His body, meanwhile, fed on easy luxuries instead of forced to toil for every crust of sustenance, loses its self-reliance. Strip away those two things — self-respect and self-reliance — and all the sparse, fragile virtues of indigenous life vanish too, leaving a wide, easy road that leads straight to begging, theft, drunkenness, and idleness.

Down that very road poor Ayacanora seemed to be speeding at first. The warrior-prophetess of the Omaguas swiftly devolved into what looked for all the world like a very naughty, willful child. The Diana of the Meta, after she’d satisfied her wide-eyed wonder at the great floating house by wandering from deck to deck peeping into every cupboard and crack, developed a terrible habit of stealing and hiding (too proud or too shy to ever ask permission) every trinket that caught her fancy. When Amyas forbade her from taking anything without leave, she threatened to drown herself, and hid in her cabin sulking for the whole rest of the day. Still, she obeyed him — except when it came to sweets. Perhaps she craved the familiar vegetable fare of her Amazon forests; at any rate, the bishop’s stores of dried fruit and sweetmeats dwindled at a shocking rate. Worse still, the small stock of fine Spanish wine Amyas had set aside as a daily cordial for sick Lucy vanished too. Amyas gave her a sharp lecture, telling her how cruel it was to rob a poor invalid, and Ayacanora flew into a rage, threatened to throw herself overboard there and then, and ran for the deck to do it. Amyas caught her before she could climb the rail, and forgave her. She burst into a storm of tears, swore penitence, and made all kinds of good promises. A week later, Amyas found out she’d cheated both her own conscience and the rules: she’d badgered the Portuguese steward into giving her a different supply of wine behind his back. But luck was on his side: the first time she drank too much, she behaved so foolishly through the evening, and woke up with such a splitting headache the next morning, that Amyas’s warnings about wine making men mad came true for her. The whole secret spilled out, and Amyas called for the steward to give the man a sound flogging — but Ayacanora, to her credit, not only begged him off, but offered to take the lash in his place, confessing that the poor wretch had only given her wine because she’d threatened to kill him if he refused, and he’d acted in fear for his life.

That headache of hers, plus Amyas’s quiet, disappointed look, was lesson enough. After one more half-hearted attempt to drown herself, the wilful beauty settled down for a good long stretch — and better yet, she would never, for the rest of her life, willingly touch fermented drink.

Still, Amyas wore himself ragged trying to figure out how to care for a lady who threatened suicide at the slightest provocation. He dared not punish her, even if he’d had the heart to. And as for putting her ashore, he had a deep, un-superstitious instinct that her strange, fierce affection for the English was no accident of Heaven, that God had placed her in his charge, and that he would have to answer for the fate of that lost fair lamb when the time came.

At his wits’ end, he prayed earnestly, many times, for guidance on what to do with her before she killed either herself, or one of the crew — which seemed just as likely. It struck him that the best path forward was to have Parson Jack Brimblecombe teach her the rudiments of the Christian faith, so she could be properly baptized when they sailed home to England.

A new trouble arose instantly: she flatly refused to learn from Jack, or anyone at all, but Amyas himself. And Amyas had a dozen excellent reasons to decline being her schoolmaster. For two more weeks, Ayacanora remained untaught in scripture — though she picked up spoken English with a marvellous, almost unnatural speed.

Worse followed. She developed a violent dislike not only for Parson Jack, whose gait and voice she would openly mimic to make the rest of the crew snort with laughter, but for Will Cary, whom she would not allow to speak to her or come within arm’s length. Perhaps she was jealous of his close friendship with Amyas; or perhaps, with a woman’s quiet instinct, she guessed he was the only other man on board who might dare to court her (though Will, to do him justice, had no such intention whatsoever). When remonstrated with, her only reply was that Cary was a cacique — chief — just like Amyas, and no ship could have two caciques. One day she even outright proposed that Amyas kill his supposed rival, and keep the whole ship for himself. She sulked for three days straight when Amyas, to the crew’s howls of laughter, repeated her precious advice straight to Will’s face.

She also showed unutterable, unprovoked repugnance for the Black members of the crew, at first hiding from them whenever she could, then throwing every loose object within reach at their heads, until the poor men, fearing for their lives, complained to Amyas, who finally got her to leave them alone.

Over the rest of the sailors, she ruled like a born princess, calling them away from their work to run her errands and carve her little toys, and enforcing her commands with a sharp, well-placed box on the ear when they dragged their feet. The good fellows, especially old Salvation Yeo, doted on her, obeyed her, and jested with her — much as one might treat a tamed leopard, whose claws could spring out to catch you unawares at any moment. She amused them all, and she amused Amyas too. Sailors always needed a ship’s pet, and no pet they could have found was half so pretty. For Amyas, her constant presence, even the constant worry of her wilfulness, kept his mind occupied, and chased away the heavy, brooding melancholy that had haunted him ever since he set sail: the dread of facing his mother, of telling her the terrible tragedy that had befallen his brother, the weight that would have turned all his worldly success and miraculous deliverance to worthless ash in his mouth if he’d been left to dwell on it unbroken.

At last, matters came to a head — as most lucky things do.

The ship slipped steadily day after day before a fair, favorable breeze. They’d passed the ring of the West India islands, and were now crawling safely out of sight of any pursuit, through the vast tangled weed beds of the Sargasso Sea. There, for the first time, the captains decided it was safe to relax the strict discipline they’d enforced all voyage, and “rummage” their captured Spanish prize ship. The gold, silver, jewels, and merchandise they found on board would interest no reader here. Suffice it to say the hoard was large enough, after paying out all agreed shares to the crew, Will Cary’s full cut, and Mr. Salterne’s third as the ship’s owner, to make Amyas wealthy for the rest of his life. Tucked away in the dead captain’s private cabin, they found two large chests: one full of gorgeous Mexican featherwork robes, the other stuffed full of fine Spanish and East Indian ladies’ finery, bound for a noblewoman in Caracas after stopping in Havana and Cartagena. At Will Cary’s suggestion, the crew voted by acclamation to give both chests to Ayacanora, as her fair share of the plunder, to reward her Amazonian courage and her invaluable help in the fight.

The poor girl seized the treasure with greedy delight, had the chests carried straight to Lucy’s cabin, and knelt poring over the contents for hours at a time. She scorned the Mexican featherwork as “savage” fit only for the Omaguas; but the Spanish dresses were a treasure beyond her wildest dreams. For three days straight she paraded the quarterdeck like a peacock, decked out in Seville mantillas, oversize Madrid hats, and stiff Indian brocade farthingales, all worn in the most absurd, mismatched ways possible.

The crew snickered behind their hands. Amyas felt far more like crying. Nothing is more heartbreaking than a child’s vanity, save a grown person aping a child’s vanity, and nothing more pitiable than the moment they realize everyone is laughing at them, not admiring them. Amyas wanted to speak up, but he was afraid to break her heart. The farce went on, as far as Ayacanora was concerned, perfectly successfully, until the end of the third day. Unable to contain her pride any longer, she turned to Amyas and asked, “Ayacanora is quite English girl now, is she not?”

She heard a snicker behind her, spun around, and saw a dozen honest tars grinning broadly. She understood it all in an instant. She darted down the companion ladder and vanished.

Amyas, fully convinced she would throw herself overboard, ran after her as fast as he could. She’d locked herself in the cabin with Lucy, and he could hear her heaving, violent sobs, and Lucy’s faint, weak voice begging her to say what had gone wrong.

He knocked in vain. She refused to come out all day, and by evening they had no choice but to break the door down, lest poor Lucy starve to death trapped inside. Ayacanora was there, half her finery torn off and scattered across the floor in a fit of rage, crying as if her heart would break; poor Lucy was crying too, half from fear and hunger, half from sympathy for the girl.

Amyas tried to soothe her, swore the men would never laugh at her again. “But then,” he added, “you must not be so — so —” He could not find the right word.

“So what?” she asked, crying even more bitterly.

“So like a wild girl, Ayacanora.”

Her hands dropped to her knees. A sharp, painful spasm ran through her throat and chest, and she fell on her knees before him, looking up at his face with imploring eyes.

“Yes — wild girl. Poor bad wild girl. But I will be English girl now.”

“Fine clothes will never make you English, my child,” Amyas said gently.

“No! Not English clothes — English heart! Good heart, like yours! Yes, I will be good, and Sir John shall teach me!”

“There’s my good maid,” Amyas said. “Sir John shall start teaching you tomorrow.”

“No! Now! Now! Ayacanora cannot wait. She will drown herself if she is bad another day! Come, now!”

She made him fetch Brimblecombe at once, sat patiently listening to the good man for more than an hour, and told Lucy every word he’d said that very night. From that day on, whenever Jack went in to read and pray with the sick girl Lucy, Ayacanora no longer slipped away to the deck. She stood by patiently, trying to make sense of every word, knelt when he knelt, and tried to pray too — that she might have a good English heart. And her prayers, dumb as they were, were surely not unheard.

Things went on smoothly, full of hope, for several days, until one morning just after they’d sailed clear of the Sargasso weed beds. The ship sailed on under steady breeze, the men lounged on deck under the canvas awning mending nets, patching clothes, and chatting. Brimblecombe was catechising his new pupil in the cabin. Amyas and Will Cary, cigars in their mouths, were chatting about every subject under the sun — and above all, planning their next adventure against the Spaniards as soon as they returned to England, while Amyas poured out the dark, seething hatred he held for the whole Spanish nation, the grim purpose of avenging his brother and Rose Salterne that had settled like murky fog into every cranny of his heart.

Suddenly there was a scuffle and a shout from below that made both men leap to their feet. Jack Brimblecombe came rushing up on deck, holding his head on with both hands.

“Save me! Save me from that she-fiend! She is possessed of a whole legion of demons! She has broken my nose, torn out half my hair — and I had none to spare to begin with! Here she comes! Stand by me, gentlemen both! Satanas, I defy thee!” He ducked behind Amyas and Will, as Ayacanora whirled onto the deck like a raging Maenad, and skidded to a stop the moment she saw Amyas.

“If you’d defied Satan down in the cabin,” Will said, laughing, “I suspect he would never have burst out on you so boldly, Master Jack.”

“I am innocent! Innocent as the unborn babe!” Jack sputtered indignantly, while Amyas asked what on earth had happened.

“He looked at me,” Ayacanora said sturdily.

“Well, a cat may look at a king,” Amyas said, bewildered.

“But he shall not look at Ayacanora. Nobody shall but you, or I’ll kill him!”

Jack protested his innocence, swore he had not even glanced at her, but the fancy had seized her and would not shift. She refused to go below for any more lessons that day. Jack wandered off grumbling, his scalp bleeding, and walked around with a black eye for the whole next week.

“At all events,” Will said, re-lighting his cigar, “that’s a fault on the right side.”

“God give me grace,” Amyas said quietly, “or it may turn out a fault on the very wrong side for me.”

“He will, old heart-of-oak!” Will said, throwing his arm around Amyas’s neck. The gesture visibly disgusted Ayacanora, who stomped off to the ship’s rail, fetched a fishing line, and amused herself dangling it over the side. The ship slipped on quietly and silently, only broken when Ayacanora laughed and clapped her hands watching flying fish dart away from pursuing bonitos. At last, tired of doing nothing, she wandered forward to the poop rail to listen to John Squire the armorer, who sat tinkering a steel headpiece and humming a song he’d rewritten to fit his native home:
> “Oh, Bideford is a pleasant place, it shines where it stands,
> And the more I look upon it, the more my heart it warms;
> For there are fair young lasses, in rows upon the quay,
> To welcome gallant mariners, when they come home from say.”

“Tis Sunderland the song’s about, not Bideford, John Squire,” his mate teased.

“Bideford’s just as good as Sunderland any day, even if we’ve no sea-coal blacking up the roads,” John Squire said. “Rymes just the same, Tommy Hamblyn —
> “Oh, if I was a herring, to swim the ocean o’er,
> Or if I was a say-dove, to fly unto the shoor,
> To fly unto my true love, a waiting at the door,
> To wed her with a goold ring, and plough the main no moor.”

Salvation Yeo interrupted then. “Aren’t you ashamed at your age, John Squire, singing such carnal vanities, after all the providences the Lord has shown us? Sing the songs of Zion, man, if you’re determined to make a caterwauling noise all day long.”

“Well then, you sing ’em yourself, gunner,” John Squire challenged.

“And why shouldn’t I?” Yeo pulled out his worn psalm book, and struck up a rousing verse of the old hymn:
> “Such as in ships and brittle barks Into the seas descend,
> Their merchandise through fearful floods To compass and to end;
> There men are forced to behold The Lord's works what they be;
> And in the dreadful deep the same, Most marvellous they see.”

“Humph,” John Squire grumbled. “Very good and godly, but still I do love a merry catch now and then. Wouldn’t you let a body sing ‘Rumbelow’ even when heaving up the anchor?”

“I don’t know,” Yeo said. “But the Lord’s people ought to praise Him then too, and pray for a good voyage, instead of howling out —
> “A randy, dandy, dandy O,
> A whet of ale and brandy O,
> With a rumbelow and a Westward-ho!
> And heave, my mariners all, O!”

“Is that fit talk for immortal souls? How does that child’s trumpery sound next to the Psalms, John Squire?”

As it happened, Salvation Yeo had sung that old shanty in full, rich nasal tone, just to mock the silly thing — the exact same way he’d sung it a dozen years prior, heaving up John Oxenham’s anchor in Plymouth Sound. And Ayacanora, standing at Amyas’s side trying to follow the men’s chatter, heard it, started, and half to herself, picked up the strain, singing every word perfectly, in the exact same tune and tone.

Salvation Yeo jumped, and turned deathly pale.

“Who sung that?” he demanded sharply.

“The little maid here,” Amyas said. “She’s coming on wonderfully well with her English.”

“The little maid?” Yeo said, paler still. “Why do you try to scare an old man talking about little ma

*It fell about the Martinmas, when nights were lang and mirk, That wife's twa sons cam hame again, And their hats were o' the birk. It did na graw by bush or brae, Nor yet in ony shough; But by the gates o' paradise That birk grew fair eneugh.*
— The Wife of Usher's Well

It is the evening of February 15, 1587, and Mrs. Leigh—whom we return to now, to the familiar West Country lanes and faces of Burrough House—paced slow, measured turns along the terrace walk, gazing out across the winding Torridge, the hazy sandhills, and the wide, heaving western sea. This was no new ritual for her: for three long, weary years, fair weather or foul, she had kept this vigil. Three years and more had slipped by, and still there was no word of Frank, of Amyas, of their gallant ship the *Rose*, or all the brave souls who had sailed out of Bideford harbor aboard her. Loving eyes across the coast, in Bideford, Appledore, Clovelly, and Ilfracombe, had grown hollow from watching and weeping for the crews who had sailed west, just as John Oxenham had sailed before them, and vanished like a half-forgotten dream into the vast unknown.

There had been one fleeting flash of hope, once. Good Sir Richard Grenville, without Mrs. Leigh’s knowledge, had dispatched a horseman riding hard for Plymouth the moment news arrived that Drake, Frobisher, and Carlisle had returned from raiding the Spanish Main. The messenger brought back glorious tidings of the sacking of Cartagena, San Domingo, and Saint Augustine, of the relief of Raleigh’s struggling Virginian colony—but no word at all of the *Rose*. Mrs. Leigh had bowed her head in quiet worship, and whispered, “The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord.”

Her hair was fully grey now, her cheeks wan, her step slow and unsteady. She seldom left Burrough save for church, or to visit the sick and poor in the neighboring cottages. She never spoke her sons’ names aloud, never let a single word slip that might hint she still ached for them—but every high tide, when the red flag flew from the sandhills to mark the bar was deep enough for passage, she paced that terrace, devouring the horizon with hungry eyes in search of a sail that never came. Stately merchant ships came and went as they always had; white sails hung off the bar for hours, day after day, month after month, year after year, but some instinct deep in her bones told her none were the sails she sought. She knew the *Rose* down to every line of her hull, the cut of every sail, could have picked her out miles off across a whole fleet—but she never came. And Mrs. Leigh would bow her head again, and turn to walk among the poor, who had come to see her as a sacred figure, a woman God had drawn near to Him through the mysterious, fiery depths of grief they all knew too well. Lone widows and bedridden men would look into her steady, quiet eyes and draw strength from them, even though she never spoke of her own sorrow—for they could tell she had walked unharmed through the hottest part of the furnace, at the side of One whose form is like unto the Son of God. All the while, she blamed herself for her “earthly” longings, confessing nightly to the Lord the weakness she could not shake off, the one that drew her feet to the terrace beneath the wind-bent line of trees every time the tide rose high.

This evening, though, the whole village of Northam is in an uproar. The pebble ridge thunders far below, just as it did years before, but the village is loud even over the roar of the surf: the church tower rocks with pealing bells, crowds throng the streets shouting and dancing around bonfires, burning effigies of the Pope, drinking healths to the Queen and roaring “So perish all her enemies!” Bonfires blaze red on every village hill for miles, and the bells of Bideford far off answer Northam’s chimes, just as they did seven years prior when Amyas sailed home from circumnavigating the globe. News has just arrived: Mary Queen of Scots has been beheaded at Fotheringay. All of England, like a man shaking off a decades-long nightmare, has burst into one thunderous shout of jubilation, for the terror and danger of seventeen years of Catholic plots and treason has been lifted from the nation’s heart forever.

She is gone now, to answer to a higher tribunal than the English Estates for all the noble English blood spilled in her name, for all the hearts she tempted into treachery, rebellion, and murder. Elizabeth’s own words, long-delayed, have at last come to pass:
*The daughter of debate, That discord aye doth sow, Hath reap'd no gain where former rule Hath taught still peace to grow.*

She can work no more harm now. The murder, the adultery, the heart that knew no forgiveness, the tongue that could not speak truth even to save itself, are gone, and perhaps atoned for. Her fair, famous face lingers as a sad, half-pitied dream even in the memories of those who knew full well that either she or Protestant England must fall.

For all her staunch Protestant faith, Mrs. Leigh breathes a quiet prayer that the Lord may have mercy on that soul Mary once described as “clear as a diamond, and as hard.” Surely the final moments before the block could not have been all performance? Seven years of sorrow had taught Mrs. Leigh lessons beyond price; might not seventeen years of imprisonment have taught Mary Stuart some small wisdom too? A former courtier, Mrs. Leigh knew well the godless, profligate French court culture the young Mary had been raised in, among the Medicis, the Guises, and Cardinal Lorraine—and she shuddered, and sighed to herself, “To whom little is given, of them shall little be required!” But still the bells pealed on, and would not cease.

Then, out in the fast-darkening twilight, something answered them. A flash, then the thunder of a gun fired at sea.

Mrs. Leigh froze. The flash was right outside the bar. It could not be a ship in distress: the wind was light and westerly, the tide a high spring flood, perfect for entering the harbor. Another flash. Another gun. The noisy crowds in Northam fell silent at once, all hurrying to the churchyard that overlooks the wide mudflats and the river.

Outside the bar rode a gallant large ship, nearly a thousand tons, sailing in under full sail, her rigging nothing like an English craft’s. For a moment the crowd panicked: was this a Spanish cruiser come to make reprisals for Drake’s raid on Cadiz? But no—no Spanish Don would dare such a reckless, unorthodox bit of warfare. If they came, they would come with full admirals, transports, and dispatches, following every approved rule of war, not a single rogue craft slipping in unannounced.

Drifting fast on the fair westerly wind and flooding tide, she glided up the channel between the two lines of sandhills, drawing level with Appledore. She was no enemy, and even if she were a foreigner, she was a daring one: she had never struck her topsails, the required courtesy every foreign ship must show within sight of an English port, or risk being treated as hostile. Everyone there knew the old story: years before, a Spanish admiral sailed into Plymouth Sound in peacetime, his flag flying high without so much as lowering it, come to fetch Anne of Austria, Philip II’s new bride, home from Flanders. Port Admiral John Hawkins, that old lion of the sea, rushed out in his famous ship *Jesus of Lubec*, fired a shot across his bows, and when the admiral paid no mind, ran alongside and put a shot straight through his hull. The Spanish flag came down at once, apologies were made, and Hawkins did not accept them for days, so fiercely did he guard the Queen’s honor. If Hawkins would do that to a Spanish fleet in peacetime, there were a dozen old sea dogs in Appledore who would do the same to any hostile ship in wartime, even if they only had an old iron pot to burn their gunpowder in.

The strange sail vanished behind the hill of Appledore, and a cheer burst up from a hundred throats, ringing across the quiet evening. Another gun boomed off the water, and another cheer. For twenty minutes, Mrs. Leigh stood perfectly still, a pale, scarcely breathing statue, her eyes fixed on the Viking rock at the harbor mouth.

At last the ship rounded the dark Hubbastone rocks, turning up the Bideford river. Drums, fifes, shawms, and trumpets blared from her decks, waking echoes off every wooded knoll and slate ledge along the shore. As she came full into view of Burrough House, another cheer burst from her crew, rolling a full mile up the hills from the silver surface of the river below.

Mrs. Leigh turned and walked fast back to the house, calling for her maid. “Grace, bring me my hood. Master Amyas is come home!”

“Praise the Lord, madam, what joy! But how did you know?” Grace gasped.

“I heard his voice over the water. But I did not hear Mr. Frank’s with him, Grace.”

“Oh, never fear, madam—they were never parted. Where one goes, the other follows, you’ll see. Here is your hood, madam.”

And the two set off at a quick pace for Bideford. Was it true? Was it some trick of her overtaxed mind? Or did a mother’s divine instinct truly let her pick out her son’s voice over the din of a whole ship’s crew, from a mile and more away? Grace turned the question over in her head a dozen times on the walk to the quay, but when they reached the end of Bridgeland Street—then lined with rope-walks and sailmakers’ shops, long before the Virginia trade tobacco warehouses were built—the question answered itself.

A great crowd rounded the corner: sailors, housewives, apprentices, hurrahing, laughing, weeping, shouting. When they spotted Mrs. Leigh, the whole procession came to a stop.

“Here she is!” someone shouted. “Amyas Leigh’s mother!”

“His mother? Not *their* mother,” Mrs. Leigh thought, turning pale, but her heart was long past breaking now.

The next moment, Amyas’s giant frame, head and shoulders above every other man in the crowd, rounded the corner. “Make way! Make room for Madam Leigh!” the crowd roared, and Amyas dropped to his knees in the mud at her feet.

She threw her arms around his neck, and bent her grey head over his, while the rough sailors, apprentices, and weathered harbor women fell into a reverent hush, forming a quiet circle around mother and son. Mrs. Leigh asked no question at first: she could see at a glance he was alone.

At last he whispered, “I would have died to save him, mother, if I could.”

“You need not tell me that, Amyas Leigh, my son.”

Another long silence. “How did he die?” she whispered.

“He is a martyr. He died in the—” Amyas could not finish the words.

“The Inquisition?”

“Yes.”

A sharp shudder ran through Mrs. Leigh’s frame, then she lifted her head, steady again. “Come home, Amyas. I little expected such an honor—such an honor, to have a son who died a martyr! A very St. Stephen! God, have mercy on me, and let me not go mad before these folk, when I ought to be thanking Thee for Thy great mercies! Amyas, who is that?” She pointed to Ayacanora, the wild Indian girl standing close at Amyas’s heels, watching the whole scene with sharp, curious eyes.

“She is a poor wild Indian girl—my daughter, I call her. I will tell you her full story later.”

“Your daughter? Then she is my granddaughter. Come hither, child, and be my granddaughter.”

Ayacanora came obediently, and knelt, because she had seen Amyas kneel.

“Child, no—do not kneel to me. Come home with us, and let me find out if I am awake or dreaming, alive or dead.” She pulled her hood over her face, turned to walk back, holding Amyas tight by one hand, and Ayacanora by the other. The crowd let them go twenty yards in reverent silence, then burst into a cheer that made the old stone houses of Bideford ring.

Mrs. Leigh stopped short suddenly. “I forgot, Amyas. I will not stand in the way of your duty. Where are your men?”

“Half of them are half-kissed to death by the harbor lasses by now, all the ones that are left.”

“Left?”

“We sailed out a hundred strong, mother. We came home forty-four, all told. Is this real? Is this you? Is this old Bridgeland Street again? As I live, there is Evans the smith, standing in his doorway tankard in hand, just as he did when I was a boy!”

The brawny smith crossed the street to greet them, but stopped short when he saw Amyas alone, no Frank at his side. “Better one than none, madam,” he said, offering rough comfort. Amyas shook his hand, but Mrs. Leigh did not see or hear him at all.

“Mother,” Amyas said, when they crossed the causeway, “we are rich for life.”

“Yes. A martyr’s death was the fittest end for him.”

“I brought home treasure untold. Cary has promised to see to it all tonight.”

“Very well. I only wish Frank could have come home to sleep under our roof again. He was such a kind lad, he loved Frank so—when did he—”

“Died three years ago, within two months of us sailing.”

“Ah, yes. He came to visit me in my sleep, many a time. But you never came. I guessed how it would be, as it should be.”

“But I loved you none the less, mother!”

“I know that, too. You were busy caring for your men, you see, sweet, so your spirit could not wander home to me like his, which was set free. All is as it should be. My maid, do you not find the English cold, after all those hot southern lands?”

“Ayacanora’s heart is warm. She does not think about cold.”

“Warm? Perhaps you will warm my heart for me, then.”

“Would God I could, mother!” Amyas said, half-reproachful.

Mrs. Leigh looked up at his face, and burst into a flood of long-held tears. “Sinful, sinful that I am, to grieve so!”

“Blessed, blessed creature!” Amyas cried, “if you speak that way I shall go mad. I have dreaded this meeting for months, it hung over me like a great black thundercloud, a cliff miles high with its top hidden in mist that I had to climb and dared not. I longed to leap overboard and flee into the sea like a coward, for fear you would ask me if I was not my brother’s keeper, and demand his blood at my hands. Now that it has come, to find you all love, and trust, and patience—mother, it is more than I can bear!” He broke down, weeping so hard his broad shoulders shook.

Mrs. Leigh knew Amyas well enough to understand that such an outburst from his normally quiet, steady nature meant months of terrible hidden struggle, and she forgot all her own grief instantly to soothe him. They walked home arm in arm, while Ayacanora clung fast to the hem of Mrs. Leigh’s cloak like a small child, the bold self-reliance she had learned in the South American forests melting away into shy, trembling modesty, newly arrived in a strange world of strange faces, strange hopes, and strange fears.

“Will your mother love me?” she whispered to Amyas as they stepped through the door of Burrough House.

“Yes. But you must do whatever she tells you.”

Ayacanora pouted. “She will laugh at me, because I am wild.”

“She never laughs at anyone.”

“Humph,” Ayacanora said. “Then I will not be afraid of her. I thought she would be tall, like you. She is not even as big as me.” That did not sound promising for her obedience, but before twenty-four hours had passed, Mrs. Leigh had won her over completely. The girl later explained she had expected the great hero Amyas to have a mother as grand and terrible as the goddess Juno, and instead had found a soft, healing angel.

Her story was soon told to Mrs. Leigh, who, true to her kind heart, never once doubted the girl’s royal Indian identity. The sweet mother had never pressed a prouder, fonder kiss to her son’s forehead than the one she gave him after he confessed he had kept his charge, guarding Ayacanora unspotted, like a gentleman and a Christian.

“Then you have forgiven me, mother?” Amyas asked.

“Years ago, right in this very room, I said ‘what shall I render to the Lord, for giving me two such sons?’ I say it again now. Tell me every last detail of my other son, so that I may honor him as I honor you.”

With the iron strength good women carry in their bones, she made Amyas recount every detail, from Lucy Passmore’s tale to the terrible last night at Guayra. When he finished, she led Ayacanora upstairs to tend to her comforts, moving as calmly as if Frank and Amyas were still small boys sleeping in their cribs in the next room.

She had barely gone upstairs when a loud bang at the front door was followed by Sir Richard Grenville striding unceremoniously into the hall, tall, stately, his usual composure utterly gone. Amyas dropped to his knees instinctively. The stern old warrior was quite unmanned, and as he bent over his godson, a tear dropped from his weathered iron cheek onto Amyas’s own.

“My lad! My glorious lad! Where have you been? Get up, tell me every word. The sailors told me bits and pieces, but I must hear the whole story. I knew you would do something grand. I told your mother you were too good a workman for God to throw away. Truth be told, I ran three quarters of the way here, I was in

“The daughter of debate, That discord still doth sow, Shall reap no gain where former rule Hath taught still peace to grow. No foreign banish'd wight Shall anker in this port Our realm it brooks no stranger's force; Let them elsewhere resort.”
— Queen Elizabeth, 1569

Amyas Leigh had settled soft and quiet at his Northam home, and for the next full twelve months little happened worthy of marking down in these pages. Salvation Yeo had installed himself as unofficial majordomo, with no fixed duties save to trail at Amyas’s heels everywhere he went, lanky and gray as a wolfhound at his master’s flank, and spend every evening huddled by the fireside. The old sailor kept his Bible open on his knee, his calloused hands carving endless trinkets, some useful, some utterly frivolous, for every member of the household—most especially Ayacanora. Every week he would humbly press some tiny carved toy fit only for a small child on her, at which she would pout and stamp her foot, get a gentle rebuke from Mrs. Leigh, then accept the gift without a word and tuck it away in a chest she would never open again.

Her whole soul was bent on becoming a proper English maid. She shadowed Mrs. Leigh from dawn to dusk, pestering her to teach her the secrets of the still-room, the rules of the kitchen larder, and above all the art of cutting and stitching clothes, until at last she was sewing gowns and smocks for half the poor households across Northam. First she would be a good housewife, just like Mrs. Leigh; then a new, strange idea had dawned on her: the joy of giving to those who had nothing to give back in return. She had been raised to the boundless open-handed hospitality of the Amazonian wilds, but this quiet, unselfish charity was something new entirely.

She watched Mrs. Leigh spend every spare hour stitching for the village poor, trundling out to visit them in their drafty cottages. She saw Amyas, the day after public thanks were given in church for his safe return from the seas, hand out gold, sacks of meal, casks of ale to the folk of Northam, Appledore, and Bideford, and buy up small cottages to turn them into almshouses for worn-out old mariners who could no longer sail. They told her this was his thank-offering to God. She was puzzled at first; her own idea of a thank-offering, shared by the Indians and the Spaniards both, was ritual sacrifices and decking the Great Spirit’s temple with the skulls of captives. Even plain, church-going Amyas did not neglect that old instinct to give something back to the Church: soon the Northam vicar was wearing a splendid new surplice, and the altar was graced with a rich silver flagon and salver looted from the great Spanish galleon, plate that would be lost forever in the civil wars decades later. Ayacanora could understand that much, but the almsgiving stumped her until Mrs. Leigh explained, in her soft simple way, that whoever gave to the poor gave to the Great Spirit, for the Great Spirit dwelt in every one of them, and in Ayacanora too, if only she would be still and listen, instead of pouting and stamping and doing only as she pleased. The girl took the new truth in like a child, and worked her fingers raw sewing for every old widow in the parish, wandering the lanes at Mrs. Leigh’s side, lovely and much loved, darting quick glances from under her long black eyelashes to see if she had won a smile from Amyas. On the days she earned that smile she was good and gentle from dawn till dusk; on the days she did not, she was sullen and mischievous enough to try the patience of any soul less calm and chaste than Mrs. Leigh’s.

She would not be reined in when it came to the glory of her dress. Every Sunday she swept into church draped in plundered Spanish silks and Aztec featherwork, rustling and glowing so bright the good vicar was forced to gently remonstrate with Mrs. Leigh, for she distracted the eyes and thoughts of every soul in the congregation. When Ayacanora heard of his complaint, she tossed her head and said she was not thinking of them, so they need not think of her either. If their “Piache” (as she mistakenly called the vicar, meaning conjuror) wanted a present, he could have all her Mexican feather robes—she would not wear them, for they were wild Indian things and she was an English maid now, but they would suit him perfectly. She darted upstairs, dragged the whole bundle down, and insisted so stoutly on draping the flustered vicar in the feather mantles that he beat a hasty retreat. Still, he carried off the finest one of the lot, and stitched it cleverly into an altar cloth. For years afterwards, the communion table at Northam blazed with emerald, sky blue, and crimson that had once draped the shoulders of a dead Aztec prince.

So Ayacanora flaunted on, while Amyas watched her half amused, half simply proud of her rare beauty, glancing around at all the staring villagers as if to say, “See what a fine bird I have brought home from across the seas!”

She caused Mrs. Leigh no end of trouble with the local gentry. All the ladies of the shire came calling, of course, both to congratulate Mrs. Leigh and to get a peek at the famous “fair savage” brought home by the local hero. But Ayacanora snubbed them all so thoroughly, from the vicar’s wife all the way up to Lady Grenville herself, that few dared attempt a second visit. When Mrs. Leigh remonstrated with her, the girl burst into floods of tears, protesting “They only come to gawk at a poor wild Indian girl. I will not be made a show of. I was a queen once, and everyone obeyed me. But here everyone looks down on me.” Yet when Mrs. Leigh asked her if she would rather go back to the wild Amazon forests, the poor child clung to her like a toddler, begging not to be sent away. “I would sooner be a slave in your kitchen here than go back to those bad, cruel people.”

Month after month drifted by, full of silly small storms and sunlit interludes, but all the while Ayacanora dwelt under Mrs. Leigh’s soft, steady influence—no sudden storms, no fiery outbursts, only a perpetual genial calm that softened every soul who came within its reach. Her tantrums grew rarer and rarer, and Ayacanora grew more rational day by day, though never quite fully happy.

One by one, small hints slipped out that confirmed her true identity to Mrs. Leigh and Yeo. After she had grown used to the sight of stone houses, she let them know she had seen buildings like that long before. The red English cattle seemed familiar to her, too, but the sheep puzzled her for weeks until she announced they were absurdly tiny. “Ah, madam!” Yeo cried, latching onto that clue, “That’s because she’s used to those great camel-sheep the Peruvians call llamas!”

But the thing that delighted her more than anything else was horses. The very idea of a tame riding animal was a daily wonder to her, and she pestered Amyas for a mount until he finally caved and bought her a fine jennet, for fear she would steal one from a neighbor otherwise. She learned to ride so fast that in a fortnight she was galloping across the moors as expertly as any South American gaucho. That was when a long-buried memory woke at last.

One day the whole household rode over to Stow Hall at Lady Grenville’s invitation: Mrs. Leigh rode sedately on a pillion behind the groom, while Ayacanora cantered circles around the party like a hound let off its leash, trying to goad Amyas into racing her. That night, sharing a room with Mrs. Leigh, she woke shrieking, sobbing out a long dream where the “Old Ape of Panama,” the cruel stepfather she hated, had dragged her from her bed and set her on horseback, riding with her over high moors and Andes peaks down into a dark wood, where the horses and the Indian man vanished and she found herself a tiny lost savage child again. The memory was so vivid they could not persuade her it had not really happened, and Mrs. Leigh soon decided it was a true vision from God, revealing that Don Xararte, jealous that she was the child of the captured English privateer Oxenham, had abandoned her in the forests beyond Mount Cotopaxi to die. No one in the household doubted the truth of it.

Other small fragments of her past emerged slowly, all tied to some animal or sight of the natural world. The long years wandering the wild forests had wiped away all memory of her mother, any early lessons of love or morality. That missing piece finally clicked into place at the baptism of the Virginia Indian Wanchese, whom Sir Richard Grenville had brought back from his New World colony. The man was to be baptized at Bideford Church, given the Christian name Raleigh, with Sir Walter Raleigh and Sir Richard Grenville standing as his godfathers. All the town’s notables turned out to see the first “red man” ever baptized on British soil, the mayor and corporation parading in their full robes with maces aloft to honor the first fruits of the Gospel being brought to the West.

Mrs. Leigh went as a matter of course, and Ayacanora tagged along too, curious to see what they intended to do with the “Carib.” When they told her they were going to make him a Christian, she pouted and demanded to know why they had not done the same for her. They assured her she was surely already a Christian, baptized as an infant, but she was not so certain, and grumbled that the ugly foreign red man was getting a privilege she might not have.

The congregation gathered, the tall stately son of the forest stood at the font, and the service was halfway done when a heavy groan burst from the crowd. Ayacanora had fainted clean away, collapsing into Mrs. Leigh’s arms. They carried her out to a nearby house, and when she woke, she told them the strange story: as she stood there trying to remember if she had ever stood at a font like that, the church around her had grown huge, the priest’s vestments grew rich and glowing, the walls were lined with gilded paintings, and above the altar a jeweled statue of the Virgin Mary held a baby in her arms. Soft music rang in her ears, the air was thick with sweet incense like mist, and through it she saw dozens of other native Indians standing at the font, a noble lady holding her own small hand, and she was a tiny little girl again.

After hours of gentle questioning, Yeo thumped his fist on the table and declared she spoke the truth. “She was christened, madam, in the grand cathedral at Quito. I know that building well, too well—once I was forced to stand there three whole hours in a penitent’s San Benito robe, listening to a friar preach his Popish lies, not knowing if I’d be burned alive at the stake the next morning.”

So Ayacanora went home to Burrough, and Raleigh the Indian went to Sir Richard’s service. His baptism entry still survives, scrawled in wobbly hand on the old parchment Bideford parish register for 1587: *“Raleigh, a Winganditoian: March 26.”* A year and a month later, another entry appears, not in the baptism register: *“Rawly, a Winganditoian, April 1589.”* The free forest wanderer had pined to death, homesick for his deer hunts in the fragrant cedar woods and lazy paddles across lagoons dotted with white water lilies. He was buried in the narrow, crowded Bideford churchyard, hemmed in by dingy town houses whose tenants would never waste a single tear on his grave. The two old entries stand there still, a quiet, pathetic emblem of the sad fate awaiting the whole displaced red race, to whom civilization came too late to save them, but soon enough to hasten their decay.

Though Amyas kept his promise to his mother to stay home for twelve months, England did not rest. That spring, Sir Walter Raleigh sent a larger, new fleet of colonists to Virginia under the command of John White. Raleigh had written again and again to beg Amyas to take command of the expedition, and if he had done so, the history of what would become the United States might well have begun twenty years earlier than it did. But Amyas had given his solemn word to his grieving widowed mother to stay home with her for at least a year, and who could blame her for asking, or him for agreeing? Instead of sailing himself, Amyas sent five hundred pounds of his galleon prize money to fund the colony, money that never came back to him, and must still lie, at least in spirit, buried somewhere on the Virginia coast.

But soon a sharper trial came that tested Amyas’s promise, making him moody, peevish, and restless for the first time in his life, furious that other men were fighting Spaniards while he sat idle at home. His whole soul was filling with a slow, sullen, burning hatred for Don Guzman de Soto. His single, simple purpose was fraying, and a dark shadow settled over him day and night. No companion could feed that shadow more than Salvation Yeo, who grew more fiercely fanatical day by day, and found in Amyas a too willing listener. Mrs. Leigh was seriously angry, for perhaps the only time in her whole gentle life, when she walked in on the two of them calmly debating how they had committed a grievous sin by not killing every last Spanish prisoner they had rescued from the galleon.

It must be said, as all the plain facts of this history prove, that if the English held that bitter temper in those days, the Spaniards had given them no end of cause to feel so, and were then preparing to do far worse. The year 1588 was fast approaching, a year an astronomer in Konigsberg had predicted more than a century earlier would shake the world, and German chronologers had called the climacterical year of all human history. The prophecies proved true enough. That was the year that would decide once and for all the fate of every European nation, and of the whole vast American continent.

It was no wonder that, like in every great crisis of human history, a strange awed instinct spread across the land that the Day of the Lord was at hand, that a great war was waged in heaven between fiends of darkness and angels of light fighting for the souls of men. No conjunctions of planets were needed for wise men to see that the long, scattered, half-hidden duel between Spain and England was about to erupt into a final, deathly grapple. The war had so far been confined to the Netherlands, the West Indies, and the coasts of Africa, places where Spain had no rightful claim, or had forfeited it through cruelty. But in 1585, the Spaniards struck without warning, laying an embargo on every English merchant ship that sailed into their European ports, seizing them all to add to the build of a massive, world-ending armada. They claimed they were assembling the fleet to crush the rebellious Dutch, but Queen Elizabeth, her ministers, and at last the English people knew better: they were coming to crush England. Drake, Frobisher, and Carlisle swept the Spanish Main with fire and sword, cutting off their supply of gold from the Americas, while the crafty, honest Walsingham arranged a financial gambit that stopped the Venetian merchants from lending Spain the money they needed to build their fleet, buying the nation a year of reprieve.

Across Europe, the Jesuits preached openly to their followers of their true goal: to crush the heretic English, sink their sacrilegious ships blessed by the Pope’s own hand, and drag the excommunicate Queen Elizabeth in chains across the Alps to beg for mercy at the feet of the Pope, while placing their own chosen monarch on her throne. They called for a new crusade, promising all Catholic knights eternal glory if they struck down the heretic islanders. To their shock, almost all the English Catholic gentry refused to betray their country, stubbornly clinging to the strange unorthodox belief that no matter their faith, they were first and foremost Englishmen, and would not open their doors to a Spanish invasion that would seize their ancestral lands and treat them as cruelly as the conquistadors had treated the native caciques of Hispaniola.

With their promises of heavenly glory falling flat, the Jesuits turned to a far more earthly Mary: Mary Queen of Scots, who lived in English custody, plotting the murder of Queen Elizabeth and the overthrow of the realm with every spare penny of her French royal dowry. She was beautiful, she was famous for her misfortunes, and the Jesuits played her as their best possible card in their great game against England, until the card was worn thin and finally torn to pieces. The whole English nation, from the highest noble to the lowest ploughboy, rose up with one voice: after seventeen years of plots, treachery, and terror, this woman must die, or the whole commonwealth of England would perish. And the world knew which choice they made.

“Oh, where be these gay Spaniards,
Which make so great a boast O?
Oh, they shall eat the gray-goose feather,
And we shall eat the roast O!”

Imagine the crowds flocking to Plymouth’s fine harbour last summer, chests swelling with rightful pride as they gaze out at the vast breakwater stretching across the Sound, the bustling arsenals and docks, the twin estuaries crammed full of sleek, proud ships. They watch great iron screw liners turn in their own length as if by invisible magic, or weave through crowded fleets as easily as a tiny rowing skiff. What if, by some sudden, strange twist of fate, all that nineteenth-century grandeur—all its wealth, all its scientific marvels, which may vanish as swiftly as they came one day—dissolved away, and they were thrown back 300 years, into the soft summer sun of 1588?

Mount Edgecombe still rises green and lovely along the shore, but where are the modern docks? Where is sprawling Devonport? No vast dry-dock roofs jut above the waterline. Drake’s Island carries nothing but a tiny, makeshift battery, only just built by the man who gave the isle its name. Mount Wise is nothing but a lone country gentleman’s house, set among empty fields. The citadel is a rickety pop-gun fort, which a third-class modern steamer could shell to rubble in a lazy afternoon’s amusement. And what of the shipping? The great floating fortresses that crowd the Hamoaze today have shrunk to a handful of slow, scruffy lime barges. The Catwater is packed tight, not with modern merchant craft, but with a small, ragged squadron of ships that will, in just a day’s time, fight the greatest sea battle the world has ever known.

To modern eyes, that fleet looks absurdly small. The largest vessel afloat there is no bigger than a 36-gun frigate of later eras, carries less total weight of shot than a single new-model gunboat, and can fire that shot barely a quarter of the distance modern naval guns can reach. Would our day-tripping modern visitors, who hopped on a train to watch cavalry embark and plan to head home in time for dinner tomorrow, glance down at the tiny port and its tiny fleet with a sneer, spouting flippant lines about the triumphs of modern intellect and the foolishness of benighted ancestors? No doubt most of them would—for the masses who gawk at great engineering feats as if they are nothing more than a fairground sideshow, who enjoy those comforts without gratitude or understanding the way an ox eats clover tossed in its rack, will always look down on the hard-won work of past generations. But the thinkers, the builders, the men who themselves have sweated through the setbacks, the self-denial, the slow stumbling work of turning a new idea into reality? Their smile would not be one of pity, but of filial reverence. They would see in those unassuming little wooden hulls the very seed of the mighty fleet that now patrolled the Black Sea waves. They would know that to build that fleet, to sail it, to dare all on the open ocean with the limited materials and knowledge of their day, demanded a level of intellect, energy, and inventive daring equal to anything we boast of today.

But if that visitor was a student of men, not of machines, he would find no finer company to study than the crowd gathered on the little terrace bowling green behind the Pelican Inn, on the afternoon of July 19th. Groups of captains chatted together, or leaned on the low stone wall that looked out over the Sound and the anchored fleet below. Almost every notable mariner of the Plymouth squadron was there, every last one of England’s soon-to-be-forgotten great worthies. The Spanish Armada had been scattered by a gale. Lord Howard had sailed out as far as the Spanish coast to hunt for it, but when the wind shifted south, he feared the Dons would slip past him to attack the English shore, so he had returned to Plymouth, uncertain if the great invasion fleet would appear at all. Slip on a tavern drawer’s apron, like Prince Hal haunting the Boar’s Head, step through the rose-draped back door carrying a tray of long-necked Dutch glasses and a silver tankard of spiced wine, and look around you. These gallant captains wait for the Armada as lions in their lair wait for a herd of unsuspecting deer to pass by.

See five men talking earnestly in the centre of a loose ring, where the rest of the crowd hangs back, curious to overhear but far too respectful to intrude. Those soft long eyes and pointed chin you know already: that is Walter Raleigh. The fair young man in the flame-red doublet with his arm slung around Raleigh’s neck is Lord Sheffield. Opposite them, standing beside the stately Sir Richard Grenville, is Sheffield’s uncle, Lord Charles Howard of Effingham, Lord High Admiral of England. Next to him is his son-in-law Sir Robert Southwell, captain of the *Elizabeth Jonas*. And that short, sturdy plainly dressed man, legs planted slightly apart, hands behind his back, sharp grey eyes looking up into every speaker’s face? He holds his cap in one hand, so you can see his bullet-shaped head of crisp brown hair, his lined forehead, high cheekbones, broad square face, and thick lips set as firm as granite. He looks like a coarse, common working man—yet every line of his stance breathes unshakable determination, calm self-possession, and unquenchable energy. When he finally speaks a few blunt, plain words, every eye turns to him with respect. That is Francis Drake.

A burly, grizzled old sailor in sea-stained, slightly greasy clothes that contrast wildly with the heavy gold chain slung around his neck waddles over, as if he was born and raised forever in the middle of a gale. The upper half of his sharp, stubborn face is brick-red and weathered as old leather, the lower half covered in grizzled badger-like fur. He claps Drake hard on the back, and booms out in a thick Devon twang: “Are you coming to drink your wine, Francis Drake, or are you not? Saving your presence, my lord,” he adds with a nod to the High Admiral. Howard only laughs, and waves Drake off to go drink with the old man. This is John Hawkins, port admiral of Plymouth, the patriarch of all Devon’s seamen. If Drake is their beloved hero, Hawkins is their gruff, beloved uncle who says and does exactly as he pleases no matter what company he is in. And today, with the great battle of Armageddon looming over them, he has shaken free of his usual crabby reserve, brimming over with loud, jolly good humour even towards his old rival Drake.

They push through the crowd, which is full of other men you would give much to speak to face to face. Martin Frobisher and John Davis sit on a bench smoking long silver pipes of tobacco. Nearby are Fenton and Withrington, who both tried and failed to follow Drake’s route around the world, through no fault of their own. The man raising a tankard to them, wishing them better luck next time, is George Fenner, the famed sailor known to seven different Portuguese ports, a favourite of the Earl of Leicester, and captain of the galleon Queen Elizabeth bought from him. That neat little man with the enormous yellow ruff, sharp chin, tiny pointed goatee, and self-satisfied smile is Richard Hawkins, the “Complete Seaman”, Admiral John’s son, who will one day win fame and meet a tragic fate. The older man talking to him is his good uncle William, whose monument still stands in Deptford Church, erected by Admiral John a year after this day, inscribed with words praising him as a faithful servant of the true faith, a generous friend to poor sailors, a just arbiter in the trickiest disputes, and a man of rare faith, piety, and wisdom. That, and the fact that he pulled off a very daring, dangerous operation in Porto Rico with honour, is all we know of William Hawkins—but if any of us can have half as much good said of us when we die, we will have no reason to complain.

There is John Drake, Sir Francis’s brother, ancestor of the modern Drake family line. There is his nephew George, not the brightest of men, who sailed around the world with Amyas Leigh. And there is Amyas himself, talking in low, fierce, curt tones to Captain Barker of Bristol, brother of the ill-fated Andrew Barker who recovered John Oxenham’s hidden guns, only to die at the hands of the Spaniards in Honduras twelve years before after a mutiny among his men. Barker now captains the *Victory*, one of the queen’s finest ships, and he has a long account to settle with the Dons, just as Amyas does. So the two of them growl together in a corner, while the rest of the crowd is as merry as the flies buzzing over the vine trellises above their heads.

But who is that ancient man sitting on a bench against the sun-warmed south wall of the tavern? His long white beard flows almost to his waist, his hands rest on his knees, his palsied head turns slowly back and forth to catch every scrap of talk from the passing captains. His great-granddaughter, a curly-haired little girl of six, rests her head on his knees, and his granddaughter, a buxom, dark-eyed woman of thirty, stands at his side tending him, half as nurse, half as show-woman. He is a curiosity to all the captains, and she has to beg again and again: “Please, sirs, don’t give him any strong drink, poor old soul, the doctor says he can’t have it.” This is old Martin Cockrem, father of the Pelican’s landlord, so old he outlives the usual span of man. He can still remember Plymouth’s bells ringing for the coronation of Henry VIII, and he was very likely the first Englishman ever to set foot on New World soil. There he sits, as solid and ancient as a prehistoric granite tor rising out of green wheat fields, a living relic of old Europe’s death pangs and the new world’s first birth throes. His senses are still sharp as ever, and right now he seems restless, peering out at the crowd with rheumy eyes, listening for a familiar voice.

“There he is again! Why doesn’t he come over, then?”
“Quiet, grandad, don’t pester his worship.”
“He’s been here an hour and never spoken to poor old Martin! I say, sir—” he feebly tugs Amyas’s cloak as he passes by. “Captain, go tell young master old Martin’s looking for him.”
“Mercy, grandad, where are your manners? Don’t take offence, sir, he’s half a babe at this age, and tedious as can be sometimes,” the woman says, blushing.
“Young master who?” Amyas asks, bending down to smile at the old man, gesturing for her to let him speak.
“Master Hawkins! He hasn’t come near me all day.”
Amyas hurries off, and grabs the sleeve of young Richard Hawkins to pass on the message, before the granddaughter catches his arm, laughing and blushing harder.
“No, sir, not Mr. Richard—Admiral John, his father. He always calls him young master, poor old soul,” she says, pointing at the grizzled, weather-beaten old port admiral.
Amyas goes to Hawkins and delivers the old man’s message.
“Mercy on my soul, where are my wits? Aye, aye, I’m coming!” the old hero booms in his thickest Devon accent, waddling over to the bench and digging around in his pockets. “Here, Martin, I’ve got what you want, man alive! But his lordship kept me tied up in talk so long I forgot. Look here, now! I’m getting so fat and lazy these days I can hardly reach deep into my own pockets!”
He pulls out a tangled piece of tarred string, a bundle of crumpled papers, a brass thimble, a hunk of pudding tobacco, and finally a tiny paper of Muscovado sugar—then as rare and precious a treat as any modern French bonbon—thrusting it into the old man’s eager, trembling hand.

Old Martin dips his finger in the sugar, rubbing it over his toothless gums, grinning and nodding thanks to his “young master”, while the little girl at his knee helps herself to a handful too, and no one stops her.
“There you go, Admiral Leigh! Two ends of life meeting, grandfathers and babies! You and I will be like that one day, young Samson.”
“Let’s hope we’ve slain a good many Philistines before then,” Amyas laughs.
“Amen, so be it. But look at him now—one of the finest sailors that ever drank a tot of rum, and all he cares for now is a little bit of sweet stuff. It’s pitiful, but I bring him a bit every time I come, and he waits for it. He’s blood of my blood, old Martin is. He sailed with my father Captain Will when they were both two lads barely more than boys aboard a trawler. My father sailed on to better things, and here I am. Old Martin didn’t, and here he is. We Hawkinses have climbed high. We may fall low again some day.”
“I hope never,” Amyas says.
“Hope doesn’t get the work done, young man. You go out and *do* it. I hear far too much of that soft hope talk from my own son. Let the preachers shout themselves black in the face—hard work is what counts! Faith can’t save you, and charity won’t feed you. You sit with him now, while I go play bowls with Drake. He’ll tell you a thousand stories. Ask him about good King Hal, now, just—” and he waddles off again.

“You saw good King Henry, then, father?” Amyas asks, sitting down beside him.
The old man’s eyes light up at once, he stops munching his sugar.
“Seen him? Aye, I should say I have. I was with Captain Will when he went to meet the French king at Calais, at the Field, the Field—”
“The Field of the Cloth of Gold, grandad,” his granddaughter prompts.
“That’s the one! Seen him? Aye, he was a proper king! His face shone like the rising sun, his back was as broad as my two arms spread out like this, and his voice! Oh, when he swore merry oaths, it was grand! Seen him? Aye, and I saw him do what few men have seen—seen him cry like a little child.”
“Cry? I never thought there was much crying in Henry VIII,” Amyas says, surprised.
“You can think what you like—”
“Grandad, don’t be rude now—”
“Let him go on,” Amyas says.
“I saw him cry, and oh dear, how he covered his face with his hands, and he shouted ‘Oh my gentlemen! Oh my gallant men!’ Those were his very words.”
“When was that?”
“Why, Captain Will had just sailed into Portsmouth Hard to speak to him, the royal barge laid right up against the quay, and our little boat tied right up next to it. The king stood right there above my head on the quay edge, and the *Mary Rose* came gliding right up abreast of us, looking as grand as could be, poor dear, and then she started to heel over. Captain Will said, ‘Those lower gun ports are far too close to the water,’ they barely had sixteen inches of clearance from the waves. And before the words were even out of his mouth, a sharp little flaw of wind blew from the west, my cap flew off my head and hit the stone wall. When I stooped to pick it up, I heard a scream, and it was all over.”
“He’s telling the story of the *Mary Rose* sinking, sir,” the granddaughter explains.
“I guessed as much.”
“All over. The screaming! The yelling went right up to the heavens! And the king screamed out like a little maid, ‘Oh my gentlemen! Oh my gallant men!’ And as she lay on her side, just sinking under the waves, the last two faces I saw bobbing above the water were those two men’s fathers. I recognised their armour! That Sir George Carew and that Sir Richard Grenville standing over there. Aye! Drowned like rats, every last one of them.”
“Now, you mustn’t pester his worship any more,” the granddaughter says.
“Pester? Let him tell stories till midnight, I’ll be only too happy,” Amyas says, settling in on the bench beside him. “Drawer! Bring ale, and a parcel of tobacco.”
Amyas leans back to listen, while the old man purrs happily to himself: “Aye, they all like to hear old Martin’s stories. All the great captains look up to old Martin.”

“Hillo, Amyas!” Cary calls, wandering over. “Who’s your friend here? There’s a man over there telling me wonders about the River Plate. We ought to sail there for our next cruise, make our fortune.”
“The River Plate?” old Martin says. “I know the River Plate better than any man alive. Who’d ever heard of it, let alone been there, before Captain Will and I sailed there? I lived a whole year among the natives, and they were as civil as could be, if only they’d had a shirt to their backs. The prince of their tribe came home with Captain Will to King Henry, though he died on the voyage. The natives took it very well, for we were always as kind to them as proper Christians ought to be. If we hadn’t been, I never would have lived to tell the tale.”
“What year was that?”
“1530. But I was there even before that, learned their language, that’s why Captain Will left me behind as a hostage when he took their prince aboard his ship.”
“Before that?” Cary says, astonished. “Hardly anyone knew that country existed back then.”
The old man’s eyes flash with pride.
“Knew it? Aye, you can say that! Look around you at all these captains! I’m the father of all of them now that poor Captain Will’s gone to heaven, me, Martin Cockrem! Aye, I’ve seen such changes. I remember when Tavistock Abbey was stuffed full of friars and golden idols and all that noxious Popish rubbish, as full of sin as a wheat rick is full of rats. I remember

“Britannia needs no bulwarks, No towers along the steep, Her march is o'er the mountain wave, Her home is on the deep.”
—CAMPBELL, *Ye Mariners of England*

Now began the epochal twelve-day sea fight that would decide whether popery and despotism, or Protestantism and freedom, would shape half of Europe, and the whole unwritten future of the Americas. It is an epic fit for the thunder-roll of Homer’s verse, not plain prose, but I will tell it as faithfully as I can, leaning whenever possible on the accounts of those who lived through it rather than my own fancy.

The Lord High Admiral of England sent a small pinnace named the *Defiance* ahead to fire its ordnance and formally declare war. He then drew within musket-shot range, his own ship the *Ark Royal* (or *Triumph*) thundering broadsides, and struck what he believed to be the Spanish admiral’s flagship—though it proved to be the vessel of one Alfonso de Leon. Moments later, Drake, Hawkins, and Frobisher hammered the rearmost Spanish squadron, commanded by Recalde, with relentless, well-aimed fire. The Spaniards quickly learned the bitter truth of how much more nimble the English ships were; Recalde’s men, finding they took far more damage than they could give even at their commander’s best efforts, rushed to scramble back into the main body of the fleet.
Medina Sidonia, the Spanish grand admiral, saw his ships scattering fast, and herded them into a tight half-moon formation. The great Armada moved forward with stately, deliberate pomp, like a herd of hulking buffaloes marching across a prairie, disdaining to notice the snarling wolves nipping at their heels. But the English were no wolves: they were cunning, swift-mounted hunters, and as Drake himself put it, they would “shamefully shuffle” that vast, proud herd from the Lizard to Portland, and from Portland all the way to Calais Roads. Even in those first two hours of fighting, no small number of Spaniards had already begun to doubt the much-boasted invincibility of their “invincible” Armada.

One of the four massive Spanish galleasses was already riddled through with shot, its pulpits, chapels, and the friars who served there thrown into chaos. The rest of the Armada had to cluster tight around it to shield it, or Drake and Hawkins would sink it outright. In the chaos of that maneuver, the principal galleon of Seville, carrying Don Pedro de Valdez and a whole company of blue-blooded Spanish nobles, collided with a neighboring ship, snapped off its foremast, and was abandoned by the rest of the fleet in spite of all their boasts of chivalry. The signs did not bode well for a Spanish victory, but the Spaniards told themselves to take heart: Our Lady, the saints, and the *Coena Domini* bull were still on their side, and to doubt that would be blasphemy. But they could not escape the obvious question: if they fared this badly against only a third of the Plymouth fleet, what would become of them when the forty lagging English ships already dotting the blue water between them and the Mewstone arrived to join the fight?

So ended the first day of battle. Not a single English ship was lost, and barely a single English seaman was seriously hurt. That single day shattered forever the English public’s awe of Spain’s supposed unbeatable might. It fully vindicated Lord Howard’s choice, taken on the advice of Raleigh and Drake, to fight a running, hit-and-run battle instead of blindly lashing their ships together to board, a mistake Raleigh swore would have ended in total defeat had Howard not been wiser than the scores of petty, foolish critics who complained he was not fighting “honorably” enough.

Amyas Leigh and the rest of the Bideford contingent spent the whole two hours of that first day hammering at a huge galleon that bore a statue of a maiden with a wheel on its sterncastle: the *Sta. Catharina*. The flag at its bowsprit bore a coat of arms, likely belonging to the captain of its soldiers, but the whole banner had been shot away early in the fight, so Amyas could never confirm if it had belonged to De Soto or not. He knew there would be time enough for his private revenge later, and when the admiral’s signal called him off at last, he tumbled into his hammock and slept soundly.

He had not been asleep an hour when Cary shook him awake to ask for new orders. “We were supposed to follow Drake’s lantern, Amyas, but I can’t see it anywhere. Unless the man was plucked right up into the stars to become a new planet named Drakium Sidus.”
Amyas grumbled as he stumbled back on deck, but there was no sign of the guiding lantern. Only a sudden explosion and a great burst of flame on one of the distant Spanish ships, which the crew managed to put out eventually, while almost the whole English fleet was forced to heave to and drift through the dark.

Dawn found them off Torbay, and a small pinnace pulled up alongside Amyas’ ship carrying a personal letter from Drake. Its spelling was as whimsical as most men’s of the era, but the content ran roughly thus:
> I was wool-gathering all night after five great hulks that the pixies tricked me into mistaking for Spanish galleons, until daylight revealed them to be nothing more than harmless German merchantmen. I let them go with my blessing, and on my way back, to my great joy, stumbled straight across Valdez’ abandoned galleon. It was stuffed full of fine plunder that Valdez’ so-called valiant comrades had left behind for him, even as Lord Howard had sailed right past thinking the crew had already deserted. I’ve sent off to Dartmouth nearly fifty Spanish noblemen and gentlemen as prisoners. When I first sent my pinnace over to Valdez to demand his surrender, the fool insisted on haggling over terms to save his punctilio. I told him I had no time to waste arguing: if he wanted to die, I would happily oblige him, and if he wanted to live, he would get good quarter. He wrote back bragging he was Don Pedro Valdez, that it was beneath his honor and that of the Dons with him to surrender unconditionally. I wrote back to say I was Francis Drake, and my matches were already lit. At that, he decided my name was honor enough to salve his own pride, and came aboard to kiss my fist, spouting all sorts of Spanish lies about how fortunate he was to fall into the hands of the famous lucky Drake, and a lot more nonsense he could have kept to cool his porridge for all I cared.
> I got all sorts of useful intelligence out of him, the old leaky tub, and one piece in particular concerns you: your Don Guzman is serving on the *Sta. Catharina* as commandant of its soldiers. He flies his own coat of arms at the bowsprit, right next to that carved maiden with the wheel on the stern. It’s a tall three-decker packed with ordnance. God keep you from harm, and send you the same good luck I’ve had.
> Your deare Friend and Admirall,
> F. Drake.
> She sails in Recalde’s squadron. The Armada had planned to smoke us out of Plymouth harbor, and thank God they never tried: their orders from home are so tight they fight like a bull tied to a tether, no further than the rope will let them, and they’re finding the devil a hard master as most men do in the end. They can’t wrap their heads around how nimbly we tack and turn, and they all swear we must be witches. We know their tricks of old. Time and light can catch any hare, and they will find the stretch from Start Point to Dunkirk a very long way indeed.

“Our admiral’s in a fine mood to send you such a long, friendly letter, Leigh,” Cary said.
“St. Catherine? That was the galleon we hammered for two whole hours yesterday!” Amyas roared, stamping his boot hard on the deck.
“Of course it was. We’ll find her again, never fear. Cunning old Drake, even managed to line his own pockets with plunder while the rest of the fleet was left waiting for him all night.”
“Well, he certainly left Lord Howard out of the loot,” Amyas grumbled.
“Lord Howard is too high-hearted to stop and chase plunder, even if he is a Papist,” Cary said. Amyas only growled in response: he worshipped Drake, and was never very fair to Catholics.

They did not catch up with Lord Howard’s flagship until nightfall. He and Lord Sheffield had sailed hard after distant Spanish lanterns all the previous night with only two ships between them, and their courage put an end to all slurs cast by fanatics that England’s Roman Catholic nobility might betray the crown. All through the rest of the campaign, the Howards fought with reckless, desperate valor, as if determined to prove their loyalty beyond any doubt. Soon a derelict half-burnt Spanish galleon drifted past them, and Amyas’ men called out to stop and board her, but he refused outright. “We came out here to fight, not loot. The nearest ship to her can have the prize without us grudge.” They sailed on, and his men pulled long faces when they saw that galleon claimed by the next ship in line and towed off to Weymouth: it proved to be the vessel of vice-admiral Miguel d’Oquenda, nearly blown up the night before by a Dutch gunner who, sick of Spanish abuse, had tried to blow the whole ship sky high to pay off his old scores against his tyrants.
