So ended the second day of fighting. The next morning found both fleets off the Isle of Portland. The wind had shifted north, blowing sharp and cool off the white chalk downs above Weymouth Bay. The Spaniards turned to face the English, clearly meaning to stand back and forth on the tide until the wind shifted again, so they could make a run for the Needles. But the English were determined they would not get past Purbeck Isle without a very hard fight.

The English sailed west for a little while, only to tack back on the opposite bearing, and for a long stretch both fleets maneuvered furiously to try to gain the windward advantage. Before noon, the English had slipped close-hauled between the Armada and the shore, and were bearing down on the Spaniards directly before the wind.
Then began the fiercest, most brutal battle of the campaign so far. The English fought through the chaos to rescue a squadron of London merchant ships that had been hemmed in by the Spaniards, while the Spanish fought just as hard to pull Recalde out of danger when his ship was trapped. The thunder of thousands of cannon roared nonstop, though most of the Spanish shots flew far over the top of the smaller English vessels without doing harm. Only one English captain, a Devon man named Cock, died fighting honorably in his tiny small ship, deep in the thick of the Spanish ranks. The far smaller, far more agile English ships would dash in, unleash a full broadside, then dart away across the deep water, their shots never missing the sides of the slow, unwieldy floating castles the Spaniards sailed. Though the fight only ended in the capture of one huge Venetian galleon and a handful of small craft, it was the bloodiest clash of the whole run. Lord Howard himself was fighting deep inside the Spanish formation when he spotted one of his captains, George Fenner, lingering far back. “O George, what doest thou?” he roared. “Wilt thou now frustrate all the hope and good opinion I have of thee? Wilt thou forsake me now?” Stung by the shout, Fenner charged straight into the fight and fought like a lion, as every other captain and seaman did that day.

Night fell over the heaving, sulphurous floating battlefield, and dawn found them far past Purbeck, the white chalk peak of Freshwater Head rising dead ahead. Ships poured out of every southern English harbor to join the chase: the flower of English chivalry, Lords Oxford, Northumberland, Cumberland, Pallavicin, Brooke, Carew, Raleigh, Blunt, and dozens more honorable names, sailing in vessels they had fitted out at their own expense, flocking to the fight as if it was a great tournament where immortal fame could be won. Spain had staked all her greatest noble houses on this throw of the dice: every family of rank in Aragon and Castile had sent at least one son or brother to fight, and many would never return.
Lord Howard sent light small craft racing back to Portsmouth to resupply with gunpowder, but the wind died completely that evening before they could return, and both fleets drifted helpless on the tide, shouting idle challenges back and forth across the water with trumpets, fifes, and drums. The glassy, calm sea held them all night long.

It was the 25th of July, St. James’s Day, the patron saint of Spain. The Spaniards told themselves St. James, who they swore had been seen riding a white horse at the head of their armies to slaughter infidels for centuries, would surely grant them a fair wind at least, or ride to their aid himself. Three of the four great galleasses—their crippled sister left limping in the main fleet—thrashed the sea to foam with three hundred oars apiece and charged the English line. They did not see St. James leading them to victory, however: they saw Lord Howard’s *Triumph*, his brother’s *Lion*, Southwell’s *Elizabeth Jonas*, Lord Sheffield’s *Bear*, Barker’s *Victory*, and George Fenner’s *Leicester* towed out to meet them, firing broadsides of chain-shot that smashed oars to splinters and tore rigging to rags. Only a sudden shift of wind toward noon and the arrival of the main Spanish fleet saved the galleasses from being captured, and after that day none of them dared to venture out to fight again.
The battle raged general all afternoon beneath the sheer white chalk cliffs of Freshwater. Thousands of sea birds rose screaming from every ledge, their black wings blotting the bright chalk, and a lone shepherd tending his flock on the high slopes hurried to the cliff edge to peer down, forgetting the small wheatear fluttering trapped in his snare, staring trembling through the great leagues of rolling sulphur smoke to catch glimpses of tall masts and bright silk flags glinting far below.

St. James’s Day ended exactly like the day the priests of Baal cried out on Mount Carmel: their patron saint did not answer, not with fire from heaven, not with victory—only the fire of English cannon. The Armada huddled itself into a tight defensive circle, and refused to give battle any more, turning all sails for Calais, where they hoped the Guises would send a French army to support them, before sailing on to Dunkirk to join the Duke of Parma and his great flotilla of Netherland troops.
A fair steady south-southwest wind carried both fleets onward past Brighton Cliffs, Beachy Head, Hastings, and Dungeness. It felt less like a running battle and more like a victory procession. Lord Howard stopped to reward his bravest captains on the deck of his flagship: after Lord Thomas Howard, Lord Sheffield, Townsend, and Frobisher had all been knighted—an honor far greater than a peerage in those days—old Admiral John Hawkins knelt to receive the accolade, rose up Sir John, and joked that his wife would never know herself again when people started calling her “My Lady.”
All the while, the cliffs behind them were lined with pikemen, musketeers, and every countryman, groom, and farmer who could bear arms, marching eastward under their squires and sheriffs as fast as they could travel toward the Dover shore. Not just soldiers, either: women, children, and aged folk in wagons had trudged from miles inland to add their thin shouts and fierce, unshakable prayers to the roar of mixed faith and fear rising toward heaven from the spectators of Britain’s Salamis.

They had every right to pray. The danger was far from over. Lord Howard had received word from Newhaven that the Guises would not dare to stir against England, and Seymour and Winter had sailed away from their watch post off the Flemish coast to join the main English fleet, bringing its total number of ships up to 140: slightly more than the Spanish fleet, but less than half its total tonnage, and only a third as many men. The Spaniards were battered and dispirited, but still fully intact as a fighting force. They dropped anchor in Calais Roads on the Saturday evening of that most memorable week, and every prudent man in the fleet knew well: the church bells that would ring across all Christendom the next morning, the Sabbath, would be either the triumphal peal for the English, or the death knell of the Reformed faith across the whole of Europe.

That Sabbath was a solemn, trembling day across every English town and village. Many a light-hearted coward who had once scoffed at the very idea the Armada could ever reach England, now fell abjectly to his knees shaking, seeing the threat he had refused to acknowledge laid bare right across the Channel. Many a brave man knelt beside his wife and daughters at church, his heart sinking to the cold stone floor at the thought of what those he loved would suffer at the hands of Spain’s brutal, fanatical soldiery, or the far more deliberate cruelty of the Inquisition. The spectres of the St. Bartholomew’s Day massacres, the Smithfield burnings, the extermination of the Moors and the West Indian peoples, the unspeakable horrors of the Piedmontese persecutions, were not dim, distant tales from centuries past then: they were fresh, gaping wounds, flitting before every Englishman’s eyes and stoking the fire in their breasts. They knew that one single defeat, and the same unspeakable fate that had befallen the people of Magdeburg a generation later would have fallen on every English town from London to Carlisle.

Amyas Leigh, however, barely noticed the grand stakes of the national battle unfolding around him. Day after day, he had hunted the *Sta. Catharina* through the thickest parts of the fight, and had not been able to find her, or even hear word of her. One hour he feared she had sunk overnight, robbing him of his vengeance; the next he feared she had repaired her damage and slipped away from him forever. He was moody, restless, and for the first time in his life, snappish and sharp with his own men. He could talk of nothing but Don Guzman. Every spare minute he pulled his sword out of its

*“Full fathom five thy father lies;
Of his bones are corals made;
Those are pearls that were his eyes;
Nothing of him that doth fade,
But doth suffer a sea-change
Into something rich and strange.
Sea-nymphs hourly ring his knell:
Hark! now I hear them,—ding-dong, bell.”
— *The Tempest*, William Shakespeare*

It is done. The great Spanish Armada is vanquished.

The long, unceasing cosmic war waged since the dawn of time — the battle of light against dark, of Michael’s angels against Satan and his fiends — has for one brief, rare, judgement-day moment stepped out of the spiritual plane and into flesh and blood. Ordinary men did not need to puzzle out where they stood: they could strike a blow for good not just with prayers and pens, but with cannon shot and cold steel. The lines were drawn clean, the false grandeur of Spain’s vaunted imperial might crumpled like a punctured bladder at the first real, brave blow. All across the Protestant world, from England’s southern shores to the frozen Alpine valleys where generations of Vaudois shepherd saints lay buried, their bones the seed of coming religious freedom, a shout of holy joy went up that had not been heard for centuries. It was the birth cry of a new world: of free settlement across North America, Australia, and the Pacific, of free trade, of the age of discovery and science soon to come.

As the contemporary chronicler Van Meteran records, Elizabeth ordered a national public holiday on November 29, where every man, woman and child was enjoined to attend church to give public thanks for the deliverance. The queen herself rode through London in a triumphal procession, following the old Roman custom, her retinue of lords and civic officers lining her route, the streets hung with blue cloth, the defeated Spanish standards hanging from the rafters of St Paul’s Cathedral as she entered to give public praise to God and urge her people to the same. The crowds cheered so loud their voices shook the rafters, calling down long life on their queen and ruin on all her enemies.

Medals struck to mark the day bore the simple, mocking legend: *It came, it saw, it fled*. The remnants of the once-invincible Armada ran north like frightened deer, past Orkney and Shetland, past the coast of Norway where the descendants of the Vikings turned them away without food or fresh water, ever closer to the arctic midnight sun. Their stores ran out, they threw their dying horses overboard to lighten the load, the northern sea echoing with the animals’ screams. The duke of Medina Sidonia fled for home with 25 of his best, most seaworthy ships, leaving the rest of the battered fleet to fend for themselves. Gale after gale rose out of the Atlantic, and barely a handful of those desperate remaining vessels ever made it back to Spanish shores.

All this while, where were Amyas Leigh and his ship the *Vengeance*?

The main English fleet had turned south for home off the 57th parallel, their powder spent, their rations running thin, choosing to leave the scattered Spaniards to the mercy of the storm-tossed northern seas. But Amyas begged his superior, Lord High Admiral Howard, for leave to stay on the chase, and went to Sir John Hawkins to beg for whatever spare ammunition and provisions he could spare, promising to pay the full sum back out of his plunder if he survived, or out of his own family estate if he died, signing formal promissory notes that Hawkins, ever the practical man, stuffed into his pocket alongside his twine, thimbles, and pouch of tobacco. When that was done, he gathered his crew of Bideford men, reminded them again of the terrible wrong done to the Rose of Torridge by Don Guzman de Soto, the Spaniard they had hunted across the Atlantic for years, and asked them straight: “Men of Bideford, will you follow me still? There is plunder for those who want wealth, revenge for those who have lost kin to Spanish cruelty, and for all of us the honour of never quitting the chase as long as a Spanish flag flies in English seas.”

Every single man on board swore they would follow Amyas Leigh around the whole world.

The sixteen days of chase that followed were brutal and winding, a nonstop cat and mouse game that took them past the Outer Hebrides, where they skirmished with Skye islanders who first cursed them in a tongue no one on board knew, then embraced them as long-lost kin, past the wreck of a great Venetian galleon off Islay that gave the crew enough plunder to pay back all their arrears, down the west coast of Wales, through Cardigan Bay where the Santa Catharina (the great galleon carrying Don Guzman) ran aground for a time on the sunken pebble dyke of Sarn David, the legendary remains of the drowned Welsh kingdom. They fought four separate running skirmishes, Amyas refusing every chance to seize rich disabled prizes along the way, all to keep his single-eyed focus on the one man he meant to bring to justice.

By the sixteenth day, the two ships had worked their way down the Bristol Channel. The previous evening, the Welsh coast had loomed black against the glow of inland thunderstorms, and lightning had flickered all through the small hours under a soft south-west breeze. Dawn broke grey, drizzly, and windless, the sky a low roof of dingy inky cloud, the sea rolling in glassy, foam-flecked troughs, no land visible through the thick, clinging mist.

Amyas paced the soaked deck, his jaw tight with the fury of his months-long obsession. He knew the Spaniard could not escape, but every extra moment that dragged between him and his revenge felt like a knife twisted in his gut. The men sat hunched along the bulwarks, whistling pointlessly for wind, the sails slapping idly against the masts, the *Vengeance* rolling so far her yardarms dipped close to the water on each side.

“Watch those guns,” Amyas growled. “One of them’s going to break loose if we keep rolling like this.”

“We’ll watch the guns well enough,” old Yeo rumbled back, “if the Lord will only send us a wind to move this ship.”

“We’ll have wind enough before night,” Will Cary laughed, “and thunder to go with it.”

“So much the better,” Amyas snapped. “Let the heavens split if they want, so long as I get my work done first.”

“He can’t be far off, depend on it,” Cary said, squinting through the mist. “One break in the cloud, and we’ll spot his masts.”

“Probably to windward of us, the devil himself is fighting for him,” Amyas snarled, shaking the sword in his hand. “Sixteen days at his heels, and I haven’t run this through his black heart yet!”

The morning dragged on. Not a single gull crossed the glassy grey water. Amyas’s dark, brooding fury hung over the whole ship like a shroud. “God,” he cried out suddenly, unthinking, “let me just drive this blade through his accursed heart, and then — strike me dead, if You will!”

Parson John Brimblecombe went white. “Sir! What sort of a vow is that to make?”

“None of your business, parson,” Amyas grunted. “They’re piping dinner. Go below. I won’t be joining you.”

Brimblecombe trundled down to the mess, whispering to the rest of the crew that Amyas’s words boded nothing but ill luck. Only old Yeo shrugged it off. “What better end could a man ask for, than to be called home the second he’s finished his work? I say let the Don meet his reckoning, and if lightning takes me the second I see him safe on shore, I’ll die a happy man.”

Before anyone could answer, Amyas’s shout boomed across the deck: “There she is!”

The crew scrambled up the hatches as fast as the rolling deck would let them. The mist had torn open for a minute, a ragged patch of blue sky spilling a single shaft of golden sunlight straight down onto the Santa Catharina, rolling four or five miles to their east, her tall galleon masts rising clear above the water.

“Now we see her,” Cary said, “but where in God’s name are we? Where’s the land?”

“Tide’s running hard up the Channel right now, sir,” the master called back.

“Who cares about the tide?” Amyas said, his cold blue eyes devouring the distant Spanish galleon. “Can’t we get to her?”

“Not unless someone jumps over the side and shoves her,” Cary laughed, before he sobered at the look on Amyas’s face. “You’re not yourself, old friend. You haven’t been for three days now.”

“I’m not Amyas Leigh right now. I’m my family’s avenger,” Amyas said, passing a shaking hand over his brow. “When this is over, I’ll be the old merry fool you know again. I swear it.”

He pulled the sharpening stone out of his pocket and began running it up and down the edge of his sword, over and over, as if he meant to hone the blade to a supernatural sharpness. The patch of blue closed back over, the air grew thick and sultry, a low distant mutter of thunder rolling from the west. They had no boats big enough to tow the *Vengeance* through the calm, so there was nothing to do but wait.

By two in the afternoon, Yeo came up to him. “The tide’s been running east for two hours now. He’s trapped. Can’t escape us.”

“Safe as a fox in a snare. Not even Satan can pull him out of this,” Amyas snarled.

“But God can,” Brimblecombe said, quiet and earnest.

Amyas rounded on him, ready to snap, when a great thunderclap boomed directly over their heads, the sound rolling across the empty water. A black line of storm had appeared on the north-west horizon, cutting the sea clean off from the sky.

“Here comes the wind!” Amyas yelled. “Hard round with the yards!”

The sails filled with a roar, the *Vengeance* heeled over, and they surged forward before the rising gale, straight for the becalmed Santa Catharina. Thunder peal followed peal, lightning zigzagging down out of the black storm arch, and the rain hit a few minutes later, sharp as hail, lashing the sea into a white froth. Amyas refused to shorten sail, determined to reach the Spaniard before the squall blew itself out.

Ten minutes later, Yeo came sprinting back from the bow, his face as white as bone. “Land dead ahead! Port the helm, sir! For the love of God, port the helm right now!”

Amyas hauled the tiller over with all his strength, the fore-sail splitting with a crack like a cannon shot, and the *Vengeance* swung hard around. A hundred and fifty yards away, the Santa Catharina was already careening straight for the dark, cliff-bound mass of Lundy Island rising out of the storm, the jagged black fang of the Shutter rock sticking up out of the foaming breakers directly in her path. The clumsy galleon lurched, her helmsman fighting to turn her out of the trap, but her heavy hull refused to answer the tiller. For a heartbeat she hung half-buried in spray, then she pitched forward, and rushed headlong onto the waiting rock, to tear her hull apart on the jagged granite teeth of the Bristol Channel.

"Would you hear a Spanish lady,
How she woo'd an Englishman?
Garments gay and rich as may be,
Deck'd with jewels had she on."
— Elizabethan Ballad

The first of October dawned bright and wind-still. Soft grey autumn clouds streaked the wide Devonshire sky from east to west, as if all heaven and earth had paused to rest after the brutal, storm-torn summer of sea battles and near-mutinies off the Spanish Main. The battered galleon *Vengeance* slid silent over Appledore Bar, as if she herself was hushed with grief, glided past the drowsy sand hills, and dropped her anchor with her flag flying half-mast high. Stowed in her hold lay the body of Salvation Yeo.

A small boat pulled for the western edge of the strand, where crowds of villagers had gathered, and Cary and Brimblecombe stepped out first to steady Amyas Leigh, newly blinded, leading him slow up the hill toward his childhood home at Burrough. The onlookers clustered close, their cheers softening into murmurs of blessing and quiet sobs from kind-hearted local women: every soul in Bideford and Appledore knew full well the torments Amyas had endured at the hands of the Spanish, and the sight of that great, broad-shouldered sailor moving unseeing through familiar sand brought tears to even the hardest old fisherman’s eyes.

“Spare me, good friends,” Amyas said gently, holding up a hand. “I landed here to come home quiet, no parading through the town to be gawked at. Think not of me, go not on about me. But follow behind me decently, as Christian men, and see to the grave a far better man than I will ever be.”

As he spoke, the second boat pulled for the shore, and there was Yeo’s coffin, draped in the cross of St. George. The people took him at his word, sending a runner ahead to Burrough House to warn Mrs. Leigh her son was home at last. When the coffin was heaved to its bier, Amyas and his two oldest friends took their place as chief mourners behind it, the rest of the *Vengeance*’s crew falling in line, and the crowd swelling behind them until by the time they reached Northam churchyard, the funeral train numbered close to five hundred souls. They had sent word via fishing skiff the day before to the sexton to dig the grave, and the parson waited for them at the lychgate.

Mrs. Leigh had stayed home, too soft-hearted to face the crowd, and content (as she always was) to let her son honor the faithful old servant who had sailed half the world over to watch over him. She sat in the old bay window with Ayacanora at her side, and when the church bell stopped tolling, she opened her prayer book and began to read the burial service aloud.

“Ayacanora, child,” she said, pausing mid-line, “they are burying old Master Yeo, who loved you, who hunted for you across the wide southern seas, who pulled you from the jaws of a crocodile when you were little. Why do you look so bright and happy today, are you not sorry for him?”

Ayacanora blushed bright red and hung her head. She had not been thinking of Yeo at all, the poor wild thing: every thought in her head was fixed on Amyas, home at last.

When the service ended and the blessing was spoken, the crowd lingered, clustering round the fresh grave to stare from the dirt to the blind giant standing at its head, as if they sensed there was more to come. And more did come. Amyas drew himself up to his full six and a half feet of height, waved one broad hand to call for quiet, and the eyes of every man there locked on his sightless face. Twice he tried to speak, twice his voice choked in his throat, until at last the words came out, deep and ringing:

“Good people all, you seamen among whom I was raised, to whom I come home blind today to dwell among you until my last breath—here lies the very pattern of all bold English mariners. The truest of friends, the most terrible of foes, unshakable in his purpose, sharp in council, quick to strike. Modest in triumph, as patient through failure as no other mortal man I ever knew. He was the first of all Britons to humble Spanish pride at Rio de la Hacha and Nombre, the first to sail the South Seas that by God’s grace will soon be as open to our English keels as the bay outside this bar. Purged of his young sins by unspeakable torments at the hands of the papist enemy, he learned to fear God and naught else on this earth. He served as a righteous scourge to the Spaniard, and a faithful soldier of Christ, and now he has gone to his reward, as Elijah did of old, carried up to heaven in a chariot of fire. I pray his mantle of courage and godliness falls on every one of you left behind, that these shores may never want for brave, pious mariners who count their lives worth nothing if they are spent for their country, their Bible, and their Queen. Amen.”

He reached out to find his friends’ hands, and walked slow out of the churchyard, across the village street and up the lane to Burrough gates, the crowd stepping back to make way for him in solemn silence, as if he was some sacred figure wrapped in the dark of his own strange, heavy fate. He knew the place by muscle memory, unlocked the gate himself, and strode up the gravel path without stumbling, while Cary and Brimblecombe followed trembling, half afraid of a burst of wild grief from him or his mother. He stepped across the threshold, and called soft: “Mother!”

In an instant she was pressed to his chest. Neither spoke for long minutes: she sobbed inwardly, tears never spilling down her face, while he stood firm and steady, his great arms wrapped tight around her.

“Mother,” he said at last, “I’m home, you see. Could not stay away. Will you take me in, look after this useless old carcase? I won’t be very troublesome, I promise. Won’t I?” He smiled down at her, and kissed her forehead.

She said not a single word, but slipped her arm gentle around his waist and led him inside. “Mind your head, dear child. The doorways are low.”

When Amyas turned to call Cary and Brimblecombe inside, the two good men had already slipped away down the path.

“Thank God we’re gone,” Cary said, wiping his eyes on his sleeve. “I’d have blubbered like a babe in another minute, watching that angel of a woman. The way her face worked and she never let a tear fall!”

“Ah, well,” Brimblecombe sighed. “There’s a brave servant of the Queen, his work not a quarter done. I’d best go see my old father now, and then—”

“Then you’re coming home with me,” Cary laughed, clapping him on the back. “We’ve pulled the same oar in the same boat for years, Jack. We’re not parting now you’re ashore. I won’t let you blow all your prize money buying ale for half Bideford for a week running. I’ve got to look after you.”

“Oh, Mr. Cary, what a thing to say!” Brimblecombe sputtered, scandalized. “Come home with me instead, we’ll beg my father the parson to give you the best rectory in the shire.” So the two set off for Clovelly that very day.

Left alone, Amyas settled into the old bay window where he’d sat as a tiny boy, poring over tattered copies of *King Arthur*, Fox’s Book of Martyrs, and the old pamphlet detailing Spanish cruelties across the Atlantic. He reached out, and found the three books sitting exactly where they had lain twenty years before. The window was open, and the cool autumn air carried the same scents he remembered: four-season roses, rosemary, and sweet gillyflower. On the table beside him sat a dish of apples, their sharp sweet scent instantly recognizable—the very same cider apples he used to climb the orchard trees to gather as a boy.

He picked them up one by one, fingering their smooth skins, and all his life flashed through his mind, the way visions are said to flash before a drowning man. He saw every port he’d ever docked in, heard every word ever spoken to him, until he was thinking of that fairy island on the Meta river, the roar of the cataract, the palm fronds glowing green over the spray, the wild girl Ayacanora he’d rescued from the jungle. How he’d wronged her, he thought. Well, he’d been punished, hadn’t he? Who would want to tend a great blind oaf like him, who’d need to be fed and led about like a toddler for the rest of his days?

He fumbled with the apples, and one slipped clean through his fingers, clattering onto the floor. He stooped to feel for it, could not find it, turned too fast, and cracked his head hard against the table leg. That small, silly, trivial frustration snapped every last taut nerve he’d held in check for months. He stamped his foot once in petty irritation, then burst out crying, great heaving sobs wracking his broad frame.

A soft rustle passed his side. The apple was pressed right back into his palm, and Ayacanora’s voice sobbed right next to his ear. “There! There it is! Do not weep, I beg you! I cannot bear it! I will fetch everything you ever want, tend you, feed you, lead you, I will be your slave, your dog! Only let me stay with you!” She fell to her knees at his feet, seizing both his hands and covering them over and over with kisses. “I have you safe now! You cannot sail off to sea, you cannot turn your back on me any more! You cannot escape!” She paused, and her voice broke. “Oh, what a wretch I am, to rejoice that you are blind! I am just a poor wild Indian savage, I know—but I love you, Amyas. I love you more than the sea loves the shore.”

A great, shuddering breath shook Amyas from head to toe. He sat silent for a long minute, and then spoke soft and solemn: “Is this grace still possible? Then God have mercy on me, a sinner.”

He bent down, lifted her as easily as a man lifts a lamb, crushed her to his chest, and kissed her tear-streaked face over and over.

The door creaked open. Ayacanora sprang away from him with a little cry, half trembling and half defiant, as if to say no one on earth could take him from her now.

“Who is there?” Amyas asked.

“Your mother,” Mrs. Leigh said, stepping forward.

“You see I am bringing forth fruits meet for repentance, mother,” Amyas said, smiling. He heard her step closer, felt her soft hand brush his cheek.

“Amyas, my son,” she said, her voice soft and clear as distant angel chimes, “do not fear to hold her to your heart. It was I, your mother, who laid her there for you to find.”

Amyas breathed deep, and pressed Ayacanora tighter to his chest. “It is true, then,” he murmured to himself. “What God has joined together, no man can put asunder.”

From that hour, Ayacanora’s lost gift of song returned to her. Day after day, year after year, her voice rose bright and sweet through the quiet rooms of Burrough House, soaring upward like a lark’s wings, carrying Amyas’s peaceful, happy thoughts back to the green sunlit paradises of the west they had seen together. And all around them, generation after generation of brave English mariners set sail to build a new, greater England across the uncharted ocean, crying the old proud cry that had carried them so far: Westward Ho!
